### [1] [Quantity, quality, and timing: Guiding glacier data assimilation strategies in the high Arctic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08767)
*Wenxue Cao,Kristoffer Aalstad,Louise S. Schmidt,Thomas V. Schuler*

Main category: physics.data-an

TL;DR: This work uses synthetic twin experiments with a Particle Batch Smoother on Kongsvegen glacier to evaluate how observation quantity, quality, and timing affect glacier surface mass balance data assimilation. An optimally timed high-quality observation improves CRPS by up to 80%, while asynchronously timed joint assimilation of albedo, snow depth, and surface temperature sustains 85-97% improvements in the ablation zone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate glacier surface mass balance simulation is critical for predicting sea level rise and freshwater availability, yet it is constrained by uncertainties in meteorological forcing and model parameters. Understanding how to best deploy observations to improve these simulations is essential for designing effective monitoring and reanalysis strategies.

Method: Synthetic twin experiments are conducted on Kongsvegen glacier, Svalbard, using a Particle Batch Smoother with 1000 ensemble members. Synthetic observations of albedo, snow depth, and surface temperature are assimilated at two quality levels, under two climatic scenarios, over 12 years. Assimilation benefit is quantified as the percentage improvement in the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) of the posterior glacier surface mass balance relative to the prior.

Result: A single optimally timed high-quality observation yields mean improvements up to 80%; more low-quality observations partially compensate for lower per-observation gains. Additional snow depth observations in the accumulation zone degrade performance via particle degeneracy. Optimal timing follows seasonal transitions of the truth trajectory (not just prior ensemble spread), with windows shifting up to six weeks between early and late melting years. Asynchronously optimally timed joint assimilation of all three variables sustains 85-97% improvements in the ablation zone.

Conclusion: Observation timing is a dominant factor in assimilation benefit, and optimal scheduling should be guided by expected seasonal dynamics rather than ensemble spread alone. Joint assimilation yields value through temporal diversity rather than simultaneous observational diversity, providing practical guidelines for adaptive observation scheduling in glacier monitoring and reanalysis systems.

Abstract: Accurate simulation of glacier surface mass balance is essential for predicting sea level rise and freshwater resources, but it is constrained by uncertainties in meteorological forcing and model parameters. Here, we deploy glacier data assimilation strategies to assess the value of observations for improving surface mass balance simulation, focusing on observation quantity, quality, and timing. We perform synthetic twin experiments on Kongsvegen glacier, Svalbard, using a Particle Batch Smoother with 1000 ensemble members. Synthetic observations of albedo, snow depth, and surface temperature are assimilated at two quality levels, under two climatic scenarios, and over 12 years. Assimilation benefit is measured as the percentage improvement in the continuous ranked probability score of the posterior glacier surface mass balance relative to the prior. A single optimally timed high quality observation yields mean improvements of up to 80\%. Larger numbers of low quality observations partially compensate for lower improvement. In the accumulation zone, however, additional snow depth observations degrade performance through particle degeneracy. Optimal timing is governed by the seasonal transitions of the truth trajectory rather than by prior ensemble spread alone. The optimal windows shift by up to six weeks between early and late melting years. Joint assimilation adds value through temporal diversity rather than observational diversity, while independently timed observations outperform same day combinations. The asynchronously optimally timed combined assimilation of three variables sustains improvements of 85 to 97\% across all years in the ablation zone. These findings provide guidelines for adaptive observation scheduling in glacier monitoring and reanalysis.

</details>

### [2] [A Heterogeneous General Model for Neuromorphic-Inspired Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05482)
*Matteo Mirigliano*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper introduces a general computational model based on an input-dependent stochastic weight network called a "substrate," where weights evolve through input-triggered stochastic updates with correlations described by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. Implemented using quadratic polynomial weight functions, the model shows that correlations in stochastic weight evolution significantly affect system response, offering a potential mechanism for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional training approaches in computational architectures are computationally expensive, and there is a lack of general models and design guidelines for emerging biological-inspired computational systems and hardware.

Method: The authors introduce a substrate model based on an input-dependent stochastic weight network. Weights evolve through input-triggered stochastic updates, with correlations captured by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. The framework is implemented using quadratic polynomial weight functions, where input amplitude controls stochastic perturbation magnitude and substrate-dependent distance determines correlation structure.

Result: Numerical simulations demonstrate that correlations in stochastic weight evolution significantly affect system response, suggesting a viable mechanism for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training.

Conclusion: The work provides a general formulation of the stochastic weight network model and identifies its main properties and characteristics, aiming to establish design guidelines for emerging computational systems and hardware.

Abstract: In recent years, both academia and industry have focused on the development of computational architectures inspired by the distributed, adaptive, and event-driven characteristics of biological neural systems, with the aim of reducing the computational cost associated with conventional training approaches [1]. However, a major challenge is the lack of general models and design guidelines for emerging computational systems and hardware. This work introduces a general model based on an input-dependent stochastic weight network, referred to as a substrate. The substrate weights evolve through input-triggered stochastic updates, with correlations between weight coefficients described by a matrix-valued covariance kernel. The proposed framework is implemented using quadratic polynomial weight functions, where the input amplitude controls the magnitude of the stochastic perturbation and a substrate-dependent distance determines the correlation structure. Numerical simulations show that correlations in the stochastic weight evolution significantly affect the system response, suggesting a potential mechanism for neuromorphic-inspired computation without conventional weight training. The aim of this work is to provide a general formulation of the model and identify its main properties and characteristics. 1 H. Jaeger, Towards a generalized theory comprising digital, neuromorphic and unconventional computing, Neuromorphic Comput. Eng., vol. 1, no. 1, p. 012002, Sep. 2021

</details>


### [3] [Autonomous Chaotic Time Series Prediction using Physical Neuromorphic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06395)
*Akshaya Rajesh,Yinhao Xu,Wave Ngampruetikorn,Zdenka Kuncic*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This study demonstrates fully autonomous closed-loop prediction of Mackey-Glass chaotic time series using a simulated neuromorphic nanowire network as a physical reservoir. Two strategies are compared: virtual node (VN) temporal multiplexing and a non-VN approach using all physical node readouts. The VN approach achieves ~90% prediction accuracy at two chaos levels over a 100-timestep horizon, while the non-VN approach achieves ~76-82%, and both reproduce qualitative attractor structure over 500 timesteps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether neuromorphic nanowire networks can support brain-inspired information processing through reservoir computing, specifically testing whether their intrinsic emergent nonlinear dynamics are sufficient for autonomous chaotic time series prediction without virtual node augmentation, and extending evaluation to a more complex chaotic regime (τ=21) not previously studied.

Method: A simulated neuromorphic nanowire network serves as the physical reservoir for closed-loop prediction of Mackey-Glass chaotic time series. Two reservoir readout strategies are compared: (1) the virtual node (VN) method using temporal multiplexing to expand feature space, and (2) a non-VN approach reading all physical node outputs directly. Evaluations are performed at two MG delay parameters (τ=18, τ=21) over short (T=100) and long (T=500) prediction horizons.

Result: Over T=100, the VN approach achieves 90.4% and 89.7% accuracy at τ=18 and τ=21, respectively; the non-VN approach achieves 81.5% and 76.2%. Over T=500, both approaches reproduce qualitative attractor structure and dominant spectral content with bounded trajectories. Non-VN performance suggests intrinsic network dynamics suffice for meaningful prediction, with potential for improved performance at hardware-scale node counts (millions).

Conclusion: Neuromorphic nanowire networks can support meaningful autonomous chaotic time series prediction even without virtual node augmentation, suggesting intrinsic dynamics are sufficient computational resources. However, since the study uses simulated networks, experimental validation on physically fabricated large-scale arrays remains necessary.

Abstract: Physical reservoir computing (PRC) with neuromorphic networks offers a promising approach to brain-inspired information processing, exploiting emergent nonlinear dynamics of physical neural networks as a computational resource. This study demonstrates fully autonomous closed-loop prediction of the Mackey--Glass (MG) chaotic time series using a simulated neuromorphic nanowire network as the physical reservoir. Two strategies are evaluated: the virtual node (VN) method, which expands the feature space by temporal multiplexing of reservoir states, and a non-VN approach that uses all physical node readouts directly without temporal multiplexing. Results are reported for two values of the MG time delay parameter, $τ= 18$ and $τ= 21$, the latter representing a more complex chaotic regime not previously evaluated for this class of physical reservoir. Over a short prediction horizon of $T = 100$ timesteps, the VN approach achieves autonomous prediction accuracies of $90.4$% and $89.7$% at $τ= 18$ and $τ= 21$, respectively, while the non-VN approach achieves $81.5$% and $76.2$%. Long-horizon analysis over $T = 500$ timesteps shows that both approaches reproduce the qualitative attractor structure and dominant spectral content of the true MG signal, with trajectories remaining bounded throughout. These results suggest that the intrinsic dynamics of neuromorphic nanowire networks are sufficient to support meaningful autonomous chaotic time series prediction without virtual node augmentation, and that performance may improve further as physical network sizes scale to the millions of nodes achievable in hardware. As this study uses simulated networks, extrapolation to physically fabricated large-scale arrays remains to be validated experimentally.

</details>


### [4] [Finite-rank multiplicative perturbations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07220)
*Pierre Bousseyroux,Marc Potters*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper studies finite-rank multiplicative perturbations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices (models of form A(I+T)), characterizing outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuations, and eigenvector overlaps, providing a multiplicative non-Hermitian analogue of the BBP framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the classical additive BBP (Baik–Ben Arous–Péché) phase transition framework for outlier eigenvalues to multiplicative deformations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices, which have not been systematically studied in this setting.

Method: The authors analyze the model A(I+T) where A is a large rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrix and T is a finite-rank normal perturbation. They characterize the emergence of outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuation behavior, and the associated eigenvector overlaps for this multiplicative deformation structure.

Result: The paper fully characterizes when outlier eigenvalues emerge under multiplicative perturbations, their fluctuation statistics, and the overlaps of corresponding eigenvectors, thereby establishing a multiplicative non-Hermitian counterpart to the classical BBP theory.

Conclusion: The work provides a complete multiplicative non-Hermitian generalization of the BBP phase transition framework, applicable to rotationally invariant ensembles.

Abstract: We study finite-rank multiplicative deformations of rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrices. More precisely, we consider models of the form $\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{T})$, where $\mathbf{A}$ is a large rotationally invariant non-Hermitian random matrix, $\mathbf{T}$ is a finite-rank normal perturbation, and $\mathbf{I}$ denotes the identity matrix. We characterize the emergence of outlier eigenvalues, their fluctuations, and the associated eigenvector overlaps. Our results provide a multiplicative non-Hermitian counterpart to the classical Baik--Ben Arous--Péché framework.

</details>


### [5] [Storing Infinite Dynamical Attractors in Nonreciprocal Associative Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07341)
*Miguel Aguilera,Daniele De Martino*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper develops a dynamical mean-field theory for nonreciprocal associative networks capable of storing extensive numbers of dynamical attractors (from limit cycles to strange attractors). The authors derive self-consistent mean-field equations via path integral methods under quenched disorder and show that memory retrieval capacity is governed by the spectral structure of coupling matrices. Coherently aligned eigenvalues lead to destructive feedback between retarded self-interactions and quenched noise—recovering classical capacity bounds at zero eigenphase but collapsing for limit cycles. Uniformly distributed eigenphases cancel retarded self-interactions and most quenched noise, reducing the system to an effective single-spin process and substantially amplifying storage capacity. Theory is validated against microscopic simulations for limit-cycle and chaotic attractors, identifying eigenvalue decoherence as the mechanism enabling enhanced dynamical memory storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify how nonreciprocal (asymmetric) associative neural networks can store dynamical attractors—such as limit cycles and chaotic attractors—beyond the classical fixed-point memory framework, and to identify the mechanism that controls their storage capacity.

Method: The authors employ a dynamical mean-field theory approach using a path integral formalism under quenched disorder. They derive self-consistent dynamical mean-field equations for pattern overlaps, autocorrelations, and response functions, analyzing how the spectral structure (eigenvalue distribution) of the coupling matrices encoding stored patterns governs retrieval capacity.

Result: For coherently aligned eigenphases, retarded self-interactions and quenched noise interfere destructively: at zero eigenphase (fixed-point attractors), the classical equilibrium capacity bound is recovered, while retrieval capacity collapses for limit cycles. For uniformly distributed eigenphases, retarded self-interactions and most quenched noise cancel, reducing dynamics to an effective single-spin process and substantially amplifying capacity. Microscopic simulations for limit-cycle and chaotic attractors validate the theory.

Conclusion: Eigenvalue decoherence (uniformly distributed eigenphases of the coupling matrix) is identified as the mechanism enabling enhanced storage of dynamical memories, as it mitigates destructive feedback and effectively suppresses quenched noise, boosting the system's storage capacity well beyond classical limits.

Abstract: We develop a dynamical mean-field theory for nonreciprocal associative networks that store an extensive number of dynamical attractors, from limit cycles to strange attractors. Using a path integral calculation under quenched disorder, we derive self-consistent dynamical mean-field equations for pattern overlaps, autocorrelations and response functions. Memory retrieval capacity is governed by the spectral structure of the coupling matrices encoding stored patterns. When their eigenvalues are coherently aligned, retarded self-interactions and quenched noise feed back destructively: at zero eigenphase (fixed point attractors) the classical equilibrium capacity bound is recovered, while for limit cycles retrieval collapses far below it. In contrast, for uniformly distributed eigenphases, retarded self-interactions and much of the quenched noise cancels, reducing the dynamics to an effective single-spin process and amplifying capacity substantially. We validate the theory against microscopic simulations for limit-cycle and chaotic attractors, identifying eigenvalue decoherence as the mechanism enabling enhanced storage of dynamical memories.

</details>


### [6] [Graph neural networks and the energetic cavity method for combinatorial optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07456)
*Joe Bacchus George,George T. Cantwell*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper studies GNNs for approximating ground states of Ising models, finding that vanilla GNNs underperform classical heuristics (eigenvector-based and min-sum/cavity methods). Incorporating these heuristics into GNN architectures via small modifications considerably improves performance, making them competitive with other deep learning approaches, but simulated annealing remains at least as good for equal computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding approximate ground states of Ising models is broadly significant because many combinatorial optimization problems can be formulated as Ising models. While exact solutions are hard, existing heuristic methods such as mean-field approximations (leading eigenvector methods and the min-sum/energetic cavity method) perform well, whereas unmodified GNNs underperform these classical baselines. The authors seek to bridge this gap.

Method: The authors compare vanilla GNNs against two mean-field-based heuristics: an eigenvector approach and the min-sum algorithm (energetic cavity method). They introduce small architectural modifications to GNNs that incorporate these classical heuristics (likely by informing message passing or loss structure with these priors), and evaluate performance against deep learning baselines and simulated annealing.

Result: Unmodified GNNs perform worse than both eigenvector-based and min-sum heuristics. Incorporating the heuristics into GNNs considerably improves performance, making the modified approach competitive with other deep-learning approaches. However, simulated annealing is reliably at least as good as all deep learning methods at matched computational cost.

Conclusion: While the hybrid GNN-heuristic approach improves over plain GNNs and is competitive among deep learning methods, the practical advantage over classical methods is limited: simulated annealing remains the strongest baseline at equivalent computational cost, questioning the practical value of deep learning for this problem class without further innovation.

Abstract: We study the use of graph neural networks (GNNs) for finding approximate ground states of Ising models. Efficiently finding these ground states is of broad significance because many combinatorial optimization problems can be formulated as an Ising model with the appropriate choice of couplings and fields. Exactly solving these problems is hard but there are many good heuristic methods. A lineage of these heuristics build from mean-field approximations: one approach uses the leading eigenvector of an appropriately defined matrix, another is the min-sum algorithm, also known as the energetic cavity method. Without modification, GNNs perform worse than both of these methods. We consider small modifications to the GNN to incorporate these heuristics and find that this considerably improves performance. While the modified approach is competitive against other deep-learning approaches, we still find that simulated annealing is reliably at least as good as deep learning methods for the same computational cost.

</details>


### [7] [A Bifurcation Theory for the Equilibria of Modern Hopfield Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07757)
*Vincenzo Maria Schimmenti,Matteo Ciarchi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This paper develops a general bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks (MHNs), deriving stability and bifurcation criteria for fixed points under arbitrary statistics of stored patterns. It shows how memory correlations systematically produce hierarchical attractors through successive bifurcations, validated on MNIST and hematopoietic cell data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern Hopfield networks unify associative memory, transformer attention, diffusion models, and biological attractor dynamics through common energy-based dynamics. While the organization of the energy landscape governs network dynamics in all these settings, a general theory of fixed-point bifurcations has remained unavailable beyond specific architectures and idealized pattern ensembles.

Method: The authors derive stability and bifurcation criteria for fixed points of general convex-dual Modern Hopfield networks under general stored-pattern statistics. They apply this framework to random, block-correlated, and infinitely hierarchical pattern ensembles, showing how memory correlations organize hierarchical attractor emergence through successive bifurcations.

Result: The derived criteria quantitatively describe retrieval bifurcations in MHNs storing MNIST patterns and recapitulate the hierarchical organization of hematopoietic cell identities.

Conclusion: The results establish a general bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks and identify fixed-point organization as a unifying principle underlying their computational and biological behavior.

Abstract: Modern Hopfield networks provide a unifying framework for associative memory, transformer attention, diffusion-based generative models, and biological attractor dynamics, linking these systems through a common energy-based dynamics in which states are updated toward weighted combinations of stored patterns. Across these settings, network dynamics is determined by the organization of the energy landscape and the bifurcations of its fixed points. Despite their central role, a general theory of these bifurcations has remained unavailable beyond specific architectures and idealized pattern ensembles. Here we derive stability and bifurcation criteria for the fixed points of general convex-dual Modern Hopfield networks for a general statistics of the stored patterns. Applying this framework to random, block-correlated, and infinitely hierarchical pattern ensembles, we show how memory correlations systematically organize the emergence of hierarchical attractors through successive bifurcations. We further demonstrate that these predictions quantitatively describe retrieval bifurcations in MHNs storing patterns sampled from MNIST, and recapitulate the hierarchical organization of hematopoietic cell identities. Our results establish a general bifurcation theory for Modern Hopfield networks and identify the organization of fixed points as a unifying principle underlying their computational and biological behavior.

</details>
