### [8] [Superdiffusive two-dimensional superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05447)
*Iogann Tolbatov,Luca Salasnich*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper formulates a non-local field-theoretic description of 2D superconductors using the Riesz fractional Laplacian, showing that vortex-antivortex interactions transition from logarithmic to power-law confinement, which suppresses thermal vortex proliferation and stabilizes true long-range order at finite temperatures despite Mermin-Wagner considerations. The framework also yields a fractional London equation connecting to the anomalous Pippard limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the standard local-only-phase (Popov) description of 2D superconductors to include non-local physics via fractional operators, thereby addressing limitations of conventional Kosterlitz-Thouless phenomenology and the Mermin-Wagner restriction in two dimensions, while also providing a unified framework for anomalous non-local electrodynamics.

Method: Generalizes the Popov action by replacing the spatial Laplacian with the Riesz fractional Laplacian $-(-\nabla^2)^{\alpha/2}$ for $1<\alpha<2$. Proves the vortex-antivortex interaction becomes power-law confined, $V(r)\propto r^{2-\alpha}$, via a generalized Kosterlitz-Thouless energetic-entropic analysis. Constructs a gauge-invariant fractional action to derive a fractional London equation yielding a real-space power-law kernel.

Result: Demonstrates that non-local (power-law) confinement strictly suppresses thermal vortex proliferation and stabilizes true long-range order at finite temperatures. The derived fractional London equation produces a real-space power-law kernel that maps onto the anomalous Pippard limit for superconductors with long coherence lengths.

Conclusion: The fractional/non-local framework provides a unified phenomenological model connecting non-local electrodynamics (Pippard regime) with topological phase behavior in 2D superconductors, circumventing Mermin-Wagner constraints and offering a consistent description of both phase fluctuations and electromagnetic response.

Abstract: We formulate a non-local, field-theoretic description of phase fluctuations and topological transitions in two-dimensional (2D) superconductors by generalizing the local only-phase Popov action to a framework utilizing non-local fractional operators. By replacing the standard spatial Laplacian with the Riesz fractional Laplacian $-(-\nabla^2)^{α/2}$ (where $1<α<2$), we prove that the vortex-antivortex interaction transitions from logarithmic confinement to a stronger power-law confinement of the form $V(r)\propto r^{2-α}$. Through a generalized Kosterlitz-Thouless energetic-entropic analysis, we demonstrate that this non-local confinement strictly suppresses thermal vortex proliferation. Because the non-local operator safely places the system outside the strict domain of validity of the Mermin-Wagner restriction, this power-law confinement natively stabilizes true long-range order at finite temperatures. Finally, by constructing a gauge-invariant fractional action, we formulate a fractional London equation. This approach yields a real-space power-law kernel that maps directly onto the anomalous Pippard limit of superconductors with long coherence lengths, providing a unified phenomenologically motivated framework for non-local electrodynamics.

</details>


### [9] [Pair density wave order from non-symmorphic momentum symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05603)
*Matteo Dürrnagel,C. Alexander Baum,Michael Klett,Lennart Klebl,Ronny Thomale*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper develops microscopic lattice models where pair density wave (PDW) order emerges in the asymptotically exact weak-coupling limit, driven by a non-symmorphic momentum-space symmetry from a projective space group representation of electrons. Moiré systems and extended s-wave altermagnets are proposed as potential PDW hosts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a controlled microscopic mechanism for the emergence of pair density wave order, which is often theoretically challenging to establish from weak coupling.

Method: Construction of microscopic lattice models with projective space group representations of electrons, exploiting non-symmorphic momentum symmetry to stabilize PDW order in the asymptotically exact weak-coupling limit.

Result: PDW order is rigorously shown to emerge in these lattice models in the weak-coupling regime, and moiré systems and extended s-wave altermagnets are identified as concrete candidate material platforms.

Conclusion: A general and controlled weak-coupling route to PDW order is established, rooted in non-symmorphic projective symmetries, with experimentally relevant predictions for moiré materials and altermagnets.

Abstract: We develop a class of microscopic lattice models in which pair density wave order emerges in the asymptotically exact weak coupling limit. The underlying mechanism is due to non-symmorphic momentum symmetry implied by the models' projective space group representation of electrons. Our mechanism suggests moiré systems and extended s-wave altermagnets as potential hosts for pair density wave order.

</details>


### [10] [Extended strange metal regime in a pure nematic quantum critical superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05875)
*Kousuke Ishida,Kiyotaka Mukasa,Shusaku Imajo,Andrew Hardy,Mingwei Qiu,Mikihiko Saito,Aavishkar A. Patel,Kohei Matsuura,Yuichi Sugimura,Yu Uezono,Takumi Otsuka,Nigel E. Hussey,Takao Watanabe,Koichi Kindo,Takasada Shibauchi*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using high pulsed magnetic fields, the authors uncovered a strange metallic ground state hidden below the superconducting dome of nonmagnetic FeSe1-xTex, which hosts a quantum critical point (QCP) of pure electronic nematicity. Unlike conventional quantum criticality, this strange metallic state spans a wide compositional range rather than appearing in a fan-shaped region above a singular QCP. Disorder-induced spatial randomness of nematic fluctuations likely broadens the QCP into an extended critical region, enhancing superconducting pairing—in contrast to the cleaner FeSe1-xSx system which exhibits a conventional quantum critical fan.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the origin of strange metallicity (linear-in-temperature resistivity) and how it promotes high-temperature superconductivity, particularly testing whether magnetic critical fluctuations are required or whether pure electronic nematic (nonmagnetic) critical fluctuations can drive the effect.

Method: High pulsed magnetic field experiments were used to suppress superconductivity and expose the hidden normal-state ground state in nonmagnetic FeSe1-xTex and FeSe1-xSx systems. Observations were compared with hybrid Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to account for disorder effects on the nematic QCP.

Result: A strange metallic ground state was found below the superconducting dome of FeSe1-xTex, spanning a wide compositional range rather than a narrow fan around a QCP. This broadened quantum critical region contrasts with the quantum critical fan observed in cleaner FeSe1-xSx. The difference is attributed to disorder-induced spatial randomness of nematic fluctuations, which enlarges the QCP into an extended region of criticality, consistent with hybrid Quantum Monte Carlo simulations.

Conclusion: Superconductivity in FeSe1-xTex is promoted by a unique interplay between pure nematic critical fluctuations, strange metallicity, and disorder. This provides new insight into the emergence of non-Fermi-liquid transport and superconductivity in correlated materials, indicating that magnetic fluctuations are not strictly necessary for strange metallicity.

Abstract: High-temperature superconductivity often emerges from a strange metallic state, where the electrical resistivity exhibits a linear-in-temperature dependence over an anomalously extended temperature range. The prevailing belief is that magnetic critical fluctuations gives rise to strange metallicity, enhancing the superconducting transition temperature. Here, using high pulsed magnetic fields, we have uncovered the strange metallic ground state hidden below the superconducting dome of nonmagnetic FeSe$_{1-x}$Te$_{x}$, which harbors a quantum critical point (QCP) of pure electronic nematicity, characterized by spontaneous rotational symmetry breaking. Unlike the conventional quantum criticality, this strange metallic state does not appear in a fan-shaped region above the singular QCP but spans a wide compositional range, where pairing interactions are strengthened by nonmagnetic nematic critical fluctuations. This stands in sharp contrast to the much cleaner system FeSe$_{1-x}$S$_{x}$, which displays a quantum critical fan above nematic QCP, indicating that disorder-induced spatial randomness of the nematic fluctuations likely enlarges the QCP of FeSe$_{1-x}$Te$_{x}$ into an extended region of criticality, as suggested by relevant hybrid Quantum Monte Carlo simulations. These observations highlight superconductivity promoted by a unique interplay between pure nematic critical fluctuations, strange metallicity and disorder, providing new insight into the emergence of non-Fermi-liquid transport in various correlated materials.

</details>


### [11] [Microscopic calculation of coherence lengths and magnetic penetration depth in multiband superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06014)
*Tristan Ryoma Fuchs,Takuya Nomoto,Hikaru Watanabe,Ryotaro Arita*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper presents an extended Ginzburg-Landau (GL) method that calculates superconducting coherence length and magnetic penetration depth at temperatures well below Tc by expanding only the covariant-gradient terms perturbatively while retaining full dependence on order parameters. This approach works for both single-band and multiband superconductors, agrees well with real-space Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) calculations over a wide temperature range, and requires substantially less computational effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional GL theory, which expands the free energy in both order parameters and their gradients, cannot accurately describe superconducting properties well below the transition temperature Tc. A more efficient and accurate framework is needed that extends GL theory to low temperatures and accommodates realistic electronic structures including multiband superconductors.

Method: The method uses a perturbative expansion applied only to the covariant-gradient terms rather than to order parameters themselves, preserving full dependence on superconducting order parameters. Coefficients of these gradient terms are obtained from finite differences of microscopic free energies computed at small imposed pair momenta. The framework is applicable to both single-band and multiband superconductors.

Result: The extended GL method agrees well with real-space Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) calculations over a wide temperature range for the models examined, while achieving this accuracy with substantially reduced computational cost.

Conclusion: The extended GL method provides a computationally efficient and accurate framework for calculating superconducting coherence lengths and magnetic penetration depths well below Tc, and offers a pathway to incorporate more realistic electronic structures through its support for both single-band and multiband superconductors.

Abstract: We present an extended Ginzburg-Landau (GL) method for calculating the superconducting coherence length and magnetic penetration depth at temperatures well below the transition temperature $T_{\mathrm c}$. In contrast to conventional GL theory, which expands the free energy in both the order parameters and their gradients, our method applies a perturbative expansion only to the covariant-gradient terms, while retaining the full dependence on the superconducting order parameters. The coefficients of these terms are determined from finite differences of microscopic free energies evaluated at small imposed pair momenta. The method applies to both single-band and multiband superconductors and therefore provides a framework for incorporating more realistic electronic structures. For the models examined here, the extended GL method agrees well with real-space Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) calculations over a wide temperature range, while requiring substantially less computational effort.

</details>


### [12] [Effect of non-homogeneous non-magnetic Impurities in Superconductors: A comparison between Microscopic and Macroscopic theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06236)
*Carlos Redondo Herrero,Tejas Guruswamy,Orlando Quaranta,Akira Miyazaki*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper theoretically compares microscopic (Eilenberger) and macroscopic (London) approaches for modeling non-magnetic impurities in superconductors, showing they produce identical field distributions but different field-limiting quantities. The microscopic theory yields the experimentally relevant superheating field, and specific impurity profiles can raise it to the maximum achievable value equal to the critical field H_c.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-homogeneous non-magnetic impurities in superconductors are promising for reducing surface resistance and increasing the superheating field, but their role requires deeper theoretical understanding. The authors aim to validate the applicability of a simpler London-based phenomenological approach versus the full microscopic Eilenberger formalism.

Method: The authors employ Eilenberger's equation for the microscopic theory and London's equation for the macroscopic/phenomenological approach. They compare the electromagnetic field distributions and magnetic field-related quantities predicted by both theories for arbitrary spatial impurity distributions, and search for impurity profiles that optimize the superheating field.

Result: Both approaches produce identical electromagnetic field distributions for any impurity spatial distribution. However, they differ in field-related quantities: the macroscopic model only captures the Bean-Livingston barrier, while the microscopic theory provides the superheating field. The microscopic formalism identifies a family of impurity profiles achieving H_sh equal to the maximum possible value, the critical field H_c.

Conclusion: The microscopic (Eilenberger) theory is necessary for computing the experimentally relevant superheating field, while the London-based description is insufficient despite matching field distributions. Impurity engineering can, in principle, push the superheating field to the thermodynamic critical field H_c, the theoretical maximum.

Abstract: Non-homogeneous, non-magnetic impurities in superconductivity have recently gained traction as a promising approach to reduce surface resistance and increase the superheating field. We aim to deepen the understanding of impurities' role from a theoretical perspective by utilizing Eilenberger's equation, and to investigate the validity of a more phenomenological approach based on London's equation for determining the magnetic field profile within the superconductor. We show that the microscopic and macroscopic theories produce identical electromagnetic field distributions for any spatial distributions of impurities inside the superconductors. However, the two approaches provide different magnetic field-related quantities. The macroscopic model only gives the Bean-Livingston barrier, while the microscopic theory provides the experimentally-relevant superheating field. Based on the microscopic formalism, we determine a family of impurity profiles that make the superheating field $H_{\rm{sh}}$ equal to the critical field $H_c$, which is the maximum achievable.

</details>


### [13] [$^{139}$La nuclear quadrupole resonance studies of pressurized La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06453)
*Meng Zhang,Zhuo Wang,Yantao Cao,Yang Yuan,Kangjian Luo,Shanxiang Gao,Hanjie Guo,Yongkang Luo*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper uses 139La nuclear quadrupole resonance to investigate density-wave (DW) orders in the nickelate superconductor La4Ni3O10, finding that spin and charge DW orders are intertwined, that short-range DW order emerges near 150 K before long-range order at ~139 K, and that applying 2.3 GPa hydrostatic pressure suppresses these transitions at rates of 1 K/GPa and 10 K/GPa, respectively, confirming DW orders compete with superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Density-wave orders are considered competing orders to unconventional superconductivity and appear in many superconducting families including the recently discovered Ruddlesden-Popper-phase nickelates. Understanding the nature of these DW orders and their evolution under pressure is essential for clarifying the interplay between DW orders and superconductivity in this new material family.

Method: The authors employ 139La nuclear quadrupole resonance (NQR) to systematically probe the nature of DW orders in La4Ni3O10 and track their evolution under applied hydrostatic pressure (up to 2.3 GPa), resolving both spin and charge DW components and their associated transition temperatures.

Result: Spin and charge DW orders are found to be intertwined in La4Ni3O10, contrasting with the behavior in La3Ni2O7. Short-range DW order appears near 150 K, well above the long-range DW order transition at ~139 K. Under 2.3 GPa hydrostatic pressure, the short-range and long-range order transition temperatures decrease at rates of 1 K/GPa and 10 K/GPa, respectively.

Conclusion: The results affirm that both spin density wave and charge density wave orders act as competing orders with the superconducting state in La4Ni3O10, providing new insight into how DW order and unconventional superconductivity interact in Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates.

Abstract: Density-wave (DW) orders are considered as competing orders to unconventional superconductivity and are commonly seen in a variety of superconductors including but not limited to the recently discovered Ruddlesden-Popper-phase nickelates. By utilizing $^{139}$La nuclear quadrupole resonance, we systematically investigate into the nature of DW orders and their evolution under pressure in La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$. Spin and charge DW orders are found to be intertwined in this material, which is in stark contrast to those in La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$. Short-range DW orders are observed near 150 K, well above the development of long-range DW orders at around 139 K. Upon applying a hydrostatic pressure of 2.3 GPa, the transition temperatures of the short-range and long-range orders decrease at rates of 1 K/GPa and 10 K/GPa, respectively. Our results thus affirm that both spin density wave and charge density wave as competing orders with the superconducting state in La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$, and provide new insights into the interplay between DW orders and unconventional superconductivity.

</details>


### [14] [Discovery of Superconductivity in a Bulk Moire Superlattice Material](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06622)
*Subham Naik,Paul Monson,Susanta Manna,Prabuddhakant Mishra,Soumyojit Chatterjee,Sandip Kuila,Partha Pratim Jana,M. B. Sreedhara,Rahul Sharma,Gohil S. Thakur*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reports the discovery of bulk superconductivity (Tc = 2.5 K) in single crystals of the intrinsically grown bulk moiré material (Sr6TaS8)1+x(TaS2)8 under ambient pressure, confirmed by transport, magnetic susceptibility, and heat-capacity measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Moiré materials generate emergent electronic states via flat-band-enhanced correlations, but most studies have relied on artificially assembled van der Waals heterostructures. Intrinsically grown bulk moiré crystals offer a complementary platform, and demonstrating emergent correlated phases like superconductivity in such bulk systems is a key open question.

Method: Electrical transport, magnetic susceptibility, and heat-capacity measurements were performed on single-crystal and polycrystalline samples of (Sr6TaS8)1+x(TaS2)8 under ambient pressure to characterize its superconducting and other electronic transitions.

Result: The material exhibits a bulk superconducting transition at Tc = 2.5 K, consistently evidenced across all three measurement techniques. Transport also reveals a pronounced anomaly near 270 K suggestive of a charge-density-wave transition.

Conclusion: The observation establishes superconductivity as an emergent phase in intrinsically synthesized bulk moiré materials, validating them as a promising platform for exploring correlated quantum phenomena beyond artificially assembled 2D heterostructures.

Abstract: Moire materials provide a versatile platform realizing emergent electronic states arising from enhanced correlation due to flat bands. A variety of phenomena including superconductivity, low dimensional ferromagnetism, Mott insulating phase, topological phenomena have been reported in such systems. To date, moire phenomena have been predominantly explored in artificially assembled low-dimensional van der Waals heterostructures, where relative twist and lattice alignment are controlled during device fabrication. Recently, intrinsically grown bulk moire crystals have emerged as a complementary materials platform, in which lattice mismatch between constituent layers generates a coherent moire superlattice throughout the bulk crystal. Here we report the evidence of bulk superconductivity in single crystals of a recently reported bulk Moire materials (Sr6TaS8)1+x(TaS2)8 under ambient pressure conditions. The material exhibits a superconducting transition at Tc = 2.5 K, evidenced consistently by electrical transport, magnetic susceptibility, and heat-capacity measurements on single-crystal and polycrystalline samples. Transport measurements reveal a pronounced anomaly near 270 K, suggestive of a charge-density-wave transition in this moire system. The observation of bulk superconductivity establishes superconductivity as an emergent phase in this intrinsically synthesized moire material and highlights bulk moire crystals as a promising platform for investigating correlated quantum phenomena beyond artificially assembled two-dimensional heterostructures.

</details>


### [15] [Symmetry-selective field-induced triplet superconductivity in Ising-superconductor monolayer NbSe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06752)
*Sudipta Biswas,Sudhansu S. Mandal,A. Taraphder*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The authors investigate Ising superconductor monolayer NbSe2 under an in-plane Zeeman field using self-consistent Bogoliubov-de Gennes calculations, finding that the field induces symmetry-selected equal-spin triplet pairing whose symmetry is inherited from the parent singlet state. Nonlocal chiral-d pairing favors a chiral-p equal-spin triplet component with opposite chirality, and Rashba spin-orbit coupling suppresses superconductivity and reduces the critical field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how in-plane magnetic fields in Ising superconductors like monolayer NbSe2 can induce symmetry-selected equal-spin triplet pairing, and to establish a direct link between the symmetry of the parent singlet condensate and the emergent triplet superconductivity for field-tunable mixed-parity superconducting states.

Method: The authors employ a self-consistent Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) approach with realistic hopping parameters for monolayer NbSe2 on a triangular lattice. They determine energetically favored singlet pairing states in a range of chemical potentials across onsite, nearest-neighbor, and next-nearest-neighbor pairing channels, perform momentum-resolved analysis of order parameters near Gamma, K, and K' points, and map the phase diagram in the (h_x, mu) plane. Triplet pairing is induced by opening the triplet interaction channel under an in-plane magnetic field.

Result: The in-plane Zeeman field induces triplet pairing with symmetry dictated by the parent singlet state. Nonlocal chiral-d pairing is energetically favored and induces a chiral-p equal-spin triplet component with opposite chirality. Rashba spin-orbit coupling suppresses superconducting orders and reduces the critical field.

Conclusion: The results establish a direct symmetry correspondence between parent singlet condensates and emergent triplet superconductivity, identifying monolayer NbSe2 as a promising platform for field-tunable mixed-parity superconducting states.

Abstract: We investigate superconductivity in monolayer NbSe$_2$, an Ising superconductor, under an in-plane Zeeman field $h_x$, focusing on the emergence of symmetry selected equal-spin triplet pairing. Using a self-consistent Bogoliubov--de Gennes approach with realistic hopping parameters for monolayer NbSe$_2$ on a triangular lattice, we determine the energetically favored singlet pairing states in a range of chemical potentials $μ$ for onsite, nearest-neighbor, and next-nearest-neighbor pairing channels, and map the resulting phase diagram in $(h_x,μ)$ plane. A momentum-resolved analysis reveals distinct dominant contributions in order parameters arise from the surroundings of $Γ$, $K$, and $K'$ points of the Brillouin zone. We find that an in-plane magnetic field helps to induce a triplet pairing whose symmetry is determined by the parent singlet state, upon opening the triplet interaction channel. For the nonlocal pairing channels, chiral-$d$ pairing is energetically favored and it induces chiral-$p$ equal-spin triplet component with opposite chirality. We further find that Rashba spin-orbit coupling, relevant to substrate coupling and electrostatic gating, suppresses the superconducting orders and reduces the critical field. Our results establish a direct link between the symmetry of the parent singlet condensate and the emergent triplet superconductivity, highlighting monolayer NbSe$_2$ as a promising platform for field-tunable mixed-parity superconducting states.

</details>


### [16] [Nearly Isotropic Vortex Solid in $\mathbf{(La,Pr)_{3}Ni_{2}O_{7}}$ Thin Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07510)
*Yaolong Bian,Yaqi Chen,Heng Wang,Guangdi Zhou,Fei Peng,Zichen Lv,Jiaqiang Cai,Yifan Chen,Wenjie Meng,Ze Wang,Haoliang Huang,Daohua Zhang,Mingliang Tian,Jinfeng Jia,Qi-kun Xue,Zhuoyu Chen,Jinglei Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Through simultaneous resistance and diamagnetism measurements on (La,Pr)3Ni2O7 thin films, the authors map the vortex melting phase diagram and find that the anisotropy parameter γ_Hm approaches unity at low temperatures, indicating nearly isotropic superfluid density. This contradicts strictly 2D dx2-y2 bands but is naturally explained by a substantial interlayer superfluid contribution from the dz2-derived γ band, providing thermodynamic evidence for the γ band's role in bilayer nickelate superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The discovery of superconductivity in bulk bilayer nickelates provides a new platform for studying high-Tc superconductivity beyond cuprates. However, the role of the Ni 3dz2-derived γ band in the superconducting mechanism remains unresolved, motivating this investigation.

Method: The authors performed simultaneous resistance and diamagnetism measurements on (La,Pr)3Ni2O7 thin films to map the vortex melting phase diagram for both in-plane and out-of-plane magnetic fields, and used anisotropic Ginzburg-Landau scaling to analyze the anisotropy parameter of the vortex melting field.

Result: For H∥c, geometric confinement creates pancake vortices. The anisotropy parameter γ_Hm ≡ Hm^ab/Hm^c decreases monotonically with decreasing temperature and approaches unity at low temperatures. Within anisotropic GL scaling, this corresponds to γ_Hm = √(ρs^ab/ρs^c), indicating nearly isotropic superfluid density.

Conclusion: The nearly isotropic superfluid density is incompatible with strictly two-dimensional dx2-y2-derived bands but is naturally explained by a substantial interlayer superfluid contribution from the dz2-derived γ band. This provides thermodynamic evidence for the γ band's significant contribution to superconductivity in bilayer nickelate thin films.

Abstract: The discovery of superconductivity in bulk bilayer nickelates has established a new platform for exploring high-$T_c$ superconductivity beyond the cuprates. The role of the Ni $3d_{z^2}$-derived $γ$ band in the superconductivity of bilayer nickelates remains unresolved. By performing simultaneous resistance and diamagnetism measurements on (La,Pr)$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ thin films, we map the vortex melting phase diagram for both in-plane and out-of-plane magnetic fields. For $H\parallel c$, the geometric confinement effect gives rise to pancake vortices. Remarkably, the anisotropy parameter of the vortex melting field $γ_{H_m} \equiv H_m^{ab}/H_m^c$ decreases monotonically with decreasing temperature and approaches unity at low temperatures. Within the anisotropic Ginzburg--Landau scaling, $H_m^{ab}/H_m^c = \sqrt{ρ_s^{ab}/ρ_s^c}$ tracks the superfluid-density anisotropy. Such a vortex solid implies a nearly isotropic superfluid density, which is irreconcilable with the strictly two-dimensional $3d_{x^2-y^2}$-derived bands, but naturally explained by a substantial interlayer superfluid contribution from the $3d_{z^2}$-derived $γ$ band. Our results provide thermodynamic evidence for a substantial contribution of the $γ$ band to superconductivity in bilayer nickelate thin films.

</details>


### [17] [Doping-controlled topological superconducting transition in misfit layer compounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07545)
*Hugo Le Du,Robin Salvatore,Justine Cordiez,Ludovica Zullo,Arindam Mukherjee,Daniel Schmieg,Dominik Volavka,Francois Debontridder,Marie Herve,Tomas Samuely,Shunsuke Sasaki,Florent Pawula,Etienne Janod,Laurent Cario,Tristan Cren*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a doping-controlled topological quantum phase transition in the misfit compound (LaxPb1-xSe)1.14(NbSe2)2, revealing two distinct superconducting regimes separated by a non-superconducting phase, with disorder sensitivity and edge-mode signatures consistent with time-reversal-symmetric crystalline topological superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To find a robust platform that allows continuous tuning of a material through a topological quantum phase transition, enabling direct observation of the evolution from trivial to topological superconductivity—a key step toward fault-tolerant quantum computers.

Method: Doping-controlled tuning of the bulk misfit layer compound (LaxPb1-xSe)1.14(NbSe2)2 across a topological phase transition, combined with spectroscopic investigation of the superconducting states and Bogoliubov-de Gennes calculations to interpret emergent spatial signatures.

Result: A non-monotonic phase diagram with two distinct superconducting regimes separated by a non-superconducting phase at a precise doping. In the highly doped regime, superconductivity becomes highly sensitive to non-magnetic disorder and exhibits orientation-selective in-gap modes at atomic step edges, consistent with a time-reversal-symmetric crystalline-topological order parameter.

Conclusion: Misfit layer compounds are established as a viable platform for engineering topological superconductivity, providing a controllable route to drive and observe topological quantum phase transitions in the bulk.

Abstract: Achieving topological superconductivity is a key goal in quantum physics, offering a path to fault-tolerant quantum computers. A central challenge in this field is to continuously drive a material through a topological quantum phase transition to directly observe the evolution from trivial to topological superconductivity. However, finding a robust platform that allows such extreme and precise tuning remains a challenge. Here, we demonstrate a doping-controlled phase transition from a conventional to a topological superconducting state in the bulk misfit layer compound (LaxPb1-xSe)1.14(NbSe2)2. We reveal a non-monotonic phase diagram characterized by two distinct superconducting regimes separated by a non-superconducting phase at a precise doping. In the highly doped regime, the superconducting phase becomes remarkably sensitive to non-magnetic disorder, and orientation-selective in-gap modes emerge at atomic step edges. Supported by Bogoliubov-de Gennes calculations, these emergent spatial signatures are consistent with a time-reversal-symmetric crystalline-topological order parameter. Our results establish misfit compounds as a platform to engineering topological superconductivity.

</details>


### [18] [Interference-controlled magnetic bound states and subgap transport in a Square--octagon superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07546)
*Ravi Kiran*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper studies magnetic adatoms on an s-wave superconducting square-octagon lattice, revealing that flat-band spectral weight does not automatically imply strong adatom-host coupling. Symmetric square-hollow adsorption suppresses hybridization with the flat band due to destructive interference, while top and octagon-hollow sites retain strong coupling, leading to distinct magnetic bound states and nonlocal subgap conductance signatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how adsorption geometry in a multisublattice superconductor (the square-octagon lattice) controls the interplay between flat/narrow-band states, impurity-host hybridization, and subgap transport for magnetic adatoms.

Method: The authors study magnetic adatoms on an s-wave superconducting square-octagon lattice, analyzing distinct adsorption geometries (square-hollow, top, octagon-hollow), examining the spectral weight, hybridization with flat-band manifolds, magnetic bound states, and nonlocal conductance in a normal-superconductor-normal junction.

Result: Square-hollow adsorption suffers destructive interference that suppresses overlap with the flat band (yielding an atomic-like bound state), whereas top and octagon-hollow sites retain strong hybridization. In an NSN geometry, square-hollow suppresses low-bias conductance relative to the clean device while octagon-hollow enhances it, with markedly different dependence on exchange splitting. These effects persist as bands flatten and remain robust in realistic parameter regimes.

Conclusion: Magnetic-adatom responses in flat/narrow-band superconductors are governed not just by spectral weight availability but critically by the symmetry and wave-function structure of the impurity-host hybridization, highlighting geometry-selective control of subgap transport.

Abstract: The square--octagon lattice provides a useful setting for studying magnetic adatoms because its multisublattice structure allows different adsorption geometries to probe distinct combinations of the host electronic states. We study magnetic adatoms in an $s$-wave superconducting square--octagon lattice, focusing on the interplay between adsorption geometry, narrow and flat-band states, and subgap transport. We find that the large spectral weight associated with a flat band does not necessarily imply strong impurity--host coupling. For symmetric square-hollow adsorption, destructive interference strongly suppresses the overlap with the flat-band manifold, whereas top and octagon-hollow adsorption retain substantial overlap. This selectivity is reflected in the magnetic bound states, with the square-hollow channel supporting a predominantly atomic state, while the top and octagon-hollow states are strongly hybridized with the superconducting host. The effect develops continuously as the relevant band becomes flatter and remains pronounced in a material-motivated parameter regime. In a normal--superconductor--normal geometry, these differences are also reflected in the nonlocal subgap conductance. At a representative exchange splitting, square-hollow adsorption suppresses the low-bias conductance relative to the clean device, whereas octagon-hollow adsorption enhances it, with the square-hollow response showing a much sharper dependence on exchange splitting. Our results show that the magnetic-adatom response in narrow and flat-band superconductors is governed by the available spectral weight and by the symmetry and wave-function structure of the impurity--host hybridization.

</details>


### [19] [Signatures of nodal superconductivity in stoichiometric FeTe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08116)
*Cequn Li,Zi-Jie Yan,Yang Ge,Zihao Wang,Bing Xia,Stephen Paolini,Pu Xiao,Lok-Kan Lai,Jiatao Song,Austin R. Kaczmarek,Lujin Min,Kenji Yasuda,Peter J. Hirschfeld,Jiabin Yu,Cui-Zu Chang,Katja C. Nowack*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The authors investigate the superconducting pairing state of stoichiometric FeTe thin films using SQUID microscopy, transport, STM/S, and mean-field calculations. Despite being stoichiometric, FeTe exhibits micrometer-scale spatial inhomogeneity in superfluid stiffness and Tc, with non-saturating London penetration depth following a power law (~1-1.5 exponent), a V-shaped density of states, and evidence for nodal or deep-minimum gaps. This establishes FeTe as a distinct superconducting regime departing from the trend toward isotropic gaps in intermediate Se/Te compositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stoichiometric FeTe is a newly accessible endpoint of the Fe(Se,Te) phase diagram, but its superconducting pairing state is unresolved. Understanding its gap symmetry and superfluid response is essential for constraining microscopic theories of iron-chalcogenide superconductivity.

Method: A multi-technique approach combining scanning SQUID microscopy, electrical transport, scanning tunneling microscopy/spectroscopy (STM/S), and mean-field calculations to map local superfluid response and probe the pairing symmetry in FeTe thin films with tunable stoichiometry.

Result: Spatial variations in superfluid stiffness and Tc at the micrometer scale are observed even in stoichiometric FeTe. The London penetration depth does not saturate down to 0.02Tc, following a power-law temperature dependence with exponent ~1-1.5. V-shaped low-energy DOS and two-gap modeling suggest gap nodes or deep minima, consistent with d-wave or nodal s-wave pairing. A crossover from weak to rapid Tc suppression with decreasing superfluid stiffness is also observed.

Conclusion: Stoichiometric FeTe represents a distinct superconducting regime with nodal or deep-minimum gaps, different from the more isotropic gaps at intermediate Se/Te compositions, providing a benchmark for iron-chalcogenide superconductivity theories and connecting FeTe to broader unconventional superconductor phenomenology.

Abstract: Superconductivity in stoichiometric FeTe opens access to the FeTe endpoint of the Fe(Se,Te) phase diagram, yet the nature of its superconducting pairing state remains unresolved. In this work, we combine scanning superconducting quantum interference device (SQUID) microscopy, electrical transport, scanning tunneling microscopy and spectroscopy (STM/S), and mean-field calculations to investigate the local superfluid response and pairing state of FeTe thin films with tunable stoichiometry. Even in stoichiometric FeTe, we observe micrometer-scale spatial variations in both superfluid stiffness and superconducting transition temperature $T_c$, while the London penetration depth remains non-saturating down to 0.02$T_c$ and follows a power-law temperature dependence with an exponent of approximately 1-1.5. Together with a V-shaped low-energy density of states and two-gap modeling, these results indicate a superconducting state with gap nodes or deep minima, consistent with either a $d$-wave or nodal $s$-wave superconducting state. Our findings establish stoichiometric FeTe as a distinct superconducting regime that departs from the trend toward more isotropic gaps at intermediate Se/Te compositions, providing a new benchmark for modern microscopic theories of iron-chalcogenide superconductivity. Our work also reveals a crossover from weak to rapid suppression of $T_c$ as superfluid stiffness decreases, connecting FeTe to the broader phenomenology observed in unconventional superconductors.

</details>


### [20] [Magnetochiral anisotropy of thermally activated phase slips in noncentrosymmetric superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08237)
*Alex Levchenko*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper develops the theory of thermally activated phase slips in superconducting nanowires lacking inversion symmetry (with Rashba spin-orbit coupling in an in-plane magnetic field). It shows the phase-slip resistance acquires magnetochiral anisotropy (nonreciprocal under field or current reversal), originating from the asymmetry of the phase-slip activation barrier that is odd in both current and field. The barrier asymmetry is tied to the superconducting diode effect through a universal numerical ratio, with no adjustable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand phase-slip phenomena in non-centrosymmetric superconducting wires, where resistive tails below Tc are governed by thermal (and quantum) phase slips, and to characterize how spin-orbit coupling and in-plane magnetic fields lead to nonreciprocal transport in the phase-slip regime.

Method: Microscopic modeling of a diffusive wire formed from a 2D electron gas with Rashba spin-orbit coupling in an in-plane magnetic field. Development of the theory of thermally activated phase slips in this non-centrosymmetric setting, analyzing the free energy with Lifshitz invariants (cubic gradient term and momentum-odd quartic vertex) and even gradient terms promoted to odd ones by the helical ground state. Analysis of the activation barrier asymmetry and fluctuation prefactor (kinetic Lifshitz invariant).

Result: The phase-slip resistance exhibits magnetochiral anisotropy: nonreciprocal under field or current reversal at fixed conjugate parameter, while linear-response resistance remains field symmetric per Onsager reciprocity. The nonreciprocity arises from field- and current-odd asymmetry in the activation barrier, tied to the same combination that determines the superconducting diode effect. The barrier asymmetry is fixed by the diode efficiency through a universal numerical ratio with no free parameters. The kinetic Lifshitz invariant only affects the prefactor subleadingly. Since the asymmetry is in the activation exponent, the magnetochiral signal is amplified over the diode asymmetry by factor barrier height/temperature, appearing as field-antisymmetric splitting of exponential slopes of differential-resistance traces.

Conclusion: Thermally activated phase slips in inversion-asymmetric superconducting wires produce a pronounced magnetochiral anisotropy set by the same microscopic mechanisms as the superconducting diode effect. The barrier-asymmetry/diode-efficiency ratio is universal, enabling parameter-free predictions, and the effect leads to observable field-antisymmetric splitting in differential-resistance slope measurements used to detect thermally activated phase slips.

Abstract: Resistive tails of narrow superconducting wires below the critical temperature are governed by phase slips, thermally activated near the transition and quantum at lower temperatures. We develop the theory of thermally activated phase slips in wires lacking inversion symmetry, modeled microscopically as a diffusive wire formed from a two-dimensional electron gas with Rashba spin-orbit coupling in an in-plane magnetic field. The phase-slip resistance acquires magnetochiral anisotropy: at fixed current it changes upon reversal of the field, and at fixed field upon reversal of the current, while the linear-response resistance remains field symmetric as required by Onsager reciprocity. The nonreciprocity originates from an asymmetry of the phase-slip activation barrier that is odd in both current and field. It is generated by the Lifshitz invariants of the free energy, the cubic gradient term and the momentum-odd quartic vertex, together with even gradient terms promoted to odd ones by the helical ground state, precisely the combination that determines the superconducting diode effect. The barrier asymmetry is therefore fixed by the diode efficiency of the same wire through a universal numerical ratio, with no adjustable parameters. The kinetic Lifshitz invariant, which makes relaxation and noise of the order parameter nonreciprocal, affects only the fluctuation prefactor at a parametrically subleading level. Because the asymmetry resides in the activation exponent, the magnetochiral signal is amplified relative to the diode asymmetry by the ratio of barrier height to temperature, and appears as a field-antisymmetric splitting of the exponential slopes of differential-resistance traces routinely used to identify thermally activated phase slips.

</details>


### [21] [Néel-Vector Control of the Josephson Diode Effect in $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric Antiferromagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08278)
*Xian-Tang Xu,Xun-Jiang Luo,Mingliang Tian,Ning Hao*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper theoretically studies Josephson junctions made of s-wave superconductors and PT-symmetric collinear antiferromagnets (modeled on CuMnAs), demonstrating both the Josephson diode effect and φ₀-junction states, both controlled by the Néel vector direction. A channel-resolved scattering theory and Green-function reduction reveal the microscopic mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how the interplay of superconductivity and magnetism in Josephson junctions with PT-symmetric antiferromagnets can yield tunable anomalous Josephson phenomena, establishing antiferromagnets as platforms for field-free Josephson diodes and φ₀ junctions.

Method: Microscopic modeling and symmetry analysis of s-wave superconductor/PT-symmetric collinear antiferromagnet (CuMnAs) junctions; a channel-resolved scattering theory is developed to capture anomalous phases and derive the exact diode condition; a Green-function reduction identifies a single renormalized PT-degenerate band as the transport carrier.

Result: The junctions exhibit both Josephson diode effect and φ₀-junction states. Both effects are controlled by the Néel vector: a 90° rotation switches them off, while reversal flips diode polarity. A sizable, tunable diode efficiency arises from the interplay of channel current-phase relations and is tunable by exchange field magnitude and direction. The Green-function reduction precisely reproduces the full current amplitudes.

Conclusion: PT-symmetric antiferromagnets serve as versatile, field-free platforms for highly tunable Josephson diodes and φ₀ junctions, with the Néel vector providing a robust control knob for these effects.

Abstract: The interplay of superconductivity and magnetism gives rise to rich phenomena in Josephson junctions. In this Letter, we study Josephson junctions formed by conventional $s$-wave superconductors and a $\PT$-symmetric collinear antiferromagnet modeled on CuMnAs. Using microscopic modeling and symmetry analysis, we show that these junctions exhibit both the Josephson diode effect and $\varphi_{0}$-junction states. Remarkably, both effects are controlled by the Néel vector: rotating it by $90^{\circ}$ switches off both, while reversing it switches the diode polarity. To reveal the microscopic mechanism, we develop a channel-resolved scattering theory that accurately captures the anomalous phases and establishes the exact condition for the diode effect. The interplay of the channel current-phase relations yields a sizable diode efficiency, tunable by both the magnitude and direction of the exchange field. Furthermore, a Green-function reduction identifies a single renormalized $\PT$-degenerate band as the transport carrier and precisely reproduces the full current amplitudes. Our work establishes $\PT$-symmetric antiferromagnets as versatile platforms for field-free, highly tunable Josephson diodes and $\varphi_{0}$ junctions.

</details>


### [22] [$1/3$-Flux Bound States in Multicomponent Superconductor Exhibiting Non-Abelian Statistics of $\mathbb {Z}_3$ Parafermions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08326)
*Xing-Yu Wu,Y. H. Wang,X. C. Xie,Yijia Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper proposes that 1/3-flux bound states (1/3-FBSs) in multicomponent superconductors with C3 rotational symmetry exhibit non-Abelian Z3 parafermion statistics, and demonstrates how to realize complete Z3 parafermion braiding operations with parity readout via fermionic occupation and a crossed-Andreev-reflection-induced Hadamard gate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multicomponent superconductors are predicted to host fractional magnetic flux quanta, but the braiding statistics of electrons bound to such fractional fluxes (e.g., 1/3 flux quanta) have not been explicitly explored despite long-standing expectations that they form anyons.

Method: The authors analyze 1/3-FBSs under a no-double-occupancy constraint, showing that two successive braiding operations are equivalent to a single Z3 parafermion braiding operation. They propose reading out the parafermion parity via the fermionic occupation number of the bound states and combine this with a crossed-Andreev-reflection-induced Hadamard gate to realize a complete set of braiding operations.

Result: The 1/3-FBSs exhibit non-Abelian Z3 parafermion statistics under the no-double-occupancy constraint, with braiding outcomes encoded in fermionic parity that can be read out, and the complete set of Z3 parafermion braiding gates is realizable.

Conclusion: This work establishes that fractional-flux bound states in multicomponent superconductors provide a physical platform for Z3 parafermion non-Abelian statistics and topological quantum computation.

Abstract: Multicomponent superconductors (MSCs) are predicted to host magnetic flux quanta carrying arbitrary fractions of the superconducting flux quantum. Recent advances have raised the expectation that $1/3$ flux quanta may emerge in MSCs with $C_3$ rotational symmetry. Electrons bound to such fractional flux are long regarded to form anyons, yet their explicit braiding statistics remain unexplored. We propose that these 1/3-flux bound states (1/3-FBSs) exhibit the intriguing non-Abelian statistics of $\mathbb{Z}_3$ parafermions. Under no-double-occupancy constraint, two successive braiding operations of 1/3-FBSs are equivalent to a single $\mathbb Z_3$ parafermion braiding operation. The parafermion parity encoding the braiding outcome can be read out via the fermionic occupation number of the 1/3-FBSs. Combined with a crossed-Andreev-reflection-induced Hadamard gate, one can realize the complete set of $\mathbb{Z}_3$ parafermion braiding operations.

</details>


### [23] [Geometry-induced current decrowding in superconducting 3D constrictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08349)
*Igor Bogush,Oleksandr Dobrovolskiy*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that 3D shaping of superconducting nanoarchitectures overcomes current crowding through a geometry-induced decrowding effect, enabling field-controlled critical-current modulation, non-reciprocal critical currents, and new 3D vortex dynamics (apex-mediated vortex entry followed by splitting into two filaments) that are absent in planar 2D geometries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current crowding is a ubiquitous problem in nanoscale devices that distorts current flow and creates localized current-density hotspots, causing premature failure. In superconductors, this suppresses the order parameter and promotes vortex nucleation, reducing the critical current below the intrinsic depairing limit. The authors seek to overcome this fundamental limitation using 3D geometry.

Method: Finite-element modeling based on the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equation is used to simulate current flow and vortex dynamics in 3D superconducting constrictions, comparing them against planar 2D geometries.

Result: 3D constrictions exhibit a pronounced and tunable response to moderate in-plane magnetic fields—functionality absent in planar geometries. This enables critical-current modulation, non-reciprocal critical current, and new vortex-dynamics regimes. Additionally, curvature and finite thickness enable apex-mediated entry of single vortex lines followed by their 3D splitting into two vortex filaments, a mechanism impossible in 2D manifolds.

Conclusion: 3D geometric engineering represents a new design paradigm for superconducting nanoarchitectures, offering control over current distribution and vortex dynamics in devices approaching the intrinsic depairing limit.

Abstract: Current crowding is a ubiquitous limitation in nanoscale devices, where confinement and sharp features distort current flow, generating localized current-density hotspots and premature failure. In superconductors, this effect suppresses the order parameter and promotes vortex nucleation, reducing the critical current below the intrinsic depairing limit. Here, we demonstrate that 3D shaping of superconducting nanoarchitectures overcomes geometric current crowding through spatial current redistribution, establishing a geometry-induced decrowding effect. Using finite-element modeling based on the time-dependent Ginzburg--Landau equation, we reveal that 3D constrictions exhibit a pronounced and tunable response to moderate in-plane magnetic fields---a functionality absent in planar geometries. This field-controlled geometry enables critical-current modulation and unveils new vortex-dynamics regimes, including a non-reciprocal critical current. Furthermore, curvature and finite thickness fundamentally alter vortex nucleation by enabling apex-mediated entry of single vortex lines, followed by their 3D splitting into two vortex filaments---a mechanism that does not occur in 2D manifolds. Our findings demonstrate 3D geometric engineering as a design paradigm for superconducting nanoarchitectures, offering control over current distribution and vortex dynamics in devices approaching the depairing limit.

</details>


### [24] [Thomas-Fermi screening of electrostatic fields in a type-I superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08516)
*Nikhil Seeja Sivakumar,Tyler James,Frederick Carlisle,Philip Moriarty,Brian Kiraly*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using qPlus atomic force microscopy and scanning tunneling microscopy/spectroscopy on single-crystal Pb(111) at ~340 mK, the authors test two competing predictions for longitudinal electrostatic screening in superconductors. They find the change in screening between normal and superconducting states is less than ~1%, consistent with unmodified Thomas-Fermi screening, and two to three orders of magnitude smaller than predicted by Hirsch's alternative theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The London equations predict that transverse electrodynamic response changes in superconductors (giving the Meissner effect), while longitudinal response should remain unchanged (Thomas-Fermi screening). Hirsch proposed an alternative formalism predicting that longitudinal screening should instead be governed by the London penetration depth below Tc, which would constitute a fundamental departure from conventional understanding.

Method: Combination of qPlus atomic force microscopy and scanning tunneling microscopy/spectroscopy on single-crystal Pb(111) at ~340 mK (T/Tc ~ 0.05). Superconductivity was quenched with a 200 mT magnetic field normal to the surface. Force-distance curves and local field-emission resonance spectra were measured at the same sample position with and without the magnetic field to constrain screening changes between normal and superconducting states.

Result: The change in electrostatic screening between the normal and superconducting states was constrained to less than ~1%, which is two to three orders of magnitude smaller than Hirsch's theory predicts.

Conclusion: The experimental results are entirely consistent with unmodified Thomas-Fermi screening across the superconducting transition, rejecting Hirsch's prediction that longitudinal screening should be governed by the London penetration depth.

Abstract: The empirical London equations make distinct predictions for a superconductor's longitudinal and transverse electrodynamic response. Conventionally, this framework attributes the Meissner effect to the transverse component, with the longitudinal response predicted to remain unchanged on entering the superconducting state, i.e. a static electric field is screened over the Thomas-Fermi length ($λ_{\mathrm{TF}} \sim 1$~Å), just as in the nonsupercondcuting metal. J.~E.~Hirsch~[\textit{Phys.~ Rev.~B}~\textbf{69},~214515~(2004)] has developed an alternative formalism, which predicts that the longitudinal screening length should instead be governed by the London penetration depth as the system is cooled below the superconducting transition temperature ($T_{\mathrm{c}}$). Here we use a combination of qPlus atomic force microscopy and scanning tunneling microscopy/spectroscopy of single-crystal Pb(111) at $\sim$~340~mK~ \mbox{($T/T_{\mathrm{c}}\sim0.05$)} in an attempt to detect any modifications in electrostatic screening of the longitudinal tip field upon entering the superconducting phase. We quench superconductivity using a magnetic field of 200 mT normal to the Pb(111) surface. By measuring force-distance curves and local field-emission resonance spectra at the same sample position in the presence and absence of the magnetic field, we constrain the change in screening between the normal and superconducting state to less than $\sim 1 \%$. This is between two to three orders of magnitude smaller than that predicted by Hirsch's theory, and entirely consistent with unmodified Thomas-Fermi screening across the superconducting transition.

</details>


### [25] [Fabrication And Characterization Of High-Quality Nb/Al-AlO$_x$/Nb Cross-Type Josephson Tunnel Junctions Utilising CMP-based Planarisation Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08569)
*Alexander Stoll,Andreas Reifenberger,Daniel Hengstler,Andreas Fleischmann,Christian Enss*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper presents a fabrication process for Nb/Al-AlOx/Nb cross-type Josephson tunnel junctions embedded in sputter-deposited SiO2, using chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP) instead of lift-off for planarization. The process achieves over 90% yield on wafer-scale, with junctions down to 1 μm × 1 μm, and the authors characterize junction quality via IV-characteristics and Fraunhofer patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Window-type Josephson junctions used in DC SQUIDs suffer from photolithography alignment inaccuracies and parasitic capacitances that limit device energy sensitivity. Cross-type junction geometry can circumvent these issues by enabling smaller junction areas and eliminating parasitic capacitances, but requires a reliable fabrication process suitable for wafer-scale production.

Method: The authors fabricate Nb/Al-AlOx/Nb cross-type JJs with the trilayer embedded in sputter-deposited SiO2 for planarization and sidewall insulation of the bottom electrode. Instead of time-consuming lift-off processes that may leave unwanted wings along microstructure edges, they employ chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP) to remove excess SiO2, producing smooth and uniform surfaces. Fabricated junctions range down to 1 μm × 1 μm and are distributed across the wafer for characterization.

Result: The CMP-based fabrication approach is significantly faster than lift-off and yields smooth, uniform surfaces, resulting in a high fabrication yield exceeding 90% on wafer-scale. Junction-specific quality parameters were extracted from IV-characteristics and Fraunhofer patterns for junctions of various areas down to 1 μm × 1 μm.

Conclusion: Cross-type JJs fabricated with embedded trilayers and CMP planarization provide a reliable, high-yield alternative to window-type junctions, overcoming alignment and parasitic capacitance limitations while enabling wafer-scale scalability for superconducting devices such as DC SQUIDs and qubits.

Abstract: Josephson tunnel junctions (JJs) are the fundamental building blocks of today's most advanced superconducting electronic components, such as qubits and superconducting quantum interference devices (SQUIDs). Given the ongoing demand for scalability of these devices on the wafer-scale, it is crucial to control the fabrication process as precisely as possible to ensure uniform quality and reproducibility. Today, window-type JJs are often used in the production of DC SQUIDs and while they are reliable and reproducible, they suffer from alignment inaccuracies caused by photolithography and unavoidable parasitic capacitances, thus limiting the energy sensitivity of DC SQUIDs. These problems can be circumvented by producing JJs with cross-type geometry, which allows for smaller junction areas and eliminates parasitic capacitances. Here we discuss the fabrication of Nb/Al-AlOx/Nb-based cross-type JJs, where the trilayer is embedded in sputter-deposited SiO$_2$ to allow for planarisation of the structures and to ensure the reliable insulation of the sidewalls of the bottom electrode. Instead of lift-off processes that require a lot of time and potentially leave unwanted wings along the edges of microstructures behind that might compromise subsequent layers, we use chemical-mechanical polishing (CMP) for the removal of excess SiO$_2$. This is not only much faster, but produces smooth and uniform surfaces, which in turn effectively improves the reliability of our JJ production process resulting in a high yield of over $90\,\%$ on wafer-scale. In addition, we discuss the influence of fabrication details on the quality and electrical properties of our JJs with different junction areas down to $1\,\mathrm{μm} \times 1\,\mathrm{μm}$ distributed across the wafer and extract the junction specific quality parameters from their IV-characteristics and their Fraunhofer patterns.

</details>


### [26] [Junction-free polarity-tunable superconducting diode effect enables XNOR logic operation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09097)
*Alapan Bera,Soumik Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reports a junction-free superconducting diode effect (SDE) in an all-van der Waals NbSe2/Fe3GeTe2 heterostructure, achieving up to 30% diode efficiency at 50 mT, with field-free, polarity-tunable rectification and demonstration of an XNOR logic gate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional superconducting diodes rely on weak-link Josephson junctions, which introduce fabrication complexity, electrical noise, and dependence on junction quality. The authors aim to realize a highly efficient, polarity-tunable SDE without such junctions to simplify device architecture and improve performance.

Method: The authors construct an all-vdW NbSe2/Fe3GeTe2 heterostructure where proximity-induced symmetry breaking from the strong uniaxial ferromagnet (F3GT) breaks inversion symmetry in the superconductor, enabling a junction-free SDE. They measure diode efficiency, field dependence, and polarity tunability via F3GT spin polarization, and demonstrate XNOR logic gate operation using active-field dependence.

Result: A superconducting diode efficiency of up to 30% is achieved at a small magnetic field of 50 mT. Field-free diode rectification is realized with polarity tunable by the F3GT spin polarization. A two-input exclusive-NOR (XNOR) logic gate is demonstrated.

Conclusion: Magnetic proximity effects in vdW heterostructures enable junction-free superconducting diodes with high efficiency and tunability, highlighting a promising route toward dissipationless, energy-efficient logic circuits.

Abstract: The realization of the superconducting diode effect (SDE), characterized by non-reciprocal supercurrent, is a major step towards dissipationless logic circuits. A polarity-tunable superconducting diode with high diode efficiency primarily consists of Josephson-junction architectures that couple two superconducting regions via a weak link. Realizing an SDE without a weak-link junction would reduce fabrication complexity, lower electrical noise, and minimize dependence on junction quality. Here, we report an all-vdW, highly efficient, junction-free SDE in NbSe2, enabled by proximity-induced symmetry breaking from a strong uniaxial ferromagnet Fe3GeTe2 (F3GT). Superconducting diode efficiency of up to 30% is achieved at a small magnetic field of 50 mT. A field-free diode rectification effect is realized with diode polarity that is tunable by the F3GT spin polarization. Furthermore, by employing the active-field dependence of the SDE, a two-input exclusive-NOR (XNOR) logic gate operation is demonstrated. The findings highlight the potential utilization of magnetic proximity effect towards realizing junction-free superconducting diodes for high-performance, energy-efficient logic circuits.

</details>
