### [27] [Physical-Field Reconstruction from Sparse Observations: When Are Diffusion Models Preferable to Deterministic Regression?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06135)
*Hao Zhou,Rui Zhang,Qi Wang,Hao Sun*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a fair comparison of deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion for reconstructing physical fields from sparse observations across 2D Poisson, 2D Navier-Stokes, and 1D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky settings. Key findings: no systematic accuracy advantage for diffusion methods across complexity/sparsity regimes; ensemble means improve phase-aligned accuracy while individual samples preserve variability; conditional diffusion offers cheaper and more reliable uncertainty estimates while prior-guided diffusion is more robust to mask-distribution shifts but at higher computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse field reconstruction is an ill-posed inverse problem, and there is no consensus on when a single point estimate suffices versus when a distribution of plausible reconstructions is more useful. The paper aims to clarify this by conducting a fair, matched comparison of representative deterministic and generative approaches.

Method: A controlled experimental comparison is conducted under matched settings of a deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion on three benchmark tasks: 2D Poisson equation, 2D Navier-Stokes flow, and 1D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics.

Result: Three findings: (1) accuracy is field- and regime-dependent with no systematic diffusion advantage under higher complexity or sparser observations; (2) ensemble means improve phase-aligned accuracy while individual samples better preserve variability and high-wavenumber power in selected regimes; (3) conditional diffusion gives more reliable uncertainty estimates at lower cost, whereas prior-guided diffusion is more robust to mask-distribution shifts but incurs substantially higher inference cost and guidance tuning.

Conclusion: The study clarifies when generative reconstruction is beneficial versus when point estimates suffice, and offers practical guidance for improving uncertainty estimation, fine-scale sample fidelity, robustness to distribution shifts, and computational efficiency in sparse physical field reconstruction.

Abstract: Reconstructing physical fields from sparse observations is central to system identification, forecasting, and control, yet sparse measurements generally underdetermine the full field. This makes reconstruction an ill-posed inverse problem rather than simple interpolation. Although many deterministic and generative methods have been developed, there is still no clear consensus on when a single point estimate is sufficient and when a distribution of plausible reconstructions is more useful. We conduct a fair comparison of a deterministic U-Net, conditional diffusion, and prior-guided diffusion under matched experimental settings, including 2D Poisson equation, 2D Navier-Stokes flow, and 1D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky dynamics. Through this comparison, we make three observations. First, accuracy is field- and regime-dependent, with no systematic advantage for diffusion under higher complexity or sparser observations. Second, ensemble means improve phase-aligned accuracy, whereas individual samples better preserve variability and can retain high-wavenumber power in selected regimes. Third, conditional diffusion provides more reliable uncertainty estimates at lower cost, while prior-guided diffusion is more robust to mask-distribution shifts but requires substantially higher inference cost and guidance tuning. These results clarify when generative reconstruction is useful and provide guidance for improving uncertainty estimation, fine-scale sample fidelity, robustness, and computational efficiency in sparse field reconstruction.

</details>


### [28] [A Sparsity-Promoting Electromagnetic Inversion Method Regularized by $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-Norm of the Model Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06524)
*Lingqi Gao,Hakan Bagci*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A nonlinear EM inversion method using ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularization on the model gradient is proposed. The scale-invariant ratio of ℓ1 to ℓ2 norms better characterizes sparsity than conventional ℓ1-norm. The nonconvex, nonsmooth problem is solved via ADMM with five sub-steps, incorporating Gauss-Newton for linearization. Numerical experiments on synthetic and experimental data show superior permittivity reconstructions with better edge preservation and artifact suppression than Tikhonov and total variation, at no extra computational cost, with robustness to noise and reduced sensitivity to regularization weight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ℓ1-norm regularization has limitations in accurately characterizing sparsity. The ℓ1/ℓ2-norm ratio, being scale-invariant, offers a more faithful sparsity measure, motivating its application to nonlinear electromagnetic (EM) inversion problems to improve reconstruction quality.

Method: The inversion problem is formulated with ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularization of the model gradient. To handle the nonconvexity and nonsmoothness from the quotient structure, the problem is reformulated and solved via the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM), decomposed into five sub-steps. The Gauss-Newton method is embedded in the first sub-step to linearize the nonlinear inversion.

Result: Numerical experiments on synthetic and experimental datasets demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms standard regularizations (Tikhonov and total variation) in reconstructing permittivity profiles, better preserving sharp edges and suppressing artifacts without additional computational cost. It also shows strong noise robustness and reduced sensitivity to the regularization weight.

Conclusion: The ℓ1/ℓ2-norm regularized EM inversion solved via ADMM provides a more effective sparsity prior than ℓ1-norm or TV, yielding superior reconstructions with edge preservation and artifact suppression, while maintaining computational efficiency and robustness to parameter and noise variations.

Abstract: A nonlinear EM inversion method regularized by $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm of the model gradient is proposed. The $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm, defined as the ratio of $\ell_1$-norm to $\ell_2$-norm, exhibits a scale-invariant property that enables it to more accurately characterize sparsity in the solution compared with the conventional $\ell_1$-norm. To address the nonconvexity and nonsmoothness introduced by the quotient structure of $\ell_1 / \ell_2$-norm, the inversion problem is reformulated and solved using the alternating direction method of multipliers, resulting in an efficient optimization algorithm comprising five sub-steps. The Gauss--Newton method is incorporated into the first sub-step to linearize the nonlinear inversion. The proposed method is validated by a series of numerical examples using both synthetic and experimental datasets, and is compared against standard regularization methods such as Tikhonov and total variation. The results demonstrate that the proposed method yields superior reconstructions of permittivity profiles, particularly in preserving sharp edges and suppressing artifacts, without incurring additional computational cost. Furthermore, the method exhibits strong robustness to measurement noise and reduced sensitivity to the choice of the regularization weight.

</details>


### [29] [A HIP-Compatible Accelerator Backend for Fourier-Bessel Particle-in-Cell Simulations on CPU/DCU Heterogeneous Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06680)
*Jingliang Fan,Ruiqing He,Yang Wan,Jiandong Shang,Hengliang Guo,Qiang Chen*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a HIP-compatible accelerator backend for FBPIC, a Fourier-Bessel particle-in-cell simulation code, enabling efficient execution on DCU (Deep Computing Unit) platforms while preserving its Python interface. The backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over the original NVIDIA CUDA/Numba implementation on V100 and enables efficient DCU execution, with multi-DCU tests showing 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four accelerators and 2.72x throughput increase at ~68% weak-scaling efficiency. The work also distills key lessons for porting Python-based scientific computing applications to heterogeneous accelerator platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FBPIC, a high-performance simulation code for relativistic plasma and accelerator physics, is built on Numba CUDA, restricting its deployment on accelerators that use the HIP programming environment such as DCU accelerators. The authors aim to extend FBPIC's portability to HIP-based platforms while preserving its Python user interface and high-level workflow.

Method: The authors develop a new accelerator backend compatible with HIP for FBPIC, replacing the Numba CUDA dependency. The approach involves porting FBPIC's computational kernels to HIP, maintaining the Python frontend intact, and validating the implementation on both NVIDIA V100 GPUs (via HIP's CUDA compatibility) and DCU platforms. Multi-accelerator scalability is evaluated through strong and weak scaling experiments with communication analysis.

Result: The proposed HIP backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over the original FBPIC on an NVIDIA V100 GPU and supports efficient execution on DCU platforms. Multi-DCU experiments demonstrate 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four accelerators and 2.72x aggregate throughput increase at approximately 68% weak-scaling efficiency. Communication analysis identifies inter-node communication and synchronization as the main scalability bottlenecks.

Conclusion: The HIP-compatible backend successfully extends FBPIC to DCU platforms with performance gains over the original CUDA-based implementation. The porting lessons and methodology serve as a practical reference for migrating other Python-based scientific computing codes to heterogeneous accelerator environments beyond GPUs.

Abstract: FBPIC (Fourier-Bessel particle-in-cell) is a high-performance simulation code for relativistic plasma and accelerator physics. Its original accelerator backend relies on Numba CUDA, which limits its direct deployment on accelerators using the HIP (Heterogeneous-Compute Interface for Portability) programming environment, such as DCU (Deep Computing Unit) accelerators. In this work, we develop an accelerator backend compatible with HIP that enables FBPIC to run efficiently on DCU platforms while preserving its Python user interface and high level simulation workflow. For the evaluated LWFA (laser-wakefield acceleration) workloads, the proposed backend achieves 1.32-1.54x speedups over the original FBPIC implementation on an NVIDIA V100 GPU and enables efficient execution on the DCU platform. We also summarize the key lessons learned from porting FBPIC to the DCU platform. Multi-DCU experiments achieve a 1.88x strong-scaling speedup on four accelerators and a 2.72x increase in aggregate throughput at approximately 68\% weak-scaling efficiency, with communication analysis identifying inter-node communication and synchronization as the main scalability limitations. Beyond FBPIC, the proposed approach provides a practical reference for porting and optimizing other scientific computing applications developed with Python on heterogeneous accelerator platforms.

</details>


### [30] [Beyond Residuals: Energy based solutions of partial differential equations using scientific machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08239)
*Timon Rabczuk,Yizheng Wang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper systematically revisits the Deep Energy Method (DEM) for solving PDEs arising from variational principles, clarifying its relationship to PINNs and VPINNs, extending it to nonlinear/history-dependent/time-dependent problems via incremental variational formulations, and demonstrating through numerical examples that DEM offers a principled alternative to residual-based methods for variational and inverse problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Residual-based physics-informed neural networks (PINNs and VPINNs) enforce strong or weak forms of PDEs through loss minimization, which can suffer from high-order derivative requirements and instability. Energy-based methods like DEM offer a physically consistent alternative that avoids residual weighting and high-order derivatives, yet their scope, advantages, and limitations relative to residual-based approaches have not been systematically characterized.

Method: The paper provides a systematic framework analysis of DEM within physics-informed learning and variational modeling. It clarifies conceptual relationships between DEM, PINNs, and VPINNs; identifies problem classes where energy minimization offers intrinsic advantages (stability, robustness, interpretability); extends DEM through incremental variational formulations to nonlinear, history-dependent, and time-dependent problems such as phase-field fracture and dissipative systems; and uses the variational structure as a foundation for optimization and inverse problems where the energy functional serves as a physics-based constraint. Validation is performed through a series of numerical examples.

Result: The numerical experiments demonstrate that DEM provides a principled and effective alternative to residual-based methods for variational problems, showcasing its strengths (stability, robustness, interpretability, no high-order derivatives, natural constraint enforcement) and limitations relative to existing physics-informed approaches, including applicability to phase-field fracture and dissipative systems through incremental variational formulations.

Conclusion: DEM, grounded in energy minimization and variational structure, is a competitive and physically consistent alternative to residual-based physics-informed methods for the class of problems that admit an energy or incremental potential formulation, offering particular advantages in stability, robustness, interpretability, and natural support for inverse and optimization problems.

Abstract: Energy-based approaches provide a natural and physically consistent framework for a large class of partial differential equations arising in solid and fluid mechanics, where the governing equations follow from variational principles. In contrast to residual-based physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) and their weak-form variants, which enforce the strong or weak form of the equations through loss minimization, the Deep Energy Method (DEM) directly computes the solution as the minimizer of an energy or incremental potential functional. This eliminates the need for residual weighting, avoids high-order derivatives, and enables the direct enforcement of physical constraints through the variational structure.In this work, we systematically revisit the Deep Energy Method, placing it in the broader context of physics-informed learning and variational modeling. We clarify the relationship between DEM, PINNs, and VPINNs, and identify the class of problems for which energy minimization provides intrinsic advantages in terms of stability, robustness and interpretability. Particular emphasis is placed on incremental variational formulations, which allow DEM to be applied to nonlinear, history-dependent and time-dependent problems, including phase-field fracture and dissipative systems. The variational structure underlying DEM further provides a natural foundation for optimization and inverse problems, where the energy functional acts as a physics-based constraint rather than a residual penalty. Through a series of numerical examples, we demonstrate that DEM offers a principled and effective alternative to residual-based methods for variational problems, highlighting its strengths and limitations relative to existing physics-informed approaches.

</details>


### [31] [A finite-strain logarithmic viscoelastic model for Antarctic ice shelves based on an additive split](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08451)
*Maxime Nutte,Sebastian Skatulla,Carlo Sansour*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper formulates a finite-strain Maxwell viscoelastic model for glacier ice using logarithmic (Hencky) strain space, combining isotropic Hencky elasticity with a Glen-type power-law dashpot. The model is implemented in a finite-element framework and applied to idealized ice tongue configurations with realistic density, modulus, and temperature profiles, revealing how viscoelasticity and front morphology (basal foot, undercutting) control near-terminus tensile stress fields relevant to calving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ice shelves lose mass primarily through calving, a process governed by the near-front stress field over timescales ranging from elastic flexure to viscous creep. Existing models often neglect the coupled elastic-viscous response or use small-strain formulations, prompting the need for a finite-strain viscoelastic framework that accurately captures the interplay between deformation regime and front morphology.

Method: A finite-strain Maxwell model for glacier ice is formulated in logarithmic strain space. The Hencky strain is split additively at the rate level into elastic (isotropic Hencky spring) and viscous (Glen-type power-law dashpot on logarithmic strain rate) parts. The model is integrated using a midpoint evaluation with backward-Euler correction of the trial dual, implemented in a finite-element setting, then applied to an idealized ice tongue with depth-dependent density/moduli, temperature-dependent fluidity, and cliff geometries featuring frontal foot or basal undercutting.

Result: The dashpot reduces to Glen's flow law at infinitesimal strain, and elastic strains remain in that regime for the studied ice-shelf configurations. After validation against a viscoelastic column benchmark, the ice-tongue simulations show that viscoelasticity and front morphology control tension near the terminus, with distinct stress-field signatures depending on whether a frontal foot or basal undercutting is present.

Conclusion: The finite-strain Maxwell formulation in logarithmic strain space provides a thermodynamically and kinematically consistent framework for modeling glacier ice near ice-shelf fronts. The results demonstrate that both material viscoelasticity and terminus geometry are essential for predicting the near-front stress state that governs calving.

Abstract: Ice shelves lose mass primarily by calving, a process controlled by the near-front stress field on timescales that span elastic flexure and viscous creep. We formulate a finite-strain Maxwell model for glacier ice in logarithmic strain space. The Hencky strain of a fixed reference configuration is split additively at the level of rates into elastic and viscous parts; the spring is isotropic Hencky elasticity and the dashpot is a Glen-type power law written on the logarithmic strain rate and its work-conjugate stress. At infinitesimal strain the dashpot coincides with Glen's flow law; the elastic strains in the ice-shelf configurations of this paper remain in that regime. The model is integrated with a midpoint evaluation and a backward-Euler correction of the trial dual, and implemented in a finite-element setting. After a viscoelastic column benchmark, the formulation is applied to an idealised ice tongue, including depth-dependent density and moduli, temperature-dependent fluidity, and cliff geometries with a frontal foot or basal undercutting. The resulting stress fields show how viscoelasticity and front morphology control tension near the terminus.

</details>


### [32] [Structure-Informed Bayesian Inference of Anomalous Transport and Hidden Molecular Trapping in Amorphous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08780)
*Andrey Ananev,Maria Potapova,Nikolay Kondratyuk,Timur Vostroknutov,Aleksey Khlyupin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a structure-informed Bayesian regularization framework that leverages discrete Morse theory to extract the time-invariant topological skeleton of fluctuating amorphous media, enabling accurate segmentation of single-particle tracking trajectories into localized trapping states. The method eliminates systematic biases of conventional geometric algorithms, achieves near-exact trapping parameter recovery at linear O(N) computational cost, and is demonstrated on hydrogen and methane diffusion in kerogen matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting localized trapping states from single-particle tracking trajectories in fluctuating amorphous materials is fundamentally challenging because thermal structural breathing continuously reconfigures pore boundaries, causing conventional geometric algorithms to systematically mis-merge distinct states during cyclic molecular returns.

Method: The approach shifts from local geometric recurrence to structure-informed Bayesian regularization. Discrete Morse theory extracts a time-invariant topological skeleton of the fluctuating host matrix, which provides robust, molecule-specific physical priors accounting for individual molecular dimensions. Trajectory steps are then sequentially partitioned via a two-stage probabilistic refinement that dynamically adapts to the transport landscape.

Result: Benchmarked against synthetic particles exploring the actual interconnected matrix graph, the method eliminates systematic biases, restricting macroscopic trapping parameter deviations to a few percent while maintaining optimal linear O(N) computational scaling. Applied to hydrogen and methane transport in type-I kerogen, it successfully decodes hidden microscopic mechanisms of confined diffusion. Open-source code and data are publicly released.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers an accessible, broadly adaptable strategy for trajectory segmentation across diverse tracking applications, including ion transport in battery polymers and protein trafficking in cellular environments.

Abstract: Molecular diffusion in fluctuating amorphous and macromolecular media governs key transport processes across soft-matter physics, energy storage, and biological membranes. Extracting localized trapping states from single-particle tracking trajectories remains a fundamental challenge; because thermal structural breathing continuously reconfigures pore boundaries, conventional geometric algorithms suffer from severe systematic biases, erroneously merging distinct localized states during cyclic molecular returns. Here, we address this deadlock by shifting the paradigm from local geometric recurrence to a structure-informed Bayesian regularization. Leveraging discrete Morse theory, we extract the time-invariant topological skeleton of the fluctuating host matrix to construct robust, gas-specific physical priors that account for individual molecular dimensions. Trajectory steps are sequentially partitioned via a two-stage probabilistic refinement that dynamically adapts to the transport landscape. Benchmarked against a rigorous environment where synthetic particles explore the actual interconnected matrix graph, our approach eliminates systemic biases, restricting macroscopic trapping parameter deviations to just a few percent under optimal linear $O(N)$ computational scaling. Applied to hydrogen and methane transport within a type-I kerogen matrix, serving as a prototype for highly tortuous, flexible macromolecular networks, the method successfully decodes the hidden microscopic mechanisms of confined diffusion. To ensure immediate broad impact, the documented open-source code and data are made publicly available, offering an accessible strategy readily adaptable to a broad spectrum of tracking phenomena, from ion transport in battery polymers to protein trafficking within cellular environments.

</details>


### [33] [The Path Integral Monte Carlo Sign Problem Is Not Always NP-Hard: Harmonic Fermions Can Be Solved in Quadratic Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09071)
*Aarif Chaudhary,Jonas Valenzuela,Siu A. Chin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper derives a closed-form, finite-bead partition function for Path Integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) simulations of fermions in a harmonic trap using λ-ring reformulation of the recursion relation. The result yields an O(n²) algorithm that exactly reproduces energies for up to 10⁴ or more fermions, completely bypassing the numerical instability of the fermionic sign problem and serving as a counterexample to the claim that all genuine fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fermionic sign problem in PIMC simulations is a notorious numerical bottleneck, often believed to be NP-hard. The authors aim to show that in specific analytically tractable setups—fermions in a harmonic trap with optional pairwise harmonic interactions—the sign problem can be completely bypassed, challenging the prevailing complexity-theoretic view.

Method: Starting from the contracted determinant form of the propagator, the authors derive the partition function for any number of beads and any short-time propagator. They reformulate the resulting recursion relation in the language of λ-rings, obtaining a closed-form finite-bead partition function in two dimensions expressed through permutation statistics. This closed form is then evaluated via a specialized O(n²) algorithm, yielding numerically stable results.

Result: The closed-form partition function enables exact and numerically stable evaluation of energies for fermion PIMC simulations with n = 10⁴ or more fermions, effectively reproducing results of the original (otherwise undoable) fermion PIMC calculation without sign-problem instability.

Conclusion: The work provides a concrete analytical framework where the fermionic sign problem is completely circumvented, serving as a counterexample to the prevailing assumption that all truly fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.

Abstract: In the Path Integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) simulation of fermions in a harmonic trap, with and without pairwise harmonic interactions, the partition functions for any discrete number of imaginary time slices (or beads) and for any choice of the short-time propagator can be analytically obtained from the contracted determinant form of the propagator. This work shows that the resulting recursion relation can be reformulated in the $λ$-ring language, yielding a closed-form finite-bead partition function in two dimensions in terms of permutation statistics. This closed-form partition function can be evaluated by a special algorithm in $O(n^2)$ time, providing an exact and numerically stable scheme for reproducing the energies of the original (undoable) fermion PIMC simulation for $n=10^4$ or more fermions. This result provides a concrete framework in which the numerical instability of the sign problem is completely bypassed, and serves as a counterexample to the prevailing view that all truly fermionic PIMC sign problems are NP-hard.

</details>

### [34] [Floquet Interpretation of Avoided Crossings in AC Stark-Shifted Rydberg-EIT Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06062)
*Rajavardhan Talashila,Nikunjkumar Prajapati,Noah Schlossberger,Christopher L. Holloway*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper combines experimental measurements with Floquet theory (using the Shirley method) to explain avoided crossings in AC Stark-shifted electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) spectra of Rydberg atoms. The authors show that conventional AC Stark maps fail to fully explain observed structures because they only provide energy shifts without revealing state composition and coupling pathways. By analyzing Floquet eigenstates, they track state mixing, bare-state composition, and higher-order Floquet coupling pathways to reveal the physical mechanisms behind avoided crossings across different principal quantum numbers and RF frequencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Avoided crossings observed in AC Stark-shifted Rydberg-EIT spectra cannot be fully explained by conventional AC Stark maps alone, since these maps only give energy shifts and do not reveal the underlying state composition and coupling pathways responsible for the spectral features.

Method: Combined experimental and Floquet-theoretical approach using the Shirley method (Floquet formalism) to analyze eigenstates of periodically driven Rydberg systems. The authors track state mixing, bare-state composition, and dominant higher-order Floquet coupling pathways to connect measured EIT spectra to dressed-state dynamics.

Result: The Floquet-eigenstate analysis successfully establishes a direct connection between measured avoided-crossing spectra and the underlying dressed-state dynamics, revealing the physical mechanisms governing avoided-crossing formation across various principal quantum numbers and RF frequencies.

Conclusion: Floquet-eigenstate analysis provides a powerful framework for interpreting complex AC Stark-shifted Rydberg-EIT spectra and the underlying higher-order Floquet coupling interactions, going beyond what conventional AC Stark shift maps can offer.

Abstract: We present a combined experimental and Floquet-theoretical study of avoided crossings in the AC Stark-shifted electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) spectra of Rydberg atoms. Observed avoided-crossing structures cannot be fully explained by conventional AC Stark maps alone, which provide the energy shifts but do not reveal the underlying state composition and coupling pathways. Using the Shirley method, we analyze the Floquet eigenstates to establish a direct connection between the measured spectra and the underlying dressed-state dynamics. By tracking the evolution of state mixing, bare-state composition, and dominant higher-order Floquet coupling pathways, we reveal the physical mechanisms responsible for the formation of avoided crossings across different principal quantum numbers and RF frequencies. Our results demonstrate that Floquet-eigenstate analysis provides a powerful framework for interpreting complex AC Stark-shifted Rydberg-EIT spectra and the underlying higher-order Floquet coupling interactions.

</details>

*Jiaqi Yuan,Yunlong Xue,Yongjie Cheng,Ruidong He,Jiteng Sheng,Yanpeng Zhang,Zhengyang Bai,Yu-Qiang Ma,Min Xiao,Zhaoyang Zhang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a high-sensitivity heterodyne shortwave sensor using microwave-dressed Rydberg atoms with precisely modulated Floquet sidebands. By leveraging coherent channels created around local- and microwave-field-engineered Floquet sidebands, the steep response gradient amplifies heterodyne intermediate-frequency signals, improving sensitivity by four orders of magnitude over the same setup without microwave modulation, achieving -122.7 dBm/Hz for 30 MHz shortwave signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rydberg atomic electric-field sensors can overcome the Chu limit (size-to-wavelength constraint) but suffer from substantially lower sensitivity for decametric-wavelength shortwave fields compared to microwave fields, due to the off-resonant nature of low-frequency signals with Rydberg transitions.

Method: A heterodyne shortwave sensor based on microwave-dressed Rydberg atoms is implemented. Precisely modulated Floquet sidebands are engineered via local and microwave fields. These Floquet sidebands create additional coherent channels that enhance atom-shortwave interaction, producing a steep response gradient that amplifies the heterodyne intermediate-frequency signal.

Result: The Floquet-sideband-enhanced shortwave measurement improves sensitivity by four orders of magnitude compared to the same atomic heterodyne setup without microwave modulation, achieving a sensitivity of -122.7 dBm/Hz for shortwave signals at 30 MHz.

Conclusion: The work offers a potential route to high-sensitivity portable shortwave receivers for applications in radio astronomy, radar, and long-distance communications.

Abstract: Rydberg atomic electric-field sensors, under the framework of optical excitation and readout, can overcome the size-to-wavelength constraint imposed by the Chu limit. However, their sensitivity for decametric-wavelength shortwave electric fields is substantially lower than that for microwave ones, stemming from the off-resonant nature of low-frequency signals with Rydberg transitions. Here, we demonstrate a high-sensitivity heterodyne shortwave sensor based on microwave-dressed Rydberg atoms, leveraging precisely modulated Floquet sidebands. Around local- and microwave-field-engineered Floquet sidebands, the steep response gradient, arising from the enhanced atom-shortwave interaction through additionally created coherent channels, induces pronounced amplification of the heterodyne intermediate-frequency signal. As a result, compared to the same atomic heterodyne setup without microwave modulation, such Floquet-sideband-enhanced shortwave measurement boosts the sensitivity by four orders of magnitude, yielding a sensitivity of -122.7 dBm/Hz for shortwave at 30 MHz. This work offers a potential route to high-sensitive portable shortwave receivers in radio astronomy, radar and long-distance communications.

</details>


### [36] [Roadmap on Quantum Sensors for Vacuum Metrology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06626)
*D. S. Barker,J. L. Booth,S. P. Eckel,J. Grosse,K. Jousten,J. Kłos,R. V. Krems,K. W. Madison,T. Rubin,E. Tiesinga*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Laser-cooled and trapped alkali-metal atoms can serve as primary pressure standards in high- and ultra-high-vacuum regimes, achieving relative uncertainties just over a percent across pressures from a few nPa to tens of μPa. Unlike conventional gauges, these sensors require no calibration since pressure is determined solely from collision cross sections, which can be measured or computed at percent accuracy. The technology offers a viable alternative to the costly orifice flow standard and can calibrate cheaper sensors, though it is not yet mature enough for broader research use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an alternative to the existing, expensive orifice flow standard used as a pressure standard in the ultra-high vacuum regime, by leveraging the advancing technology of laser-cooled and trapped atoms as primary pressure sensors that require no external calibration.

Method: Using laser-cooled and trapped alkali-metal atoms as pressure sensors, with pressure determination based solely on collision cross sections between atoms and molecules. These cross sections are either measured independently or computed using rigorous quantum methods, eliminating the need for calibration against other devices.

Result: The sensors achieve relative uncertainties just over a percent at pressures from a few nPa up to tens of μPa, demonstrating performance sufficient to serve as a primary pressure standard and to calibrate cheaper sensors based on ionization gauges.

Conclusion: The trapped-atom pressure sensing technology has advanced enough to serve as an alternative pressure standard in the UHV regime and for calibrating cheaper sensors, but has not yet matured for broader research applications due to complexity and cost. The authors propose a roadmap for advancing this new pressure standard for vacuum metrology.

Abstract: We describe how laser-cooled and trapped alkali-metal atoms have been used as pressure sensors in the high- and ultra-high-vacuum regimes operating with relative uncertainties of just over a percent at pressures from a few nPa and up to a few tens of $μ$Pa. These sensors can serve as primary standards of pressure as the determination of pressure requires only knowledge of collision cross sections between atoms and molecules, eliminating the need for calibration with other devices. The collision cross sections can be measured independently or computed with rigorous quantum methods at percent relative accuracies. We argue that this technology has advanced sufficiently to provide an alternative to the existing, expensive pressure standard in the ultra-high vacuum regime, the orifice flow standard. Experiments with trapped atoms can be used to calibrate cheaper pressure sensors (such as devices based on ionization gauges). However, the technology has not yet matured enough to be of use in other research fields because of the complexity and cost of the experiments. We propose a roadmap for this new pressure standard for vacuum metrology.

</details>


### [37] [Observation of the liquid-gas transition in trapped ions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06776)
*Eliana Ruth Wallach,Yohay Halfon,Yosef Alkoby,Yair Rajmiel,Nevo Werner-Reiss,Ilan Kleinman,Yuval Shagam*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel ion trap with integrated velocity map imaging to directly measure ion velocities, tracking the full liquid-to-gas transition in trapped ion systems. It reveals that the liquid regime begins at warmer temperatures than commonly assumed (marked by radial localization onset) and identifies a maximum density threshold via an abrupt change in heating-rate power-law at higher temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the motional dynamics of trapped ions is critical for precision metrology, quantum information, and quantum-controlled chemistry. However, the intermediate liquid-like regime between Coulomb-crystal and gaseous phases has largely eluded experimental investigation due to lack of direct measurement techniques.

Method: The authors developed a new ion trap with integrated velocity map imaging to enable direct velocity measurements of ions, allowing them to track the entire liquid-to-gas transition process experimentally.

Result: They demonstrate that colder ensembles exhibit more viscous ion motion until radial localization emerges, marking the onset of the liquid regime at a temperature warmer than previously assigned. At hotter temperatures, an abrupt change in the heating-rate power-law indicates a maximum density threshold, revealing the full transition from a fluid with short-range order to a weakly-interacting gas.

Conclusion: Direct velocity measurements provide enhanced sensitivity that reveals the complete liquid-to-gas transition process in trapped ion systems, clarifying the boundaries and properties of the intermediate liquid-like regime.

Abstract: Precision metrology, quantum information, and quantum-controlled chemistry studies all have many implementations in trapped ion systems that require a precise understanding of the motional dynamics of ions. Between the well-studied Coulomb-crystal and gaseous regimes lies the intermediate liquid-like regime whose properties and boundaries have largely eluded experimental investigation. Here, we track the entire liquid-to-gas transition process by leveraging direct velocity measurements using our newly developed ion-trap with integrated velocity map imaging. We demonstrate that the colder the ensemble, the more viscous the ion motion becomes until radial localization emerges, which marks the onset of the liquid regime at a temperature warmer than commonly assigned. At even hotter temperatures, we observe an abrupt change in the heating rate power-law, which indicates the maximum density threshold. The enhanced sensitivity of direct velocity measurements reveals the full transition process from a fluid with short-range order to a weakly-interacting gas.

</details>


### [38] [Magnetic characterization of electronic components for portable atomic sensors using a zero-field optically pumped magnetometry platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07270)
*Hyeonjae Kim,Sangkyung Lee,Sin Hyuk Yim,Taek Jeong,Younghoon Lim*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a zero-field optically pumped magnetometry platform used to characterize the magnetic properties of two electronic components—a photodetector (PD) board and a resistance temperature detector (RTD)—intended for use in a portable atomic magnetometer. The platform measures static fields from dispersive-response shifts, assesses off-axis field effects from absorptive admixture, and quantifies magnetic-field noise via quadrature ASD differences. The platform achieved 71.5 Hz bandwidth and 57.6 fT/√Hz median ASD. The PD board produced ~8 nT static fields (opposite signs by orientation) with magnetic noise contributions of 36–63 fT/√Hz, while the RTD generated only −0.6 nT with no measurable noise or response degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Portable atomic magnetometers targeting sub-0.1 pT/√Hz sensitivity require careful characterization of stray magnetic fields and magnetic noise from nearby electronic components. This work aims to provide a quantitative method and reference data for evaluating the magnetic impact of photodetector boards and RTDs so that component design and placement can be optimized.

Method: A zero-field optically pumped magnetometer is used as the characterization platform. For each component: (1) static magnetic field magnitude and direction along the measurement axis are extracted from the shift in the center of the dispersive response; (2) off-axis field-induced response distortion is assessed via the absorptive component admixture; and (3) magnetic-field noise is quantified using the quadrature difference in amplitude spectral density. Both PD board and RTD are tested in powered/unpowered and orientation-dependent configurations.

Result: The platform demonstrated a −3 dB bandwidth of 71.5 Hz and a median ASD of 57.6 fT/√Hz (20–70 Hz). The unpowered PD board produced static fields of ~8 nT with opposite signs in front- vs. back-facing orientations; the back-facing orientation also showed degraded dispersive response indicative of field inhomogeneity. PD board magnetic-noise contributions were 36.0 fT/√Hz (front) and 63.2 fT/√Hz (back). The RTD readout generated only −0.6 nT static field, with no response degradation or additional noise.

Conclusion: The characterization framework effectively separates static-field, field-uniformity, and noise contributions of electronic components in portable atomic magnetometers. The results indicate that the PD board is a significant magnetic disturbance source whose orientation matters substantially, whereas the RTD has negligible magnetic impact. These findings guide component placement and design in systems targeting sub-0.1 pT/√Hz sensitivity.

Abstract: We present a zero-field optically pumped magnetometry platform for magnetic characterization of a photodetector (PD) board and a resistance temperature detector (RTD) used in a portable atomic magnetometer. For each component, the static magnetic field along the measurement axis is determined from the shift in the center of the dispersive response, while response distortion caused by off-axis magnetic-field components is assessed from the absorptive admixture. Magnetic-field noise is evaluated based on the quadrature difference in the amplitude spectral density (ASD). The platform achieved a -3 dB bandwidth of 71.5 Hz and a median ASD of 57.6 $\mathrm{fT}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ over 20-70 Hz. For the unpowered PD board, static magnetic fields of approximately 8 nT in magnitude and opposite signs were measured in the front- and back-facing orientations. The back-facing orientation also exhibited a degraded dispersive response consistent with magnetic-field inhomogeneity. The magnetic-noise contributions associated with the PD board were estimated at 36.0 and 63.2 $\mathrm{fT}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ in the front- and back-facing orientations, respectively. By contrast, operation of the RTD readout generated a static magnetic field of -0.6 nT, with no measurable response degradation or additional magnetic-field noise. These results provide guidance for the design and placement of electronic components in portable atomic magnetometers targeting sensitivities below 0.1 $\mathrm{pT}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$.

</details>


### [39] [A Portable Dual-Color Two-Photon Rubidium Optical Frequency Standard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08083)
*Sarah K. Scholten,Emily Ahern,Clayton Locke,Christopher J. Billington,Nicolas Bourbeau Hébert,Montana Nelligan,Ashby P. Hilton,Rachel F. Offer,Elizaveta Klantsataya,Christopher Perrella,Andre N. Luiten*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A fully autonomous and portable optical frequency standard based on a dual-color two-photon transition in $^{87}$Rb is demonstrated, achieving a fractional frequency stability of $1.9\times10^{-13}$ at 1s and $3.5\times10^{-15}$ at 8000s without drift removal, using commercial telecommunications technology and a fully integrated portable optical frequency comb.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Portable atomic clocks are critical for applications such as global navigation satellite systems, but existing microwave-based portable clocks are being surpassed by next-generation optical frequency standards. While optical standards offer superior frequency stability, they have only recently reached sufficient technical maturity to operate outside laboratory environments, motivating the development of a fully autonomous, portable optical frequency standard.

Method: The system employs an efficient dual-color excitation of the $5S_{1/2}\rightarrow5D_{5/2}$ two-photon transition in $^{87}$Rb, combining robust commercial-off-the-shelf telecommunications technologies with a fully integrated portable optical frequency comb to provide both optical and microwave outputs for interfacing with existing electronic systems.

Result: The portable demonstrator achieves a fractional frequency stability of $1.9\times10^{-13}$ at 1s of integration time, reaching $3.5\times10^{-15}$ at 8000s without the need for drift removal.

Abstract: Portable atomic clocks are essential in a wide variety of applications, most notably in the operation of global navigation satellite systems. Existing portable atomic clocks utilizing microwave-based interrogation schemes are now routinely eclipsed by the next generation of atomic frequency standards based on optical interrogation. While optical frequency standards demonstrate greatly improved frequency stability, they have only recently reached a level of technical maturity required to demonstrate this improved performance outside of well curated laboratory environments. Here, we demonstrate a fully autonomous and portable optical frequency standard based on an efficient dual-color excitation of the $5S_{1/2}\rightarrow5D_{5/2}$ two-photon transition in $^{87}$Rb. The standard utilizes a combination of robust, highly developed commercial-off-the-shelf telecommunications technologies and a fully integrated portable optical frequency comb, providing the optical and microwave outputs vital for interfacing with existing electronic systems and infrastructure. The system demonstrates a fractional frequency stability of $1.9\times10^{-13}$ at 1s of integration time, reaching $3.5\times10^{-15}$ at 8000s of integration time without the need for drift removal. This portable demonstrator unit marks a significant achievement in the development of Rb optical atomic frequency standards, and for the deployment of optical atomic frequency standards outside of the laboratory.

</details>


### [40] [Nuclear slowing-down factors in alkali-metal vapors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08261)
*Vasiliki Koutrouli,Georgios Vasilakis,Kostas Mouloudakis*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper refines the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics in the SERF regime by showing that longitudinal and transverse spin dynamics have different polarization-dependent nuclear slowing-down factors. Transverse dynamics use the conventional q(p), while longitudinal relaxation uses q(p)+p·dq(p)/dp, which differ substantially at high polarization and can lead to factor-of-2–4 errors in inferred rates. The paper also derives closed-form expressions for residual spin-exchange relaxation of transverse components at finite longitudinal magnetic field, which is quadratic in the field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To correct and refine the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics in the SERF regime, since the single-factor slowing-down framework inaccurately describes longitudinal dynamics at high polarization and neglects field-dependent residual spin-exchange relaxation contributions that are relevant to atomic magnetometers and comagnetometers.

Method: The authors employ perturbation theory on the density-matrix dynamics linearized around the stationary state to derive the polarization-dependent slowing-down factors and the residual spin-exchange relaxation contributions. Results are verified by numerical solutions of the microscopic density-matrix equation.

Result: Longitudinal and transverse spin dynamics are governed by different polarization-dependent slowing-down factors: q(p) for transverse vs. q(p)+p·dq(p)/dp for longitudinal. These differ substantially at high polarization, where the single-factor description overestimates pumping and relaxation rates by factors of 2–4 depending on nuclear spin. Residual spin-exchange relaxation of transverse components at finite longitudinal field is quadratic in the field and can be comparable to the zero-field linewidth within the SERF regime.

Conclusion: The findings refine effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics, providing corrected slowing-down factors and characterizing field-dependent residual spin-exchange relaxation, with direct relevance to atomic magnetometers and alkali-metal–noble-gas comagnetometers used in precision physics searches.

Abstract: Nuclear slowing-down factors account for the sharing of angular momentum between the electron and the nucleus in the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics in the spin-exchange-relaxation-free (SERF) regime. For collinear optical pumping and magnetic field, we find that longitudinal and transverse spin dynamics are characterized by different polarization-dependent slowing-down factors. Transverse dynamics are governed by the conventional factor $q(p)$, with $p$ the electron spin polarization, whereas longitudinal relaxation is governed by $q(p)+p\,dq(p)/dp$. The two factors coincide at zero polarization but differ substantially at high polarization, where the single-factor description overestimates the underlying pumping and relaxation rates inferred from longitudinal transients by factors approaching two to four, depending on the nuclear spin. We further derive closed-form expressions, valid at arbitrary polarization, for the residual spin-exchange relaxation of the transverse spin components at finite magnetic field along the pumping axis. This contribution is quadratic in the magnetic field and can become comparable to the zero-field linewidth at fields well within the SERF regime. The results are obtained by perturbation theory on the density-matrix dynamics linearized around the stationary state and are verified by numerical solutions of the microscopic density-matrix equation. These findings refine the effective Bloch description of alkali-metal spin dynamics and are directly relevant to atomic magnetometers and alkali-metal--noble-gas comagnetometers, including those used in precision searches for physics beyond the Standard Model.

</details>


### [41] [Ultralow Frequency Magnetic Comb Using Pulse Modulated Spin Maser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08340)
*Weiyu Wang,Qingchang Li,Erwei Li,Lan Wu,Qianjin Ma,Shougang Zhang,Guobin Liu*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Researchers implemented an ultralow frequency magnetic comb using a pulse-modulated spin maser in a Rb-Xe hybrid gaseous spin system. The comb's spectral distribution depends on pulse duration, feedback field strength, and phase shift, and matches predictions from a spin echo chain model. It achieves frequency resolution down to tens of nanohertz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frequency combs are important tools across fundamental physics and practical applications, but conventional combs operate at optical or radio frequencies. This work aims to extend the frequency comb concept to the ultralow frequency (magnetic) range to enable high-resolution sensing and measurements in that regime.

Method: The authors report the realization of a magnetic frequency comb using a pulse-modulated spin maser in a Rb-Xe hybrid gaseous spin system. The spectral distribution is controlled by pulse duration, feedback field strength, and phase shift. A theoretical model based on the spin echo chain is provided to explain the comb behavior.

Result: The magnetic comb operates in the ultralow frequency range and achieves a frequency resolution down to tens of nanohertz. The spin echo chain model agrees well with experimental observations.

Conclusion: The successful realization of an ultralow frequency magnetic comb extends frequency comb technology into the magnetic/ultralow-frequency domain, offering new capabilities for precision measurements that benefit from nanohertz-level frequency resolution.

Abstract: Frequency combs are widely used in fundamental physics and practical applications at various areas. Here we report the realization of an ultralow frequency magnetic comb using a pulse modulated spin maser in the Rb-Xe hybrid gaseous spin system. The magnetic frequency comb has a spectral distribution depending mainly on the pulse duration, the strength and phase shift of the feedback field. A simple theoretical model is given in terms of the spin echo chain and agrees well with the experimental results. The magnetic frequency comb works in an ultralow frequency range and reaches a frequency resolution down to tens of nanohertz.

</details>


### [42] [Nonlinear dynamics in an artificial feedback spin maser II: dependence on feedback configurations and time crystal effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08359)
*Lan Wu,Qingchang Li,Weiyu Wang,Qianjin Ma,Guobin Liu*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores nonlinear dynamics of artificial feedback spin masers by using feedback field polarity and geometric configuration as control parameters. By tuning feedback field strength and phase delay, the authors uncover periodic, quasiperiodic, and chaotic spin oscillations, including a unique time-crystal-like spin oscillation mode in a specific parameter regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial feedback spin masers exhibit rich nonlinear dynamics, but there is no systematic framework for understanding and controlling their behaviors. The authors aim to fill this gap by identifying feedback field polarity and geometric configuration as key control parameters.

Method: The study uses feedback field polarity and geometric configuration as control parameters, cooperatively tuning feedback field strength and phase delay to investigate spin dynamics across various feedback field configurations.

Result: The research uncovers periodic, quasiperiodic, and chaotic spin oscillations in the spin maser system. A unique spin oscillation mode resembling time crystal effects emerges in a specific parameter regime.

Conclusion: This work provides a new perspective for understanding the nonlinear dynamics of spin masers, including time crystal phenomena, by systematically controlling feedback field parameters.

Abstract: Artificial feedback spin masers exhibit rich nonlinear dynamics, yet a systematic framework for understanding and controlling their behaviors remains elusive. In this paper, we consider the polarity and geometric configuration of the feedback field as important control parameters for studying the nonlinear dynamics of spin masers. By cooperatively tuning the strength and phase delay of feedback field, we investigate the spin dynamics at various configurations of the feedback field. Periodic, quasiperiodic and chaotic spin oscillations are uncovered. Specifically, a unique spin oscillation mode akin to the time crystal effects emerges within a certain parameter regime. This work presents a different viewpoint understanding the nonlinear dynamics of spin masers including the time crystal.

</details>


### [43] [Hyperfine-driven polarization effects in elastic muon scattering by atomic nuclei](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08835)
*Ivan A. Moiseev,Daria M. Vasileva,Konstantin N. Lyashchenko,Igor A. Zhosan,Deyang Yu,Oleg Yu. Andreev*

TL;DR: This paper presents a theoretical study of polarization dynamics in elastic scattering of muons and antimuons by high-magnetic-moment nuclei (using 209Bi as a benchmark), showing that nuclear-structure corrections significantly alter differential cross sections and polarization observables, and that measuring scattered muon polarization provides a sensitive probe for nuclear charge radii and magnetic dipole moments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding polarization transfer in muon-nucleus scattering is important for probing nuclear structure, but nuclear-structure corrections have not been fully accounted for in previous theoretical treatments of this process.

Method: The study performs a theoretical analysis tracking the evolution of (anti)muon polarization and its transfer to the nucleus, incorporating nuclear-structure corrections, with 209Bi serveing as a benchmark high-magnetic-moment nucleus.

Result: Nuclear-structure corrections significantly modify both the differential cross sections and polarization observables. The analysis shows that scattered muon polarization is a sensitive observable for extracting nuclear charge radii and magnetic dipole moments.

Conclusion: Measuring the polarization of scattered muons provides a sensitive experimental probe for nuclear charge radii and magnetic dipole moments, highlighting the importance of including nuclear-structure corrections in theoretical calculations of muon-nucleus elastic scattering.

Abstract: We present a theoretical study of polarization dynamics in the elastic scattering of muons and antimuons by high-magnetic-moment atomic nuclei, using ${}_{83}^{209}$Bi as a benchmark. The analysis tracks the evolution of (anti)muon polarization and its transfer to the nucleus, emphasizing nuclear-structure corrections. These corrections significantly alter both the differential cross sections and the polarization observables. Our results demonstrate that measuring scattered muon polarization serves as a sensitive probe for nuclear charge radii and magnetic dipole moments.

</details>


### [44] [Simultaneous sub-Doppler laser cooling and optical trapping of bosonic $^{39}$K-$^{133}$Cs and $^{41}$K-$^{133}$Cs mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09003)
*Mateusz Bocheński,Jakub Dobosz,Koray Dinçer,Paweł Arciszewski,Mariusz Semczuk*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper reports the first realization of a laser-cooled and optically trapped 41K-Cs ultracold mixture, alongside simultaneous sub-Doppler cooling (~10 μK) of both 39K-Cs and 41K-Cs Bose-Bose mixtures. For the 39K-Cs mixture, spin-resolved Feshbach spectroscopy (via Stern-Gerlach separation) revealed 14 heteronuclear loss features, 9 of which are new including p-wave features and resonances in additional spin channels. The 41K-Cs mixture exhibits substantially stronger nonexponential loss, currently hindering systematic Feshbach spectroscopy, but provides a starting point for future spectroscopy aimed at ultracold ground-state 41KCs molecules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to produce ultracold K-Cs mixtures and ultimately ultracold ground-state KCs molecules. The 41K-Cs isotope combination is previously unexplored at ultralow temperatures, and characterizing its cooling, trapping, and loss dynamics is essential groundwork for Feshbach-resonance and photoassociation spectroscopy needed for magnetoassociation and coherent optical transfer to ground-state molecules.

Method: The authors implement sub-Doppler cooling using D1-line gray molasses for potassium isotopes and D2 transitions for cesium. Both species are spin-polarized and confined in a 1064 nm optical dipole trap. For 39K-Cs, parallel spin-resolved Feshbach spectroscopy is enabled by Stern-Gerlach separation. Decay dynamics of both mixtures are studied under comparable temperature and density conditions to characterize losses, particularly in the 41K-Cs mixture.

Result: Both 39K-Cs and 41K-Cs mixtures are cooled to ~10 μK, matching single-species performance. This is the first demonstration of a laser-cooled and optically trapped 41K-Cs mixture. For 39K-Cs, 14 heteronuclear Feshbach loss features were observed, of which 9 are new (including p-wave features and resonances in additional spin channels), with 5 agreeing with previously reported resonances. The 41K-Cs mixture exhibits substantially stronger nonexponential loss compared to 39K-Cs under comparable conditions, limiting systematic Feshbach spectroscopy in the current trap.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates simultaneous sub-Doppler cooling and optical trapping of both K-Cs mixtures with state-of-the-art performance, achieving the first ultracold 41K-Cs mixture and discovering new Feshbach resonances in 39K-Cs. While the shortened 41K-Cs lifetime currently obstructs Feshbach spectroscopy, the prepared samples provide a foundation for future studies that could identify pathways to ultracold ground-state 41KCs molecules.

Abstract: We report simultaneous sub-Doppler cooling and optical dipole trapping of $^{39}$K-Cs and $^{41}$K-Cs mixtures. Both mixtures are cooled to temperatures $\sim10$ $\mathrmμ$K, achieving performance comparable to that obtained with each species individually. To the best of our knowledge, this constitutes the first realization of a laser-cooled and optically trapped $^{41}$K-Cs mixture. For the $^{39}$K-Cs mixture, we additionally implement parallel spin-resolved Feshbach spectroscopy enabled by Stern-Gerlach separation.
  For potassium isotopes, we implement sub-Doppler cooling with $D_1$-line gray molasses, while for cesium the entire cooling sequence is implemented using $D_2$ transitions. We spin-polarize the atoms and confine Bose-Bose mixtures in a 1064 nm optical dipole trap. Using the resulting $^{39}$K-Cs samples, we observe 14 heteronuclear Feshbach loss features. Five agree with resonances reported previously, while nine have, to the best of our knowledge, not been observed experimentally before, including $p$-wave features and resonances in additional spin channels.
  The shorter lifetime of the $^{41}$K-Cs mixture in the optical dipole trap currently hinders systematic Feshbach spectroscopy, which we therefore do not pursue in this work. To characterize this limitation quantitatively, we study the decay dynamics of $^{39}$K-Cs and $^{41}$K-Cs mixtures under comparable temperature and density conditions, revealing a substantially stronger nonexponential loss in the $^{41}$K-Cs mixture. The demonstrated preparation of ultracold $^{41}$K-Cs mixtures provides a starting point for Feshbach-resonance and photoassociation spectroscopy of this previously unexplored isotopologue. Such measurements are essential for identifying suitable pathways for magnetoassociation and coherent optical transfer, and ultimately for the production of ultracold ground-state $^{41}$KCs molecules.

</details>

### [45] [Slow dynamics in concrete: Effects of temperature, strength variation, and microcracking damage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05456)
*Clayton Malone,Jinying Zhu*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This study examines slow dynamic behavior in concrete with varying compressive strengths (32-56 MPa) and ASR-induced damage using coda wave interferometry. Results show higher strength concrete recovers faster from compressive loading, while ASR-damaged specimens exhibit increased softening, faster recovery rates, but longer recovery times—demonstrating slow dynamics as a tool for concrete characterization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether slow dynamic behavior can serve as a characterization tool for concrete, by evaluating how compressive strength and ASR damage affect recovery behavior after compressive loading.

Method: Concrete prisms of varying compressive strengths (32-56 MPa) and ASR-damaged specimens were conditioned via compressive loading. Coda wave interferometry (CWI) monitored the recovery process, with a self-referencing temperature correction technique applied to minimize ambient temperature effects. Recovery rate (m_v) and velocity drop magnitude (|c|) were extracted from fits, and recovery times were computed from these parameters.

Result: For intact specimens, recovery rate and velocity drop increased with compressive strength; higher-strength concrete recovered faster (9.9 h for 32 MPa vs. 6.6 h for 56 MPa). ASR-damaged specimens under identical stress showed increased softening, faster recovery rates, but significantly longer recovery times (up to 458 h).

Conclusion: Slow dynamics-based measurements are viable for concrete characterization: they can assess strength of intact samples and are sensitive to microcracking in damaged samples, particularly ASR-induced damage.

Abstract: In this study, we investigated the slow dynamic behavior of concrete with varying compressive strengths ($f'_c = 32\sim56$ MPa) and alkali-silica reaction (ASR) damage. Concrete prisms were conditioned by compressive loading and recovery was monitored by coda wave interferometry (CWI). A self-referencing temperature correction technique was used to minimize the effect of ambient temperature changes, demonstrating the importance of careful temperature control and correction. For intact specimens, both the recovery rate, $m_v$, and velocity drop magnitude, $|c|$, generally increased with compressive strength. Recovery times were calculated from the fit parameters, and higher strength specimens were found to recover faster (9.9 h for $f'_c = 32$ MPa, 6.6 h for $f'_c = 56$ MPa). When the same stress was applied, ASR-damaged specimens had increased softening and faster recovery rates, but longer recovery times (up to 458 h). These findings demonstrate the potential of slow dynamics in concrete characterization, through its ability to evaluate the strength of intact samples and its sensitivity to microcracking in damaged samples.

</details>


### [46] [Physics-Constrained Inverse Estimation of Irradiation-Induced Strain in He-H Ion-Implanted 4H-SiC Using Nanoindentation and Finite Element Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07157)
*M. Bensalem,N. Daghbouj,J. Duchon,B. S. Li,A. T. AlMotasem,S. Magalhaes,A. Yie,F. Munnik,Xin Ou,W. J. Weber,T. Polcar*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: A physics-constrained inverse modeling framework combining nanoindentation, FEM, and simplex-based optimization is used to estimate depth-dependent irradiation-induced strain in He/H ion-implanted single crystal 4H SiC, yielding a peak tensile strain of 0.91% and validated against nano-beam precession electron diffraction measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While nanoindentation is widely used to evaluate mechanical properties of irradiated materials, its potential for quantifying irradiation-induced subsurface strain remains underexplored, motivating the development of a framework to extract strain distributions from this technique.

Method: An integrated experimental-numerical approach is employed combining depth-sensing nanoindentation, finite element modeling, and a simplex-based inverse optimization routine. The irradiation-induced strain is modeled as a lognormal distribution, consistent with SRIM damage simulations, and implemented in FEM via depth-dependent thermal expansion. Peak strain and mechanical parameters (Young's modulus, yield strength) are calibrated by minimizing squared error between simulated and experimental force-displacement curves.

Result: The peak tensile strain is estimated at 0.91%, with an effective Young's modulus of 310 GPa and yield strength of 16.4 GPa. Independent validation using nano-beam precession electron diffraction (N-PED) confirms good agreement between reconstructed and experimentally measured out-of-plane strain profiles in both magnitude and spatial distribution.

Conclusion: Nanoindentation combined with physics-based inverse modeling provides a practical, complementary tool to diffraction-based techniques for quantifying irradiation-induced strain and residual stress in nuclear ceramics, relevant to assessing subsurface damage in advanced nuclear systems.

Abstract: Nanoindentation is widely used to evaluate the mechanical properties of irradiated materials however its potential for quantifying irradiation induced subsurface strain remains underexplored. In this work, an integrated experimental numerical framework based on a physics constrained inverse modeling approach is employed to estimate the magnitude of a depth dependent irradiation induced strain distribution in single crystal 4H SiC following sequential He and H ion implantation. The approach combines depth sensing nanoindentation, finite element modeling FEM, and a simplex based inverse optimization routine to calibrate a physically motivated eigenstrain profile derived from ion damage simulations. The strain field is assumed to follow a lognormal distribution consistent with independently determined damage profiles SRIM, and is implemented in the FEM model through a depth dependent thermal expansion formulation. By minimizing the squared error between simulated and experimental force displacement curves, the peak tensile strain is estimated to be 0.91, accompanied by an effective Young s modulus of 310 GPa and a yield strength of 16.4 GPa. Independent validation using nano beam precession electron diffraction N PED confirms good agreement between the reconstructed and experimentally measured out of plane strain profiles in both magnitude and spatial distribution. The results demonstrate that nanoindentation, when combined with physics based inverse modeling, can provide a practical tool for quantifying irradiation-induced strain and residual stress in nuclear ceramics. This methodology offers a complementary approach to diffraction based techniques for assessing subsurface damage in ion irradiated materials relevant to advanced nuclear systems.

</details>


### [47] [Boundary-induced medium mapping enables air-equivalent acoustic propagation and perfect absorption in water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07171)
*Mingyu Duan,Xiangjun Peng,Ying-Jing Qian,Tian Jian Lu*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper overcomes the extreme water-air impedance contrast using flexible boundaries that create direct physical mapping between water and air acoustics, enabling a water-filled channel to emulate air-equivalent wave propagation and achieve broadband underwater sound absorption at deep-subwavelength thickness approaching the causal limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The extreme water-air impedance contrast (~3600) has fundamentally separated airborne and underwater acoustics, preventing the transplantation of acoustic functionalities and metamaterial designs between these two distinct media.

Method: The authors employ flexible boundaries to establish a direct physical mapping between disparate acoustic media, enabling a water-filled channel to emulate air-equivalent wave propagation. Through vibroacoustic coupling, the effective wave velocity is rescaled in an approximately nondispersive manner, producing slow waves with tunable attenuation.

Result: The approach demonstrates broadband underwater sound absorption at deep-subwavelength thicknesses approaching the causal limit.

Conclusion: Acoustic media can be reshaped via boundary dynamics rather than bulk composition, paving the way for transplanting acoustic functionalities and metamaterial design across distinct media.

Abstract: The extreme water-air impedance contrast (~3600) has long acted as a fundamental barrier separating airborne and underwater acoustics. Here, we overcome this barrier with flexible boundaries, which establish a direct physical mapping between disparate acoustic media, enabling a water-filled channel to emulate air-equivalent wave propagation. Through vibroacoustic coupling, the effective wave velocity is rescaled in an approximately nondispersive manner, producing slow waves with tunable attenuation. Leveraging this concept, we demonstrate broadband underwater sound absorption at a deep-subwavelength thickness approaching the causal limit. Our findings reveal that acoustic media can be reshaped via boundary dynamics rather than bulk composition, paving the way for transplanting acoustic functionalities and metamaterial design across distinct media.

</details>


### [48] [An integrated viscoelastic modeling framework combining analytical and FEM approaches: Application to WSe2 coatings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07292)
*Mohamed Bensalem,Fateh Bahadur,Yue Wang,Nabil Daghbouj,Tomas Polcar*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents an integrated analytical and finite element (FEM) viscoelastic modeling framework for characterizing nanoindentation responses of coatings, validated on WSe2 coatings with accurate and computationally efficient results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a robust and efficient tool for characterizing viscoelastic behavior of coatings from nanoindentation data, enabling access to internal stress/strain fields and deeper insight into deformation and failure mechanisms.

Method: Combines an analytical Burgers-based model for extracting rheological parameters from load-displacement data with a 2D axisymmetric ABAQUS FEM model using Prony series viscoelasticity. An automated Nelder-Mead simplex inverse optimization minimizes discrepancy between experimental and simulated responses.

Result: Validation on WSe2 coatings shows agreement between analytical predictions, FEM simulations, and experimental measurements, with accurate predictions and high computational efficiency despite geometric simplifications.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a robust, computationally efficient tool for viscoelastic characterization of coatings from nanoindentation while offering access to internal fields for studying plastic deformation, crack initiation, and failure mechanisms.

Abstract: This work presents an integrated methodology combining analytical and finite element FEM based viscoelastic modeling for characterizing the nanoindentation response of coatings. The proposed framework combines two complementary modeling approaches: an analytical model based on the Burgers formulation to analyze nanoindentation load displacement data and extract rheological parameters, and a numerical FEM model implemented in ABAQUS using a 2D axisymmetric indenter coating substrate configuration with viscoelasticity represented through Prony series. An automated inverse optimization routine employing the Nelder Mead simplex algorithm minimizes the discrepancy between experimental and simulated responses. The methodology is demonstrated and validated on WSe2 coatings, showing an agreement between the analytical predictions, FEM simulations, and experimental measurements. Despite the geometric simplifications, the FEM approach provides accurate predictions while maintaining high computational efficiency of time dependent mechanical behavior. The proposed framework provides a robust tool for characterizing viscoelastic behavior from nanoindentation data while enabling access to internal stress and strain fields, thereby offering deeper insight into plastic deformation, crack initiation, and failure mechanism.

</details>


### [49] [Dynamic distortion from discretized coupling feedback in soft-spin Ising machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07347)
*Victor H. González,Natalia G. Berloff*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes "dynamic feedback distortion" as a universal metric to quantify the effects of coupling field discretization in time-multiplexed Ising machines, and demonstrates analytically and numerically that this distortion can be mitigated via global bias tuning or proper discrete coupling element selection, plus colored noise injection. These mitigations yield two-to-three orders of magnitude improvements in success rates for complex combinatorial optimization problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most Ising machine literature relies on empirical benchmarks with low universality, lacking a principled framework to quantify hardware-specific effects like coupling field discretization that degrade optimization performance.

Method: The authors introduce dynamic feedback distortion as a universal analytical metric derived from realistic hardware transfer functions. They analytically show distortion mitigation through global bias (or selecting the correct discrete coupling element) and colored noise injection, supported by numerical experiments on tunable Möbius ladders and 2D tiling problems.

Result: The proposed mitigation protocols are highly effective, particularly in complex energy landscapes, achieving a two-to-three order of magnitude increase in success rates for hard COPs when distortion is analogously reduced.

Conclusion: Dynamic feedback distortion provides a universal, first-principles metric for quantifying discretization effects in time-multiplexed IMs, and practical mitigation strategies can dramatically enhance solution quality for difficult combinatorial optimization problems.

Abstract: Although Ising machines (IMs) have grown in popularity due to their potential as physics-based accelerators for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs), most literature focuses on empirically-obtained benchmarks with low universality. In this work, we propose dynamic feedback distortion as a universal metric to quantify the effect of coupling field discretization in time-multiplexed IMs. Starting from the transfer functions of realistic hardware, we analytically show how this distortion can be mitigated using a global bias (or choosing the correct discrete coupling element) and injecting coloured noise. Our numerical results show that our mitigation protocols are very effective, especially in complicated energy landscapes (namely tunable Möbius ladders and 2D tiling problems); where an analogue reduction of the distortion leads to a two--three order of magnitude increase in the success rate of complex COPs.

</details>


### [50] [Quantum Sensing for Spatial Spin Noise Mapping via Nitrogen-Vacancy Magnetic Quenching in Diamond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07684)
*Ashley J. Tyler,Thomas D. Bateman-Price,Bradley T. Flinn,William J. Cull,Tom J. P. Irons,Andrei N. Khlobystov,Melissa L. Mather*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate a microwave-free magnetic quenching (MQ) method using nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centres in diamond for wide-field spin-noise mapping under continuous LED illumination and low-frequency modulated magnetic fields, validated with gadobutrol series and applied to boron nitride and carbon nanotube samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
