Motivation: Bulk spin-noise measurements average over spatial heterogeneity while scanning-probe maps require sequential rastering; there is a need for a wide-field, spatially resolved approach that avoids complex resonant microwave delivery or pulsed optical hardware.

Method: The protocol uses continuous LED illumination combined with a low-frequency, amplitude-modulated magnetic field to induce field-induced spin-state mixing that reduces NV photoluminescence, providing contrast. Spin-noise responses were characterized as functions of optical power and modulation amplitude, interpreted with a spin-state mixing model, and validated against ODMR measurements.

Result: The method showed gadobutrol concentration trends consistent with ODMR, mapped spin-noise across boron nitride and SWCNT samples with differing defect/catalyst contributions supported by EM and spectroscopy, and detected a spin-noise response in boron nitride nanotubes silent in bulk EPR spectroscopy.

Conclusion: The MQ framework provides a practical, microwave-free wide-field approach for spatially resolved spin-noise sensing, applicable to quantum technologies and functional materials.

Abstract: Detecting and mapping spin noise can reveal spatial variations in surface defect states, catalytic residues, free radicals, and spintronic materials. However, bulk measurements average over spatial heterogeneity, while scanning-probe maps require sequential rastering. Here, we demonstrate a microwave-free magnetic quenching (MQ) method for wide-field spin-noise mapping using nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centres in diamond. The protocol combines continuous LED illumination with a low-frequency, amplitude-modulated magnetic field, avoiding resonant microwave delivery or pulsed optical hardware. Field-induced spin-state mixing reduces NV photoluminescence, providing the primary contrast mechanism. We examine the response as a function of optical power and magnetic modulation amplitude, interpreting the results using a spin-state mixing model. The method is validated with aqueous gadobutrol concentration series, showing trends consistent with optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) measurements. We map spin-noise variations across boron nitride and single-walled carbon nanotube samples with differing defect and metallic catalyst contributions, supported by electron microscopy and spectroscopy. Notably, MQ detects a spin-noise response in boron nitride nanotubes that is silent in bulk EPR spectroscopy. This approach provides a practical framework for spatially resolved spin-noise sensing across quantum technologies and advanced functional materials.

</details>


### [51] [Wave-based reading of mechanical memory in multistable mass-in-mass metamaterials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07889)
*Audrey Watkins,Giovanni Bordiga,Vincent Tournat,Katia Bertoldi*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper experimentally demonstrates a mass-in-mass bistable mechanical metamaterial where both writing and reading of stored mechanical information can be achieved solely through boundary-applied dynamic excitations, leveraging state-dependent stiffness. The architecture also functions as an amplitude sensor and a reconfigurable wave-control device.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While bistable mechanical metamaterials can store information as mechanical bits (written via global inputs), existing readout strategies rely predominantly on visual inspection. There is a need for a purely mechanical, boundary-excitation-based approach to both write and read stored information.

Method: The authors experimentally construct a mass-in-mass bistable metamaterial exhibiting state-dependent stiffness. They apply boundary-applied dynamic excitations to both write information (switch bistable states) and read the stored mechanical bits based on how the structure's dynamic response reflects its current state.

Result: The metamaterial successfully enables both writing and reading of mechanical information using only boundary-applied dynamic excitations. The architecture also demonstrates functionality as a mechanical sensor for input amplitude and as a reconfigurable wave-control device.

Conclusion: Bistable metamaterials are versatile multifunctional platforms capable of integrating mechanical memory, sensing, and adaptive wave manipulation without relying on visual inspection for readout.

Abstract: Mechanical metamaterials with integrated bistable elements have emerged as promising platforms for mechanical information storage, where transitions between stable states encode information as mechanical bits. While it has been shown that information can be written into such metamaterials by applying global inputs, existing readout strategies rely predominantly on visual inspection. Here, we experimentally demonstrate a mass-in-mass bistable metamaterial with state-dependent stiffness that enables both writing and reading of mechanical information using only boundary-applied dynamic excitations. We further show that such metamaterial architecture functions as both a mechanical sensor of input amplitude and a reconfigurable wave-control device. Together, these results highlight the versatility of bistable metamaterials as multifunctional platforms that integrate mechanical memory, sensing, and adaptive wave manipulation.

</details>


### [52] [Capacitance of Undoped Thin-Film Diodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08047)
*Oskar J. Sandberg,Mathias Nyman,Stefan Zeiske,Staffan Dahlstrom,Johannes Benduhn,Ardalan Armin*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives an analytical model for the capacitance of thin-film diodes based on undoped semiconductors, where capacitance is dominated by injected charge carriers. The model accounts for both injected carriers and electrode charge effects, and is used to develop a method for extracting built-in voltage. The framework is validated via drift-diffusion simulations and experiments on organic solar cells.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While capacitance in undoped semiconductor thin-film diodes is known to be governed by injected charge carriers rather than doping-induced carriers, no analytical framework had been established to describe this capacitance, leaving a fundamental theoretical gap in understanding these devices.

Method: The authors derive an analytical expression for the capacitance that fully incorporates injected charge carrier and electrode charge effects. The theory is validated numerically using drift-diffusion simulations and experimentally on organic solar cells, and is used to formulate a method for extracting the built-in voltage.

Result: The derived analytical capacitance model is substantiated by both numerical drift-diffusion simulations and experimental measurements on organic solar cells, and a built-in voltage extraction method based on the framework is presented.

Conclusion: An analytical capacitance framework for undoped thin-film diodes is established, providing a theoretically grounded and experimentally validated method for built-in voltage extraction from capacitance measurements.

Abstract: The capacitance of thin-film diodes based on undoped semiconductors are dominated by injected charge carriers rather than doping-induced carriers. However, an analytical framework of the capacitance in these devices has remained elusive. Here, we derive an analytical description of the capacitance, fully accounting for injected charge carrier and electrode charge effects. Based on these findings, a method to extract the built-in voltage in these devices is presented. The theoretical framework is substantiated numerically by drift-diffusion simulations and experimentally on organic solar cells.

</details>


### [53] [Dynamic Sensing via Photomodulated Gas Desorption in Plasmonic Nanoparticle Chemiresistors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08883)
*Lukas Mielke,Dahee Heo,Gabriele Carelli,Hendrik Schlicke*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a photothermal heating approach using LED excitation matched to the plasmon resonance of gold nanoparticle composites to dynamically perturb the sorption equilibrium of chemiresistive sensors. The induced dynamic sensor responses enable VOC detection via differential signal component analysis and improve baseline stability, overcoming the need for reference gas calibration in quasi-static sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional quasi-static chemiresistive sensing relies on baseline measurements in reference gas, which is prone to baseline drift and requires repeated calibration with reference gas—impractical for inexpensive miniaturized point-of-care sensors. Dynamic excitation offers an alternative to obtain reliable baseline information and reduce drift effects in a miniaturizable way.

Method: The authors employ photothermal heating of hybrid chemiresistive gold nanoparticle composites by LED excitation tuned to the plasmon resonance. This dynamically shifts the analyte sorption equilibrium, driving the sensor out of equilibrium in a controlled manner. The resulting transient sensor responses are recorded and interpreted via differential signal components.

Result: The photothermally induced dynamic sensor responses enable detection of volatile organic compounds (VOC) through interpretation of differential signal components, and this dynamic approach improves the chemiresistors' baseline stability compared to quasi-static sensing.

Conclusion: Light-activated dynamic excitation is a powerful and miniaturizable sensing paradigm that circumvents the need for reference gas calibration, provides reliable analyte identification via differential signal analysis, and enhances baseline stability in chemiresistive gas sensing.

Abstract: Quasi-static sensing, i.e., measurement of a sensor signal change induced by analyte presence with respect to a baseline signal observed in a reference gas beforehand, is the state of the art for qualitative and quantitative analyte identification in chemiresistive sensing. However, this approach is prone to baseline drift and, especially for inexpensive miniaturized point-of-care sensors, repeated calibration with reference gas is not feasible. To access reliable baseline information, active switching between analyte and reference gas would be required, causing a limitation for miniaturized implementation. Dynamic excitation of sensors offers an alternative: Via external stimuli the sensor is reversibly driven out of equilibrium in a controlled way, and the resulting transient sensor response, which is dependent on analytes present, is recorded and interpreted. Correlation of excitation and response signals can reduce effects of baseline drift and furthermore, dynamic features in sensor responses may contain valuable information. Especially light activation is a powerful and potentially miniaturizable approach to agitate sorption processes and therefore induce dynamics. In this work we report photothermal heating of hybrid, chemiresistive gold nanoparticle composites via LED excitation matching the plasmon resonance, to dynamically shift the analyte sorption equilibrium and induce dynamic sensor responses in a highly controlled way. We demonstrate that these sensor responses enable the detection of volatile organic compounds (VOC) via interpretation of differential signal components and that this approach improves the chemiresistors' baseline stability.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [54] [Bridging simulation length scales with cellular automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05696)
*John J. Karnes,Esteban D. Gadea,Shakkira Erimban,Ignacio J. Bombau,Valeria Molinero*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a cellular automata (CA) approach to generate arbitrarily large lattice starting configurations for multiscale simulations, learning the CA rules from fine-grained MD configurations via logistic regression, and demonstrating the method on hydrated anion exchange membranes benchmarked against MD-derived "ground truth" in kMC simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multiscale simulation, coarse-grained models must inherit their starting configurations from higher-fidelity simulations, so the length scales accessible to coarse-grained models are limited by the largest fine-grained configuration that can be affordably equilibrated. This bottleneck prevents coarse-grained simulations from reaching the length/time scales the method itself could otherwise achieve.

Method: The authors introduce a cellular automata (CA) approach where short-ranged local rules drive lattice evolution, producing emergent long-range order. A fine-grained simulation configuration is used as the training set, and CA rules are learned via logistic regression. The method is validated by generating starting configurations for a hydrated anion exchange membrane and benchmarking them in mesoscale kMC simulations against MD-derived ground truth.

Result: CA-generated configurations produce kMC simulation behavior consistent with MD-derived configurations. The method successfully generates substantially larger lattice configurations whose kMC behavior remains consistent with the smaller CA benchmark configurations.

Conclusion: The CA-based approach effectively decouples the length scale of coarse-grained simulations from the equilibration cost of fine-grained models, enabling access to arbitrarily large lattice starting configurations while preserving physically meaningful morphology.

Abstract: Multiscale simulation requires coupling physics models that operate at different characteristic length and time scales, because no single method spans the range needed for most problems of interest. Moving to a coarser-grained representation unlocks longer length and time scales, but it discards the microscopic interactions that build morphology. A fine-grained model can be initialized from an arbitrary packing and left to self-assemble into a physically meaningful structure; a lower-resolution model cannot, and must inherit its starting configuration from a higher-fidelity simulation. The length scales accessible to the coarse-grained model are therefore set not by the coarse-grained method itself, but by the largest fine-grained configuration that can be affordably equilibrated. A representative example is the scale-up from particle-based molecular dynamics (MD) to a lattice-based representation such as kinetic Monte Carlo (kMC). In this work, we present a cellular automata (CA) approach for generating arbitrarily large lattice starting configurations. CA is a natural fit for this task: short-ranged local rules drive the evolution of a lattice, and their repeated application gives rise to emergent long-range order, thematically mirroring how short-ranged interactions in MD produce self-assembled morphology. We use a configuration from a higher-fidelity simulation as a training set and learn the CA rules from it via logistic regression. As a proof of principle, we develop these rules for a hydrated anion exchange membrane (AEM), generate new starting configurations, and benchmark their performance in mesoscale kMC simulations against an MD-derived "ground truth." We then demonstrate the ability to generate substantially larger lattices and show that their behavior in kMC is consistent with that of the smaller CA benchmark configurations.

</details>


### [55] [FPBench: Application-Oriented Error Decomposition for Foundation Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05714)
*Kiyan Amirian,Ramanuja Srinivasan Saravanan,Felix Adams,Charles E Schwarz,Yifei Mo*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: FPBench is an application-oriented benchmark that evaluates foundation potentials (FPs) for atomistic modeling on three fundamental computational tasks—force prediction, energy ranking of orderings, and ion/vacancy migration—revealing that average error metrics often fail to predict practical performance and introducing physically-grounded error-decomposition metrics for targeted model development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While foundation potentials (FPs) show near-DFT accuracy when evaluated using average energy and force errors, their performance in practical computational studies is inconsistent, suggesting that standard aggregate error metrics do not adequately capture true task-level reliability.

Method: The authors develop FPBench, an application-oriented benchmark that assesses multiple state-of-the-art FPs on three tasks: force prediction for atomistic simulations, energy ranking for substitutional and vacancy orderings, and ion/vacancy migration. They introduce error-decomposition metrics that resolve performance by physically consequential quantities and configurations, including fractions of highly accurate and large-force-error atoms, far-from-equilibrium atoms, relative phase-stability and convex-hull agreement, and along-path errors in ion migration.

Result: Benchmarking demonstrates that average force and energy errors often fail to predict task performance, revealing substantial differences in practical reliability across FPs. The error-decomposition metrics successfully identify where FP errors arise within specific computational tasks.

Conclusion: FPBench provides an open benchmark, evaluation code, and a public leaderboard for rigorous FP assessment and development, offering targeted guidance by pinpointing error sources within application contexts.

Abstract: Foundation potentials (FPs) have emerged as a new basis for atomistic modeling. While their evaluation using average energy and force errors often indicates near-DFT accuracy, their performance in practical computational studies remains inconsistent. Here, we present FPBench, an application-oriented benchmark that evaluates FPs on representative computational tasks. Multiple state-of-the-art FPs are assessed on three fundamental tasks: force prediction for atomistic simulations, energy ranking for substitutional and vacancy orderings, and ion/vacancy migration. Benchmarking these FPs shows that average force and energy errors often fail to predict task performance, revealing substantial differences in practical reliability. FPBench introduces application-oriented error decomposition through metrics that resolve performance according to the physically consequential quantities and configurations governing computational tasks, including the fractions of highly accurate and large-force-error atoms, far-from-equilibrium atoms, relative phase-stability and convex-hull agreement, and along-path errors in ion migration. These error-decomposition metrics identify where FP errors arise within specific computational tasks, providing targeted guidance for model development. FPBench provides an open benchmark, evaluation code, and a public leaderboard for rigorous FP assessment and development.

</details>


### [56] [Plasmon drag and photoinduced magnetic effects in plasmonic and magnetic metals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05717)
*Md Afzalur Rab,Terence Baker,Natalia Noginova*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates unexpectedly large photoinduced electric signals in permalloy thin films under pulsed laser illumination. The signals contain both magnetically dependent and independent components that follow the laser pulse temporal profile, while the predicted Anomalous Nernst Effect contribution is much weaker and slower. The results suggest ultrafast photoexcited hot electrons, not thermal mechanisms, dominate photovoltage generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Photoinduced electric effects in plasmonic and magnetic materials under pulsed laser illumination exceed electromagnetic momentum-transfer predictions by orders of magnitude. This discrepancy requires deeper investigation into the nature of these effects.

Method: Kinetic studies of photovoltages were performed on permalloy thin films under various photoexcitation configurations, with measurements comparing signal components against predictions from the Anomalous Nernst Effect.

Result: Photoinduced electric signals comprise magnetically dependent and magnetically independent components of large magnitude that mainly track the laser pulse temporal profile. The Anomalous Nernst Effect contribution is predicted to be much weaker with substantially slower kinetics.

Conclusion: Ultrafast photoexcited hot electrons, rather than thermal mechanisms, are the dominant source of the observed photovoltage in these materials.

Abstract: Photoinduced electric effects in plasmonic and magnetic materials under pulsed laser illumination exceed the prediction of the electromagnetic momentum-transfer mechanism by orders of magnitude. In order to get more information on the nature of the effect we study the kinetics of photovoltages in permalloy thin films at different photoexcitation configurations. The photoinduced electric signals consist of magnetically dependent and magnetically independent components which are of significant magnitude and mainly follow the temporal profile of the laser pulse. In contrast, the predicted contribution from the Anomalous Nernst Effect is much weaker and exhibits substantially slower kinetics. These observations suggest that ultrafast photoexcited hot electrons, rather than thermal mechanisms, play the dominant role in generating the observed photovoltage.

</details>


### [57] [Diffusion Quantum Monte Carlo Benchmark of Interlayer Binding and Charge Redistribution in Chemically Distinct Two-Dimensional Van Der Waals Bilayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05845)
*Kayahan Saritas,Hyeonedok Shin,Jaron T. Krogel,Anouar Benali,P. Ganesh,Paul R. C. Kent*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper establishes a controlled many-body benchmark for bilayer materials using diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC), spanning single-sheet materials, transition-metal dichalcogenides, and magnetic transition-metal halides. DMC achieves excellent agreement with experiments on equilibrium separations, binding energetics, interlayer vibrational modes, and charge redistribution, while revealing systematic failures across semilocal, meta-GGA, and dispersion-corrected DFT functionals. Notably, DMC uncovers long-ranged dipolar tails in magnetic Cr trihalides that persist well beyond where semilocal DFT predicts interlayer polarization. The authors provide a scalable HPC workflow for community expansion of these many-body benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting emergent interlayer phenomena in two-dimensional materials requires simultaneously resolving long-range dispersion, short-range orbital hybridization, and electronic correlation—interactions that are intrinsically nonlocal and many-body, and remain challenging even for advanced density-functional approximations. A systematically controlled many-body benchmark is needed to assess and improve existing functionals.

Method: The authors apply diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC), a systematically controllable many-body method, to compute interlayer energetics, equilibrium separations, vibrational modes, and charge redistribution across diverse bilayer systems including single-sheet materials, TMDs, and magnetic transition-metal halides. They benchmark against semilocal, meta-GGA, and dispersion-corrected DFT functionals, and develop a scalable high-performance-computing workflow.

Result: DMC results agree excellently with experiments, while revealing systematic, property-dependent failures in widely used density-functional approximations. DMC further resolves subtle interlayer charge rearrangements, particularly long-ranged dipolar tails in magnetic Cr trihalides that persist beyond where semilocal DFT predicts appreciable interlayer polarization. The resulting energies, response properties, and high-accuracy electron densities form a transferable benchmark dataset.

Conclusion: DMC provides a reliable many-body reference for bilayer materials, uncovering quantitative and qualitative deficiencies in current DFT functionals for interlayer coupling. The benchmark and scalable HPC workflow enable systematic expansion to emerging families of layered quantum materials, guiding the development of next-generation density functionals.

Abstract: Interlayer interactions in two-dimensional materials can generate emergent phenomena absent in their constituent monolayers, including unconventional magnetic order, multiferroicity, and topological magnetic phases. Predicting such emergent behavior requires simultaneously resolving long-range dispersion, short-range orbital hybridization, and electronic correlation interactions that are intrinsically nonlocal and many-body and remain challenging even for advanced density-functional approximations. Here we establish a systematically controlled many-body benchmark for diverse bilayer materials using diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC), spanning single-sheet materials, transition-metal dichalcogenides, and magnetic transition-metal halides. We obtain equilibrium separations, binding energetics, interlayer vibrational modes, and charge redistribution, finding excellent agreement with available experiments while revealing systematic and property-dependent failures across widely used semilocal, meta-GGA, and dispersion-corrected density functionals. Beyond energetics, DMC resolves subtle interlayer charge rearrangements, particularly leading to long-ranged dipolar tails in magnetic Cr trihalides that survive well beyond the regime where semilocal DFT predicts appreciable interlayer polarization, providing a many-body basis for understanding interlayer-coupled ferroic and magnetic phenomena. The resulting energies, response properties, and high-accuracy electron densities constitute a transferable benchmark for developing next-generation density functionals. Finally, we provide a scalable high-performance-computing workflow that enables systematic expansion of many-body benchmark datasets across emerging families of layered quantum materials by the scientific community.

</details>


### [58] [The electromagnetic stress tensor in cubic crystals and amorphous solids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05969)
*Richard Dengler*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper derives the electromagnetic vacuum stress tensor for classical point-like atoms on a cubic lattice directly from the electric field and via the phenomenological dielectric-derivative formula, showing agreement. It then models an amorphous solid as a mosaic of randomly oriented cubic crystal grains and averages the stress tensor over all orientations, obtaining two components—one parallel and one perpendicular to the electric field—with the transverse component matching Peierls' result for liquids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To derive the electromagnetic vacuum stress tensor in discrete atomic systems and understand whether macroscopic electrodynamics alone is sufficient, given that the stress tensor contains a nontrivial constant term that cannot be obtained macroscopically.

Method: Derivation of the stress tensor from the microscopic electric field in a cubic lattice of classical point-like atoms, cross-checked with the phenomenological formula involving the derivative of the dielectric tensor. An amorphous solid is then modeled as a mosaic of randomly oriented cubic crystal grains, and the stress tensor is obtained by orientational averaging.

Result: The microscopic and phenomenological derivations agree. The stress tensor contains a nontrivial constant absent from macroscopic electrodynamics. For the amorphous model, the orientationally averaged stress tensor retains two components: along and perpendicular to the electric field. The transverse component coincides with Peierls' liquid result derived independently.

Conclusion: Microscopic derivations reveal corrections beyond macroscopic electrodynamics, and orientation averaging connects crystalline and amorphous electromagnetic stress tensors, bridging to liquid theory via Peierls' result.

Abstract: The electromagnetic vacuum stress tensor in a system consisting of classical point-like atoms on a cubic lattice is derived directly from the electric field and, alternatively, using the known more phenomenological formula involving the (usually unknown) derivative of the dielectric tensor. The results agree. The stress tensor contains a nontrivial constant and therefore cannot be obtained from macroscopic electrodynamics alone.
  A system consisting of a mosaic of randomly oriented grains of a cubic crystal is used as a model of an amorphous solid. The electromagnetic stress tensor in this system is deduced by averaging the corresponding quantity in a cubic crystal over all orientations. Two components remain: the component in the direction of the electric field and the component perpendicular to it. The transverse component agrees with the analogous quantity for liquids derived by Peierls in an entirely different way.

</details>


### [59] [Reusable Operators for Irreducible Cartesian Tensor Decomposition and Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05971)
*Mingjian Wen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a general Cartesian construction for decomposing arbitrary-rank tensors into irreducible Cartesian tensors (ICTs) given any intrinsic symmetry, providing explicit reusable operators that extract ICTs and exactly rebuild the original tensor. The method yields Cartesian harmonics and ICT-coupling operators relevant to equivariant machine learning, is demonstrated on rank-4 and rank-6 elastic tensors, yields a rotation- and scale-invariant anisotropy measure evaluated on Materials Project data, and is implemented in the open-source package natto.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While low-rank tensor decompositions into irreducible parts are textbook results, no general construction exists for taking a tensor of arbitrary rank with a given intrinsic symmetry and decomposing it into symmetric traceless irreducible Cartesian tensors (ICTs), nor reusable operators to perform the extraction and exact reconstruction—unlike the well-established spherical formalism. This gap impedes selection rules, orientational averages, and symmetry applications at high rank.

Method: The authors develop a general algebraic construction that, for any rank and intrinsic symmetry, produces explicit operators that (1) extract the irreducible Cartesian tensor parts from a given tensor and (2) exactly rebuild the original tensor from them. These operators depend only on rank and symmetry, so each is built once and reused. The framework further derives Cartesian harmonics of a vector and operators coupling two ICTs into a third. The construction is implemented in the open-source package natto, yielding both exact symbolic and numerical operators.

Result: The construction is demonstrated on the elastic tensor in rank-4 (second-order) and rank-6 (third-order) forms. The rank-4 ICT decomposition defines a rotation- and scale-invariant anisotropy measure, which is evaluated across first-principles elastic tensors of crystalline materials from the Materials Project. The natto package provides the operators symbolically and numerically.

Conclusion: This work fills the missing Cartesian analogue of the spherical irreducible decomposition by supplying a general, symmetry-agnostic construction with reusable operators, connecting directly to Cartesian harmonics and equivariant machine-learning coupling operators. It thereby provides a practical tool (natto) for high-rank tensor analysis and symmetry in physical and computational contexts.

Abstract: Molecular and material properties, from the polarizability to the elastic constants, are described by tensors. Their behavior under rotations is made explicit when a tensor is decomposed into irreducible parts that transform independently. In Cartesian form these parts are the symmetric and traceless irreducible Cartesian tensors (ICTs), whose decomposition and coupling underlie selection rules, orientational averages, and the use of symmetry. The decomposition is a textbook result at rank two, but higher rank and the intrinsic symmetry of physical tensors make it nontrivial. What has been lacking, unlike in the well-established spherical formalism, is a general construction for a given intrinsic symmetry, together with reusable operators that extract the ICTs and rebuild the original tensor exactly. Here, we develop such a construction and obtain these operators explicitly. These operators depend on rank and symmetry alone, and therefore each need only be built once and then applied to any tensor of that class. Building on them, we further obtain the Cartesian harmonics of a vector and the operators that couple two ICTs into a third, both central to equivariant machine learning. The construction is demonstrated on the elastic tensor, in both its second-order form of rank four and its third-order form of rank six. The ICTs of the rank-4 tensor also define a rotation- and scale-invariant measure of anisotropy, which we evaluate across the first-principles elastic tensors of crystalline materials from the Materials Project. The construction is implemented in the open-source package natto, which produces the operators in both exact symbolic and numerical form.

</details>


### [60] [Oxidation of Tantalum Nano-Film by Microwave Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06019)
*Massimiliano Zamengo,Lina Grineviciute,Hsin-Hui Huang,Haoran Mu,Julianija Nikitina,Saulius Juodkazis,Junko Morikawa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper compares three techniques for oxidizing and ablating 200 nm tantalum films into Ta2O5: femtosecond laser direct write, high-temperature furnace annealing (requiring 1 hour at 600°C), and microwave cavity annealing (only minutes at ~50 W). The microwave approach exploits the dielectric contrast between Ta and Ta2O5 at 2.45 GHz, enabling real-time oxidation monitoring via cavity resonance shift and Q-factor changes, serving as a non-invasive in-situ diagnostic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore more efficient alternatives to conventional high-temperature furnace oxidation of tantalum thin films, and to enable in-situ, non-invasive monitoring of the metal-to-oxide transition during processing.

Method: Three oxidation/ablation routes were compared: (i) fs-laser direct write at 515 nm/200 fs with ~900 pulses per focal spot, showing oxidation onset at ~0.1 J/cm² fluence below the ablation threshold; (ii) tube-furnace high-temperature annealing at 600°C; and (iii) 2.45 GHz microwave cavity annealing at ~50 W. The microwave cavity's resonance frequency and Q-factor were monitored during the metal-to-oxide transition.

Result: Complete conversion of 200 nm Ta to 409 nm Ta2O5 required 1 hour at 600°C in the furnace but only minutes in the microwave cavity at ~50 W. Fs-laser oxidation occurred within a narrow fluence window (~0.1 J/cm²) between oxidation onset and ablation. The microwave cavity resonance frequency and Q-factor changed markedly at the metal-to-oxide transition, enabling real-time oxidation tracking.

Conclusion: Microwave annealing offers a vastly more rapid route to Ta2O5 formation than conventional furnace annealing, and the dielectric contrast between the metal and oxide at microwave frequencies turns the cavity into an effective in-situ sensor for real-time, non-invasive monitoring of the oxidation process.

Abstract: Oxidation and ablation of 200 nm tantalum films were carried out by three routes: (i) femtosecond (fs-)laser direct write, (ii) high-temperature annealing (HTA) in a tube furnace, and (iii) annealing in a 2.45 GHz microwave cavity. Complete conversion of the 200 nm Ta layer into 409 nm of Ta2O5 required one hour at 600 C in the furnace, but only minutes at ~ 50 W of microwave power. Fs-laser (515 nm/200 fs) oxidation of the Ta nano-film set in at an average single-pulse fluence of ~ 0.1 J/cm2 under strong pulse-to-pulse overlap (900 pulses per focal spot), i.e. within a narrow window bounded from above by the onset of ablation. Under microwave annealing, both the cavity resonance frequency and the quality factor Q changed markedly at the metal-to-oxide transition, reflecting the collapse of the real and imaginary parts of the permittivity at 2.45 GHz. This dielectric contrast turns the cavity into a sensor: the oxidation can be followed in real time from the shift of the cavity resonance, providing a non-invasive, in-situ diagnostic tool.

</details>


### [61] [Elucidating Guest-Host Mechanisms in ZIF-L for Tuneable Highly Luminescent 2D Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06028)
*Dylan A. Sherman,Lorenzo Donà,Cyril Besnard,Lars Mester,Ben Slater,Judit Farrando-Pérez,Christopher Allen,Joaquín Silvestre-Albero,Jin-Chong Tan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates luminescent organic dyes hosted in 2D ZIF-L metal-organic frameworks, using fluorescein@ZIF-L as a case study. The work establishes mechanisms of guest incorporation, demonstrates tuneable luminescence through synthesis parameter control, achieves 99.7% photoluminescent quantum yield with minimal leaching and high photostability, shows perylene@ZIF-L produces white light emission (CIE 0.33, 0.34), and develops oriented luminescent thin films on Zn foils for next-generation optoelectronic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While 3D MOF host-guest systems are well established for hosting luminescent dyes, 2D guest@MOF systems remain underexplored despite offering advantages such as optical transparency, inter-layer spacing, and vertical thinness. This work aims to understand the mechanisms of guest incorporation in the 2D ZIF-L framework and demonstrate its unique benefits for functional luminescence.

Method: The study uses fluorescein@ZIF-L as a case system, characterized via nanoscale FTIR, diffraction, and topology mapping to confirm guest location in framework cavities. Surface energy simulations correlate guest incorporation with a morphological continuum from leaf-shaped ZIF-L to rectangular F@ZIF-L. Synthesis temperature and solvent ratios were systematically modified to tune guest loading (% mol) and arrangement (monomer-to-aggregate ratio). Oriented luminescent thin films were grown in situ on malleable Zn foils.

Result: Optimized F@ZIF-L exhibited tuneable emission chromaticity, 99.7% photoluminescent quantum yield, minimal guest leaching in solution over 12 months, and high photostability. Perylene@ZIF-L produced white light emission with CIE coordinates of (0.33, 0.34), arising from combined yellow alpha-phase excimer and blue monomeric perylene emission.

Conclusion: The 2D ZIF-L architecture provides distinct advantages as a host for luminescent dyes, enabling rational tuning of photophysical properties through synthesis conditions. Luminescent dye@ZIF-L systems show strong promise for tuneable and resilient components in next-generation optoelectronics, sensors, and lighting systems.

Abstract: 3D metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) are well known effective hosts for luminescent guests that improve tuneability, photostability and material fabricability. 2D guest@MOF systems, while less explored, offer competitive advantages over 3D guest@MOF systems due to their optical transparency, inter-layer spacing, and vertical thinness. This work examines luminescent organic dyes@ZIF-L to establish the underlying mechanisms of guest incorporation in ZIF-L and demonstrate the advantages of the 2D ZIF-L architecture for the material's functional luminescence. Analysing a case study system, fluorescein@ZIF-L (F@ZIF-L), using nanoscale FTIR, diffraction, and topology mapping, confirmed that fluorescein (F) resided in the ZIF-L framework cavities. Supported by surface energy simulations, the extent of guest incorporation was found to be indicated by a morphological continuum, from the characteristic leaf-shaped ZIF-L to rectangular F@ZIF-L. By modifying synthesis temperature and solvent ratios, the luminescent properties of F@ZIF-L could be rationally tuned in terms of guest loading (% mol) and arrangement (i.e. guest monomer to aggregate ratio). When optimised, F@ZIF-L exhibited tuneable emission chromaticity, 99.7% photoluminescent quantum yield, minimal guest leaching in solution over 12 months, and high photostability. Perylene@ZIF-L exhibited unique white light emitting properties, with CIE coordinates (0.33, 0.34) arising from a combination of yellow alpha-phase excimer and blue monomeric perylene emission. Finally, oriented luminescent thin films of guest@ZIF-L materials were grown on malleable Zn foils, demonstrating an in situ fabrication technique. Together, the work highlights the potential for luminescent dye@ZIF-L systems in developing tuneable and resilient luminescent components of next-generation optoelectronics, sensors, and lighting systems.

</details>


### [62] [Momentum-Resolved Electronic Structure for Quasicrystals: Full-Band Spectra and Chern Number](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06043)
*Donglin Yang,Huajie Chen,Dexuan Zhou,Xiaoxu Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A systematically convergent reciprocal-space tight-binding framework is developed for quasicrystals, combining Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonians with an expanding hierarchy of pseudo-Brillouin zones to compute full-band momentum-resolved electronic and topological properties, validated on Penrose and Ammann-Beenker models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quasicrystals lack translational symmetry, which prevents the use of Bloch decomposition and Brillouin-zone integration, making it difficult to formulate and compute full-band momentum-resolved electronic structure.

Method: The framework combines two systematically refinable components: (1) a Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonian that yields local spectral and current-current correlation quantities at each physical momentum, and (2) an expanding hierarchy of pseudo-Brillouin zones that converts these local quantities into bulk thermodynamic observables via an exact local-to-global relation.

Result: Applied to Penrose and Ammann-Beenker models, the framework reveals full-band momentum-resolved quasibands, a multichannel mechanism for pseudogap formation, Zeeman-driven gap closings/reopenings, quantized Chern plateaus, and phason invariance of bulk spectral and topological observables.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified reciprocal-space route to computing full-band spectral and topological properties of quasicrystals, going beyond the scope of low-energy effective models.

Abstract: Quasicrystals lack the translational symmetry that underlies Bloch decomposition and Brillouin-zone integration, making full-band momentum-resolved electronic structure difficult to formulate and compute. We develop a systematically convergent reciprocal-space tight-binding framework for a broad class of quasicrystals. The method combines two systematically refinable components: a Fourier-module scattering-channel Hamiltonian that yields local spectral and current-current correlation quantities at each physical momentum, and an expanding hierarchy of pseudo-Brillouin zones that converts the resulting local quantities into bulk thermodynamic observables through an exact local-to-global relation. Applied to the Penrose and Ammann-Beenker models, the framework uncovers full-band momentum-resolved quasibands and a multichannel mechanism for pseudogap formation, both beyond the scope of low-energy effective models. It further resolves Zeeman-driven gap closings and reopenings, quantized Chern plateaus, and the phason invariance of bulk spectral and topological observables. This framework provides a unified reciprocal-space route to full-band spectral and topological properties of quasicrystals.

</details>


### [63] [Revealing the origin of ionic conduction in silver-iodide-doped silver phosphate glass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06738)
*Jennifer Freedberg,Joseph Maduzia,Andias Santoso,Ranveer Singh,Placid Ferreira,Fahad Mahmood*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using terahertz time-domain spectroscopy across varying AgI concentrations in (AgI)x(AgPO3)(1-x) glassy electrolytes, the authors reveal the intermediate mesoscopic frequency regime and identify a crossover from bound-current-dominated conduction to short-range dispersive ionic transport. Bound polarization from the P-O−-Ag+ motif is necessary but insufficient; ionic transport emerges only when this polarization sits in a sufficiently soft AgPO3 matrix with high carrier density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast ionic transport underpins solid electrolytes, yet its microscopic origin remains poorly understood, making next-generation solid-state battery development largely empirical. Existing measurements cover only low-frequency transport or high-frequency bound polarization, leaving the intermediate mesoscopic frequency regime—where ionic transport actually emerges—unexplored.

Method: Varying AgI concentration x in the glassy electrolyte (AgI)x(AgPO3)(1-x) and performing time-domain terahertz spectroscopy (TDTS) to access the intermediate mesoscopic frequency regime not covered by conventional low-frequency or high-frequency probes.

Result: The experiments reveal a crossover from bound-current-dominated conduction to conductivity arising from short-range dispersive ionic transport. Bound polarization from the P-O−-Ag+ bond-bending motion exists across all compositions but alone is insufficient for ionic transport. Transport emerges only when this polarization is embedded in a sufficiently soft AgPO3 glassy matrix with a high carrier density.

Conclusion: The combination of a soft glassy matrix and high carrier density transforms the vibrationally bound response of the P-O−-Ag+ motif into short-range dispersive ionic motion, revealing the microscopic prerequisites for fast ionic transport in this glassy electrolyte system.

Abstract: Fast ionic transport is a defining feature of many solid electrolytes, yet its microscopic origin is not fully understood. In the absence of microscopic insights, the development of next-generation solid-state batteries remains largely empirical. Most existing measurements access either the low-frequency transport response or the high-frequency bound polarization, yet the intermediate mesoscopic frequency regime is where ionic transport emerges. By varying the $\mathrm{AgI}$ concentration ($x$) and performing time-domain terahertz spectroscopy (TDTS) in a prototypical glassy electrolyte $\left(\mathrm{AgI}\right)_{x}\left(\mathrm{AgPO_3}\right)_{(1-x)}$, we reveal this intermediate frequency regime and identify a crossover from bound-current-dominated conduction to conductivity arising from short-range dispersive ionic transport. We find that bound polarization associated with the bond-bending motion of the $\mathrm{P{-}O^- -Ag^+}$ motif is present across compositions but is insufficient to produce ionic transport on its own. Transport emerges only when this polarization is embedded in a sufficiently soft $\mathrm{AgPO_3}$ glassy matrix and accompanied by a high carrier density. These ingredients together take the system from a vibrationally bound response to short-range dispersive motion.

</details>


### [64] [Altermagnetism from the viewpoint of chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06159)
*Nayana Devaraj,Anumita Bose,Md Afsar Reja,Arka Bandyopadhyay,Awadhesh Narayan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review presents a chemistry-driven perspective on altermagnets—a newly identified class of magnetic materials that merge features of ferromagnets and antiferromagnets—covering fundamental concepts, material families, experimental characterization methods, ab initio discovery strategies, technological applications, and future challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnetism is a new and rapidly developing field, and most existing reviews approach it from a physics perspective. The authors aim to offer a chemistry-grounded viewpoint, emphasizing how crystal chemistry, bonding, and electronic structure determine whether altermagnetic order can emerge, to provide complementary design principles and structure-property guidance.

Method: The paper is a review that: (1) introduces fundamental symmetry, orbital character, and electronic structure concepts for altermagnets; (2) surveys diverse material families and their coordination environments; (3) presents experimental characterization techniques; (4) discusses ab initio computational searches guided by chemical constraints; and (5) explores coupling with ferroelectricity, superconductivity, and prospective applications.

Result: The review consolidates recent progress in altermagnetism across material classes, identifies coordination environments and structure-property relationships favorable to altermagnetic order, outlines characterization approaches, and positions chemistry-informed computational screening as a promising discovery strategy—while highlighting technological opportunities and remaining challenges.

Conclusion: Altermagnetism offers a bridge between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic behavior with unique technological potential. A chemistry-based design framework—rooted in symmetry, bonding, and coordination chemistry—can accelerate the discovery and understanding of altermagnetic materials, and chemically derived guidelines will be important in addressing current challenges and advancing the field toward applications.

Abstract: Magnetism has been a central theme of research in chemistry, physics, and materials science, with chemical composition and bonding playing key roles in determining magnetic behavior. Altermagnets are a newly identified class of magnetic materials that combine features of conventional ferromagnets and antiferromagnets, arising from specific symmetry and electronic structure motifs. In this review, we present a chemistry-driven viewpoint on altermagnetism, highlighting how crystal chemistry, bonding, and electronic structure enable this unconventional magnetic order. We begin by introducing the fundamental concepts required to understand altermagnets, with an emphasis on symmetry considerations, orbital character, and electronic structure signatures. We then survey the diverse material families in which altermagnetism has been identified, drawing attention to coordination environments and structure-property relationships that favor altermagnetic order. We subsequently present experimental approaches which are useful for the characterization of altermagnetic materials. We examine ab initio materials discovery as a promising strategy for identifying new altermagnets, emphasizing how chemical constraints, such as symmetry and bonding, can guide computational searches. Other than their intrinsic importance, altermagnets provide interesting possibilities for technology. For this reason, we highlight possible applications that may be enabled through altermagnetic materials, along with their coupling with existing orders such as ferroelectricity and superconductivity. In conclusion, we point out some challenges and prospects, where chemically-based design guidelines can play an important role towards advancing altermagnetism research. In summary, this review offers an account of recent developments in altermagnetism, from basic concepts to the current state-of-the-art.

</details>


### [65] [A Transport Framework for Evaluating Nanoscale Interconnect Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06170)
*Md. Rafiqul Islam,Patrick E. Hopkins*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper establishes a quantitative transport framework for evaluating metallic interconnect materials under nanoscale confinement by combining measured electrical and thermal conductivities with transport figures of merit. Applied to Cu, Ru, W, Co, Ir, and Mo thin films, the framework reveals that although Cu has the highest intrinsic transport performance, its effective performance degrades due to thickness scaling and liner resistance, while Ru and Mo emerge as promising candidates for next-generation CMOS interconnects due to their favorable transport and barrierless integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As CMOS technology scales, metallic interconnects increasingly limit circuit performance due to rising resistivity, self-heating, and reliability issues. While alternative metals have been proposed, no quantitative framework existed for evaluating their transport performance under nanoscale confinement, making systematic material selection difficult.

Method: The authors establish a transport framework by combining independently measured thermal and electrical conductivities with normalized and unnormalized transport figures of merit. They apply this framework to Cu, Ru, W, Co, Ir, and Mo thin films, using new steady-state thermoreflectance measurements of Mo, Co, and Ir combined with previously reported Cu, Ru, and W data.

Result: Cu exhibits the highest intrinsic transport performance, but its effective performance is substantially reduced by thickness scaling and Ta liner resistance. Ru and Mo maintain favorable transport properties while enabling barrierless integration, identifying them as promising candidates for next-generation CMOS interconnects.

Conclusion: The proposed transport framework enables quantitative comparison of interconnect metals under nanoscale confinement. Ru and Mo are identified as the most promising alternatives to Cu for advanced CMOS interconnect applications due to their combination of favorable transport properties and barrierless integration capability.

Abstract: As CMOS technology continues to scale, metallic interconnects increasingly limit circuit performance through rising resistivity, self-heating, and reliability degradation. Although several alternative metals have been proposed, a quantitative framework for evaluating their transport performance under nanoscale confinement remains unavailable. Here, we establish a transport framework by combining independently measured thermal and electrical conductivities with normalized and unnormalized transport figures of merit. We apply this framework to Cu, Ru, W, Co, Ir, and Mo thin films using new steady-state thermoreflectance measurements of Mo, Co, and Ir together with previously reported Cu, Ru, and W data. While Cu exhibits the highest intrinsic transport performance, its effective performance is substantially reduced by thickness scaling and Ta liner resistance. In contrast, Ru and Mo maintain favorable transport properties while enabling barrierless integration, identifying them as promising candidates for next-generation CMOS interconnects

</details>


### [66] [Zonal dislocations in Laves phases: A coupled synchro-shear slip mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06198)
*Sang-Hyeok Lee,Mariano Forti,Thomas Hammerschmidt,Gang Liu,Julien Guénolé,Siyuan Zhang,Gerhard Dehm,Sandra Korte-Kerzel,Zhuocheng Xie*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A novel deformation mechanism called "coupled synchro-shear slip" is identified in Laves phases, involving the synchronized glide of two synchro-Shockley partial dislocations on adjacent slip planes that form extrinsic stacking faults. This mechanism, confirmed by atomistic simulations and high-resolution STEM in C15 NbCr2, plays a critical role in phase transformations between Laves polytypes and twinning behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the atomistic mechanisms underlying synchro-shear plastic deformation in Laves phases at elevated temperatures, particularly the role of zonal dislocations and localized events, and to uncover new slip pathways governing plasticity in topologically close-packed intermetallic phases.

Method: Atomistic simulations to identify the novel coupled synchro-shear slip mechanism, combined with high-resolution scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) to experimentally reveal extended core structures of coupled synchro-Shockley partial dislocations bounded by extrinsic stacking faults in C15 NbCr2.

Result: A new slip mechanism—coupled synchro-shear slip—was discovered, where two synchro-Shockley partial dislocations glide in a synchronized manner on adjacent slip planes, producing extrinsic stacking faults. STEM observations confirmed such extended cores and their involvement in twinning in C15 NbCr2.

Conclusion: Coupled synchro-shear slip is a critical deformation pathway enabling Laves polytype phase transformations and governing twinning behavior, offering new atomistic understanding of the kinetic nature of plasticity in topologically close-packed intermetallic phases.

Abstract: Synchro-shear is the primary plastic deformation mechanism in Laves phases at elevated temperatures, mediated by synchro-Shockley partial dislocations-zonal dislocations that proceed via localized events such as kink-pair nucleation and propagation. Using atomistic simulations, we identified a novel slip mechanism in Laves phases, namely coupled synchro-shear slip, involving the synchronized glide of two synchro-Shockley partial dislocations on adjacent slip planes, leading to the formation of extrinsic stacking faults. High-resolution scanning transmission electron microscopy revealed the extended core structures consistent with coupled synchro-Shockley partial dislocations bounded by extrinsic stacking faults in C15 NbCr2 and their involvement in twinning. These results highlight the critical role of coupled synchro-shear slip in enabling phase transformations between Laves polytypes and in governing twinning behavior, providing new atomistic insight into the kinetic nature of plasticity in topologically close-packed intermetallic phases.

</details>


### [67] [$β-(Al_{0.19} Ga_{0.81})_2 O_3/Ga_2 O_3$ Modulation-Doped Field-Effect Transistors with $>$ 6 kV Breakdown](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06414)
*Julian Gervassi Saga,Joshua T Buontempo,Nabasindhu Das,Advait Gilankar,Hari P Nair,Nidhin Kurian Kalarickal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates β-(Al0.19Ga0.81)2O3/Ga2O3 modulation-doped field-effect transistors (MODFETs) with Al2O3 gate dielectric and SiNx passivation for high-voltage operation, achieving a record breakdown voltage of 6.6 kV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore and demonstrate the potential of modulation-doped β-Ga2O3 heterostructures for next-generation multi-kV power devices by achieving high breakdown voltages.

Method: Fabricated β-(Al0.19Ga0.81)2O3/Ga2O3 MODFETs with high-quality Al2O3 gate dielectric and SiNx passivation, and characterized device performance including three-terminal breakdown measurements in Fluorinert as a function of gate-drain spacing.

Result: Devices achieved a maximum drain current of 54 mA/mm, on-resistance of 149 Ω·mm, minimum subthreshold slope of 94 mV/dec, ION/IOFF ratio exceeding 10^8, breakdown voltage of 6.6 kV (at LGD = 28 μm), average lateral electric field approaching 2.4 MV/cm, and a power figure of merit of 222 MW/cm²—the highest reported breakdown voltage for this material system.

Conclusion: The demonstrated 6.6 kV breakdown represents the highest reported value for a β-(AlxGa1-x)2O3/Ga2O3 MODFET, highlighting the potential of modulation-doped β-Ga2O3 heterostructures for next-generation multi-kV power devices.

Abstract: $β-(Al_{0.19} Ga_{0.81})_2 O_3/Ga_2 O_3$ modulation-doped field-effect transistors (MODFETs) incorporating a high-quality Al2O3 gate dielectric and SiNx passivation are demonstrated for high-voltage operation. The devices exhibited a maximum drain current of 54 mA/mm, an on-resistance of 149 Ohm.mm, a minimum subthreshold slope of 94 mV/dec, and an ION/IOFF ratio exceeding $10^8$. Three-terminal breakdown measurements performed in Flourinert demonstrated a breakdown voltage that increased with gate-drain spacing, reaching 6.6 kV for a device with LGD = 28 microns. The corresponding average lateral electric field approached 2.4 MV/cm, while a power figure of merit of 222 MW/cm2 was achieved. To the best of the authors knowledge, the demonstrated breakdown voltage represents the highest reported value for a $β-(Al_x Ga_{1-x})_2 O_3/Ga_2 O_3$ MODFET and highlights the potential of modulation-doped $β$-Ga2O3 heterostructures for next-generation multi-kV power devices.

</details>


### [68] [Chemical and interface confinement effects in promoting plastic co-deformation in high-strength nano-scale eutectics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06334)
*Arkajit Ghosh,Amit Misra*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates laser-rapid-solidified Al-(Si,Ge) eutectic composites with hierarchical microstructures featuring an Al-rich fcc matrix and (Si,Ge)-rich diamond-cubic fibers. Despite coarser eutectic spacing with increasing Ge content, the material shows enhanced yield strength and ductility compared to Al-Si, enabled by coupled deformation mechanisms: Ge segregation to sub-grain boundaries in the Al phase and planar fault formation in the (Si,Ge) fibers, allowing co-deformation of metallic and covalent phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eutectics provide a route to overcome plastic incompatibility in disparate-phase heterostructures, but the mechanisms enabling co-deformation of metallic and covalent/semiconducting phases, and how to design eutectics beyond conventional length-scale-controlled strengthening, remain underexplored.

Method: The authors designed Al-(Si,Ge) eutectic composites solidified along the univariant ternary eutectic path via laser rapid solidification. They employed in situ SEM micromechanical testing combined with post-mortem STEM and TEM to characterize deformation mechanisms, alongside microstructural analysis of the hierarchical eutectic structures.

Result: Increasing Ge content coarsens eutectic spacing yet slightly enhances yield strength relative to finer Al-Si, and improves tensile ductility. In the Al phase, Ge segregates to deformation-induced sub-grain boundaries confining glide dislocations; the (Si,Ge) fibers remain crack-resistant at large plastic strain and exhibit deformation-induced planar faults from localized partial-dislocation activity at stressed interfaces and twin boundaries.

Conclusion: Coupled deformation mechanisms—interface confinement in the metallic phase and hard-phase chemistry in the covalent fibers—enable co-deformation of metallic and covalent phases, offering a pathway for designing high-tensile-strength and ductile hierarchical eutectics beyond conventional strengthening paradigms.

Abstract: Eutectics offer a route to overcome plastic incompatibility in disparate-phase heterostructures by coupling microstructural refinement with phase-specific chemical and crystallographic hierarchy. Here, we investigate laser-rapid-solidified Al-(Si,Ge) eutectic composites designed along the univariant ternary eutectic path, solidifying with an Al-rich face-centered cubic (fcc) matrix and (Si,Ge)-rich diamond-cubic (dc) fibers as constituent eutectic phases with faceted interfaces. The microstructure was hierarchical in nature with finer structures within eutectic phases: nanoscale (Si,Ge) clusters in the Al phase and growth twins within the (Si,Ge) fibers with some Al retention. Although increasing Ge content coarsens the eutectic spacing, the yield strength is slightly enhanced compared to the relatively finer Al-Si, and tensile ductility of Al-(Si,Ge) is higher than that of Al-Si. In situ SEM micromechanical testing combined with post-mortem STEM and TEM showed that in the Al-rich phase, Ge segregated to the deformation-induced sub-grain boundaries that confined glide dislocations. Simultaneously, the (Si,Ge)-rich fibers remain crack-resistant at large plastic strain and exhibit deformation-induced planar faults, consistent with localized partial-dislocation activity at highly stressed interfaces and twin boundaries. These coupled mechanisms due to interface confinement and hard phase chemistry enable co-deformation of the metallic and covalent phases, providing a pathway for designing high-tensile-strength and ductile hierarchical eutectics beyond conventional length-scale-controlled strengthening.

</details>


### [69] [First Principles Thermodynamics of Zr B Segregation at Grain Boundaries in Recycled Nd2Fe14B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06335)
*Avik Mahata,Miha Zakotnik*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper uses spin-polarized DFT+U calculations to explain why nanoscale ZrB2 precipitates preferentially form at grain boundaries in recycled Nd2Fe14B permanent magnets. Excess B and Zr are independently thermodynamically stabilized at the boundary, and this preference is retained when they combine in ZrB2-like configurations. All structures remain in the same high-moment Fe-sublattice regime, with boundary-localized defects perturbing magnetization less than matrix counterparts, providing a first-principles basis for the microstructural stability needed in high-coercivity recycled magnets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grain-boundary chemistry governs coercivity and thermal stability of Nd2Fe14B permanent magnets. In recycled magnets, recovery of the hard-magnetic phase alone does not restore the intergranular microstructure. The authors seek to understand why nanoscale ZrB2 precipitates localized at grain boundaries (at only ~0.1 at.% Zr) stabilize boundaries and suppress grain coarsening.

Method: Spin-polarized density-functional theory with a Hubbard correction (DFT+U) is applied to a systematic set of composition-matched bulk/grain-boundary supercells, providing a common correlated-electron description across boundary chemistries. B, Zr, Dy, and ZrB2-like local configurations are compared in bulk and grain-boundary environments of Nd2Fe14B, along with magnetic-state analysis.

Result: Excess B is strongly stabilized at the boundary; Zr shows an independent thermodynamic interfacial preference. The ZrB2-like configuration retains the boundary preference as Zr-B coordination develops. All compared structures remain in the same high-moment Fe-sublattice regime, and boundary-localized defects perturb normalized magnetization less than matrix counterparts.

Conclusion: A thermodynamic pathway exists for co-localization of Zr and B at grain boundaries before ZrB2 formation. Boundary-localized states can support the microstructural stability required for high-coercivity recycled Nd-Fe-B magnets, providing a first-principles explanation for intergranular Zr-B concentration.

Abstract: Grain-boundary chemistry is central to the coercivity and thermal stability of Nd2Fe14B permanent magnets, particularly in recycled magnets where recovery of the hard-magnetic phase does not by itself restore the intergranular microstructure. Our recent experimental study showed that nanoscale ZrB2 precipitates can emerge at grain boundaries and triple junctions during recycling despite an overall Zr concentration of only about 0.1 at.%, where they are associated with boundary stabilization and suppression of grain coarsening. Here we use spin-polarized density-functional theory with a Hubbard correction (DFT+U) to determine the atomistic thermodynamics underlying this preferential localization. A systematic set of composition-matched bulk/grain-boundary DFT+U supercells provides a common correlated-electron description across boundary chemistries. We compare B, Zr, Dy, and ZrB2-like local configurations in bulk and grain-boundary environments of Nd2Fe14B. Excess B is strongly stabilized at the boundary, while Zr also shows an independent thermodynamic preference for the interfacial region. When Zr and B are combined in a ZrB2-like configuration, the boundary preference is retained, indicating that the interface remains favorable as Zr-B coordination develops. These results establish a thermodynamic pathway for the co-localization of Zr and B prior to ZrB2 formation. Magnetic-state analysis further shows that all compared structures remain within the same high-moment Fe-sublattice regime, and boundary-localized defects generally perturb the normalized magnetization less than their matrix counterparts. The calculations therefore provide a first-principles explanation for why Zr-B chemistry concentrates at intergranular regions and how such boundary-localized states can support the microstructural stability required for high-coercivity recycled Nd-Fe-B magnets.

</details>


### [70] [High-Field Electron Transport in AlGaN alloys: A Full-Band Monte Carlo Study Based on Ab Initio Supercell Calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07773)
*Animesh Datta,Matinehsadat Hosseinigheidari,Uttam Singisetti*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses a supercell-based full band Monte Carlo method to investigate high-field electron transport in AlGaN alloys across various Al compositions, examining steady-state velocity-field characteristics, scattering mechanisms, transient velocity overshoot, and temperature dependence for ultra-wide-bandgap compositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AlGaN alloys are promising wide/ultra-wide-bandgap semiconductors for next-generation power and RF electronics. Understanding electron transport is crucial for predicting device performance and identifying material limits under various operating conditions.

Method: A supercell-based full band Monte Carlo method is used to explicitly capture the true disorder of the alloy system, enabling realistic descriptions of carrier transport. Velocity-field characteristics are computed across a range of Al compositions, along with analysis of scattering mechanisms, transient electron dynamics for velocity-overshoot, and temperature dependence.

Result: Key transport metrics including peak velocity, saturation velocity, and critical electric field are evaluated as functions of Al composition. Scattering mechanism contributions are detailed, velocity-overshoot behavior is characterized for various Al fractions, and temperature-dependent transport in Al0.75Ga0.25N is assessed for high-temperature operation.

Conclusion: The results provide a detailed understanding of high-field transport in AlGaN alloys and offer guidance for designing AlGaN-based RF and power electronic devices.

Abstract: AlGaN alloys are promising wide and ultra-wide-bandgap semiconductors for next-generation power and RF electronics applications. To realize the full potential of AlGaN based devices, it is important to understand the electron transport to accurately predict device performance and identify material limits under various operating conditions. In this work, the high-field electron transport properties of AlxGa1-xN are investigated using a supercell based full band Monte Carlo method. The supercell approach is employed to explicitly capture the true disorder of the alloy system, enabling a more realistic description of carrier transport. The velocity field characteristics are calculated across a range of Al compositions to evaluate key transport metrics, including peak velocity, saturation velocity, and critical electric field. The role of different scattering mechanisms is studied in detail to understand the high field transport mechanism in the AlGaN alloy system. In addition to steady state transport, transient electron dynamics are examined for various Al fractions to study velocity-overshoot behavior, which is especially important for improving the performance of scaled RF devices. Finally, the temperature dependence of the velocity field characteristics in ultra-wide-bandgap Al0.75Ga0.25N is investigated to assess its transport performance under high temperature conditions. These results provide a detailed understanding of high-field transport in AlGaN alloys and offer guidance for the design of AlGaN-based RF and power electronic devices.

</details>


### [71] [Barrierless Water Dissociation on Rare-Earth Sesquioxide Surfaces from First Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06350)
*Shuxiang Zhou,Jay A. LaVerne,Hanna Hlushko*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses machine-learning force field-accelerated ab initio molecular dynamics to study water dissociation on (110) surfaces of three cubic bixbyite rare-earth sesquioxides (Sc2O3, Y2O3, Lu2O3). Sampling 25 independent trajectories per material reveals two dissociation pathways: a conventional proximal mechanism with a ~0.1 eV barrier and a previously unreported distal mechanism that is effectively barrierless and energetically preferred. The low barriers are attributed to the periodic array of undercoordinated RE3+ sites in the bixbyite lattice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Water dissociation on metal oxide surfaces is a critical elementary step in heterogeneous catalysis, photocatalysis, and radiation chemistry, but its mechanistic details on rare-earth sesquioxides are poorly understood. Conventional static calculations provide incomplete pictures of the reaction landscape, motivating the use of unbiased dynamical sampling.

Method: The authors employed molecular dynamics combining ab initio calculations with on-the-fly machine-learning force field acceleration to investigate water dissociation on the (110) surfaces of Sc2O3, Y2O3, and Lu2O3 (cubic bixbyite oxides). They sampled 25 independent trajectories per material to obtain an unbiased view of the reaction landscape.

Result: Two distinct dissociation pathways were identified: (1) a conventional proximal mechanism with a small but finite barrier of ~0.1 eV, and (2) a previously unreported distal mechanism that is effectively barrierless and energetically preferred at both adsorption and dissociation stages.

Conclusion: The low barriers align with the periodic arrangement of inherently undercoordinated RE3+ sites in the bixbyite lattice, suggesting that ordered intrinsic coordination defects play a role analogous to stochastic oxygen vacancies in conventional oxides, thereby providing mechanistic insight into water activation on rare-earth sesquioxides.

Abstract: Water dissociation on metal oxide surfaces is a key elementary step in heterogeneous catalysis, photocatalysis, and radiation chemistry, yet its mechanistic details on rare-earth (RE) sesquioxides remain poorly understood. Here, we investigate water dissociation on the (110) surfaces of three cubic bixbyite oxides, Sc$_2$O$_3$, Y$_2$O$_3$, and Lu$_2$O$_3$, using molecular dynamics combining ab initio calculations with on-the-fly machine-learning force field acceleration. By sampling 25 independent trajectories per material, we obtain an unbiased picture of the reaction landscape inaccessible to conventional static calculations. Two distinct dissociation pathways are identified: a conventional proximal mechanism with a small but finite barrier of $\sim$0.1 eV, and a previously unreported distal mechanism that is effectively barrierless and energetically preferred at both the adsorption and dissociation stages. The low barriers are consistent with the periodic array of inherently undercoordinated RE$^{3+}$ sites in the bixbyite lattice, suggesting that ordered intrinsic coordination defects play a role analogous to stochastic oxygen vacancies in conventional oxides.

</details>


### [72] [First-Principles Study of Magnetism, Electronic Structure, and Bonding in Nb-Mn-Ge Kagome Compounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06362)
*Wei-Shen Tee,Shiya Chen,Weiyi Xia,Peter Minch,Cai-Zhuang Wang,Vladimir Antropov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations predict three stable Nb-Mn-Ge compounds (NbMn6Ge6, NbMn6Ge5, NbMn6Ge4) with distinct magnetic ground states: collinear antiferromagnetic for NbMn6Ge6, ferromagnetic for the other two, with varying magnetic anisotropy behaviors and no topological magnetism features near the Fermi level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically investigate magnetic ground states, electronic structures, and bonding characteristics of computationally predicted stable NbMn6Ge6, NbMn6Ge5, and NbMn6Ge4 compounds, which may have potential applications in magnetism.

Method: First-principles calculations based on density functional theory, including magnetic ground state determination, electronic structure analysis, charge density difference, and electron localization function calculations.

Result: NbMn6Ge6 is structurally stable with a collinear antiferromagnetic configuration, while metastable rhombohedral NbMn6Ge5 and NbMn6Ge4 exhibit ferromagnetic ground states. Mn moments are nearly localized. Magnetic anisotropy is uniaxial for AFM NbMn6Ge6 and FM NbMn6Ge5 but in-plane for FM NbMn6Ge4. No topological magnetism features exist near the Fermi level.

Conclusion: The predicted magnetic properties require experimental verification through synthesis, structural characterization, and magnetic measurements. The absence of suitable electronic features near the Fermi level rules out topological magnetism studies for these compounds.

Abstract: In this work, we systematically investigate the magnetic ground states, electronic structures, and bonding characteristics of the computationally predicted stable NbMn6Ge6, NbMn6Ge5, and NbMn6Ge4 using first-principles calculations. Our results show that structurally stable NbMn6Ge6 has a collinear antiferromagnetic configuration, while the metastable rhombohedral NbMn6Ge5 and NbMn6Ge4 favor ferromagnetic ground states. Magnetic moments on Mn atoms are nearly localized, suggesting the applicability of a generalized spin Hamiltonian. Magnetic anisotropy in AFM NbMn6Ge6 and FM NbMn6Ge5 has uniaxial behavior, while FM NbMn6Ge4 has in-plane anisotropy. Charge density difference and electron localization function analyses further show charge redistribution and bonding features within the Mn-Ge Kagome network and between adjacent structural layers. Electronic structures near the Fermi level show no features suitable for topological magnetism studies. Experimental synthesis, structural characterization, and magnetic measurements are required to verify our predictions.

</details>


### [73] [Machine learning for the design and prediction of soft-magnetic electromagnetic shielding FeCo-based alloys in laser cladding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06417)
*Luting Wang,Suiyuan Chen,Xiancheng Zhu,Zhiqing Fang,Mei Wang,Yifan Li,Lei Shen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study proposes multiple machine learning strategies, combined with experimental data and soft magnetic material databases, to accelerate the design of multifunctional FeCo-based alloys for electromagnetic interference (EMI) shielding within an almost infinite compositional space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electromagnetic shielding materials are critical for aerospace and daily life applications, but their design and manufacturing still face persistent challenges. There is a need to accelerate material development and optimize compositions for such multifunctional materials.

Method: The study employs multiple machine learning strategies based on experimental derivation and soft magnetic material databases. It also leverages laser additive manufacturing for fabricating multi-component, multifunctional electromagnetic shielding materials with tailored properties.
