Result: The approach enables rapid and automated discovery of multifunctional FeCo-based alloys with optimized EMI shielding effectiveness, as well as enhanced magnetic and electrical properties.

Conclusion: This work presents a novel approach combining machine learning and laser additive manufacturing for the rapid discovery of multifunctional electromagnetic shielding alloys across a vast compositional space.

Abstract: Electromagnetic shielding materials play a pivotal role in both aerospace applications and daily life. However, their design and manufacturing still face persistent challenges. Machine learning demonstrates significant potential in accelerating material development and compositions optimization. Furthermore, laser additive manufacturing provides powerful technical support for fabricating multi-component, multifunctional electromagnetic shielding materials with tailored properties. In this study, the multiple machine learning strategies have been proposed, based on experimental derivation and soft magnetic material databases, to accelerate the design of multifunctional FeCo-based alloys for electromagnetic interference (EMI) shielding within an almost infinite compositional space. This work presents a novel approach for the rapid and automated discovery of multifunctional alloys with optimized EMI shielding effectiveness, as well as enhanced magnetic and electrical properties.

</details>


### [74] [First-principles-based Prediction of Phase Fields: Part I. Binary and Ternary Refractory Alloys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06627)
*Pravan Omprakash,Nicholas Crnkovich,John Cavin,Nathan Curtis,Adrien Couet,Rohan Mishra*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents a computationally efficient framework combining DFT-calculated binary mixing enthalpies with sub-regular solution models to predict solvus phase boundaries and phase fields in refractory multi-principal element alloys (Cr, Hf, Mo, Nb, Ta, Ti, V, W, Zr). Validated against 36 binary and 15 ternary phase diagrams with good agreement, the method captures miscibility gaps, solid solution stability, and intermetallic formation with miscible temperatures typically within 300 K of experiments. A public web interface is provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Refractory MPEAs have complex phase equilibria and vast compositional spaces, making phase diagram prediction challenging. First-principles methods are prohibitively expensive, while CALPHAD is limited by scarce experimental data, motivating a scalable and efficient predictive framework.

Method: The approach combines DFT-calculated binary mixing enthalpies with sub-regular solution models to construct phase diagrams without fitting higher-order interactions. It incorporates lattice-dependent energetics via sub-regular solution models and includes temperature-dependent elemental phase transitions. Validation is performed against experimental and CALPHAD results for 36 binary and 15 ternary phase diagrams.

Result: Good agreement with both experimental results and CALPHAD calculations was achieved for 36 binary and 15 ternary phase diagrams. Prediction accuracy was enhanced by lattice-dependent energetics and temperature-dependent elemental phase transitions. The framework captures miscibility gaps, solid solution stability, and intermetallic formation, with predicted miscible temperatures typically within 300 K of experimental values.

Conclusion: This work establishes a scalable, first-principles-based route for high-throughput prediction of phase diagrams in refractory MPEAs, with a publicly accessible interactive web interface.

Abstract: Multiple principal element alloys (MPEAs) exhibit complex phase equilibria involving multinary solid solutions and intermetallics, which makes it challenging to predict their temperature-composition phase diagrams. Their vast compositional space makes first principles methods prohibitively expensive, while CALPHAD is limited by scarce experimental data. Here, we present a computationally efficient framework to predict the solvus phase boundaries, and hence, phase fields, in refractory MPEAs composed of Cr, Hf, Mo, Nb, Ta, Ti, V, W, and Zr. The approach combines DFT calculated binary mixing enthalpies with sub regular solution models to construct phase diagrams without fitting higher order interactions, enabling efficient scaling across composition space. Validation against 36 binary and 15 ternary phase diagrams demonstrates good agreement, with both experimental results and CALPHAD calculations. We find that the prediction accuracy is enhanced by incorporating lattice dependent energetics through sub regular solution models and including temperature-dependent elemental phase transitions. The framework captures miscibility gaps, solid solution stability, and intermetallic formation, with predicted miscible temperatures typically within 300 K of experimental values. Overall, this work establishes a scalable, first principles based route for highthroughput prediction of phase diagrams in refractory MPEAs. A publicly accessible web interface has also been developed to allow interactive exploration of the predicted phase diagrams, available at https://raptor.engr.wustl.edu.

</details>


### [75] [Coupled structural and electronic evolution under pressure in CuIr2Se4, CuRh2S4, and CuRh2Se4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07106)
*M. Emi,M. Shiomi,K. Kojima,K. Sugimoto,T. Karasawa,H. Suzuki,M. Takahashi,K. Oka,H. Kadobayashi,S. Kawaguchi-Imada,N. Hirao,T. Ohashi,D. Ito,T. Kubo,M. Matsushita,M. Nohara,K. Matsubayashi,N. Katayama*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper investigates the pressure evolution of three spinel chalcogenides (CuIr2Se4, CuRh2S4, CuRh2Se4) using synchrotron X-ray diffraction and resistivity measurements, revealing pressure-induced monoclinic supercells with bond-disproportionated structural ordering, a close coupling between structural transformation and insulating-like transport, and previously unreported ambient-pressure bulk superconductivity in CuIr2Se4 at 0.29 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay among metallic, superconducting, and pressure-induced insulating states in spinel chalcogenides, and to explore pressure ranges beyond those previously studied to establish structural-electronic relationships.

Method: Combined synchrotron powder X-ray diffraction and electrical-resistivity measurements across extended pressure ranges, with constrained profile fits guided by density functional theory (DFT) relaxed structural models to identify monoclinic supercell ordering patterns.

Result: All three compounds develop closely related monoclinic supercells under pressure. CuIr2Se4 and CuRh2S4 adopt Phase-IV-type bond-disproportionated structures, while CuRh2Se4 shows a compatible monoclinic cell without resolved atomic ordering. Insulating-like transport emerges abruptly in CuIr2Se4 but gradually in the Rh compounds, tracking their structural transformations. Bulk superconductivity in CuIr2Se4 at ambient pressure was discovered with zero resistance at 0.29 K and nearly complete ac diamagnetic shielding.

Conclusion: The formation of high-pressure monoclinic phases is closely related to the evolution toward insulating transport, and transition-metal/chalcogen substitution tunes the characteristic pressure scales and superconductivity competition within a shared structural framework.

Abstract: Spinel chalcogenides provide a platform for investigating the interplay among metallic, superconducting, and pressure-induced insulating states. Here, we combine synchrotron powder X-ray diffraction and electrical-resistivity measurements to investigate the pressure evolution of CuIr2Se4, CuRh2S4, and CuRh2Se4 over pressure ranges extending beyond those previously explored. High-pressure diffraction reveals closely related monoclinic supercells in all three compounds. For CuIr2Se4 and CuRh2S4, constrained profile fits based on structural models relaxed using density functional theory are compatible with Phase-IV-type bond-disproportionated structures, whereas the data for CuRh2Se4 establish a compatible monoclinic unit cell without resolving its atomic-scale ordering pattern. Insulating-like transport develops abruptly over a narrow pressure range in CuIr2Se4 but more gradually over broader pressure ranges in the Rh-based compounds, in close correspondence with their respective structural transformations. We also establish previously unreported bulk superconductivity in CuIr2Se4 at ambient pressure: zero resistance is attained at 0.29 K, and the accompanying ac diamagnetic response is consistent with nearly complete superconducting shielding. These results establish a close relationship between the formation of the high-pressure monoclinic phases and the evolution toward insulating transport, and demonstrate that transition-metal and chalcogen substitutions tune the characteristic pressure scales and the competition with superconductivity within a closely related structural framework.

</details>


### [76] [Collective Excitonic Structure Governs Anomalously Weak Thermal Optical Dephasing in Conjugated Polymers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06742)
*Henry J. Kantrow,Elizabeth Gutiérrez-Meza,Eric R. Bittner,Hao Li,Carlos Silva-Acuña*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses coherence-detected and population-detected two-dimensional electronic spectroscopies to measure temperature-dependent homogeneous optical linewidths across a series of semiconducting/conjugated polymers. Despite diverse molecular architectures and solid-state organizations, all polymers exhibit remarkably weak thermal linewidth scaling. However, absolute homogeneous linewidths depend on the detection modality, reflecting how coherence- and population-detected measurements project population relaxation and pure dephasing differently onto the spectroscopic response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conjugated polymer aggregates experience optical decoherence under strong vibronic coupling and have diverse chemical structures, solid-state organizations, and excitonic characters. The authors aim to understand how temperature affects homogeneous optical linewidths across these structurally diverse systems and whether the measured decoherence is universal or observable-dependent.

Method: Two complementary 2D electronic spectroscopic techniques are employed: coherence-detected and population-detected spectroscopies. Temperature-dependent measurements of homogeneous optical linewidths are acquired across a series of semiconducting polymers differing in molecular architecture and solid-state organization, allowing systematic comparison of thermal linewidth scaling and absolute linewidth values between detection modalities.

Result: All semiconducting polymers studied exhibited remarkably weak thermal linewidth scaling over the measured temperature range, despite substantial differences in molecular architecture and solid-state organization. While this weak temperature dependence is robust across the polymer series, the absolute homogeneous linewidth depends on the detection observable—coherence- and population-detected measurements yield different absolute linewidth values.

Conclusion: The results establish that weak thermal scaling of optical decoherence is a general feature across diverse conjugated polymers. Moreover, the experimental manifestation of this decoherence must be interpreted in the context of the detection observable, since coherence- and population-detected spectroscopies project population relaxation and pure dephasing onto the spectroscopic response in fundamentally different ways.

Abstract: Conjugated polymer aggregates exhibit optical decoherence in the presence of strong vibronic coupling and substantial diversity in chemical structure, solid-state organization, and excitonic character. Here, we use coherence-detected and population-detected two-dimensional electronic spectroscopies to examine the temperature dependence of the homogeneous optical linewidth across a series of semiconducting polymers. Despite substantial differences in molecular architecture and solid-state organization, all polymers studied exhibit remarkably weak thermal linewidth scaling over the measured temperature range. Comparison between complementary detection modalities further shows that, while this weak temperature dependence is obust, the absolute homogeneous linewidth depends on the measured observable. This behavior reflects the different ways in which coherence- and population-detected measurements project population relaxation and pure dephasing onto the spectroscopic response. These results establish the weak thermal scaling of optical decoherence across a diverse series of conjugated polymers and show that its experimental manifestation must be interpreted in the context of the detection observable.

</details>


### [77] [A Novel Nb-Based Eutectic Superalloy with Exceptional Ultrahigh-Temperature Mechanical Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06848)
*Ayeman M. Nahin,Alfredo Navarrete,Xiaokun Yang,Eric A. Lass,Mingwei Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A new castable Nb-based eutectic superalloy (NMC-1: Nb-10Mo-9.5C) is reported that achieves high strength at 1300–1500 °C, thermal stability, low density, low cost, and room-temperature ductility—a combination previously unmet by refractory alloys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing refractory alloys cannot simultaneously achieve high-temperature strength, thermal stability, low density, low cost, and room-temperature tensile ductility, which are all required for operation above 1300 °C beyond the limit of Ni-based superalloys.

Method: A castable Nb-based eutectic superalloy, Nb-10Mo-9.5C (at. %, NMC-1), was designed with a uniform lamellar microstructure comprising Nb-Mo solid solution and Nb2C carbide phases.

Result: NMC-1 achieves 0.2% yield strengths of ~300 MPa at 1300 °C and ~200 MPa at 1500 °C, among the highest for Nb-based alloys, with pronounced strain hardening, non-zero room-temperature tensile ductility, no microstructural coarsening after 100 h at 1400 °C, low density (8.65 g/cc), and low cost (~$85/kg).

Conclusion: The specific-strength-per-cost merit index of NMC-1 far surpasses commercial Nb, Mo, Ta, and W alloys, establishing a new design paradigm for ultrahigh-temperature structural materials.

Abstract: Refractory alloys operating above 1300 °C, beyond the limit of Ni-based superalloys, must balance high-temperature strength, thermal stability, low density, low cost, and room-temperature tensile ductility, a combination that existing refractory alloys have yet to achieve. Here we report a castable Nb-based eutectic superalloy, Nb-10Mo-9.5C (at. %, NMC-1), comprising a uniform lamellar structure of Nb-Mo solid solution and Nb2C carbide phases. NMC-1 achieves 0.2% yield strengths of ~300 MPa and ~200 MPa at 1300 °C and 1500 °C, respectively, among the highest reported for Nb-based alloys, with pronounced strain hardening, non-zero room-temperature tensile ductility, and no observed microstructural coarsening after 100 h at 1400 °C. Its low density (8.65 g/cc) and cost (~$85/kg) yield a specific-strength-per-cost merit index that far surpasses commercial Nb, Mo, Ta, and W alloys, establishing a new design paradigm for ultrahigh-temperature structural materials.

</details>


### [78] [Band Structure Modulation of ZrO2 Nanoparticles for Control of CO Adsorption Properties: A Combined Density Functional Theory - Density Functional Tight Binding Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08329)
*Kexin Chen,William Dawson,Aulia Sukma Hutama,Takahito Nakajima,Keisuke Kameda,Manabu Ihara,Sergei Manzhos*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A hybrid DFTB-Force Field framework with coordination-dependent Zr-C potentials is developed to study CO adsorption on experimentally relevant-sized ZrO2 nanoparticles. The study shows that termination stoichiometry engineers p-, n-, or intrinsic electronic structures, where Zr-rich (n-type) nanoparticles with low-coordinated sites activate the C-O bond via chemisorption and charge donation, while stoichiometric ones do not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the applicability of DFTB to complex oxide nanostructures of experimentally relevant sizes and understand how stoichiometry and nanostructuring govern CO adsorption and catalytic reactivity on zirconia nanoparticles.

Method: A combined DFT and DFTB study is conducted on ZrO2 nanoparticles. A hybrid DFTB-FF framework is developed: band structures rely on an existing Slater-Koster framework, but structural optimization and adsorption accuracy are improved by replacing traditional repulsive potentials with classical long-range interatomic potentials. Additionally, coordination-dependent Zr-C potentials are introduced to capture distinct local chemical environments (facet vs. tip/edge sites) for better CO adsorption description.

Result: The hybrid DFTB-FF approach substantially improves robustness of geometry optimization. Calculations reveal that termination stoichiometry can tune the intrinsic, p-type, or n-type electronic structure and adsorption activity. Stoichiometric nanoparticles do not activate the C-O bond, while low-coordinated sites in Zr-rich (n-type) nanoparticles exhibit chemisorption with charge donation into a CO antibonding LUMO-derived orbital, resulting in C-O bond activation.

Conclusion: Stoichiometry-controlled electronic structure and under-coordinated surface sites introduced by nanostructuring are key factors governing the adsorption strength and reactivity of ZrO2 nanoparticles. The hybrid DFTB-FF strategy offers a practical route for extending DFTB to complex oxide nanostructures.

Abstract: We present a combined density functional theory (DFT) and density functional tight binding (DFTB) study of zirconia (ZrO2) nanoparticles of experimentally relevant sizes of several nanometers and their interactions with the CO molecule. A hybrid DFTB - Force Field (DFTB-FF) framework is developed, whereby band structure calculations rely on an existing Slater-Koster framework, while the accuracy of structural optimization and adsorption properties is controlled by the introduction of classical long-range interatomic potentials into DFTB instead of the traditional repulsive potentials. Additionally, coordination-dependent Zr-C potentials are introduced to account for the distinct local chemical environments of bulk-like facet sites and under-coordinated tip and edge sites, thereby improving the description of CO adsorption. This hybrid DFTB-FF approach substantially improves the robustness of geometry optimization and provides a practical strategy for extending the applicability of DFTB to complex oxide nanostructures. The calculations reveal that termination stoichiometry can be used to engineer intrinsic, p-type, or n-type electronic structures and thereby tune the adsorption activity of zirconia nanoparticles. While stoichiometric nanoparticles do not activate the C-O bond, low-coordinated sites in Zr-rich (n-type) nanoparticles exhibit chemisorption accompanied by charge donation into a CO antibonding LUMO-derived orbital, resulting in C-O bond activation. These results demonstrate that stoichiometry-controlled electronic structure and under-coordinated surface sites introduced by nanostructuring play a key role in governing the adsorption strength and reactivity of zirconia nanoparticles.

</details>


### [79] [Magneto-spin-orbit graphene: Chern topology and photoinduced Hall response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06858)
*Artem V. Tarasov,Alexander V. Eryzhenkov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work uses first-principles calculations and effective four-band models to study four out-of-plane magnetized graphene/(Au, Pt)/(Co, Mn) heterostructures in both commensurate and moiré supercells. It finds that commensurate cells overestimate charge transfer and valley asymmetry, while moiré averaging strongly reduces these effects while preserving sizable spin splittings. Only Gr/Pt/Co exhibits an effective ferromagnetic Chern phase with C=2 across all supercells; the other three moiré models are ferrimagnetic and topologically trivial (C=0) but show helicity-dependent photoinduced Hall responses, which are enhanced by misfit-dislocation reconstruction in Gr/Au/Co.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magneto-spin-orbit graphene can host Chern phases, but realistic heterostructures depend on substrate chemistry, local stacking, and buried-interface reconstruction. Investigating these effects requires accounting for the long-range moiré patterns that often arise, which has been a significant challenge.

Method: First-principles calculations combined with effective four-band models for four out-of-plane magnetized Gr/(Au, Pt)/(Co, Mn) heterostructures, using commensurate (2x2) and moiré (9x9) supercells, where Co-based interfaces include misfit dislocations.

Result: Commensurate cells overestimate charge transfer and exaggerate valley asymmetry; moiré averaging reduces both while preserving sizable spin splittings. Pt mediates stronger proximity than Au, and Co-to-Mn substitution alters exchange interactions spacer-dependently. Gr/Pt/Co shows a ferromagnetic Chern phase (C=2) in all supercells, while the other three moiré compositions are ferrimagnetic and topologically trivial (C=0), yet exhibit helicity-dependent photoinduced Hall responses, strongly enhanced by misfit dislocations in Gr/Au/Co.

Conclusion: Moiré-scale stacking controls the effective Chern phase and Hall conductivity, while buried-interface defects tune the photoinduced Hall response without changing topology.

Abstract: Magneto-spin-orbit graphene can host Chern phases due to proximity interactions, but in realistic heterostructures these depend on substrate chemistry, local stacking registry and may be altered by buried-interface reconstruction. Investigation of these effects requires special efforts to take into account the long-range moire patterns that often arise for these heterostructures. We address this question using first-principles calculations and effective four-band models for four out-of-plane magnetized Gr/(Au, Pt)/(Co, Mn) heterostructures in commensurate (2 x 2) and moire (9 x 9) supercells where the latter include misfit dislocations for the Co-based interfaces. The commensurate cells overestimate charge transfer and exaggerate valley asymmetry, whereas moire averaging strongly reduces both effects while preserving sizable spin splittings. The Pt spacer mediates stronger proximity interactions than Au, while replacing Co by Mn substantially changes the balance of the effective exchange interactions in a spacer-dependent manner. Introduction of misfit dislocations into the Co-based structures is selective and tunes the Dirac gap in Gr/Au/Co or spin splittings in Gr/Pt/Co. Graphene in Gr/Pt/Co exhibits effective ferromagnetic Chern phase with C = 2 in all studied supercells. By contrast, the moire models of the other three compositions are ferrimagnetic and topologically trivial, with C = 0. Nevertheless, they exhibit helicity-dependent photoinduced Hall responses, which are strongly enhanced by misfit-dislocation reconstruction in Gr/Au/Co. This work demonstrates that moire-scale stacking controls the effective Chern phase and Hall conductivity, whereas buried-interface defects tune the photoinduced Hall response without changing the topology.

</details>


### [80] [Human-agent discovery of reconfigurable in-plane ferroelectric superdomain control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06887)
*Yu Liu,Boris Slautin,Ching-Che Lin,Jaegyu Kim,Lane W. Martin,Sergei V. Kalinin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces SPARC (Scanning Probe Agentic Research Cycle), a framework where a coding agent and human operator share a microscope, notebook, and two persistent memory files (FINDINGS.md and PITFALLS.md) to enable agentic automated experimentation under low-budget constraints. It is applied to reconfigure the in-plane superdomain direction of a (111)-oriented PbZr0.2Ti0.8O3 film.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated experimentation methods like Bayesian optimization require predefined observables, actions, and objectives. However, exploratory experiments often have variables that must be extracted from data, new operations that emerge mid-experiment, and too-small instrument budgets to learn by trial. A framework is needed to enable an agent to conduct exploratory experiments under these constraints.

Method: SPARC framework couples a coding agent with a human operator sharing one microscope and notebook, with two persistent memory files: FINDINGS.md storing graded conclusions, and PITFALLS.md recording learned failure modes. In an operator-supervised campaign, the agent reanalyzed manual measurements and devised an oriented lattice of stationary bias pulses with alternating polarity to reconfigure superdomain direction. In a subsequent agent-controlled campaign, PITFALLS.md entries were compiled into pre-write design validation checks.

Result: Experiments showed that spatial polarity alternation, rather than exact lattice-lamellar period matching, governed directional selection. The agent successfully printed the letters "UTK" into the superdomain orientation using a raster scan combined with a masked pulse lattice. The campaign also revealed practical requirements for agentic experimentation.

Conclusion: The work identifies key requirements for robust agentic experimentation: physical verification of instrument execution, explicit conditions under which stored findings remain valid, validation of new observables on instrument data, and robust control protocols. SPARC demonstrates a practical architecture for exploratory automated experimentation where objectives and operations emerge during the experiment.

Abstract: Automated experimentation is most effective when the observables, available actions, and objective are defined before the experiment starts, as is the case for Bayesian optimization. However, in many exploratory experiments, the variables that describe the sample must be extracted from the data, new operations emerge during the experiments, and the instrument budget is too small to learn the problem by trials. Here we introduce the Scanning Probe Agentic Research Cycle (SPARC) framework, in which a coding agent and a human operator share one microscope, one notebook, and two persistent memory files. FINDINGS.md stores graded conclusions about the experiment, whereas PITFALLS.md records learned failure modes of analysis and instrument. We apply SPARC to reconfigure the in-plane superdomain direction of a (111)-oriented PbZr0.2Ti0.8O3 film. In an operator-supervised campaign, the agent reanalyzed earlier manual measurements and developed an oriented lattice of stationary bias pulses with alternating polarity to reconfigure the superdomain direction. In a subsequent agent-controlled campaign, PITFALLS.md entries were compiled into checks that validate a design before any write. The experiments showed that spatial polarity alternation, instead of the exact matching between the lattice and lamellar periods, determines directional selection. Combining a raster scan with a masked pulse lattice printed the letters UTK into the superdomain orientation. The campaign also identified practical requirements for agentic experimentation where physical verification of instrument execution, the conditions under which stored findings remain valid, validation of new observables on instrument data, and robust control protocols.

</details>


### [81] [Scalable machine learning framework for multiphase identification from powder X-ray diffraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06908)
*Xinyang Tong,Ethan Jin,Jiahan Xu,Aditya Rao,Pengcen Jiang,Nathan J. Szymanski*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: GALAXI is a deep-learning framework for automated phase identification in XRD data that decouples the task into independent one-versus-all binary classifiers per phase, narrowing candidate phases before Rietveld refinement confirms the best combination. It achieves a micro-F1 score of 0.935 on experimental patterns, outperforming classical search-match and prior deep-learning methods, and scale to 64,594 structures via a modular web-accessible design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated XRD phase identification remains challenging, especially for multiphase samples with overlapping peaks and experimental artifacts. Most deep-learning approaches treat it as a single closed-set classification problem requiring one shared model to discriminate among all candidates, which limits scalability and robustness.

Method: GALAXI formulates phase identification as independent one-versus-all binary classification problems, where each pre-trained classifier specializes in recognizing a single phase. These classifiers narrow the search space to plausible phases, which are then evaluated via Rietveld refinement to identify the optimal phase combination explaining the full diffraction pattern.

Result: On curated experimental patterns, GALAXI achieves a micro-F1 score of 0.935, surpassing classical search-match and prior deep-learning models. It remains robust to experimental artifacts (low impurity fractions, small crystallite size, peak shifts, sample displacement, texture), performs well on time-resolved in-situ XRD from solid-state reactions, and scales to 64,594 COD structures via modular training.

Conclusion: The modular architecture of GALAXI enables scalable expansion to large reference libraries without retraining existing models, and a public web interface is deployed for practical use.

Abstract: X-ray diffraction (XRD) is the primary tool for identifying crystalline phases following synthesis, but automated phase identification remains challenging, particularly for multiphase samples with overlapping peaks and experimental artifacts. While deep-learning methods have been proposed to improve upon classical search-match algorithms, most formulate phase identification as a single closed-set classification problem, requiring one shared model to discriminate among all candidate phases. Here we introduce GALAXI, which instead decouples the identification task into independent one-versus-all binary classifiers that each specialize in recognizing a single phase. These pre-trained classifiers first narrow the search space to a small set of plausible phases, which are then evaluated through Rietveld refinement to identify the combination of phases that best explains the full diffraction pattern. On a curated set of experimental patterns, GALAXI identifies the correct phases with a micro-F1 score of 0.935, outperforming classical search-match and prior deep-learning models. The method remains robust to common experimental artifacts, including low impurity phase fractions, small crystallite size, peak shifts, sample displacement, and texture, and performs well when applied to time-resolved in-situ XRD data from solid-state reactions. Moreover, because the phase-specific models are independent, GALAXI can expand to large reference libraries without retraining existing models. This modular architecture enables us to train classifiers for 64,594 structures from the Crystallography Open Database and deploy them through a public web interface at https://galaxi-xrd.com.

</details>


### [82] [Restoring the Surface Magnetic Gap in MnBi$_2$Te$_4$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06915)
*Ce Bian,Hengxin Tan,Wenhui Duan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper identifies surface electrostatic potential modulation—caused by surface defects—as the origin of the elusive surface magnetic gap in MnBi2Te4. Defects lower the electrostatic potential, pushing topological surface states into subsurface layers where exchange interactions are weaker, closing the gap. External or interfacial electric fields restore the gap and enable topology control, validated in defective films with polar insulator capping, explaining enhanced quantum anomalous Hall effect under AlOx capping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Widespread experimental realization of quantized anomalous transport in MnBi2Te4 is hindered by its elusive surface magnetic gap, so uncovering the origin of the gapless states is essential to accessing its topological properties.

Method: The authors developed model calculations combined with validation in defective MnBi2Te4 films interfaced with polar insulators, demonstrating how surface defects lower the electrostatic potential, delocalize topological surface states into subsurface layers, and suppress exchange interactions that close the gap. They show that tuning surface electrostatic potential via external electric fields or interfacial fields in van der Waals heterostructures can restore the gap.

Result: The theory explains the enhanced quantum anomalous Hall effect observed in experiments with AlOx capping, and identifies electrostatically driven surface-state delocalization as a competitive origin of gap suppression in MnBi2Te4.

Conclusion: Surface electrostatic engineering via displacement fields is proposed as a practical strategy for robust quantized transport in intrinsic magnetic topological insulators.

Abstract: A widespread experimental realization of quantized anomalous transport in the intrinsic magnetic topological insulator MnBi$_2$Te$_4$ is hindered by its elusive surface magnetic gap. Uncovering the origin of the gapless states is essential for accessing its topological properties. Here we show that surface defects lower the electrostatic potential, drive topological surface states into subsurface layers, suppress exchange interactions, thereby closing the gap. Tuning the surface electrostatic potential via external electric fields or interfacial fields in van der Waals heterostructures restores the expected gap and enables control of its topology. This is confirmed by model calculations and validated in defective MnBi$_2$Te$_4$ films interfaced with polar insulators, explaining the enhanced quantum anomalous Hall effect under AlO$_x$ capping observed in recent experiments. Our theory identifies electrostatically driven surface-state delocalization as a competitive origin of gap suppression and proposes displacement-field engineering for robust quantized transport.

</details>


### [83] [Time-Dependent Density Functional Theory with Coulomb Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08983)
*Asbjørn Bækgaard Lauritsen,Mathieu Lewin,Jakob Oldenburg*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper provides the first fully rigorous proof of time-dependent density functional theory, showing that a given time-dependent density uniquely determines an external potential (up to a time-dependent constant) whose Schrödinger equation reproduces that density, proven via an iteration scheme that handles Coulomb interactions without time Taylor expansion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-dependent density functional theory (TDDFT) is widely used in physics and chemistry, but its mathematical foundations in the continuum setting had not been rigorously established. The authors aim to justify the Runge-Gross theorem—the theoretical cornerstone of TDDFT—under fully rigorous conditions.

Method: The proof establishes the existence and uniqueness of an external potential for a given time-dependent density, up to a time-dependent constant. The potential is constructed via an iteration scheme. The argument requires analyticity in space for both the density and potential (compatible with extended nuclei) and covers Coulomb interactions, avoiding any Taylor expansion in time.

Result: The authors prove that for any given time-dependent density, there exists an external potential, unique up to a time-dependent constant, such that the corresponding Schrödinger equation reproduces the given density. This is the first fully rigorous justification of TDDFT in the continuum.

Conclusion: TDDFT is rigorously justified in the continuum for Coulomb-interacting systems, with the theorem holding under spatial analyticity conditions rather than time-analyticity assumptions, making the result applicable to physically relevant settings including extended nuclei.

Abstract: We provide the first fully rigorous justification of time-dependent density functional theory in the continuum: Given a time-dependent density, we prove that there exists an external potential, unique up to a time-dependent constant, such that the corresponding Schrödinger equation reproduces the given density. This potential can be obtained with an iteration scheme. Our argument covers Coulomb interactions and does not need any Taylor expansion in time. It instead requires analyticity in space for the density and potential, which is compatible with extended nuclei.

</details>


### [84] [Metastable polar order and phase competition in Carpy-Galy LaTaO4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06943)
*Yongjin Shin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles analysis of Ca-doped LaTaO4 reveals a metastable polar monoclinic P21 phase that serves as a commensurate intermediate connecting the antipolar P21/c ground state and polar Cmc21 phase via an incommensurate regime. The P21 phase acts as a structural reference for the IC-o modulation, with mode-coupling to a Gamma_1+ relaxation stabilizing the P21 minimum along both transformation pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the structural phase-transition landscape of LaTaO4, specifically how the antipolar ground state (P21/c) and polar phase (Cmc21) are connected through an incommensurately modulated regime, and to identify symmetry-permissible pathway(s) between established polymorphs.

Method: First-principles calculations identify a metastable polar monoclinic P21 phase as a common subgroup of P21/c and Cmc21, providing a symmetry-connected route. Mode-resolved energy surfaces are computed for the symmetry-breaking pathways, examining coupling between primary order parameters and an isosymmetric Gamma_1+ relaxation. Lattice-metric and neutron-diffraction comparisons evaluate the displacement character relative to the experimental IC-o modulation, alongside composition-dependent energetics.

Result: P21 provides a symmetry-connected route between P21/c and Cmc21. Coupling between the primary order parameters and an isosymmetric Gamma_1+ relaxation stabilizes the P21 minimum along both pathways. The P21/c -> P21 transformation exhibits a finite energy barrier, while a restricted soft-mode model retains competing P21 and Cmc21 basins. The calculated commensurate P21 state shares an S_2+-dominated displacement character with the experimental IC-o modulation. Composition-dependent energetics suggest chemical tuning routes for polar-antipolar competition.

Conclusion: P21 serves as a valid commensurate structural reference for the IC-o phase in LaTaO4, providing a symmetry-connected intermediary between the antipolar and polar polymorphs. The results point toward compositional strategies for tuning the competing polar-antipolar instabilities.

Abstract: Carpy-Galy LaTaO4 undergoes a structural sequence linking the antipolar monoclinic P21/c ground state and the polar orthorhombic Cmc21 phase through an incommensurately modulated regime. First-principles calculations identify a metastable polar monoclinic P21 phase as a commensurate intermediate in the phase-transition landscape. As a common subgroup of P21/c and Cmc21, P21 provides a symmetry-connected route between the established polymorphs. Mode-resolved energy surfaces for these symmetry-breaking pathways show that coupling between the respective primary order parameters and an isosymmetric Gamma_1+ relaxation stabilizes the P21 minimum along both pathways. Consequently, the P21/c -> P21 transformation has a finite energy barrier, while a restricted soft-mode model retains competing P21 and Cmc21 basins over an illustrative range of harmonic stiffness. Lattice-metric and neutron-diffraction comparisons indicate a shared S_2+-dominated displacement character between the calculated commensurate state and the experimental IC-o modulation, supporting the use of P21 as a commensurate structural reference for IC-o. Finally, composition-dependent energetics suggest chemical routes for tuning polar-antipolar competition.

</details>


### [85] [Quantum hot carrier spectra in plasmonic catalysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06949)
*Yu Chen,Hanwen Jin,Fei Gao,Johannes Lischner,Shiwu Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates how hot carriers generated in plasmonic metal nanoparticles drive vibrational excitation and dissociation of oxygen molecules, revealing that as nanoparticle size decreases from classical to quantum regimes, quantized hot carrier distributions enhance vibrational coupling and dissociation efficiency. Vibrational excitation rates scale linearly with 1/D due to Landau damping, but become nonlinear at high light intensities from vibrational heating via multiple electron scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vibrational activation of admolecules on metal nanoparticles is a key elementary step in plasmonic catalysis, yet the underlying quantum dynamics driven by hot carriers is not fully understood—particularly how nanoparticle size affects energy transfer mechanisms at the quantum scale.

Method: The study employs an atomistic description of plasmonic hot carrier generation to investigate vibrational excitation and dissociation of oxygen on silver nanoparticles as a function of nanoparticle diameter D, ranging from classical to quantum-sized regimes.

Result: As D decreases into the quantum regime, quantized hot carrier distributions emerge with increased population in high-energy regions, delivering more efficient vibrational coupling and dissociation. The vibrational excitation rate exhibits linear 1/D scaling attributed to Landau damping, but transitions to nonlinear behavior at elevated light intensities due to vibrational heating from multiple electron scattering.

Conclusion: The discovery of quantized hot carrier distributions in plasmonic catalysis opens new avenues for selective control and nonthermal energy conversion, providing fundamental insights into size-dependent quantum effects in hot-carrier-driven surface chemistry.

Abstract: Vibrational activation of admolecules on metal nanoparticles is an elementary step in plasmonic catalysis, yet the underlying dynamics driven by hot carriers is not fully understood in the quantum regime. Using an atomistic description of plasmonic hot carrier generation, we investigate vibrational excitation and dissociation of oxygen on silver nanoparticles as a function of diameter D. As D reduces from the classical to quantum-sized regime, quantized distribution of hot carriers emerges with increasing population in the high-energy regions. These highly energetic hot carriers deliver more efficient vibrational coupling and dissociation. The rate of vibrational excitation shows a linear 1/D scaling, which results from Landau damping. It turns nonlinear at elevated light intensities due to vibrational heating generated by multiple electron scattering. The finding of quantized distribution of hot carrier in plasmonic catalysis opens new avenues for selective control and nonthermal energy conversion.

</details>


### [86] [Excitons probe intrinsic flat band Mottness in a van der Waals heterostructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07101)
*Xinyue Huang,Xintong Tan,Haowei Chen,Yingzhou Huang,Yushen Zhou,Yuchen Gao,Zhijie Ma,Chengxin Xiao,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Jianpeng Liu,Zuxin Chen,Youguo Shi,Wang Yao,Yu Ye*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors study monolayer WSe2 in contact with the Mott insulator Nb3Cl8 using gate-tunable optical spectroscopy and first-principles calculations, revealing exciton dressing by correlated flat-band electrons (Rydberg exciton → polaron branches), a correlation-reconstructed Mott gap, and magnetically induced valley-selective exciton coupling, extending exciton sensing to flat-band Mott systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Excitons are sensitive optical probes of electronic correlations in nearby 2D materials, but their coupling to intrinsic flat-band Mott systems had remained largely unexplored. This gap motivates investigating how excitons interact with correlated Mott physics.

Method: Combination of gate-tunable optical spectroscopy and first-principles calculations on a heterostructure of monolayer WSe2 directly contacting the van der Waals Mott insulator Nb3Cl8. The gate evolution of WSe2 excitonic resonances, Rydberg exciton behavior in the electron-doped regime, and magneto-optical response under out-of-plane fields are analyzed.

Result: Gate evolution reveals signatures of a correlation-reconstructed Mott gap absent in the single-particle picture. In the electron-doped regime, the WSe2 2s Rydberg exciton undergoes multistage evolution into interlayer attractive and repulsive polaron branches, showing dressing by correlated flat-band electrons. Under out-of-plane magnetic fields, spin-polarized Nb3Cl8 states induce valley-selective exciton coupling with strongly enhanced circular polarization of exciton emission.

Conclusion: The work extends exciton-based sensing and exciton-polaron physics to intrinsic flat-band Mott materials, offering an optical route to probe and engineer correlation-driven interfacial quasiparticles.

Abstract: Excitons provide a sensitive optical probe of electronic correlations in nearby two-dimensional materials, yet their coupling to intrinsic flat-band Mott systems remains largely unexplored. Here we combine gate-tunable optical spectroscopy with first-principles calculations to study monolayer WSe$_2$ in direct contact with the van der Waals Mott insulator Nb$_3$Cl$_8$. The gate evolution of WSe$_2$ excitonic resonances reveals signatures of a correlation-reconstructed Mott gap in Nb$_3$Cl$_8$ that is absent from the single-particle band picture. In the electron-doped regime, the WSe$_2$ 2s Rydberg exciton undergoes a multistage evolution and develops into interlayer attractive and repulsive polaron branches, showing that a Rydberg exciton can be dressed by strongly correlated flat-band electrons in an adjacent Mott layer. Under an out-of-plane magnetic field, spin-polarized Nb$_3$Cl$_8$ states further induce valley-selective exciton coupling, producing a strongly enhanced circular polarization of the WSe$_2$ exciton emission. These results extend exciton-based sensing and exciton-polaron physics to intrinsic flat-band Mott materials, providing an optical route to probe and engineer correlation-driven interfacial quasiparticles.

</details>


### [87] [Groove-shaped defects in as-grown (001)-oriented $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ epilayers prepared by halide vapor phase epitaxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07209)
*Yongzhao Yao,Daiki Katsube,Hirotaka Yamaguchi,Yukari Ishikawa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates groove-shaped defects (GSDs) in (001) β-Ga2O3 epilayers grown by halide vapor phase epitaxy, using X-ray topography and electron microscopy. GSDs extend millimeters along [010] with (-102) basal facets and (100) sidewalls, and their distribution correlates with wafer curvature. No one-to-one correspondence between GSDs and substrate dislocations was found. Planar defects (mostly on (1-21) planes) were observed only near terminal boundaries where faceted GSD sectors met the (001) growth region, and are interpreted as consequences of growth-sector coalescence rather than nucleation origins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GSDs degrade the surface flatness of as-grown β-Ga2O3 epilayers, necessitating costly chemical mechanical polishing before device fabrication. Understanding their formation mechanism is essential to suppress them during epitaxial growth and reduce processing costs and damage risk.

Method: The study combined synchrotron X-ray topography (including transmission topographs), electron microscopy (transmission electron microscopy), and careful spatial alignment of surface images with topographs to correlate wafer-scale GSD distribution with wafer curvature and to characterize their morphology and subsurface structure.

Result: GSDs extended several millimeters along [010], consisting predominantly of (-102) basal facets bounded by steep (100) sidewalls. Their wafer-scale distribution corresponded spatially with wafer curvature variations. No one-to-one correlation existed between GSDs and substrate defects/dislocations. Planar defects with α-fringe contrast (inclined translational planar defects, mainly on (1-21) planes) were localized only near terminal boundaries of GSD sectors, not in their middles.

Conclusion: GSD formation is promoted by local surface orientation variations related to wafer curvature and step supply, leading to three-dimensional faceted growth with persistent (-102)/(100) sectors. The localized planar defects are consequences of growth-sector coalescence, not the origins of GSD nucleation.

Abstract: Groove-shaped defects (GSDs) degrade the surface flatness of as-grown (001)-oriented $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ epilayers and necessitate chemical mechanical polishing before device fabrication, increasing processing costs and the risk of damage. We investigated the morphology, subsurface structure, and formation mechanism of GSDs in a homoepitaxial layer grown by halide vapor phase epitaxy using synchrotron X-ray topography and electron microscopy. The GSDs extended several millimeters along [010] and consisted predominantly of (-102) basal facets bounded by steep (100) sidewalls. Their wafer-scale distribution showed a spatial correspondence with variations in wafer curvature, suggesting that local surface orientation influences GSD formation. Careful alignment of surface images with transmission X-ray topographs revealed no one-to-one correspondence between GSDs and substrate defects, providing no evidence that substrate dislocations serve as their nucleation sites. Instead, transmission electron microscopy revealed planar defects localized near the terminal boundaries where the faceted GSD sectors met the surrounding (001) growth region; no such defects were observed in specimens extracted from the middle of GSDs. These defects exhibited α-fringe contrast characteristic of inclined translational planar defects, with the dominant segments assigned to the (1-21) plane. The observations suggest that variations in local surface orientation and step supply may promote three-dimensional faceted growth, producing persistent (-102)/(100) sectors. The localized planar defects are therefore interpreted as consequences of growth-sector coalescence rather than the origins of GSD nucleation. These findings provide insight into the roles of wafer curvature and surface step supply in GSD formation.

</details>


### [88] [Atomistic origin and strain control of the finite-temperature dielectric response in BaTiO3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07219)
*Ryotaro Sahashi,Po-Yen Chen,Teruyasu Mizoguchi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using electric-field-coupled molecular dynamics with a machine-learning force field, the authors show that BaTiO3's temperature-dependent permittivity arises from field-induced angular redistribution of local Ti-O off-centering, following a common orientational descriptor under both temperature and strain variations—providing a real-space counterpart to soft-mode behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dielectric response of BaTiO3 varies strongly with temperature, but its local atomic origin has remained unclear.

Method: Electric-field-coupled molecular dynamics simulations using a machine-learning force field.

Result: The permittivity is governed by the field-induced angular redistribution of local Ti-O off-centering (rather than its magnitude or mean polar angle). Temperature and biaxial strain alter the local structure differently, yet both follow a common relation with the same orientational descriptor.

Conclusion: The orientational redistribution of local Ti-O off-centering provides a real-space counterpart to soft-mode behavior, unifying the dielectric response under thermal and strain perturbations.

Abstract: The dielectric response of BaTiO3 (BTO) varies strongly with temperature, yet its local atomic origin remains unclear. Using electric-field-coupled molecular dynamics with a machine-learning force field, we show that the permittivity tracks the field-induced angular redistribution of local Ti-O off-centering rather than its magnitude or mean polar angle. Temperature and biaxial strain modify the local structure differently, but both responses follow a common relation with the same orientational descriptor, providing a real-space counterpart to soft-mode behavior.

</details>


### [89] [Metallic-Phase-Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ Enabled Interface Engineering for Self-Powered and High-Gain WS$_2$ Photodetectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07295)
*Wajid Ali,Ming Huang,Juan Li,Jianhua Huang,Liuli Yang,Sajid Ur Rehman,Chinmay K. Mohanty,Zahir Muhammad,Ziwei Li,Maciej R. Molas*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A self-powered WS₂/Fe₃GaTe₂ van der Waals heterostructure photodetector achieves high gain via interfacial carrier separation and metallic Fe₃GaTe₂ as an active contact, delivering zero-bias responsivity of 23.5 A/W and a -1 V responsivity up to 9.7×10³ A/W with a specific detectivity of 2.3×10¹³ Jones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-dimensional transition-metal dichalcogenides exhibit strong light-matter interaction but are limited by inefficient carrier separation and contact-related losses in photodetection, motivating the need for improved interfacial engineering.

Method: A WS₂/Fe₃GaTe₂ van der Waals heterostructure photodetector is constructed where metallic Fe₃GaTe₂ acts as an active interfacial contact. A built-in field arises from the work-function mismatch, interfacial charge redistribution, and asymmetric contact geometry, enabling self-powered operation.

Result: Under 450 nm light, zero-bias responsivity reaches 23.5 A/W with an apparent EQE of 6.4×10³%. At -1 V, photoresponse is observed at 450, 520, and 633 nm, with responsivity of 9.7×10³ A/W and specific detectivity of 2.3×10¹³ Jones at 100 Hz under 450 nm illumination.

Conclusion: Fe₃GaTe₂-enabled interface engineering represents an effective route for achieving self-powered, highly sensitive 2D photodetectors, with high photoresponse attributed to interfacial carrier separation, efficient extraction, and likely trap-assisted photogating in multilayer WS₂.

Abstract: Two-dimensional transition-metal dichalcogenides offer strong light-matter interaction but suffer from inefficient carrier separation and contact-related losses in photodetectors. Here, we demonstrate a high-gain WS$_2$/Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ van der Waals heterostructure photodetector, where metallic Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$ serves as an active interfacial contact. The work-function mismatch, together with interfacial charge redistribution and asymmetric contact geometry, contributes to a built-in field that supports self-powered photodetection at zero bias. Under 450 nm illumination, the device delivers a zero-bias responsivity of 23.5 A/W and an apparent external quantum efficiency of 6.4 x 10$^3$%. At -1 V biasing, the heterostructure exhibits photoresponse at 450, 520 and 633 nm, achieving a responsivity of 9.7 x 10$^3$ A/W and a noise derived specific detectivity of 2.3 x 10$^13$ Jones at 100 Hz under 450 nm illumination. The high photoresponse is attributed to interfacial carrier separation, efficient extraction, and a likely contribution from trap-assisted photogating in multilayer WS$_2$. These results establish Fe$_3$GaTe$_2$-enabled interface engineering as an effective route for self-powered, highly sensitive 2D photodetectors.

</details>


### [90] [Modular and Cost-effective Scanning Photocurrent Microscopy System for Sub-micron characterization of 2D optoelectronic devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07321)
*Nuria Jiménez-Arévalo,Dan Zheng,Yong Xie,Peng Cheng,Yi Liu,Rafael Luque Merino,Haiwen Yu,Tao Wang,Riccardo Frisenda,Andres Castellanos-Gomez,Qinghua Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors present a home-built scanning photocurrent microscopy (SPCM) platform created by retrofitting a conventional metallographic microscope with fiber-coupled lasers/LEDs, a motorized XY stage, a camera, and an electronic readout module. The system enables simultaneous photocurrent and reflection mapping with sub-micron resolution, validated on various 2D materials and substrates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial SPCM setups are costly, complex, and inflexible, limiting accessibility for routine optoelectronic characterization of 2D semiconducting devices. The authors aim to provide a low-cost, adaptable alternative.

Method: A conventional metallographic microscope is retrofitted with single-mode fiber-coupled lasers and multimode fiber-coupled high-power LEDs as light sources, a motorized XY stage, a digital camera, and an electronic readout module. Simultaneous photocurrent and reflection intensity maps are acquired, and correlated with optical micrographs and AFM topography.

Result: The system achieves sub-micron spatial resolution and high imaging fidelity, validated on devices made from different materials (InSe, MoS2, WSe2, Gr) on different substrates (Si/SiO2, compact disk).

Conclusion: This work demonstrates a reliable, accessible, and reproducible high-performance SPCM platform requiring minimal microscope modifications, easily implementable in most laboratories for microscale optoelectronic characterization of 2D devices.

Abstract: Scanning photocurrent microscopy (SPCM) is a powerful technique for probing local optoelectronic phenomena in 2D semiconducting devices. However, commercial setups remain costly, complex and often lack flexibility and adaptability. In this work, we present a home-built SPCM platform built around the retrofitting of a conventional metallographic microscope by coupling it with different light sources (single-mode fiber-coupled lasers and multimode fiber-coupled high-power LEDs), a motorized XY stage, a digital camera and an electronic readout module. This system enables simultaneous acquisition of photocurrent and reflection intensity maps, requiring minimal modifications of the microscope. We reached sub-micron spatial resolution and high imaging fidelity by correlating photocurrent maps with reflection maps, optical micrographs and AFM topography data on different devices fabricated with different materials (InSe, MoS2, WSe2, Gr), on different substrates (Si/SiO2, compact disk). This work provides a reliable, accessible and reproducible high-performance SPCM platform that can be easily implemented in most laboratories for microscale optoelectronic characterization of 2D devices.

</details>


### [91] [How to enhance the applicability of Murnaghan equation of state](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07400)
*V. V. Bannikov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper proposes an improved version of the Murnaghan isothermal equation of state for solids, which overcomes the original equation's limitation of failing to reliably reproduce both near-equilibrium and high-pressure regions simultaneously. The modified equation extends the range of reliable fitting for computational and experimental data, tested against ab initio U(V) data and experimental V-P data across a representative selection of solids, showing advantages over the ordinary Murnaghan equation and performance comparable to or better than Vinet, Birch-Murnaghan, and Mao equations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The widely used Murnaghan equation of state has a limited range of applicability; it cannot simultaneously reproduce data reliably in both the near-equilibrium region and high-pressure regimes. The authors aim to provide a simple but effective modification that extends the reliable fitting range to higher pressures.

Method: The paper reviews known isothermal equations of state for solids and their theoretical foundations, then introduces a modified form of the Murnaghan equation designed to improve high-pressure performance. The improved equation is tested by fitting: (1) U(V) data from ab initio computations, and (2) experimental V-P data for a representative selection of solids. Comparisons are made against the ordinary Murnaghan equation as well as other well-known equations (Vinet, Birch-Murnaghan, Mao). Various aspects of the fitting process and alternative formulations of the proposed equation are also discussed.

Result: The improved Murnaghan equation significantly extends the pressure range over which computational data can be reproduced reliably compared to the ordinary Murnaghan equation. For experimental data fitting, the proposed equation performs at least as well as other established equations (Vinet, Birch-Murnaghan, Mao) in the presented examples.

Conclusion: The proposed improved Murnaghan equation of state is a simple yet effective modification that substantially enhances the applicability of the original equation at high pressures while maintaining comparable accuracy in fitting experimental data to other modern equations of state.

Abstract: The paper presents the brief overview of known isothermal equations of state for solids and of theoretical background to construct them, focusing on widely used Murnaghan equation. Since the latter has a limited range of application, failing to reproduce reliably both near-equilibrium and high-pressure regions simultaneously, the paper proposes simple, but rather efficient way to improve it. The suggested modification favors its applicability at high pressures, allowing to expand considerably the region of reliable reproducing of experimental or computational data. The proposed improved equation has been tested in fitting of U(V) data, obtained within ab initio computations, as well as of experimental V-P data for a representative selection of solids. In comparison with ordinary Murnaghan equation, it has found significant advantages, extending the range, where the computational data may be reproduced trustworthy (from the equilibrium state to the pressures, appreciably higher, the ordinary equation could). As for the fitting of experimental data, the proposed improved Murnaghan equation reproduces them, in the presented examples, at least not worse than other well-known equations (Vinet, Birch-Murnaghan, Mao) do. Some aspects of fitting process, as well as some alternative approaches to obtain the proposed equation of state in various forms are also discussed.

</details>


### [92] [Quantifying thermal and driven magnon populations with femtosecond noise correlation spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07421)
*F. S. Herbst,M. A. Weiss,A. Leitenstorfer,M. Lammel,N. Beaulieu,J. Ben Youssef,R. Schlitz,S. T. B. Goennenwein*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that femtosecond noise correlation spectroscopy enables fully quantitative, time-domain measurement of both thermal and coherently excited magnon modes in bismuth-substituted yttrium iron garnet under free-running microwave driving. The experimental data is modeled to extract magnon numbers by simulating the magnon band structure, magneto-optical response, and optical spot size, establishing a new pathway for optical tomography of magnon modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise quantification of total magnon numbers (both coherent and incoherent/thermal excitations) is essential for fundamental spin-wave physics and next-generation magnonic devices, yet existing techniques cannot simultaneously provide temporal resolution for ultrafast processes while accessing incoherent magnon dynamics.

Method: The authors employ femtosecond noise correlation spectroscopy to measure both thermal and coherently excited magnon modes in bismuth-substituted yttrium iron garnet (Bi:YIG) driven by a free-running microwave. They extract magnon numbers by simulating the magnon band structure, the magneto-optical response function, and the optical spot size, connecting magnon mode calculations with experimentally accessible magnetic properties.

Result: The analysis fiducially reproduces the waveform and amplitude of the magneto-optical correlation signal across different experimental conditions, demonstrating a fully quantitative time-domain measurement of both thermal and coherently excited magnon modes.

Conclusion: Femtosecond noise correlation spectroscopy opens a new pathway towards optical tomography of magnon modes in nonlinear or non-equilibrium conditions, and can be readily extended to study ultrafast incoherent dynamics in other condensed matter systems.

Abstract: Precise knowledge of the total number of magnons, including both coherent and incoherent (e.g. thermal) excitations, is imperative for the advancement of fundamental spin-wave physics and the development of next-generation magnonic devices. In particular, quantifying magnons is key to understanding magnon transport phenomena, the nonlinear regime, or ultrafast magnetization dynamics. Typically, incoherent magnons are accessed by frequency-domain techniques, which lack the temporal resolution required for ultrafast processes, while ultrafast time-domain methods are generally sensitive only to the coherent dynamics. In this work, we demonstrate that femtosecond noise correlation spectroscopy enables a fully quantitative, time-domain measurement of both thermal and coherently excited magnon modes in bismuth-substituted yttrium iron garnet driven by a free-running microwave. We model the experimental data and extract the magnon number by simulating the magnon band structure of the sample, the magneto-optical response function, and the optical spot size used in the experiment. Our analysis establishes a connection between magnon mode calculations and experimentally accessible magnetic properties and fiducially reproduces the waveform and amplitude of the magneto-optical correlation signal for different experimental conditions. These results open a new pathway towards the optical tomography of magnon modes in non-linear or non-equilibrium conditions and can be readily extended to study ultrafast incoherent dynamics in other condensed matter systems.

</details>


### [93] [Polymorph Engineering of the Layered Rare-Earth Magnet GdAlGe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08601)
*Dmitry V. Averyanov,Ivan S. Sokolov,Alexander N. Taldenkov,Oleg E. Parfenov,Alexey N. Mihalyuk,Ivan A. Yakovlev,Igor A. Karateev,Oleg A. Kondratev,Andrey M. Tokmachev,Vyacheslav G. Storchak*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates the fabrication of epitaxial thin films of a layered polymorph of GdAlGe, formed by alternating triangular Gd and honeycomb AlGe lattices. This phase is stable up to 5 monolayers in ultrathin films, exhibits anisotropic ferromagnetism evidenced by magnetization, anomalous Hall effect, and negative magnetoresistance, and is naturally integrated with Ge semiconductor, serving as a blueprint for designing layered magnets via polymorph engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The number of available layered magnetic materials for spintronic applications and 2D magnet precursors is still low, motivating the use of phase engineering—specifically the interplay between phase stability and dimensionality—to discover and design new layered magnets.

Method: Phase/polymorph engineering is employed to synthesize epitaxial thin films of a layered GdAlGe polymorph, alternating triangular Gd and honeycomb AlGe sublattices. Structural quality is verified by electron diffraction, X-ray diffraction, and electron microscopy; magnetic and electronic properties are probed via magnetization measurements, electron transport (anomalous Hall effect and magnetoresistance).

Result: The layered GdAlGe polymorph is stable in ultrathin films up to 5 monolayers; thicker films show an unwanted secondary phase. The films are structurally high-quality and exhibit an anisotropic ferromagnetic state, corroborated by the anomalous Hall effect and negative magnetoresistance. The material is naturally compatible with Ge, a technological semiconductor.

Conclusion: The study establishes polymorph engineering as an effective route for designing layered magnetic materials and provides a blueprint that could be extended to other systems, facilitating integration with semiconductor platforms for spintronic applications.

Abstract: The recent advances in research on layered magnets have established them as invaluable materials for spintronic applications and precursors to 2D magnets. The problem is that the number of such materials available for experimentation is still low. To tackle the problem, one may employ phase engineering; in particular, the interplay between the phase stability and dimensionality provides great opportunities for design of layered magnets. Here, this interrelation is harnessed to produce epitaxial films of an overlooked layered polymorph of GdAlGe. The compound is formed by alternating triangular Gd and honeycomb AlGe lattices. It is stable in ultrathin films, up to 5 monolayers. In thicker films, an additional unwanted phase emerges. The structural quality of the ultrathin films is witnessed by electron and X-ray diffraction as well as electron microscopy. Magnetization measurements detect an anisotropic ferromagnetic state. The GdAlGe magnetism is corroborated by a study of electron transport revealing the anomalous Hall effect and negative magnetoresistance. The material is naturally integrated with Ge, a technological semiconductor, which should facilitate applications. The present study can serve as a blueprint for design of layered magnets via polymorph engineering.

</details>


### [94] [Benchmarking Universal Machine Learning Force Fields for Crystal Structure Prediction of High-Energy Molecular Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07477)
*Musiha Mahfuza Mukta,Osman Goni Ridwan,Romain Perriot,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper tests whether classical force-field (GAFF) pre-relaxation can improve the reliability of universal machine learning interatomic potentials (UMLIPs) when relaxing high-energy molecular crystals. Using three UMLIP models (MACE, MACE-OFF, UMA) on a large crystal database, they find that GAFF pre-relaxation systematically reduces relaxation failures with lower computational cost, and MACE-OFF provides the best compromise between potential energy surface smoothness, structural fidelity, and stress convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Universal machine learning interatomic potentials (UMLIPs) offer fast screening of molecular crystals via geometry relaxation and energy ranking, but their reliability across chemically diverse energetic materials is unclear—particularly whether they might over-sensitively break desired molecular connectivity during periodic crystal relaxation.

Method: Three UMLIP models (MACE, MACE-OFF, UMA) were combined with General Amber Force Field (GAFF) pre-relaxation and tested on a large database of high-energy molecular crystals. The hypothesis was that classical force-field pre-relaxation would provide a more suitable starting geometry for subsequent UMLIP relaxation.

Result: Direct MACE-OFF and UMA showed high relaxation success and closely preserved reference geometries but failed in rare cases. GAFF pre-relaxation systematically reduced the number of failed relaxations with lower computational cost. Comparative failure and robustness analyses revealed distinct trade-offs among models, with MACE-OFF achieving the best compromise between potential energy surface smoothness, structural fidelity, and stress convergence.

Conclusion: GAFF pre-relaxation improves UMLIP reliability for high-energy molecular crystal relaxation. MACE-OFF emerges as a good choice for automated structural optimization based on its balanced performance across key metrics.

Abstract: Recent developments of universal machine learning interatomic potentials (UMLIPs) offer a fast route for screening molecular crystals based on geometry relaxation and energy ranking, but their reliability across chemically diverse energetic materials remains elusive. In particular, it is unclear whether or not these UMLIPs are over-sensitive to break the desired molecular connectivity for relaxing the periodic crystals. Herein we tested the hypothesis that classical force-field pre-relaxation can provide a more suitable starting geometry for subsequent UMLIP relaxation on a large database of high energy molecular crystals. Three models (MACE, MACE-OFF and UMA) in conjunction with the General Amber Force Field (GAFF) were applied to test this hypothesis. Among them, direct MACE-OFF and UMA showed very high relaxation success and preserved the reference geometries most closely, but they still exhibit failures for some rare cases. Using GAFF pre-relaxation can systematically reduce the number of failed relaxations with lower computational costs. Our comparative failure and robustness analyses revealed distinct trade-offs among the evaluated models. Among them, MACE-OFF achieves a better compromise between potential energy surface smoothness, structural fidelity, and stress convergence, serving as a good choice to provide a reliable foundation for automated structural optimization.

</details>


### [95] [Switchable Altermagnetism in a Layered van der Waals Metal-Organic Framework Driven by Spin-Crossover](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07548)
*Diego López-Alcalá,Alberto M. Ruiz,Andrei Shumilin,José J. Baldoví*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates a novel mechanism to dynamically switch altermagnetism on and off in molecular materials using spin-crossover. Through DFT calculations on layered van der Waals MOFs MnX2(tdz)2 (X = Cl, Br), the authors show that hydrostatic pressure induces a high-spin to low-spin transition that changes the symmetry of the antiferromagnetic ground state, thereby suppressing the altermagnetic spin-splitting without directly altering the electronic structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnetism holds promise for spintronic applications, but effective and versatile switching mechanisms remain largely unexplored. The paper seeks to overcome this gap by proposing a programmable route to control altermagnetism in molecular materials.

Method: The authors use density functional theory (DFT) calculations to investigate the layered van der Waals metal-organic frameworks MnX2(tdz)2 (X = Cl, Br). They analyze the magnetic ground state, interlayer exchange interactions, momentum-space spin splitting, and the effects of hydrostatic pressure on the spin state and magnetic ordering.

Result: The materials exhibit an altermagnetic ground state stabilized by anisotropic interlayer exchange interactions, characterized by d-wave momentum-space spin splitting and spin-splitter transport. Under hydrostatic pressure, a high-spin to low-spin transition alters the Mn d-orbital occupation and magnetic exchange network, stabilizing a different antiferromagnetic state whose symmetry suppresses nonrelativistic spin splitting.

Conclusion: Spin-crossover provides an externally controllable means to switch altermagnetism by changing the symmetry of the magnetic ground state rather than the electronic structure directly. This establishes molecular spin-crossover altermagnets as a promising reconfigurable platform for spintronic devices.

Abstract: Dynamical control of altermagnetism is a key requirement for translating its unique spin-dependent functionalities into practical spintronic devices, yet effective switching mechanisms remain largely unexplored. Here, we open an unprecedented, versatile and programmable route based on spin-crossover to switch altermagnetism on demand in molecular materials. Using density functional calculations, we demonstrate an altermagnetic ground state in the layered van der Waals MOFs MnX$_2$(tdz)$_2$ (X = Cl, Br; tdz = thiadiazole), stabilized by anisotropic interlayer exchange interactions, which gives rise to a characteristic d-wave momentum-space spin splitting and an associated spin-splitter transport response. Our findings reveal that under hydrostatic pressure, a high-spin to low-spin transition reconfigures the Mn d-orbital occupation, thus modifying the magnetic exchange network, and stabilizing a different antiferromagnetic ground state whose symmetry suppresses the nonrelativistic spin splitting. Crucially, spin-crossover switches altermagnetism not by directly altering the electronic structure, but by changing the symmetry of the magnetic ground state. These results establish molecular spin-crossover altermagnets as a platform for externally reconfigurable spintronic devices.

</details>


### [96] [Exciton Coherence in CsPbBr3 Nanocrystals is Bounded by Phonon-Mediated Bright-Triplet Relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07739)
*Tara Šverko,Annette J. Jones,Chantalle J. Krajewska,Peter C. Sercel,Alexander L. Efros,Alexander E. Kaplan,Niamh L. Brown,Stefano Toso,Moungi G. Bawendi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reveals that acoustic phonon-mediated exciton fine structure relaxation (EFSR) in single CsPbBr3 perovskite nanocrystals at 4 K causes population leakage between bright exciton triplet states, reaching 0.64 ± 0.04 of the radiative rate and limiting state coherence to 0.81 times the transform limit. This leakage is intrinsic to the nanocrystal, and the authors propose fine structure and acoustic phonon engineering as routes to higher coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalable single-photon and entangled photon sources are critical for quantum photonic technologies. Colloidal lead halide perovskite nanocrystals are promising candidates, but their exciton coherent properties—particularly the mechanisms that limit coherence—are not fully understood, motivating this investigation.

Method: The authors performed spectroscopic measurements on single CsPbBr3 nanocrystals at 4 K to study exciton fine structure dynamics. They identified acoustic phonon-mediated EFSR as the pathway driving population leakage between the bright exciton triplet states and quantified its rate relative to the radiative lifetime.

Result: The EFSR leakage pathway reaches 0.64 ± 0.04 of the radiative rate, bounding state coherence to 0.81 times the transform limit at 4 K. The pathway is shown to be intrinsic to the nanocrystal rather than environmentally induced.

Conclusion: Fine structure and acoustic phonon engineering are identified as effective intrinsic routes to achieve higher coherence in perovskite quantum light sources, making this a tractable target for materials and device design.

Abstract: Scalable sources of indistinguishable single photons or entangled photon pairs are fundamental to many quantum photonic technologies. Colloidal lead halide perovskite nanocrystals are promising such sources, but their exciton coherent properties are not fully understood.
