We show that in single CsPbBr3 nanocrystals at 4 K, acoustic phonon-mediated exciton fine structure relaxation (EFSR) drives leakage of population between the bright exciton triplet states, competing with the radiative lifetime. The leakage pathway reaches 0.64 $\pm$ 0.04 of the radiative rate, bounding state coherence to 0.81 times the transform limit at 4 K. This pathway is intrinsic to the nanocrystal, not its environment, making it a tractable target for materials and device design. We identify fine structure and acoustic phonon engineering as effective intrinsic routes to higher coherence in perovskite quantum light sources.

</details>


### [97] [Temperature-Dependent nonlinear optics from first-principles: Second-Harmonic Generation in few-layers MoS$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07796)
*Anna Romani,Claudio Attaccalite,Myrta Grüning*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A first-principles real-time method for computing non-linear optical response of solids at finite temperature, incorporating electron-phonon effects via quasiparticle renormalization and lifetimes, applied to second-harmonic generation in monolayer and trilayer MoS₂.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a theoretical framework capturing finite-temperature effects in non-linear optical response of solids, which is largely unexplored and needed to explain experimental observations such as the temperature-dependent increase of SHG intensity in monolayer MoS₂.

Method: Real-time first-principles approach combining Density-Functional Perturbation Theory for electron-phonon matrix elements, Fan and Debye-Waller self-energy terms to compute quasiparticle renormalization and lifetimes (included as dephasing), with electron excitations treated at the independent-particle level.

Result: A non-trivial temperature dependence of SHG emerges from strongly crystal-momentum-dependent quasiparticle renormalization. Phonon-mode analysis shows acoustic modes dominate the momentum dependence in monolayer MoS₂, while shear modes do so in trilayer MoS₂. Results rationalize the experimentally observed increase of SHG intensity with temperature in monolayer MoS₂.

Conclusion: Temperature-dependent quasiparticle renormalization with crystal-momentum resolution is essential to capture finite-temperature non-linear optical responses, providing insight into experimental SHG trends in MoS₂ systems.

Abstract: We present a first-principles real-time approach to study non-linear response of solids at finite temperature. Finite temperature effects are included as renormalization of the quasiparticle energies and a dephasing term proportional to the quasiparticle lifetimes. We evaluate electron-phonon matrix elements from Density-Functional Perturbation Theory for lattice dynamics and then calculate quasiparticles renormalization and lifetime from the Fan and Debye-Waller terms of the electron self-energy. Electron excitations are treated at the independent particle level of approximation. We apply the approach to the second-harmonic generation (SHG) in monolayer and trilayer MoS$_2$. We observe a nontrivial temperature-dependence of the SHG due to a strong crystal-momentum dependent quasiparticle renormalization. From the phonon-mode analysis we find that the coupling with acoustic and shear modes determines the overall crystal-momentum dependence respectively in monolayer and trilayer MoS$_2$. The nontrivial temperature-dependence of the SHG can help rationalize the increase of SHG intensity with increasing temperature observed in monolayer MoS$_2$ at a given laser energy [Adv. Optical Mater. 8, 2000441 (2020)].

</details>


### [98] [Atomistic modeling of molecular beam epitaxy growth of SrTiO3 and Sr2TiO4 thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08114)
*Guangfu Luo,Dane Morgan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations reveal three unexpected atomistic mechanisms during MBE growth of perovskite SrTiO3 and Ruddlesden-Popper Sr2TiO4 films on SrTiO3 substrates: dynamically induced oxygen vacancies accelerate diffusion on defect-free surfaces, TiSr defects promote SrO island formation, and insertion of Ti species into SrO bilayers causes unexpected growth sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MBE promises atomic layer control, but unexpected growth mechanisms can compromise precision. Understanding the fundamental atomistic processes governing oxide film growth is essential for achieving true layer-by-layer control.

Method: First-principles (DFT-based) calculations are employed to investigate gas-phase molecular species, surface reactions, and diffusion dynamics on film surfaces and layer edges for perovskite SrTiO3 and Ruddlesden-Popper Sr2TiO4 growth on SrTiO3 substrates.

Result: Three previously unrecognized mechanisms were uncovered: (1) dynamically induced oxygen vacancies during surface diffusion on defect-free substrates significantly accelerate diffusion; (2) TiSr defects can promote SrO island formation, deviating from the expected single-layer growth mode; (3) adsorbed Ti atoms and TiO2 molecules can insert into SrO bilayers, producing an unexpected growth sequence.

Conclusion: These findings provide atomistic insight into MBE growth of metal oxide films and offer guidance for improved control over oxide thin-film growth processes under MBE conditions.

Abstract: Molecular beam epitaxy (MBE) is renowned for its potential for atomic layer control, but unexpected growth mechanisms can potentially compromise this level of precision. In this study, we employ first-principles calculations to investigate the atomistic processes governing the MBE growth of perovskite SrTiO3 and Ruddlesden-Popper Sr2TiO4 films on a SrTiO3 substrate. We systematically explore the potential molecular species in the gas phase and their reactions and diffusion dynamics on the film surfaces and layer edges. Our analyses uncover three mechanisms of importance for understanding this type of growth. First, oxygen vacancies can be dynamically induced during surface diffusion on defect-free substrate and noticeably accelerate the diffusion processes. Second, while the SrO layer is expected to grow in a single-layer growth mode, the presence of potential TiSr defects may promote the formation of SrO islands. Lastly, adsorbed Ti atoms and TiO2 molecules on the SrO bilayer can insert into SrO bilayers, resulting in an unexpected growth sequence. These findings may have broader implications for the MBE growth of metal oxide films and provide guidance for achieving improved control over their growth processes.

</details>


### [99] [Pressure Evolution of Atomic Volume Systematics in Transition Metals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08197)
*Masaaki Geshi,Yuichi Akahama*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DFT calculations up to 400 GPa reveal that the ambient-pressure parabolic dependence of atomic volume on atomic number in transition metals evolves into a cubic-like behavior at high pressures, attributed to higher compressibility of bcc transition metals with large increases in total energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the well-established parabolic relationship between atomic volume and atomic number in transition metals transforms under extreme compression, since prior knowledge has been largely based on ambient-pressure behavior.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) calculations applied to transition metals at pressures up to 400 GPa.

Result: The ambient-pressure parabolic volume-versus-atomic-number trend becomes cubic-like under high pressure. This is traced to the higher compressibility of bcc transition metals, which is associated with comparatively large increases in total energy under compression.

Conclusion: The high-pressure evolution of the periodicity in atomic volume of transition metals is determined by crystal-structure-dependent compressibility effects; findings are discussed against prior experimental and theoretical data.

Abstract: We investigated the evolution of the well-known parabolic dependence of atomic volume on atomic number in transition metals under extreme compression at pressures up to 400 GPa using density functional theory calculations. Our results reveal that the ambient-pressure parabolic trend transforms into a characteristic cubic-like behavior at high pressures. This evolution is attributed to the higher compressibility of bcc transition metals associated with comparatively large increases in the total energy. The present findings are discussed in relation to previous experimental observations and first-principles calculations.

</details>


### [100] [Light-facilitated ferroelectric switching in wurtzite crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08270)
*Ricardo Jiménez-Sánchez,Fernando Salazar,Miguel Cruz-Irisson,Riccardo Rurali,Claudio Cazorla*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper proposes a field-free, light-based strategy to lower the coercive field in wurtzite ferroelectrics via above-bandgap photoexcitation, using LaN as a representative case, potentially enabling faster and more energy-efficient non-volatile memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wurtzite ferroelectrics are promising for non-volatile memory due to large remanent polarization and CMOS compatibility, but their deployment is hindered by an intrinsically large coercive field arising from the high energy barrier between the polar wurtzite phase and the nonpolar hexagonal phase that mediates polarization switching.

Method: First-principles calculations are used to demonstrate that above-bandgap electronic photoexcitation introduces light-induced carriers that dramatically reduce the energy difference between the hexagonal intermediate and wurtzite ground states, lowering the ferroelectric switching barrier. The mechanisms is validated for LaN (wurtzite) and also confirmed for the rocksalt polymorph.

Result: Light-induced carriers sharply reduce the energy barrier to ferroelectric switching in LaN wurtzite ferroelectric. This arises from photoinduced partial metallization of the polar phase, which screens dipole-dipole interactions stabilizing ferroelectric order and favors the competing nonpolar structure. The mechanism is robust, also confirmed for the rocksalt polymorph.

Conclusion: Light is established as a powerful, non-invasive route to controlling ferroelectric switching in wurtzites, opening a path toward faster, lower-voltage, and more energy-efficient non-volatile memory technologies.

Abstract: Wurtzite ferroelectrics combine large remanent polarization with full CMOS compatibility, positioning them as a leading platform for next-generation non-volatile memory. Their practical deployment, however, is hindered by an intrinsically large coercive field, rooted in the high energy barrier separating the polar wurtzite phase from the nonpolar hexagonal phase that mediates polarization switching. Here, using first-principles calculations, we propose an alternative, field-free strategy for lowering this barrier: above-bandgap electronic photoexcitation. Taking LaN as a representative wurtzite ferroelectric, we show that light induced carriers dramatically reduce the energy difference between the hexagonal intermediate phase and the wurtzite ground state, sharply reducing the energy barrier to ferroelectric switching. This effect originates from a photoinduced partial metallization of the polar phase, which screens the dipole-dipole interactions that stabilize ferroelectric order and thereby favors the competing nonpolar structure. The robustness of this mechanism is further confirmed for the rocksalt polymorph. Our results establish light as a powerful, non-invasive route to controlling ferroelectric switching in wurtzites, opening a path toward faster, lower-voltage, and more energy-efficient non-volatile memory technologies.

</details>


### [101] [Flexoelectric Polarization in Wrinkled Janus Transition-Metal Dichalcogenide Monolayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08295)
*Stefan Velja,Surender Kumar,Domenico Corona,Caterina Cocchi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses DFT combined with continuum elastica modeling to systematically investigate the electronic and flexoelectric response of wrinkled Janus MoSSe/MoSeS monolayers, revealing that global out-of-plane polarization scales linearly with projected aspect ratio. A geometric model with an effective 2D flexoelectric coefficient accurately predicts DFT results. Atom- and orbital-resolved analyses show Mo 4d orbitals act as a linear flexoelectric core while curvature drives asymmetric S 3p/Se 4p charge transfer, establishing geometric design rules for flexoelectric nanoelectronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish strain-gradient engineering via out-of-plane wrinkling as a controllable route to tune electronic and electromechanical properties in 2D materials, specifically investigating how wrinkle geometry can be leveraged to control flexoelectric responses in Janus monolayers without requiring complex nonlinear models.

Method: The authors couple density functional theory (DFT) calculations with continuum elastica modeling. They systematically vary wrinkle sizes and compressive strain levels (5%-20%), analyzing global out-of-plane polarization as a function of projected aspect ratio. They develop a geometric model incorporating an effective 2D flexoelectric coefficient, and perform atom- and orbital-resolved charge density analyses to uncover microscopic mechanisms.

Result: The global out-of-plane polarization exhibits linear behavior when parameterized by the projected aspect ratio of nanowrinkles. The proposed geometric model accurately predicts DFT polarizations without higher-order nonlinear parameters. Charge analysis reveals that central Mo 4d orbitals form a robust linear flexoelectric core, while local curvature drives continuous, chemically asymmetric charge transfer between S 3p and Se 4p sublayer manifolds.

Conclusion: The findings establish clear geometric design rules for harnessing Janus-based flexoelectricity, enabling rational design of wrinkled MoSSe/MoSeS monolayers for flexible nanoelectronics and optoelectronics applications with predictable polarization behavior.

Abstract: Strain-gradient engineering via out-of-plane wrinkling offers a powerful route to tune electronic and electromechanical properties in two-dimensional (2D) materials. Here, we systematically investigate the electronic and flexoelectric response of wrinkled Janus MoSSe/MoSeS monolayers using density functional theory (DFT) calculations coupled with continuum elastica modeling. Exploring varying wrinkle sizes and compressive strain levels ($5\%-20\%$), we show that the global out-of-plane polarization follows a linear behavior when parameterized by the projected aspect ratio of the nanowrinkles. On this basis, we develop a physically grounded geometric model incorporating an effective 2D flexoelectric coefficient, which accurately predicts DFT polarizations without requiring higher-order nonlinear parameters. Atom- and orbital-resolved charge density analyses reveal the microscopic origin of this behavior: while the central Mo $4d$-orbital manifold acts as a robust, linear flexoelectric core, local curvature drives continuous, chemically asymmetric charge transfer between the S $3p$ and Se $4p$ sublayer manifolds. Our findings establish clear geometric design rules for harnessing Janus-based flexoelectricity for flexible nanoelectronics and optoelectronics.

</details>


### [102] [EBSDmagus: Managing Multi-Stage Dynamical Electron Backscatter Diffraction Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08334)
*Ulrich Kerzel,Lukas Berners,Sanddra Korte-Kerzel*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: EBSDmagus is a software tool that manages and automates dynamical EBSD simulations using EMsoft, reusing compatible intermediate results, checking outputs, resuming after interruptions, and providing provenance tracking for FAIR data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large dynamical EBSD simulation studies that combine composition, crystal structure, voltage, temperature, and many orientations produce many calculations across separate EMsoft configuration, structure, scattering, and orientation files. As variations multiply, manual preparation becomes error-prone, interruptions obscure usable results, and reconstructing the origin of individual patterns is laborious.

Method: EBSDmagus lets researchers define fixed and varying parameters once, automatically prepares required EMsoft calculations, reuses compatible intermediate results, verifies generated files before execution, checks expected outputs after execution, and resumes only unresolved calculations after interruptions. It maintains a portable run record linking each output to its inputs and calculation history for provenance.

Result: The approach was assessed using a completed representative calculation, a larger study prepared and checked before submission, two observed cluster interruptions, and a controlled job cancellation. During recovery, EBSDmagus retained completed calculations and resubmitted only incomplete work.

Conclusion: EBSDmagus effectively manages large-scale EMsoft-based EBSD simulation workflows, improving reliability, reproducibility, and provenance for FAIR data deposition.

Abstract: Dynamical electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) simulations increasingly support systematic studies of how material and experimental parameters affect diffraction patterns. Investigations that combine composition and crystal structure, including changes in atomic order, with accelerating voltage, temperature, and many orientations or detector conditions can require large sets of related calculations. Several packages provide dynamical EBSD simulation; we use the open-source, scriptable EMsoft programs because they are well suited to large studies on high-performance computing systems. An EMsoft investigation nevertheless spans separate configuration, structure, scattering, and orientation files and several dependent calculation stages. As variations multiply, manual preparation becomes difficult to check, interruptions obscure which results remain usable, and the origin of individual patterns becomes laborious to reconstruct.
  With EBSDmagus, researchers define fixed and varying parameters once. The software prepares the required EMsoft calculations, reuses compatible intermediate results, and checks that the generated files represent the requested investigation before execution. After execution, it checks the expected outputs and, following an interruption, resumes only unresolved calculations while preserving completed work. A portable run record links each output to its inputs and calculation history, providing the provenance needed when the results are deposited as findable, accessible, interoperable, and reusable (FAIR) data.
  We assess this approach using a completed representative calculation, a larger study prepared and checked before submission, two observed cluster interruptions, and a controlled job cancellation. During recovery, EBSDmagus retained completed calculations and resubmitted only incomplete work.

</details>


### [103] [Davydov Splitting Without a Davydov Pair and Highly Mobile Singlet Excitons in Perylene Red Microcrystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08352)
*Chris Rehhagen,Tolibjon Abdurakhmonov,Magnus Frank,Oliver Kühn,Stefan Lochbrunner*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates excitonic properties of perylene red microcrystals (8 molecules per unit cell) through combined experimental and theoretical approaches, finding that only two of eight nominal Davydov transitions are observed, which do not form a Davydov pair. A symmetry-based analysis combined with Frenkel-Holstein exciton modeling reveals a J-like band favoring exciton transfer, and experimental dynamics (TCSPC and transient absorption) combined with Kinetic Monte Carlo simulations support incoherent hopping transport of highly mobile singlet Frenkel excitons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the excitonic structure and transport dynamics in perylene red microcrystals, specifically addressing how the complex multi-molecule unit cell affects observable Davydov transitions and exciton mobility.

Method: Combines experimental optical spectroscopy (time-correlated single-photon counting, ultrafast transient absorption) with theoretical modeling (Frenkel-Holstein exciton model parametrized from density functional theory, symmetry-based eigenstate analysis, Kinetic Monte Carlo simulations).

Result: Only two of the nominal eight Davydov transitions are experimentally observed with 610 cm⁻¹ apparent splitting reproduced by theory. The two transitions do not belong to the same Davydov pair, so the splitting is not a direct measure of Coulomb coupling. The mixed local excitations form a J-like band for dominant transitions, and experimental exciton mobility matches Kinetic Monte Carlo simulations supporting incoherent hopping transport.

Conclusion: The complex excitonic landscape in multi-molecule organic crystals can be misleading if interpreted via simple Davydov pair analysis; J-like band structure and incoherent hopping transport dominate exciton dynamics in perylene red microcrystals.

Abstract: We investigate the excitonic properties of perylene red microcrystals, whose unit cell contain eight molecules, using both experimental and theoretical methods. Only two of the nominal eight Davydov transitions are experimentally observed, with an apparent splitting of 610 cm$^{-1}$ that is reasonably reproduced by a Frenkel-Holstein exciton model parametrized from density functional theory. A symmetry-based analysis of the eigenstates reveals that the two observed transitions do not belong to the same Davydov pair, so the splitting does not directly report on the Coulomb coupling within the unit cell. The complex mixture of local excitations produces a J-like band for the dominant transitions, favouring exciton transfer through increased spectral overlap. Consistent with this picture, time-correlated single-photon counting of the emission and ultrafast transient absorption spectroscopy show that the dynamics is dominated by highly mobile singlet Frenkel excitons. The exciton mobility extracted from experiment agrees very well with that obtained from Kinetic Monte Carlo simulations, supporting a picture of incoherent hopping transport.

</details>


### [104] [A thermally grown SiO2 diffusion barrier enabling high-temperature investigation of Ag-Au-Pd-Pt thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08363)
*Elaheh Akbarnejad,Aleksander Kostka,Advika Chesetti,Alan Savan,Georg Fritz,Kamen Kozhuharov,Matthias Karl Klein,Yujiao Li,Alfred Ludwig*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study evaluates thermally grown SiO2 as a diffusion barrier for Si-based combinatorial processing platforms (CPPs) used in atom probe tomography of complex solid solutions, demonstrating that a 25 nm thermally grown SiO2 barrier suppresses substrate reactions up to 600°C, whereas native Si oxide fails above 300°C due to silicide formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CPPs integrating Si microtip arrays with combinatorial thin film synthesis and APT enable near-atomic-scale characterization of compositionally complex solid solutions (CCSSs), but their use at elevated temperatures is limited because CCSS constituents like Pd and Pt react with the Si support to form silicides. While thermally grown SiO2 works as a diffusion barrier for pure Pt/Si, its performance for multicomponent CCSS thin films was unclear, motivating this study.

Method: Using Ag-Au-Pd-Pt as a model CCSS system, the authors compared a 25 nm thermally grown SiO2 barrier with native Si oxide during annealing using atom probe tomography (APT) and transmission electron microscopy (TEM).

Result: Native Si oxide prevented interfacial reactions up to 300°C, but at 400°C Pd and Pt reacted with Si causing silicide formation and film constituent redistribution. At 600°C, extensive substrate reactions disrupted the film and produced needle-shaped silicide morphologies. In contrast, thermally grown SiO2 suppressed CCSS thin film-substrate reactions up to 600°C and retained the CCSS composition.

Conclusion: Thermally grown SiO2 extends the applicable temperature range of Si-based CPPs to at least 600°C for near-atomic-scale characterization of CCSS thin films, overcoming the silicide formation limitation of native oxide barriers.

Abstract: Combinatorial processing platforms (CPPs), integrating Si microtip arrays with combinatorial thin film synthesis and atom probe tomography (APT), enable near-atomic-scale characterization of compositionally complex solid solutions (CCSSs) under diverse processing and reaction conditions, including oxidation, thermal phase stability and electrocatalytic reactions. Their application at elevated temperatures, however, can be limited when CCSS constituents such as Pd and Pt react with the Si support to form silicides. Although thermally grown SiO2 has proven effective as a diffusion barrier between pure Pt and Si, its performance for multicomponent CCSS thin films is unclear. Here, using Ag-Au-Pd-Pt as a model system, we compare a 25 nm thermally grown SiO2 barrier with native Si oxide during annealing using APT and transmission electron microscopy. Native Si oxide prevents detectable interfacial reactions up to 300°C, but at 400°C Pd and Pt react with Si, causing silicide formation and substantial redistribution of the film constituents. At 600°C, extensive substrate reactions disrupt the CCSS film and produce a pronounced needle-shaped silicide morphology. In contrast, thermally grown SiO2 suppresses CCSS thin film-substrate reactions up to 600°C and retains the CCSS composition. The thermally grown SiO2 thus extends the applicable temperature range of Si-based CPPs to at least 600°C for near-atomic-scale characterization of CCSS thin films.

</details>


### [105] [Laser Shock Peening in Hydrogen Environments: Coupled Stress Transport Trapping Mechanisms and Application Gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08469)
*Elzbieta Gadalinska,Jan Kaufman,Jan Smaus,Jan Brajer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review critically evaluates laser shock peening (LSP) as a surface layer design strategy for mitigating hydrogen embrittlement in high-strength steels and advanced alloys, examining coupled stress, hydrogen transport, and trapping mechanisms. It identifies contradictory evidence from various material systems, highlights two critical gaps (lack of quantitative links between post-LSP architectures and hydrogen-assisted fatigue crack growth, and absence of impact toughness data after LSP under hydrogen), and proposes mechanism-informed qualification routes combining residual stress mapping, hydrogen characterization, mechanical testing, and modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hydrogen embrittlement limits the deployment of high-strength steels and advanced alloys in hydrogen infrastructure, and LSP is increasingly considered a mitigation route. However, reported trends are confounded by multiple experimental variables, and existing literature lacks critical assessment of LSP as a holistic surface layer design strategy rather than an isolated strengthening treatment.

Method: The paper is a critical review that synthesizes evidence from steels, nickel-based alloys, and additively manufactured materials, analyzing how compressive residual stresses and LSP-induced microstructural features (nanostructuring, dislocations, twins, interfaces) influence hydrogen transport, trapping, and damage. It identifies methodological confounds (hydrogen charging mode, surface roughness, contamination, residual stress depth profiling, limited structure-performance correlations) and proposes integrated qualification frameworks.

Result: Compressive residual stresses from LSP may suppress stress-assisted hydrogen transport and delay crack initiation, while LSP-induced microstructural modifications can either beneficially redistribute hydrogen or promote localized plasticity and damage. Two key design gaps are identified: (1) lack of quantitative structure-property links between post-LSP stress/defect architectures and hydrogen-assisted fatigue crack growth, and (2) near absence of impact toughness data after LSP under hydrogen exposure.

Conclusion: LSP should be viewed as a surface layer design strategy governed by coupled stress, hydrogen transport, and trapping mechanisms. The review recommends mechanism-informed qualification routes that combine residual stress mapping, hydrogen characterization, service-representative mechanical testing, and modeling to reliably deploy LSP-treated materials in hydrogen infrastructure.

Abstract: Hydrogen embrittlement limits the deployment of high-strength steels and advanced alloys in hydrogen infrastructure. Laser shock peening (LSP) is increasingly considered as a mitigation route because it combines deep compressive residual stresses with near-surface microstructural modification. This review critically assesses LSP not as an isolated strengthening treatment, but as a surface layer design strategy governed by coupled stress, hydrogen transport and trapping mechanisms. Evidence from steels, nickel based alloys and additively manufactured materials shows that compressive residual stresses may suppress stress assisted hydrogen transport and delay crack initiation, while LSP-induced nanostructuring, dislocations, twins and interfaces can either redistribute hydrogen beneficially or promote localized plasticity and damage. Reported trends are frequently confounded by hydrogen charging mode, surface roughness, contamination, residual stress depth profiling and limited structure performance correlations. Two design critical gaps are identified: the lack of quantitative links between post-LSP stress/defect architectures and hydrogen assisted fatigue crack growth, and the near absence of impact toughness data after LSP under hydrogen exposure. The review proposes mechanism informed qualification routes combining residual stress mapping, hydrogen characterization, service representative mechanical testing and modelling.

</details>


### [106] [Thermal response of an in-situ STEM MEMS chip under rapid pulse heating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08473)
*Phillip Dumitraschkewitz,Thomas Kremmer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a simple experimental setup using an arbitrary waveform generator and series shunt to measure thermal histories of a Protochips Fusion MEMS chip in-situ STEM at sub-millisecond resolution, revealing an exponential thermal relaxation time constant of 1.80 ms and average cooling rates around 7.9×10⁴ K/s, with robustness checks including repeated R(T) measurements and test loads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-situ rapid solidification studies require measurements of thermal histories with high temporal resolution to understand non-equilibrium processes during solidification, and existing approaches need simple, effective setups to quantify cooling response on commercial MEMS chips used in scanning transmission electron microscopy.

Method: The authors drive user-defined temperature programs via an arbitrary waveform generator while recording voltage drops across a series shunt to reconstruct chip resistance and temperature at sub-millisecond resolution. They compare response times inferred from current with those derived from physically linked resistance and analyze the maximum cooling step using exponential relaxation fits.

Result: Temperature from resistance evolves more slowly than current-inferred response times. The maximum cooling step exhibits exponential-like relaxation with time constant τ=1.80 ms, consistent with reported thermal lag constants for fast scanning calorimetry. Average cooling rate of ≈7.9×10⁴ K/s is measured from time to reach 95% of temperature difference. Robustness checks show modest downward drift in repeated R(T) measurements approaching an asymptote.

Conclusion: The findings define practical bounds on achievable thermal-path rates when planning in-situ electron microscopy experiments with this chip platform, establishing quantifiable limits for cooling rates on uncoated commercial Protochips Fusion MEMS devices.

Abstract: In-situ rapid solidification studies demand measurements of thermal histories with high temporal resolution. We present a simple, effective setup to quantify the cooling response of an uncoated commercial Protochips Fusion MEMS chip in an in-situ scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) context. We drive user-defined temperature programs via an arbitrary waveform generator (AWG), while recording the voltage drops across a series shunt to reconstruct chip resistance and temperature at sub-millisecond resolution. We confirm the response times inferred from the current; however, the temperature obtained from the physically linked resistance, $T(R)$, evolves more slowly. Analysis of the maximum cooling step reveals an exponential-like relaxation with time constant $τ=1.80$ ms, consistent with reported thermal lag constants for fast scanning calorimetry. From the time to reach $95\%$ of the temperature difference $ΔT$, we measure an average cooling rate of $\approx 7.9\times 10^{4}$ K/s. Robustness checks include repeated $R(T)$ measurements (revealing a modest downward drift approaching an asymptote), a 10 k$Ω$ test load, and characterization of small off-duty arbitrary waveform generator leakage/offsets. These findings define practical bounds on achievable thermal-path rates when planning in-situ electron microscopy experiments with this chip platform.

</details>


### [107] [Phonon-Programmable Hidden Unconventional Magnetism in Two-Dimensional Spin-Degenerate Antiferromagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08478)
*Xiaonong Shen,Cheng Tang,Wei Ren*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Coherent phonon pumping in spin-degenerate antiferromagnets (e.g., monolayer MnPSe3) can activate hidden unconventional magnetism by removing spin-layer symmetry operations that enforce band degeneracy, without altering the Néel order. The pump field frequency and polarization select a specific Γ-point optical phonon, programming the spin-splitting texture and thermoelectric spin currents (tunable between transverse pure-spin and longitudinal spin-polarized currents). A complete classification of 2D collinear spin layer groups identifies the phonon coordinates that enable this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin-degenerate antiferromagnets are promising for spintronics due to their ultrafast dynamics and lack of stray fields, but their compensated magnetic order suppresses conventional spin-dependent transport phenomena. This work aims to reveal and manipulate a hidden unconventional magnetism encoded in lattice degrees of freedom that is normally inactive in equilibrium.

Method: The authors use symmetry analysis combined with first-principles calculations. They show that coherent excitation of a specific Γ-point optical phonon (selected by pump frequency and polarization) removes the spin-layer antisymmetry operations that enforce band degeneracy in equilibrium, activating unconventional spin splitting (i-wave, d-wave, or s-wave depending on the phonon mode and its direction within a doublet). They also perform a complete classification of 2D collinear spin layer groups to identify which Γ-point phonon coordinates can remove degeneracy-enforcing operations.

Result: In monolayer MnPSe3, an A2u phonon mode generates i-wave spin splitting odd in the mass-weighted phonon coordinate Q (sign-reversing under Q→-Q), while orthogonal directions of the doubly degenerate Eu mode produce d-wave and s-wave splitting at a common resonance. Rotating the in-plane pump field E∥ continuously tunes the thermoelectric response between transverse pure-spin and longitudinal spin-polarized currents, enabling programmable spin-texture engineering without altering the collinear Néel order.

Conclusion: Coherent phonons provide a dynamical, all-optical route to control hidden unconventional magnetism in antiferromagnets. The pump field acts as a switch that programs both the momentum-space spin-splitting texture and spin-current directionality, and the classification of 2D spin layer groups offers a systematic framework for predicting such phenomena in other antiferromagnetic materials.

Abstract: Spin-degenerate antiferromagnets can host hidden unconventional magnetism in their lattice degrees of freedom. We show that coherent phonons activate this magnetism by removing the spin-layer operations that enforce equilibrium band degeneracy without changing the collinear Néel order. The frequency and polarization of a pump electric field select a resonant \(Γ\)-point optical phonon and, within a doublet, its coordinate direction. This choice fixes the residual spin-layer symmetry. In monolayer MnPSe$_3$, an \(A_{2u}\) mode produces \(i\)-wave splitting odd in the mass-weighted phonon coordinate $Q$, reversing sign under $Q\to-Q$, whereas two orthogonal directions of the same doubly degenerate \(E_u\) doublet produce \(d\)- and \(s\)-wave splitting at a common resonance. Rotating the in-plane pump field \(\bm E_\parallel\) therefore programs both the
  spin-splitting texture and the thermoelectric spin current, continuously tuning the
  response between transverse pure-spin and longitudinal spin-polarized
  currents. A complete classification of two-dimensional collinear spin layer
  groups identifies the \(Γ\)-point coordinates that remove the
  degeneracy-enforcing operations and activate such unconventional magnetism.

</details>


### [108] [The Hidden Cost of Alloying: Disorder-Driven Transport Collapse in TMDs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08597)
*Michele Pisarra,Clara Rebanal,Enrique Arévalo Rodríguez,Marc Meléndez,Elena Blundo,Giacomo Amadore,Jonathan J. Finley,Fabián Calleja,Marc G. Cuxart,Jesús Álvarez,Ma-ría José Capitán,Fernando J. Urbanos,Julia García Pérez,Ramón Bernardo Gavito,Daniel Granados,Ji Dai,Massimo Tallarida,Antonello Sindona,Fernando Martín,Ferry Prins,Iolanda Di Bernardo,Amadeo L. Vázquez de Parga*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates MoS2xSe2(1-x) alloys as a model system for transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) alloys, revealing that while optical and electronic band properties evolve continuously with composition and thickness, carrier diffusivity collapses asymmetrically at intermediate compositions due to real-space energetic disorder from random chalcogen substitution—disorder physics that conventional equilibrium probes fail to detect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alloying in 2D semiconductors is commonly used for bandgap tuning, but its effects on charge and energy transport remain poorly understood. The authors aim to bridge this gap by studying how composition, thickness, and disorder interact in TMD alloys, and to establish transport as a rigorous metric of electronic quality.

Method: The study uses MoS2xSe2(1-x) alloys as a model system, combining optical spectroscopy, photoemission to probe valence band dispersions, and time-resolved spatial mapping of photoexcited carriers to measure diffusivity across compositions and thicknesses. These experiments are complemented by microscopic simulations to reproduce the observed transport trends.

Result: Optical transitions and valence band dispersions evolve continuously with stoichiometry and layer number with negligible bandgap bowing and composition-dependent attenuation of thickness-driven renormalization. However, carrier diffusivity exhibits a pronounced, asymmetric collapse at intermediate compositions, unexplained by effective mass or band alignment changes. Simulations attribute this to strong real-space energetic fluctuations from random chalcogen substitution, where disorder character depends on alloying direction, yielding either scattering barriers or deep trapping sites.

Conclusion: Transport in TMD alloys is governed by disorder physics that is invisible to conventional optical and photoemission equilibrium probes. This establishes transport as a stringent measure of electronic quality and highlights intrinsic limitations of TMD alloys for layered semiconductor device applications.

Abstract: Alloying in two dimensional semiconductors is widely used to tune bandgaps, yet its implications for charge and energy transport remain poorly understood. Here, we investigate MoS2xSe2(1-x) alloys as a model system to study the interplay between composition, thickness, and disorder. Optical transitions and valence band dispersions evolve continuously with both stoichiometry and number of layers, with negligible bandgap bowing and a composition dependent attenuation of thickness driven renormalization. In contrast, time resolved spatial mapping of photoexcited carriers reveals a pronounced and asymmetric collapse of carrier diffusivity at intermediate compositions, which cannot be accounted for by changes in effective mass or band alignment, and instead emerges from strong real space fluctuations in the local energetic landscape generated by random chalcogen substitution. Microscopic simulations reproduce the experimental trends and show that the character of disorder depends critically on the direction of alloying, producing either scattering barriers or deep trapping sites. Together, these results demonstrate that transport in TMD alloys is governed by disorder physics, overlooked by conventional optical and photoemission probes at equilibrium. Our findings establish transport as a stringent metric of electronic quality and high-light intrinsic limitations in the usage of TMD alloys for layered semiconductor devices.

</details>


### [109] [Pulse-Burst Excitation Reveals Time-Dose Reciprocity Breakdown in Mixed-Halide Perovskites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08632)
*Alexandr Marunchenko,Shivam Singh,Daniel Lizotte,Bhaskar De,Yana Vaynzof,Ivan G. Scheblykin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that time-dose reciprocity (the Bunsen-Roscoe law) breaks down in mixed-halide perovskites under pulse-burst excitation, where redistributing the same total photon dose over microsecond-to-millisecond timescales drives the material into distinct metastable states via photoinduced halide segregation and remixing. Photon timing is established as a new control parameter for perovskite photochemistry, with implications for optical memory and neuromorphic photonic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Light-sensitive processes in mixed-halide perovskites often exhibit threshold-like behavior that may violate the time-dose reciprocity principle, potentially enabling control of material state through photon timing beyond conventional parameters like pulse fluence, repetition rate, and average power.

Method: Pulse-burst excitation is used to introduce a temporal control dimension by redistributing identical total photon doses over microsecond-to-millisecond timescales, creating distinct nonequilibrium excitation conditions in mixed-halide perovskites.

Result: The experiments show that mixed-halide perovskites evolve into different metastable states depending on photon timing, revealing a breakdown of time-dose reciprocity in the coupled processes of halide segregation and remixing.

Conclusion: Photon timing serves as a control parameter for perovskite photochemistry, providing a new experimental framework for disentangling competing photoinduced halide redistribution processes and enabling applications in optical memory and neuromorphic photonics.

Abstract: Time-dose reciprocity, commonly associated with the Bunsen-Roscoe law, states that the response of a photosensitive system depends only on the total exposure dose, regardless of how that energy is delivered over time. Light-sensitive processes in mixed-halide perovskites, such as photoinduced halide segregation, often exhibit threshold-like behavior that may violate this principle and enable material-state control by photon timing. We test this using pulse-burst excitation, which introduces an additional temporal control dimension beyond conventional parameters such as pulse fluence, repetition rate, and average power. By redistributing the same photon dose over microsecond-to-millisecond timescales, we create distinct nonequilibrium excitation conditions and show that mixed-halide perovskites can evolve into different metastable states, revealing a breakdown of time-dose reciprocity in the combined processes of halide segregation and remixing. This additional temporal degree of freedom not only enables control of the material state but also provides a new experimental framework for disentangling the competing processes underlying photoinduced halide redistribution. Our findings establish photon timing as a control parameter for perovskite photochemistry and open additional opportunities for optical memory and neuromorphic photonic applications.

</details>


### [110] [Pressure Dependence of Ferromagnetism in Uranium Hydride UH$_3$ up to 20 GPa Revealed by NV Centers Magnetometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08707)
*Valentin Schmidt,Loïc Toraille,Florent Occelli,Jean-François Roch,Paul Loubeyre*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the pressure dependence of ferromagnetism in β-UH₃ using diamond anvil cell synthesis combined with nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center magnetometry up to ~20 GPa, finding a linear decrease in Curie temperature with a slope of -3.86(8) K/GPa and predicting ferromagnetism suppression at ~44 GPa where a quantum critical point may emerge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The origin of ferromagnetism in β-UH₃ remains debated since it falls outside known systematics of uranium compounds due to its high Curie temperature and short U-U interatomic distance. Pressure offers a powerful means to tune magnetism toward suppression, providing insights into underlying mechanisms, yet the high-pressure behavior of β-UH₃ has remained largely unexplored.

Method: The authors combine in-situ synthesis of pure β-UH₃ within a Diamond Anvil Cell (DAC) with widefield nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center magnetometry. Two data analysis methods are employed: vector magnetic field reconstruction and statistical analysis of the optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) response, enabling direct imaging of the magnetic dipole of β-UH₃ during isobaric warming.

Result: Measurements up to ~20 GPa reveal a linear decrease of the Curie temperature with pressure, yielding a slope of dTC/dP = -3.86(8) K/GPa. Extrapolation predicts ferromagnetism reaches 0 K at approximately 44 GPa, where a quantum critical point may emerge.

Conclusion: The study establishes the pressure dependence of ferromagnetism in β-UH₃ using a novel NV-magnetometry-in-DAC approach, quantifies the suppression rate of TC with pressure, and suggests the possibility of a quantum critical point near 44 GPa.

Abstract: The origin of ferromagnetism in the stable $β$-UH$_3$ phase is still debated. With a high Curie temperature and a short uranium-uranium interatomic distance, $β$-UH$_3$ is placed outside the known systematics of uranium compounds. Pressure provides a powerful means of tuning magnetism toward its suppression, thereby offering valuable insights into its underlying mechanisms, yet the high-pressure behavior of $β$-UH$_3$ has remained largely unexplored. Here, we combine in the Diamond Anvil Cell (DAC) the development of pure $β$-UH$_3$ synthesis and widefield nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center magnetometry to measure the pressure dependence of the Curie temperature in $β$-UH$_3$ up to about 20~GPa. We present two data analysis methods, vector magnetic field reconstruction and statistical analysis of the optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) response, which enable us to directly image the magnetic dipole of $β$-UH$_3$ during isobaric warming. We observe a linear decrease in the Curie temperature with pressure, yielding a slope of $\text{d}T_C/\text{d}P=-3.86\,(8)$~K/GPa. Ferromagnetism in $β$-UH$_3$ is predicted to reach 0~K at approximately 44~GPa, where a quantum critical point may emerge.

</details>


### [111] [Study on Thickness and Temperature Dependence of Thermoelectric Properties in SnS Nanofilms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08734)
*Liqi Chen,Ziyang Wang,Donghao Li,Jingye Wang,Ning Zhao,Jun Zhou,Jie Zhu,Dawei Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper systematically investigates the thermoelectric properties of SnS nanofilms with thicknesses of 82-813 nm over 300-600 K, demonstrating that low-dimensionalization significantly enhances thermoelectric performance compared to bulk SnS due to quantum confinement, energy filtering, and intensified phonon scattering effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bulk SnS thermoelectric performance is inherently limited by intrinsic point defects and its specific band structure. Low-dimensional engineering is explored as a strategy to overcome these constraints and enhance thermoelectric performance in this environmentally friendly, cost-effective material for medium-temperature thermoelectric conversion.

Method: SnS nanofilms of four distinct thicknesses (82 nm, 199 nm, 616 nm, 813 nm) were systematically measured for thermoelectric properties across 300-600 K using time-domain thermoreflectance (TDTR) and a dedicated thin-film thermoelectric parameter test system (ZEM-3).

Result: All four SnS thin films exhibited markedly superior thermoelectric performance compared to bulk SnS, with the figure of merit (ZT) showing pronounced dependence on both film thickness and temperature. Performance enhancement is attributed to quantum confinement effect, energy filtering effect, and intensified phonon scattering induced by low-dimensional structural characteristics.

Conclusion: Low-dimensionalization effectively boosts the thermoelectric performance of SnS, providing experimental evidence and theoretical insights for performance optimization of SnS nanofilms and establishing a foundational framework for developing high-efficiency, eco-friendly medium-temperature thermoelectric materials.

Abstract: SnS as an environmentally friendly, cost-effective, and earth-abundant narrow-bandgap semiconductor material, has demonstrated significant application potential in the field of medium-temperature thermoelectric conversion. However, the thermoelectric performance of its bulk counterpart is inherently constrained by intrinsic point defects (e.g., vacancies) and the material's specific band structure. Low-dimensional engineering has emerged as a pivotal strategy for overcoming these limitations and enhancing thermoelectric performance. In this work, we systematically investigate the thermoelectric properties of SnS nanofilms with distinct thicknesses (82 nm, 199 nm, 616 nm, and 813 nm) across a temperature range of 300-600 K. Measurements were conducted using time-domain thermoreflectance (TDTR) and a dedicated thin-film thermoelectric parameter test system (ZEM-3). Our results confirm that low-dimensionalization effectively boosts the thermoelectric performance of SnS, with the thermoelectric figure of merit (ZT) displaying a pronounced dependence on both film thickness and temperature. All four SnS thin films exhibit thermoelectric performance that is markedly superior to that of bulk SnS. This enhancement is primarily attributed to the quantum confinement effect, energy filtering effect, and intensified phonon scattering, all of which are induced by the low-dimensional structural characteristics. This work provides not only experimental evidence and theoretical insights for the performance optimization of SnS nanofilms but also establishes a foundational framework for the development of high-efficiency, eco-friendly medium-temperature thermoelectric materials, thereby holding significant scientific value and practical implications.

</details>


### [112] [The absence of a central metal ion destabilizes phthalocyanine on In$_2$O$_3$(111)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08742)
*Viktoria Waidbacher,Sarah Tobisch,Faith J. Lewis,Moritz Eder,Michael Schmid,Gareth Parkinson,Ulrike Diebold,Margareta Wagner*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The adsorption of metal-free phthalocyanine (H2Pc) on In2O3(111) was characterized using STM, nc-AFM, and STS. H2Pc adsorbs at the same site as CuPc and CoPc but decomposes at ~50°C, preventing ordered monolayer formation that those metalated analogues achieve. The central metal ion acts as a structural anchor stabilizing the macrocycle and modifies frontier-orbital character, determining whether additional adsorption configurations are populated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the central metal ion in metal phthalocyanines governs adsorption behavior, thermal stability, and monolayer formation on In2O3(111), a prototypical transparent electrode and catalytic substrate relevant to organic optoelectronics and single-atom catalysis.

Method: Low-temperature scanning tunneling microscopy (STM), non-contact atomic force microscopy (nc-AFM), and scanning tunneling spectroscopy (STS) were used to characterize H2Pc adsorption on both stoichiometric and reduced In2O3(111) surfaces, with comparison to prior studies of CoPc and CuPc.

Result: H2Pc adsorbs at the identical site and configuration as CuPc and the majority species of CoPc, yet decomposes at ~50°C—far below monolayer growth temperatures for CoPc/CuPc—preventing ordered extended monolayers. No self-metalation occurs on stoichiometric In2O3(111). On reduced surfaces with In0 adatoms, new H2Pc-related features appear but cannot be identified by imaging alone.

Conclusion: The central metal ion plays a dual role in metal-Pc/In2O3(111) systems: it structurally anchors and thermally stabilizes the macrocycle against decomposition, and it tunes frontier-orbital character to control whether a second adsorption configuration is accessible.

Abstract: Metal phthalocyanines (MPc) are a versatile molecular platform for applications ranging from organic optoelectronic devices to single-atom catalysis (SAC). Their adsorption and layer formation on the prototypical transparent electrode substrates of organic optoelectronic devices is directly relevant for charge injection and transport across the organic-oxide interface. Moreover, the well-defined M-N$_4$ coordination of the metal cation defines their activity as SACs for (electro-)catalysis. Here, the adsorption of the metal-free phthalocyanine (H$_2$Pc) is characterized on In$_2$O$_3$(111) using low-temperature STM, nc-AFM, and STS. In$_2$O$_3$ is not only a model system of indium tin oxide (ITO) but also an active catalytic material for CO$_2$ reduction. H$_2$Pc adsorbs in the same site and configuration reported for copper phthalocyanine [J. Mater. Chem. C 13, 17650-17661 (2025)] and for the majority of cobalt phthalocyanine [Surf. Sci. 722, 122065 (2022)]. Despite this shared preference in adsorption site, H$_2$Pc cannot be organized into extended and ordered monolayer structures by gentle annealing: the molecule starts to decompose at $\approx$50$^\circ$C, well below the temperature used to grow monolayers of CoPc and CuPc. Self-metalation is not observed on stoichiometric In$_2$O$_3$(111). On the reduced surface where In$^0$ adatoms are present, new H$_2$Pc-related features appear but cannot be identified by imaging only. The comparison of H$_2$Pc with CoPc and CuPc identifies distinct roles of the central metal ion in the metal-Pc/In$_2$O$_3$(111) systems: it acts as a structural anchor that stabilizes the macrocycle against decomposition on the surface, and it modifies the frontier-orbital character in ways that determine whether a second adsorption configuration is populated.

</details>


### [113] [Contrasting roles of the superexchange and direct exchange interactions in the metal-insulator transitions of the 2D ferromagnetic $1T$-Fe$X_2$ ($X$=Cl, Br, I) monolayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08807)
*Hongxu Luo,Baoyang Zhou,Sai Lyu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study computationally investigates the critical behaviors of exchange interactions near metal-insulator transitions (MITs) in 2D ferromagnetic 1T-FeX2 (X=Cl, Br, I) monolayers, showing that FM superexchange interactions favor halfmetallic states with stronger FM couplings and higher Curie temperatures, while direct exchange interactions favor semiconducting states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While 2D ferromagnetic materials are of great interest and MITs in these systems are critical for designing miniaturized electronic devices, the critical behaviors of exchange interactions and their effects on Curie temperatures near MITs remain unclear.

Method: Computational study of 2D FM 1T-FeX2 (X=Cl, Br, I) monolayers using on-site Coulomb electron-electron interaction modulation to drive MITs and examine the critical behavior of exchange interactions.

Result: FM superexchange interactions and direct exchange interactions energetically favor halfmetallic and semiconducting states, respectively. Halfmetallic states exhibit stronger FM couplings and therefore higher Curie temperatures.

Conclusion: This work provides a comprehensive understanding of the interrelation between MITs and exchange interactions, offering guidance for device design requiring both MITs and exchange-interaction-related magnetic properties in 2D FM materials, especially robust magnetic ordering and higher Curie temperatures.

Abstract: Within the rapidly growing family of two-dimensional (2D) materials, 2D ferromagnetic (FM) materials have attracted a great deal of recent attention. The ferromagnetism in 2D FM materials originates from the underlying exchange interactions. In particular, metal-insulator transitions (MITs) in 2D FM materials induced via various modulation strategies is critical to the design of 2D electronic devices with reduced sizes (e.g. transistor). However, the critical behaviors of exchange interactions and their effects on the Curie temperatures near MITs remains to be clarified. By taking the 2D FM 1T-FeX2 (X=Cl, Br, I) monolayers as the testbeds, we computationally study the critical behaviors of the exchange interactions in the vicinity of the MITs driven by the on-site Coulomb electron-electon interactions. The FM superexchange interactions and the direct exchange interactions are found to energetically favor the halfmetallic and semiconducting states, respectively. In addition, the halfmetallic states have the stronger FM couplings and thus the higher Curie temperatures. This study provides a comprehensive understanding of the interrelations between MITs and exchange interactions and could be useful for the device design requiring both MITs and exchange-interaction related magnetic properties, particularly robust magnetic ordering and higher Curie temperatures, in 2D FM materials.

</details>


### [114] [Controlling Hydrogen Isotope Retention at Helium Cavities through Radiation-Induced Segregation in Fusion Steels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08878)
*Lihao Shi,Logan N. Clowers,Qing Peng,Gary S. Was,Fei Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper identifies an irradiation-enabled mechanism where solute segregation (Cr and Ta) to defect sinks in RAFM steel F82H enhances hydrogen isotope trapping, linking solute-tuned electronic structure effects to elevated-temperature tritium retention in fusion materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hydrogen isotope retention in plasma-facing and structural alloys is a central challenge for deuterium-tritium fusion. Understanding mechanisms that enhance trapping is critical for controlling tritium inventory in fusion environments.

Method: Combines triple-ion irradiation experiments on reduced activation ferritic-martensitic (RAFM) steel F82H to observe solute segregation (Cr, Ta) at cavity surfaces, with density functional theory (DFT) calculations to evaluate hydrogen binding energies, migration barriers, and electronic structure effects.

Result: Pronounced segregation of Cr and Ta to cavity surfaces is observed under irradiation. DFT reveals these segregants strengthen H binding energies and increase migration barriers at cavities, substantially enhancing H retention. The mechanism stems from solute-tuned electronic structure: Ta promotes strong H 1s-metal d orbital hybridization, while Cr shifts the surface d-band toward a more favorable bonding configuration.

Conclusion: The findings establish an atomistic link between irradiation-induced segregation and elevated-temperature hydrogen isotope retention, offering alloy chemistry routes to control tritium inventory in fusion reactor environments.

Abstract: Hydrogen isotope retention in plasma facing and structural alloys is a central materials challenge for deuterium-tritium fusion. Motivated by ion beam irradiation experiments and first principles calculations, we identify an irradiation-enabled mechanism whereby solute segregation to defect sinks enhances hydrogen isotope trapping. Triple-ion irradiation of reduced activation ferritic-martensitic steel F82H reveals pronounced segregation of Cr and Ta to cavity surfaces. Density functional theory shows that these segregants markedly increase H stability at cavities by strengthening binding energies and increasing migration barriers, leading to substantially higher attention. The effect originates from solute-tuned electronic structure: Ta promotes strong H 1s-metal d orbital hybridization, whereas Cr shifts the surface d-band toward a more favorable bonding configuration. These findings provide an atomistic link between irradiation-induced segregation and elevated-temperature hydrogen isotope retention and alloy chemistry routes to control tritium inventory in fusion environments.

</details>


### [115] [Zirconium Carbide as a High-Temperature Benchmark for the Beyond Quasi-Harmonic Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08913)
*Christopher M. Stanley*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The BQH (Beyond Quasi-Harmonic) method accurately calculates the anharmonic molar heat capacity of rocksalt ZrC from first principles, with an electronic correction bringing results into close agreement with fully anharmonic benchmarks up to ~1200 K, demonstrating that BQH captures anharmonic phonon contributions missing in standard quasi-harmonic calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molar heat capacity at constant pressure, C_p(T), is a central thermodynamic quantity in materials science, yet it remains difficult to compute accurately from first principles when anharmonic vibrational effects are significant.

Method: The BQH (Beyond Quasi-Harmonic) method is a prepared-supercell first-principles approach that extracts anharmonic vibrational energy directly from DFT energy evaluations. Rocksalt ZrC serves as a high-temperature benchmark. The BQH C_p is compared against CALPHAD reference values, a fully anharmonic thermodynamic-integration calculation, a C_v(T) curve, and a quasi-harmonic calculation. An electronic heat-capacity correction is estimated from the density of states at the Fermi level and added to the vibrational BQH result.

Result: The BQH curve yields substantial improvement over both C_v(T) and the quasi-harmonic approximation. With the electronic correction added, the calculated molar heat capacity agrees closely with the fully anharmonic theoretical benchmark up to approximately 1200 K.

Conclusion: The BQH method fully captures important anharmonic phonon contributions that are missing from ordinary quasi-harmonic calculations, validating its effectiveness for high-temperature thermodynamic property prediction in anharmonic systems.

Abstract: Molar heat capacity at constant pressure, C_p (T), is a central thermodynamic quantity in materials science, but it remains difficult to calculate accurately from first principles when anharmonic vibrational effects become important. In this work, rocksalt ZrC is used as a high-temperature benchmark for the Beyond Quasi-Harmonic (BQH) method, a prepared-supercell first-principles approach that extracts anharmonic vibrational energy directly from density-functional-theory energy evaluations. The calculated BQH molar heat capacity is compared with CALPHAD reference values, a fully anharmonic thermodynamic-integration calculation, a calculated C_v (T) curve from the present work, and a quasi-harmonic calculation. The BQH curve gives a substantial improvement beyond C_v (T) and beyond the quasi-harmonic approximation. Because ZrC is electronically conductive, an electronic heat-capacity correction was also estimated from the density of states at the Fermi level and added to the vibrational BQH result. This correction brings the calculated molar heat capacity into close agreement with the fully anharmonic theoretical benchmark through approximately 1200 K. These results show that the BQH method fully captures important anharmonic phonon contributions missing from ordinary quasi-harmonic calculations.

</details>


### [116] [Edge-Dominated Twist Mechanics at van der Waals Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08931)
*Yifan Shao,Zhaoheng Zhang,Hao Li,Oded Hod,Michael Urbakh,Quanshui Zheng,Xiang Gao,Deli Peng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates torsional mechanics at van der Waals interfaces through experiments and large-scale atomistic simulations, revealing that peak torque scales nearly linearly with contact area for both homo- and heterogeneous interfaces. An edge-dominated yielding mechanism is identified for torsion, contrasting with bulk-mediated stress transmission in translational sliding, demonstrating that torsional and translational properties are fundamentally decoupled at vdW interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although twist is known to critically modulate physical properties at van der Waals interfaces, the mechanics governing torsional response remain poorly understood. The authors aim to clarify the fundamental physics distinguishing torsional from translational motion at vdW interfaces.

Method: A unified experimental framework is used to probe twist mechanics (peak torque and sliding behaviors) at both homo- and heterogeneous vdW interfaces. Complementary large-scale atomistic simulations are performed to elucidate the underlying deformation and yielding mechanisms.

Result: Peak torque scales nearly linearly with contact area for both interface types, contradicting predictions from linear elastic and rigid models. Though sliding friction of the two interfaces diverges by over three orders of magnitude due to different scaling laws, torque follows the same linear scaling and differs only ~20-fold. Simulations reveal an edge-dominated yielding mechanism for torsion, where elastic reconstruction shifts the effective load-bearing region toward the edges.

Conclusion: Torsional loading at vdW interfaces operates via edge-dominated yielding necessitating perimeter actuation, while translational sliding involves bulk-mediated stress transmission enabling center-driven loading. This symmetry-imposed divergence shows translational and torsional properties cannot be mutually predicted, offering critical design insights for reconfigurable micro- and nanoelectromechanical devices.

Abstract: Despite the pivotal role of twist in modulating physical properties at van der Waals (vdW) interfaces, the mechanics governing torsional response remain poorly understood. Here, we probe twist mechanics at homo- and heterogeneous vdW interfaces, together with their sliding behaviors within a unified experimental framework. For both systems, the peak torque scales nearly linearly with contact area, in contrast to predictions from linear elastic and rigid models. Remarkably, while the sliding friction of the two interfaces diverges by over three orders of magnitude owing to different scaling laws, the corresponding torque follows the same linear scaling and differs by only about twenty-fold. Large-scale atomistic simulations reveal an edge-dominated yielding mechanism for torsional motion, wherein elastic reconstruction shifts the effective load-bearing region toward the edges, eliminating torque from the contact interior. This mechanism contrasts with the bulk-mediated stress transmission governing translational sliding, a distinction rooted in the different loading geometries inherent to the two motion modes, where torsional loading necessitates perimeter actuation, whereas sliding enables center-driven loading. This symmetry-imposed divergence demonstrates that translational and torsional properties cannot be predicted from one another at vdW interfaces, providing critical insights for the design of dynamically reconfigurable micro- and nanoelectromechanical devices.

</details>


### [117] [Water, vacancies, and competing exchange interactions in Prussian blue analogues: a neutron diffraction study of field and dehydration-driven magnetic transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08976)
*N. S. Dhami,C. V. Colin,V. Nassif,O. Fabelo,T. Nait,A. Bleuzen,A. Bordage,V. Balédent*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A neutron diffraction study of Prussian blue analogues reveals diffuse scattering from correlated vacancies/interstitial water, dehydration-induced thermal expansion crossover, collinear ferrimagnetic ordering (except partially frustrated MnFe), and a field- or dehydration-driven spin reorientation to the common collinear state explained by near-compensation between Mn-Fe and frustrated Mn-Mn couplings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve long-standing ambiguities in interpreting Fe K-edge XMCD data on MnFe-based PBAs and to establish water content as a key parameter controlling the structural and magnetic stability of this family, with relevance to battery electrode applications.

Method: Neutron diffraction as a function of temperature (2-450 K) and applied magnetic field on several bimetallic Prussian blue analogues, including cation-stabilized Rb2NiFe framework for comparison, alongside a minimal Heisenberg model.

Result: Broad diffuse scattering identified as a signature of correlated vacancies/interstitial water; CoFe and MnFe show a crossover from positive to negative thermal expansion upon dehydration while NiFe remains robust. All compounds order collinearly with k=(0,0,0) except MnFe which has a partially frustrated k=(1,0,0) structure. A field of Bc=1.3 T or dehydration drives MnFe to the collinear k=(0,0,0) state. Heisenberg modeling attributes the transition to near-compensation between antiferromagnetic Mn-Fe coupling and frustrated Mn-Mn interaction on the fcc Mn sublattice.

Conclusion: The results resolve the XMCD ambiguity in MnFe-based PBAs and show water content is a pivotal parameter governing both structural and magnetic behavior in this material family, relevant to their use as battery electrodes.

Abstract: We report a neutron diffraction study of the structural and magnetic properties of a family of ferro- and ferrimagnetic Prussian blue analogues (PBAs), $A_4$[Fe(CN)$_6$]$_{2.7}$ ($A$ = Co, Mn, Ni), Rb$_2$Ni$_4$[Fe(CN)$_6$]$_{3.3}$, and Mn$_4$[Cr(CN)$_6$]$_{2.7}$, as a function of temperature (2--450~K) and applied magnetic field. All bimetallic compounds of the Fm$\overline{3}$m family exhibit a broad diffuse feature at low scattering angle, which we identify, through comparison with the cation-stabilized Rb$_2$NiFe framework, as an intrinsic signature of correlated vacancies and their associated interstitial water. High-temperature diffraction reveals a continuous crossover from positive to negative thermal expansion in CoFe and MnFe upon dehydration, while NiFe remains structurally robust up to 450~K. At low temperature, all compounds order in a collinear ferrimagnetic state with propagation vector $\mathbf{k}=(0,0,0)$, except MnFe, which adopts a partially frustrated magnetic structure with $\mathbf{k}=(1,0,0)$. A moderate magnetic field of $B_c = 1.3$~T drives a spin reorientation in MnFe toward the collinear $\mathbf{k}=(0,0,0)$ ferrimagnetic state common to the other compounds; the same transition is independently induced by dehydration. A minimal Heisenberg model shows that this transition results from a near-compensation between antiferromagnetic Mn--Fe coupling and a geometrically frustrated antiferromagnetic Mn--Mn interaction on the face-centered-cubic Mn sublattice, placing MnFe in the vicinity of a magnetic compensation point. These results resolve a longstanding ambiguity in the interpretation of the Fe $K$-edge XMCD response of MnFe-based PBAs, and establish water content as a key parameter controlling both the structural and magnetic stability of this family of materials, with direct relevance to their use as battery electrodes.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mes-hall'></div>

# cond-mat.mes-hall [[Back]](#toc)

### [118] [First principles calculations of electric-field-driven topological phase transitions in silicene, germanene and stanene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05633)
*Julián Antonio Villarreal Murúa,Pablo Roura-Bas,Javier Daniel Fuhr,Ricardo Faccio*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a first-principles framework combining DFT, maximally localized Wannier functions, and Wannier charge center evolution to accurately determine the critical electric field for topological phase transitions in silicene, germanene, and stanene, yielding predictions of 0.020 V/Å for silicene and 0.250 V/Å for germanene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise determination of the critical electric field E_z^cr at which topological phase transitions occur in group-14 monolayers (silicene, germanene, stanene) remains challenging, as tight-binding models often underestimate these values.

Method: Combines density-functional theory (DFT), maximally localized Wannier functions, and Wannier charge center (WCC) evolution. At each electric field strength, fully self-consistent ab initio simulations are run to obtain the screened electronic structure accounting for dielectric response from both electrons and ions. A Wannier tight-binding Hamiltonian is then constructed at each field strength to enable gauge-invariant calculation of the Z2 topological invariant.

Result: The methodology yields significantly more accurate numerical predictions of critical electric fields: 0.020 V/Å for silicene and 0.250 V/Å for germanene, marking a marked quantitative improvement over previous approaches for predicting topological phase boundaries.

Conclusion: This improved framework for predicting topological phase boundaries is essential for guiding the design of topological field-effect transistors and electrostatically controlled quantum devices based on two-dimensional materials.

Abstract: The emergence of two-dimensional topological materials, particularly the group-14 monolayers known as silicene, germanene, and stanene has opened promising pathways for next-generation nanoelectronics and spintronics. Their buckled honeycomb structure and strong spin-orbit coupling allow for bandgap engineering via a perpendicular electric field, leading to topological phase transitions (TPTs) from non-trivial to trivial insulating states. However, precise determination of the critical electric field $E_z^{\text{cr}}$ at which these transitions occur remains challenging, with tight-binding models often underestimating these values. Here, we present a first-principles framework that combines density-functional theory (DFT), maximally localized Wannier functions, and evolution of the Wannier charge centers (WCC) to accurately characterize TPTs in silicene, germanene, and stanene through the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological invariant. In contrast to earlier work, at each electric-field strength we run fully self-consistent ab initio simulations to obtain the screened electronic structure, accounting for the material's dielectric response from both electrons and ions. From these converged results we construct a Wannier tight-binding Hamiltonian at each electric field strength, which then enables a gauge-invariant calculation of the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological invariant. This methodology yields significantly more accurate numerical predictions of $E_z^{\text{cr}}$, $0.020$ and $0.250$ V/Å for silicene and germanene, respectively. Compared to previous approaches, our framework delivers a marked quantitative improvement for predicting topological phase boundaries, essential for guiding the design of topological field-effect transistors and electrostatically controlled quantum devices based on two-dimensional materials.

</details>


### [119] [Fast universal parametric spin control in an acoustically modulated quantum dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05634)
*Mateusz Kuniej,Michał Gawełczyk*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a parametric acoustic control scheme for semiconductor quantum-dot spin qubits that overcomes limitations of prior acousto-optical spin control, achieving fast universal qubit rotation (155 ps Pauli-X gates, ~290x faster than prior acoustic and ~14x faster than optical approaches) with high fidelity (>99.9%) even in uncooled nuclear-spin environments, enabled by optical dressing of spin states and acoustic modulation of optical transition energies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing acoustic quantum-dot spin control theory has limited rotation-axis angles, leading to complex gate implementations and long gate times that leave little margin against decoherence from trion decay and quasi-static nuclear-spin noise, preventing practical integration of single-QD spin qubits into acoustically coupled hybrid quantum systems.

Method: The scheme uses far-detuned optical coupling to a trion state to dress and mix spin states, combined with acoustic modulation of the optical transition energy. Instead of direct acoustic resonance with the spin splitting, spin rotations are driven parametrically via resonance with the dressed-spin splitting, creating a spin analog of "swing-up" charge-state excitation and enabling higher-harmonic processes.

Result: The scheme provides fast universal qubit control with nearly arbitrary rotation axes. The ~155 ps Pauli-X gate is ~290x faster than the previous acousto-optical formulation and ~14x faster than optical Faraday-geometry spin rotation. Numerical simulations with ~44 GHz acoustic driving show average gate fidelity >=99.9% even for uncooled nuclear-spin environments of GaAs and InAs QDs for trion lifetimes >=1.25 ns.

Conclusion: The parametric scheme offers practically usable acoustic QD spin control with high interaction rates, versatility, and multi-phonon processes, potentially enabling a spin-phonon interface essential for future acoustically coupled hybrid architectures in quantum communication and distributed computing.

Abstract: Quantum communication, distributed computing, and hybrid architectures rely on nodes enabling coherent control of qubits and coupling to propagating quantum modes.
