While semiconductor quantum-dot (QD) spins couple to microwave and optical photons, weak interaction with mechanical waves has limited the integration of single-QD spin qubits into on-chip, acoustically coupled hybrid systems. The existing theory of acoustic QD spin control suffers from a limited range of rotation-axis angles, enforcing complex realizations of gate primitives and long gate times, leaving little margin against decoherence from trion decay and quasi-static nuclear-spin noise. We propose parametric control that overcomes these problems. We use far-detuned optical coupling to a trion state to dress and thus mix spin states, combined with acoustic modulation of the optical transition energy. Instead of relying on direct acoustic resonance with the spin splitting that leads to significant bottlenecks, we induce spin rotations parametrically via resonance with the dressed-spin splitting. We thus develop a spin analog of the ``swing-up'' charge-state excitation. Our scheme provides fast universal qubit control with nearly arbitrary rotation axes. Our ${\sim}$155 ps Pauli-$X$ gate duration is ${\sim}290\times$ faster than in the previous acousto-optical formulation and ${\sim}14\times$ faster than optical Faraday-geometry spin rotation. The parametric scheme naturally enables higher-harmonic processes. Numerical simulations for ${\sim}44$ GHz acoustic driving show average gate fidelity $\ge99.9\%$ even for uncooled nuclear-spin environments of GaAs and InAs QDs for trion lifetime $\gtrsim1.25$ ns. These metrics suggest practically usable control and may introduce a spin-phonon interface with high interaction rates, versatility, and multi-phonon processes, essential for future acoustically coupled hybrid architectures.

</details>


### [120] [Gauge-covariant magnetic Bloch sums for general multiorbital Hofstadter models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05646)
*Hao Shi,Tianyu Qiao,Wangqian Miao,Jin-Tao Jin,Quansheng Wu,Xi Dai*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified gauge-covariant framework for treating the Hofstadter problem in general two-dimensional multiorbital Peierls tight-binding Hamiltonians, where magnetic Bloch sums reduce the Hamiltonian to minimal qN_orb × qN_orb blocks at rational flux, independent of fractional orbital coordinates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing treatments of the Hofstadter problem are often tailored to specific lattice geometries or orbital contents, lacking a unified framework that can handle arbitrary Bravais geometry, intracell orbital positions, hopping ranges, and orbital content simultaneously, especially when interfacing with first-principles Wannier Hamiltonians.

Method: The authors construct a gauge-covariant magnetic Bloch-sum basis that retains the full Bravais geometry, arbitrary intracell orbital positions, and original hopping table. At rational flux Φ/Φ0 = p/q, commuting magnetic translations reduce the Peierls Hamiltonian to minimal qN_orb × qN_orb blocks. An explicit sparse matrix is derived in an oblique Landau gauge, and complementary generalized Harper equations are derived from the same hopping data.

Result: The framework is validated through numerical examples including elementary lattices, topological and flat-band models, and a spinful 22-band Wannier Hamiltonian of monolayer MoS2, demonstrating direct interface with first-principles electronic-structure calculations. The construction establishes band counting, spectral redundancy, Chern-number formulation, magnetic spatial constraints, and flux periodicity.

Conclusion: The proposed treatment provides a unified, general-purpose framework for the Hofstadter problem applicable to arbitrary multiorbital tight-binding models from first-principles, with the magnetic supercell size depending only on flux through the primitive cell regardless of irrational fractional orbital coordinates, and is governed by gauge invariance within unitary conjugation.

Abstract: We formulate a unified treatment of the Hofstadter problem for general two-dimensional multiorbital Peierls tight-binding Hamiltonians using a gauge-covariant magnetic Bloch-sum basis. The construction retains the full Bravais geometry, arbitrary intracell orbital positions, and the original hopping table, so lattice geometry, orbital embedding, hopping range, and orbital content can all be handled within the same framework. At rational flux $Φ/Φ_0=p/q$, commuting magnetic translations reduce the Peierls Hamiltonian to minimal $qN_{\rm orb}\times qN_{\rm orb}$ blocks. Their dimension depends only on the flux through the primitive cell, even when the fractional orbital coordinates are irrational. Electromagnetic gauge transformations act by unitary conjugation within the construction and do not alter the required magnetic supercell. We derive an explicit sparse matrix in an oblique Landau gauge and establish the associated band counting, spectral redundancy, Chern-number formulation, magnetic spatial constraints, and flux periodicity. Numerical examples include elementary lattices, topological and flat-band models, and a spinful 22-band Wannier Hamiltonian of monolayer $\mathrm{MoS}_2$, demonstrating a direct interface with first-principles electronic-structure calculations. As a complementary representation, we also derive exact generalized Harper equations from the same hopping data and relate them to the finite magnetic-Bloch blocks.

</details>


### [121] [A magneto-mechanical gyroscope with spintronic readout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05668)
*Andrea Meo,Francesca Garesci,Pedro Bossi Nunez,Maddalena Fiorentino,Teresa Natale,Ludovico Dindelli,Victor Lopez-Dominguez,Banibrato Sinha,Riccardo Tomasello,Francesco Dell'Olio,Pedram Khalili Amiri,Mario Carpentieri,Giovanni Finocchio*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces a MEMS-like 2-DOF gyroscope that uses magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs) as both the readout mechanism and the transducer of mechanical dynamics, replacing conventional capacitive sensing. The device was calibrated using experimentally characterized MTJs and validated through an experiment-informed magneto-mechanical model combining micromagnetic theory with 2-DOF mechanical equations. Results show linear output versus angular rate with homodyne demodulation for dynamic angular rate extraction, opening a path toward a compact, CMOS-compatible readout pathway without tight capacitive gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional MEMS gyroscopes rely on capacitive sensing, which constrains dimensional scaling to the micrometer scale due to tight capacitive gap requirements. The authors seek a readout mechanism that relaxes this limitation and enables compact, CMOS-compatible designs.

Method: The authors design a 2-DOF gyroscope exploiting the rectification functionality of magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs) as both the readout mechanism and transducer of mechanical dynamics. They calibrate the model using experimentally characterized MTJs and build an experiment-informed magneto-mechanical model that couples micromagnetic theory with 2-DOF mechanical equations. Angular rate extraction is performed via a homodyne demodulation approach.

Result: The device output is linear with respect to angular rate, and it successfully extracts angular rate in dynamic cases using homodyne demodulation. The design demonstrates a viable alternative to capacitive-sensing-based MEMS gyroscopes.

Conclusion: The work establishes a proof of concept for an MTJ-based gyroscope readout that is compact and CMOS-compatible, relaxing the tight capacitive gap requirement and motivating further multi-physics device designs.

Abstract: Gyroscopes are essential elements in navigation, consumer electronics, robotics, and aerospace applications. Most micro electro-mechanical systems (MEMS) implementations rely on capacitive sensing mechanisms, which limit the dimensional scaling to the micrometer scale. In this work, we introduce a MEMS-like two-degree-of-freedom (2-DOF) gyroscope that exploits the rectification functionality of magnetic tunnel junctions (MTJs) as its readout mechanism and as the transducer of the mechanical dynamics. Experimentally characterized MTJs have been used to calibrate and perform an experiment-informed design of the magneto-mechanical model combining micromagnetic theory with 2-DOF mechanical equations. We demonstrated that the output is linear with angular rate, and that the proposed device is able to extract the angular rate in dynamic cases exploiting a homodyne demodulation approach. The results open a path towards a compact, complementary metal-oxide semiconductor (CMOS)-compatible readout pathway that relaxes reliance on tight capacitive gaps and motivates multi-physics designs of the device.

</details>


### [122] [Spectroscopy With Intelligent Feature Tracking for automated characterization of excited-state structure in semiconductor quantum dot devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05686)
*A. R. Huffman,Daniel Schug,Merritt Losert,Sanghyeok Park,Daniel King,Yuna Chun,Giordano Scappucci,M. A. Eriksson,Justyna P. Zwolak*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: SWIFT is a machine-learning framework that combines feature identification with physics-informed geometric processing to automatically extract energy-level splittings from pulsed-gate spectroscopy data in semiconductor quantum dot devices. It reduces 2D spectroscopy analysis to 1D peak detection, uses ensemble-based confidence metrics with sequential low-SNR scan accumulation, and achieves a median splitting error of 0.05 mV (vs. 0.11 mV for classical baseline) on Si/SiGe devices with 255 manually labeled scans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterizing excited-state structure in quantum dot devices is essential for tuning them toward spin-qubit operation, but traditional manual spectroscopic analysis is time-consuming and error-prone, especially at low signal-to-noise ratios. As quantum dot devices scale up, autonomous characterization and tuning become necessary.

Method: SWIFT combines ML-assisted feature identification with physics-informed geometric processing. It isolates relevant spectral features and exploits their characteristic geometry to reduce 2D spectroscopy analysis to a 1D peak-detection problem. It uses an ensemble-based confidence metric alongside sequential accumulation of rapid, low-SNR scans so the inferred spectrum is continuously reevaluated as data accumulates. Demonstrated both offline and in real time on Si/SiGe quantum dot devices.

Result: On 255 manually labeled scans, SWIFT reduces the median splitting error to 0.05 mV from a 0.11 mV classical baseline, with the largest improvements on lower-quality measurements. SWIFT successfully enables automated tracking of QD excited states and lead resonances in real time.

Conclusion: SWIFT provides a viable path toward incorporating excited-state spectroscopy into autonomous quantum dot characterization, tuning, and optimization pipelines, which will be essential for large-scale quantum dot devices.

Abstract: The characterization of excited-state structure in semiconductor quantum dot (QD) devices is an important component of tuning them for spin-qubit operation. We present Spectroscopy With Intelligent Feature Tracking (SWIFT), a framework that combines machine-learning (ML)-assisted feature identification with physics-informed geometric processing to extract energy-level splittings from pulsed-gate spectroscopy data. SWIFT isolates the relevant spectral features and exploits their characteristic geometry to reduce the two-dimensional spectroscopy analysis to a one-dimensional peak-detection problem. It further combines an ensemble-based confidence metric with sequential accumulation of rapid, low-SNR scans, allowing the inferred spectrum to be reevaluated as experimental evidence accumulates. Using Si/SiGe QD devices, we demonstrate SWIFT both offline and in real time, including automated tracking of QD excited states and lead resonances. Benchmarking on 255 manually labeled scans shows that SWIFT reduces the median splitting error to 0.05 mV from 0.11 mV for a classical baseline, with the largest improvement on lower-quality measurements. These results provide a path toward incorporating excited-state spectroscopy into autonomous QD characterization, tuning, and optimization, which will be essential in large-scale quantum dot devices.

</details>


### [123] [Large Nernst effect in chemically derived multilayer graphene at millitesla magnetic fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05898)
*Valentin Semkin,Denis Borisenko,Yana Litun,Oleg Kononenko,Dmitry Mylnikov,Alexey Bocharov,Dmitry Svintsov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reports large transverse (Nernst) thermopower in chemically derived multilayer graphene films with strong thickness and carrier density fluctuations at low magnetic fields. At B*≈4 mT, the Nernst voltage becomes comparable to longitudinal thermoelectric voltage at metal-doped graphene contacts, with transverse thermopower reaching ~10 μV/K at 4 mT and rising sub-linearly to 250 μV/K at 315 mT using a permanent magnet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphite-based thermoelectric generators are attractive due to their simple synthesis, high conductivity, and integrability with other materials. However, the semimetallic nature of graphite and graphene causes electron-hole compensation leading to nearly-zero thermopower, motivating exploration of alternative mechanisms to generate thermoelectric voltage.

Method: The authors used scanning laser-induced heating of macroscopic chemically derived multilayer graphene films with strong fluctuations in thickness and carrier density. They measured transverse (Nernst) thermoelectric voltage at low magnetic fields generated by a centimeter-sized permanent magnet, comparing it to the longitudinal thermoelectric voltage at metal-doped graphene contacts.

Result: Transverse Nernst thermopower becomes comparable to longitudinal thermoelectric voltage at B*≈4 mT under ambient conditions. The transverse thermopower S_xy is ~10 μV/K at 4 mT and rises sub-linearly to 250 μV/K at 315 mT (maximum field of the permanent magnet). Additional enhancement is achieved when dc field lines are co-directional with the local Nernst current during voltage measurement at Hall probes.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the promise of large-scale multilayer graphene for thermoelectricity generation, showing that engineered disorder in chemical vapor-deposited graphene films can overcome the electron-hole compensation problem and produce substantial transverse thermopower at low magnetic fields achievable with permanent magnets.

Abstract: Simple synthesis of graphitic compounds, their high conductivity, and integrability with other materials motivate the effort toward graphite-based thermoelectric generators. At the same time, semimetallic nature of graphite and graphene results in nearly-zero thermopower due to electron-hole compensation. Here, we observe large transverse thermopower in chemically derived multilayer graphene films with strong fluctuations of thickness and carrier density at low magnetic fields $B$. Using the scanning laser-induced heating of macroscopic film, we find that transverse (Nernst) thermoelectric voltage becomes comparable to the longitudinal thermoelectric voltage at the metal-doped graphene contact at $B^*\approx4$ mT and ambient conditions. Estimates of transverse thermopower $S_{xy}$ based on the known laser-induced temperature show that it is as large as $\sim 10$ $μ$V/K at $B^*$, and raises in a sub-linear fashion to 250 $μ$V/K at $B\approx315$ mT, the maximum field we reach with centimeter-sized permanent magnet. Extra increase in thermoelectric signal is achieved upon voltage measurement at Hall probes when the dc field lines are co-directional with local Nernst current. Our results show the promise of large-scale multilayer graphene for thermoelectricity generation.

</details>


### [124] [Static and Ensemble-Dependent Thermodynamics of the Strain-Induced Parity Anomaly in Gapped Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05999)
*Ara Sedrakyan,Klaus Ziegler*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how a pseudomagnetic field (from static deformation) in sublattice-gapped graphene produces a valley-odd field that survives in parity-odd responses. The authors derive equilibrium thermodynamics, showing that at fixed electrochemical potential, reversing the pseudomagnetic field removes pseudo-Landau levels leaving a grand-potential difference set by the spectrally asymmetric zeroth level. The static charge response is a thermally broadened plateau (no metallic m/|μ| tail). Near band edges, pseudofield reversal transfers B·ln2 entropy per zero-mode state. The fixed-μ heat capacity shows side lobes and a universal peak of 0.439229 D_B k_B. They formulate a constant-gate-voltage circuit where measured sheet heat capacity depends on boundary conditions, find -2(ln2)² D_B k_B at fixed carrier number in the low-temperature edge limit, and propose continuous interpolation via finite geometric capacitance. A trace-free triaxial strain geometry and realistic experimental scales are provided, isolating an equilibrium electromechanical anomaly without real magnetic fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and isolate the equilibrium thermodynamic signatures of pseudomagnetic fields in sublattice-gapped graphene—an electromechanical anomaly accessible without real magnetic fields—and to separate these from finite-frequency transport, while clarifying the role of electrical boundary conditions in measurements.

Method: The authors combine continuum massive-Dirac theory with exact finite-field pseudo-Landau-level calculations. They derive equilibrium grand potentials, entropy, and heat capacity under pseudofield reversal, distinguishing fixed-μ vs. fixed-carrier-number (gate) conditions, and incorporate geometric capacitance to model boundary conditions.

Result: At fixed μ, reversing the pseudomagnetic field eliminates nonzero pseudo-Landau levels, leaving a difference determined by the asymmetric zeroth level. Charge response is a thermally broadened plateau confined to the gap without metallic tail. At band edges, pseudofield reversal transfers B ln2 entropy per zero mode. Fixed-μ heat capacity has two side lobes per edge and universal peak 0.439229 D_B k_B. At fixed carrier number, the low-temperature edge value is -2(ln2)² D_B k_B rather than a node; finite geometric capacitance interpolates continuously. Both the massive-Dirac density of states and exact finite-field calculations agree in their common limit.

Conclusion: The pseudomagnetic-field reversal protocol provides a clean route to isolate an equilibrium electromechanical anomaly in gapped graphene without a real magnetic field, with measurable calorimetric and quantum-capacitance signatures that depend sensitively on electrical boundary conditions.

Abstract: A static deformation of graphene can act on its Dirac electrons as a valley-odd magnetic field. In sublattice-gapped graphene this field makes the two valleys add in the parity-odd response rather than cancel. We derive the equilibrium thermodynamics of this effect and separate it from the finite-frequency transport response. At fixed electrochemical potential, reversing the pseudomagnetic field removes every nonzero pseudo-Landau level in the continuum theory. The remaining grand-potential difference is fixed by the spectrally asymmetric zeroth level. The static charge response is a thermally broadened plateau confined to the gap and has no metallic $m/|μ|$ tail. Near a band edge, pseudofield reversal transfers $\mathrm{B}\ln2$ of entropy per unsplit zero-mode state in the low-temperature window. The fixed-$μ$ heat capacity has two side lobes per edge and a universal peak $0.439229\,D_B k_{\mathrm B}$. We then formulate a definite constant-gate-voltage circuit and show that the measured sheet heat capacity depends on the electrical boundary condition. The full massive-Dirac density of states and an exact finite-field pseudo-Landau-level calculation give the same gate crossover in their common limit. At fixed carrier number, the low-temperature edge value is $-2(\ln2)^2 D_B k_{\mathrm B}$, rather than a node. A finite geometric capacitance gives a continuous and experimentally tunable interpolation. Finally, we give a trace-free triaxial strain geometry, a disorder--interaction window, and realistic calorimetric and quantum-capacitance scales. The field-reversal protocol isolates an equilibrium electromechanical anomaly without a real magnetic field.

</details>


### [125] [One-dimensional quasicrystals with tensor-network finite-state automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06040)
*Milla Kolehmainen,Jose L. Lado,Anouar Moustaj*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified framework that links substitution rules for quasicrystals to deterministic finite automata and matrix product states, enabling the exact construction of matrix product operators for quasicrystal Hamiltonians at any system size. This allows efficient computation of spectral densities for chains exceeding 10^9 sites via tensor-network compression and the kernel polynomial method, resolving the hierarchical spectral structure of quasicrystals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating quasicrystals has been challenging because their quasiperiodic structures lack translational symmetry (unlike crystals) and typically require costly diagonalization of large finite approximants. The authors aim to overcome this computational bottleneck by exploiting the substitution-rule description of quasicrystalline order.

Method: The authors show that substitution rules written in an adapted numeration system define a deterministic finite automaton with output (DFAO): digits of a site index are fed in and the automaton returns the occupying letter. They further exploit the equivalence to matrix product states, where transition matrices of the substitution become tensor network tensors with bond dimension equal to the number of automaton states (independent of system size). This yields exact matrix product operators (MPOs) for quasicrystal tight-binding Hamiltonians. The framework is demonstrated on metallic-mean and k-bonacci families (Fibonacci, silver-mean, Tribonacci). Spectral densities are computed using tensor-network compression and the kernel polynomial method.

Result: The framework enables exact representation of quasicrystal Hamiltonians as MPOs independent of system size. Spectral densities are computed for chains exceeding 10^9 sites, directly resolving the hierarchical (fractal) structure of the spectrum.

Conclusion: The substitution-rule description of quasicrystals can be efficiently leveraged via automata theory and tensor networks to enable large-scale simulations of quasiperiodic systems that were previously computationally prohibitive, opening new avenues for studying quasicrystal physics.

Abstract: Quasicrystals occupy a distinctive position between the translational order of crystals and the disordered amorphous matter. Simulating this physics has remained challenging, since quasiperiodic structures lack the translational symmetry exploited for crystals and generally require costly diagonalization of large finite approximants. Quasicrystalline order admits two equivalent descriptions, a cut-and-project scheme from a higher-dimensional periodic crystal, and a discrete set of substitution rules acting on a finite alphabet. We show that the latter, written in a numeration system adapted to the substitution, defines a deterministic finite automaton with output, the digits of a site index are fed, and the automaton returns the letter occupying that site. We further exploit another equivalence to a different construction, the transition matrices are exactly the tensors of a matrix product state, whose bond dimension is the number of automaton states and is independent of system size. This allows efficient representation of extremely large tight-binding Hamiltonians in the tensor-train language, thereby yielding an exact matrix product operator for the quasicrystal Hamiltonian at any system size. We show how this framework works for two families of one-dimensional quasicrystals, the metallic-mean and $k$-bonacci families and we explicitly construct the Fibonacci, silver-mean, and Tribonacci quasicrystals. By leveraging efficient tensor-network compression and the kernel polynomial method, we compute spectral densities for chains with more than $10^9$ sites and directly resolve the hierarchical structure of the spectrum.

</details>


### [126] [Purely Electric-Field Control of Topological Magnetism in Two-Dimensional Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06215)
*Zhonglin He,Naafis Ahnaf Shahed,Kai Huang,Himanshu Mavani,Evgeny Y. Tsymbal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper establishes a general approach for purely electric-field control of topological magnetism in 2D van der Waals magnetic semiconductors via electrostatic doping of an orbital-angular-momentum-polarized band edge, enabling reversible switching between ferromagnetic, skyrmion, spiral, and bimeron phases without external magnetic fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing electric-field approaches to control topological magnetism suffer from nonselective rearrangement of low-energy electronic states, leading to coarse magnetic phase control that often requires external magnetic fields. Few purely electric-field schemes are restricted to specific conducting materials, limiting their generality.

Method: The approach applies an electric field to electrostatically dope a selected orbital-angular-momentum-polarized band edge of a 2D vdW magnetic semiconductor through proximity to an adjacent nonmagnetic vdW metal. This doping selectively tunes magnetic anisotropy while leaving exchange and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions nearly unchanged.

Result: The mechanism reversibly drives systems through ferromagnetic, skyrmion, spiral, and bimeron phases. It is demonstrated for CrBr3/graphene and Cr2Ge2Te6/TaS2 vdW heterostructures with different orbital characters of electron and hole pockets.

Conclusion: This work provides a broadly applicable, purely electric-field strategy for topological spintronics, removing the need for external magnetic fields and expanding beyond conducting-material-specific limitations.

Abstract: Electrical control of topological magnetism is central to realizing energy-efficient topological spintronics. Yet most electric-field approaches modify the competing magnetic interactions through a nonselective rearrangement of low-energy electronic states, yielding coarse magnetic phase control that often requires external magnetic fields to stabilize topological quasiparticles, while few schemes solely based on electric fields are restricted to specific conducting materials. Here, we establish a general approach for purely electric-field control of topological magnetism, in which an applied electric field electrostatically dopes a selected orbital-angular-momentum-polarized band edge of a two-dimensional (2D) van der Waals (vdW) magnetic semiconductor via proximity to an adjacent nonmagnetic vdW metal. We show that the resulting electrostatic doping predominantly tunes magnetic anisotropy of the 2D magnet, while leaving exchange interaction and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction nearly unchanged, thereby reversibly driving the system through ferromagnetic, skyrmion, spiral, and bimeron phases. We demonstrate this mechanism for CrBr3/graphene and Cr2Ge2Te6/TaS2 vdW heterostructures hosting electron and hole pockets of different orbital characters. These results establish a broadly applicable strategy for purely electric-field control in topological spintronics.

</details>


### [127] [Continuum Limit of Nonlocal Electrostatics in Random Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06354)
*Prashant K. Jha,Kaushik Dayal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A two-scale continuum limit is derived for the electrostatic energy of stationary, ergodic random charge density fields on a lattice. Under charge-neutral microscopic cells with possibly fluctuating dipole moments, local and nonlocal energies converge almost surely to deterministic limits. The total energy comprises ensemble-averaged microscopic cell energy plus macroscopic electrostatic field energy, recovering periodic two-scale limits in the deterministic case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously derive a continuum limit for electrostatic energy of random charge distributions that are stationary and ergodic under lattice translations, generalizing periodic homogenization results to stochastic settings where microscopic cells may have fluctuating dipole moments with nonzero mean.

Method: The paper constructs a two-scale asymptotic framework for the Coulomb energy of random charge density fields, assuming stationarity and ergodicity under lattice translations, charge neutrality per microscopic cell, and moment conditions on the microscopic potential and finite-volume fields. An almost-sure convergence argument separates local (microscopic cell Coulomb plus depolarization) and nonlocal (effective polarization-driven) energy terms.

Result: Local and nonlocal energies converge almost surely to deterministic limits. Cell-depolarization terms cancel in the total energy, yielding ensemble-averaged microscopic energy plus macroscopic field energy. The deterministic case recovers the periodic two-scale limit. In an independent-cell random-displacement model, centered microscopic fluctuations alter the local energy but not the effective polarization or nonlocal energy.

Conclusion: The work establishes a rigorous stochastic homogenization result for electrostatic energy, showing that random microscopic fluctuations can affect local energetics without modifying macroscopic polarization-driven behavior, thus providing a unified framework that extends periodic two-scale limits to ergodic random media.

Abstract: We derive a two-scale continuum limit for the electrostatic energy of random charge density fields that are stationary and ergodic under lattice translations. Each microscopic cell is charge neutral in every realization, while its dipole moment may fluctuate and have a nonzero mean. Under assumptions on the stationary microscopic potential and the finite-volume fields, the local and nonlocal energies converge almost surely to deterministic limits. The local limit consists of the ensemble-averaged microscopic Coulomb energy and a cell-depolarization term, whereas the effective polarization determines the nonlocal limit. The cell-depolarization terms cancel in the total energy, which consists of the ensemble-averaged microscopic cell energy and the macroscopic electrostatic field energy. The deterministic specialization recovers the periodic two-scale limit. For an independent-cell random-displacement model, we verify the assumptions of the continuum-limit theorem and show that centered microscopic fluctuations can change the local energy without changing the effective polarization or the nonlocal energy.

</details>


### [128] [Near-Field Spin Seebeck Effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06411)
*Gaomin Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper establishes a photon-mediated spin Seebeck effect driven by near-field thermal radiation, where Rashba spin-orbit coupling in a monolayer transition metal dichalcogenide converts optical orbital excitations into electron spin polarization via two complementary mechanisms, unveiling a radiative pathway for nanoscale spin control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the conventional spin Seebeck effect—which relies on interfacial thermal conduction—to a radiative regime where thermal photons mediate spin generation, enabling contactless nanoscale spin manipulation.

Method: A monolayer transition metal dichalcogenide is separated from a thermal emitter by a vacuum gap, and Rashba spin-orbit coupling is exploited to convert optical orbital excitations into electron spin polarization through two mechanisms: direct angular momentum transfer from chiral thermal photons and rectification of unpolarized thermal fluctuations by a magnetized two-dimensional electron gas.

Result: Demonstration of a photon-mediated spin Seebeck effect wherein both chiral photon angular momentum transfer and magnetization-based rectification of thermal fluctuations generate spin polarization in the 2D material.

Conclusion: Near-field thermal radiation provides a new radiative pathway for manipulating electron spin at the nanoscale, expanding spin Seebeck physics beyond phonon/electron-mediated interfacial conduction.

Abstract: The conventional spin Seebeck effect generates spin currents through interfacial thermal conduction. In this Letter, we establish a photon-mediated spin Seebeck effect driven by near-field thermal radiation. Using a monolayer transition metal dichalcogenide separated from a thermal emitter by a vacuum gap, we demonstrate Rashba spin-orbit coupling can convert optical orbital excitations into an electron spin polarization. This occurs via two complementary mechanisms: the direct transfer of angular momentum from chiral thermal photons, and the rectification of unpolarized thermal fluctuations by a magnetized two-dimensional electron gas. These findings unveil a radiative pathway for nanoscale electron spin manipulation.

</details>


### [129] [Emergent Chiral Metal Phase in Compressible Quantum Hall Fluids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06532)
*Suparna Sahoo,Suvankar Purkait,Pooja Agarwal,Tanmay Maiti,Sourin Das,Vladimir Umansky,Biswajit Karmakar*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reports conductance measurements showing that the total sum of transmitted conductances in a compressible quantum Hall fluid universally approaches the Hall conductance ν(e²/h), independent of temperature, geometry, material quality, and interactions. This sum rule holds across integer and fractional quantum Hall regimes and is theoretically explained through chiral transport arising from time-reversal symmetry breaking in a nearly flat potential landscape with point-like disorder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a universal conduction law for the chiral metal phase in compressible quantum Hall fluids and to understand the microscopic origin of chiral transport in gapless 2D systems under strong magnetic fields.

Method: Transmitted conductance measurements between a source and reflection-less contacts connected to a compressible quantum Hall fluid, including floating contact measurements to confirm chiral transport. Theoretical analysis argues that time-reversal-symmetry breaking under a strong magnetic field creates unidirectional carrier trajectories in a nearly flat potential landscape with point-like disorder, valid when the screening length is smaller than the magnetic length.

Result: The sum of transmitted conductances universally approaches ν(e²/h) across both integer and fractional quantum Hall regimes, invariant under variations in temperature, sample geometry, material quality, and quasi-particle interactions. Floating contact measurements confirm chiral transport characterized by suppressed dissipative transport with distinct handedness. Longitudinal resistance originates from contact equilibration rather than bulk dissipation.

Conclusion: The sum rule constitutes a fundamental conduction law of the chiral metal phase, introducing a new paradigm for chiral transport applicable to a broad class of gapless two-dimensional systems with short-range screening and broken time-reversal symmetry.

Abstract: We report transmitted conductance measurements between a source and reflection-less contacts connected to a compressible quantum Hall fluid with filling fraction $ν$. We observe that total sum of transmitted conductances universally approaches Hall conductance $ν(e^2/h)$. The universality of this sum rule is established experimentally across integer and fractional quantum Hall regimes, remaining invariant under variation in temperatures, sample geometries, material qualities and quasi-particle interactions. Chiral transport in compressible quantum Hall fluids, characterized by suppressed dissipative transport with a distinct handedness, is confirmed by floating contact measurements. Consequently, this sum rule emerges as a conduction law of the chiral metal phase. Theoretically, we argue that time-reversal-symmetry breaking under a strong magnetic field within this chiral metal gives rise unidirectional trajectories of carriers in a nearly flat potential landscape with point-like disorder potentials, a regime realized when the screening length is smaller than the magnetic length. Within this chiral framework, longitudinal resistance does not originate from bulk dissipation but primarily from the equilibration of electrochemical potentials at the contacts. Our study introduces a new paradigm of chiral transport across a broad class of gapless two-dimensional systems characterized by short-range screening and broken time-reversal symmetry.

</details>


### [130] [Three-Dimensional Fermiology and Thickness-Tuned Magnetotransport in Single-Crystalline Antimony Flakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06561)
*Mikhail Gaponov,Jicheng Wang,Liang Zha,Rui Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates extreme magnetoresistance in single-crystalline Sb flakes grown by CVD with thicknesses from 110 to 783 nm. As thickness increases, MR at 2 K and 14 T rises nearly 30-fold to 7.13×10⁵%, while the primary quantum-oscillation frequency Fα stays ~99 T. A joint three-channel analysis of ρxx and ρxy shows the evolution is driven by increased mobility of a nearly compensated electron–hole pair, not Fermi-surface reconstruction. Results demonstrate that thickness tunes extreme MR through dimension-dependent scattering while preserving bulk-like fermiology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The extreme magnetoresistance in compensated semimetals arises from both Fermi-surface geometry and carrier relaxation, but in finite-size structures these contributions are difficult to disentangle. The authors aim to separately identify how thickness affects these two factors using Sb flakes.

Method: Single-crystalline Sb flakes of varying thickness (110–783 nm) were grown by chemical vapor deposition. Longitudinal and Hall magnetotransport measurements were combined with temperature- and angle-dependent Shubnikov–de Haas oscillations. A joint three-channel analysis of ρxx(B) and ρxy(B) was used to decompose carrier contributions, along with angle-dependent measurements to probe Fermi-surface topology.

Result: Non-saturating MR at 2 K and 14 T increased nearly 30-fold with thickness, reaching 7.13×10⁵%. The primary frequency Fα remained ~99 T without systematic shift, while a three-channel analysis revealed the MR evolution stems from increased mobility of a nearly compensated electron–hole pair. A closed 3D α pocket and a high-frequency sector (335–377 T) consistent with the electron β orbit of L-point pockets in bulk Sb were confirmed.

Conclusion: Thickness tunes extreme magnetoresistance through dimension-dependent scattering while preserving bulk-like fermiology—i.e., the Fermi surface remains unchanged, and the MR enhancement is governed by carrier mobility variation, not pocket reconstruction.

Abstract: The extreme magnetoresistance of compensated semimetals is governed by both the Fermi-surface geometry and carrier relaxation, but these contributions are difficult to disentangle in finite-size structures. Here, we combine longitudinal and Hall magnetotransport measurements with temperature- and angle-dependent Shubnikov--de Haas oscillations in single-crystalline Sb flakes grown by chemical vapor deposition (CVD), with thicknesses ranging from 110 to 783 nm. As thickness increases, the non-saturating MR at 2 K and 14 T rises nearly 30-fold, reaching $7.13\times10^{5}\%$, while the primary frequency $F_α$ remains approximately 99 T without any systematic shift. A joint three-channel analysis of $ρ_{xx}(B)$ and $ρ_{xy}(B)$ reveals that this evolution is driven by an increase in the mobility of a nearly compensated electron--hole pair, rather than by a reconstruction of the primary pockets. Angle-dependent measurements confirm the existence of a closed three-dimensional $α$ pocket, and a reproducible high-frequency sector (335-377 T) is consistent with the electron $β$ orbit of the $L$-point pockets in bulk Sb. Together, the transport and quantum-oscillation results show that thickness tunes extreme MR through dimension-dependent scattering while preserving bulk-like fermiology.

</details>


### [131] [Simple invariants for band topology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07775)
*Adam Yanis Chaou,Adolfo G. Grushin,Peru d'Ornellas*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a systematic framework using the spectral localizer operator—treated as an auxiliary zero-dimensional Hamiltonian—to construct simple, numerically efficient topological invariants for any non-interacting topological state of matter, applicable to both crystalline and non-crystalline systems. Every topological invariant is reduced to a matrix signature or the sign of a Pfaffian computable in real space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite thorough understanding of non-interacting topological states, no single systematic method exists that delivers simple, numerically efficient topological invariants applicable uniformly to all crystalline and non-crystalline systems.

Method: The authors revisit the spectral localizer operator constructed from Hamiltonian and position operators, treating it as an auxiliary zero-dimensional Hamiltonian whose topology encodes the higher-dimensional phases of the parent Hamiltonian. This reduces every topological invariant to a matrix signature or Pfaffian sign for an appropriate localizer, both interpretable and efficiently computable in real space.

Result: The approach is validated by deriving simple real-space invariants for weak and rotationally invariant crystalline phases previously beyond the spectral localizer formalism, atomic limits escaping scattering invariants, phases evading symmetry-based indicator methods, and phases with no previously known invariant.

Conclusion: The work provides a systematic way to construct any non-interacting topological invariant for crystalline or non-crystalline systems, opening avenues to classify and predict topology of previously unexplored classes of materials.

Abstract: Despite the exhaustive understanding gathered around non-interacting topological states of matter, there is no single method capable of systematically delivering simple, numerically efficient topological invariants that is applicable to all crystalline and non-crystalline systems alike. Here we revisit the spectral localizer operator, constructed from the Hamiltonian and position operators, and show how it can be treated it as an auxiliary zero-dimensional Hamiltonian whose topology encodes the higher dimensional phases of the parent Hamiltonian. Its classification reduces every topological invariant to a matrix signature or the sign of a Pfaffian for an appropriate localizer, both of which are simple to interpret and efficient to compute in real space. We validate this approach by deriving simple real-space invariants for weak and rotationally invariant crystalline phases that were previously beyond the grasp of the spectral localizer formalism, atomic limits that escape scattering invariants, phases that evade symmetry-based indicator methods, as well as phases that had no previously known invariant. Our work provides a systematic way to construct any non-interacting topological invariant for a crystalline or non-crystalline systems, opening avenues to classify and predict the topology of previously unexplored classes of materials.

</details>


### [132] [Evidence of Haldane-Chain Physics in an Fe-Dehydroindigo Coordination Polymer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06684)
*Ritam Chakraborty,Shobhana Narasimhan,T. V. Ramakrishnan*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: An Fe–dehydroindigo spin-crossover coordination polymer is investigated for Haldane-chain physics using DFT, exact diagonalization, and DMRG calculations. A nearly isotropic antiferromagnetic coupling (JH=32.206 meV) with weak single-ion anisotropy yields a nondegenerate ground state with a ~13–14 meV spin gap, consistent with spin-1 Haldane physics and placing the system in a gapped Haldane regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether an Fe–dehydroindigo spin-crossover coordination polymer can support intrinsic Haldane-chain physics in its substrate-free limit, providing a material-specific platform for studying this quantum phenomenon.

Method: Spin-orbit-coupled density-functional-theory (DFT) calculations combined with magnetic energy mapping to extract magnetic exchange couplings and single-ion anisotropy, followed by exact diagonalization and density-matrix renormalization group (DMRG) calculations to characterize the ground state, spin gap, and dynamical spin structure factor.

Result: A nearly isotropic antiferromagnetic coupling JH=32.206 meV with much weaker single-ion anisotropy was found. The calculations yield a nondegenerate ground state with a ~13–14 meV gap to triplet excitations, on the scale expected for the spin-1 Haldane gap. Anisotropy causes only small splitting of low-energy modes, and the lowest dynamical spectral weight sits near q=π.

Conclusion: The isolated cis-dehydroindigo chain represents a material-specific coordination-polymer platform whose microscopic magnetic interactions place it in a gapped regime consistent with Haldane-chain physics.

Abstract: We investigate whether an Fe--dehydroindigo spin-crossover coordination polymer can support Haldane-chain physics in its intrinsic, substrate-free limit. Spin-orbit-coupled density-functional-theory calculations and magnetic energy mapping yield a nearly isotropic antiferromagnetic coupling, $J_{\mathrm H}=32.206$~meV, together with much weaker single-ion anisotropy. Using these parameters, exact diagonalization and density-matrix renormalization group calculations give a nondegenerate ground state separated from the lowest triplet-derived excitations by a gap of about $13$--$14$~meV, on the scale expected for the spin-1 Haldane gap. The anisotropy produces only a small splitting of the low-energy modes. The dynamical spin structure factor places the lowest spectral weight near the antiferromagnetic wave vector $q=π$. These results identify the isolated cis-dehydroindigo chain as a material-specific coordination-polymer platform whose microscopic interactions place it in a gapped regime consistent with Haldane-chain physics.

</details>


### [133] [Divergent Orbital Diamagnetism from Chiral Edge States in Chern Insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09065)
*Nobuyuki Okuma*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Chern insulators under open boundary conditions exhibit a giant, disorder-robust diamagnetic susceptibility that diverges linearly with system size, analogous to the divergent orbital diamagnetism of massless Dirac systems like graphene. This reveals the topological origin of the effect and suggests a deep connection between the two phenomena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-dimensional massless Dirac systems (e.g., graphene) show divergent orbital diamagnetic susceptibility scaling linearly with system size. The authors seek to determine whether a similar divergent diamagnetism exists in topologically nontrivial systems, motivated by conceptualizing a chiral edge state as half of an enlarged benzene-ring analogue.

Method: The authors study Chern insulators under open boundary conditions and compute the diamagnetic susceptibility, drawing an analogy between chiral edge states and half of an enlarged benzene ring structure.

Result: Chern insulators under open boundaries exhibit the same divergent scaling of diamagnetic susceptibility (linear in system size) as massless Dirac systems. This giant diamagnetism is robust against disorder, confirming its topological character.

Conclusion: The divergent diamagnetism found in Chern insulators is topologically protected and robust to disorder, and it reveals a profound connection between the giant diamagnetic response of Chern insulators and that of massless Dirac systems.

Abstract: Two-dimensional massless Dirac systems, exemplified by graphene, are known to exhibit a divergent orbital diamagnetic susceptibility that scales linearly with system size. Motivated by viewing a chiral edge state as one half of an enlarged analogue of a benzene ring, we study Chern insulators under open boundary conditions and find the same divergent scaling. This giant diamagnetism is robust against disorder, revealing its topological nature. Our results further suggest a profound connection to the divergent diamagnetism of massless Dirac systems.

</details>


### [134] [Self-Trapping Enabled Highly Bright Momentum-Indirect Interlayer Excitons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06927)
*Dong Yang,Zisheng Gong,Hao Wen,Yue Hu,Kaichen Jiang,Baixu Xiang,Weibo Gao,Qihua Xiong,Dehui Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates highly bright momentum-indirect interlayer exciton (IX) emission in heterostructures of 2D perovskites and monolayer TMDs, achieving an average quantum yield of 35.2% (up to >60%), over 50× higher than constituent TMD monolayers and two orders of magnitude more efficient than momentum-direct IXs in TMD-based heterostructures. The enhanced emission is attributed to IX self-trapping from strong exciton-phonon coupling in the soft-lattice 2D perovskite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interlayer excitons (IXs) in 2D heterostructures have large binding energies and long lifetimes, but their spatial electron-hole separation reduces oscillator strength by ~100× relative to intralayer excitons. Achieving high-efficiency IX emission requires optimal material selection with momentum matching and careful device fabrication, which remains challenging.

Method: The authors fabricate heterostructures combining 2D perovskites with monolayer transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs), achieving momentum-indirect interlayer exciton emission. They characterize the quantum yield of the IX emission and compare it with constituent TMD monolayers and momentum-direct IXs in TMD-based heterostructures, analyzing the underlying mechanism via exciton-phonon coupling in the soft perovskite lattice.

Result: The IX emission quantum yield reaches 35.2% on average (exceeding 60% at best), which is over 50 times higher than the corresponding TMD monolayer. The momentum-indirect IX radiative recombination efficiency surpasses momentum-direct IXs in monolayer TMD-based heterostructures by two orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: The bright IX emission is attributed to IX self-trapping induced by strong exciton-phonon coupling arising from the soft lattice of the 2D perovskite. These findings offer new insights into achieving high IX emission efficiency and open new avenues for long-lifetime excitonic devices.

Abstract: Interlayer excitons in two dimensional material heterostructures exhibit large exciton binding energies and long lifetimes, making them ideal platforms for studying excitonic devices and many body quantum phenomena. However, the spatially separated electron and hole nature of IXs reduces their oscillator strength by two orders of magnitude compared to intralayer excitons. Achieving high efficiency IX emission remains challenging and requires optimal material selection with appropriate momentum matching and meticulous device fabrication. Here we demonstrate a highly bright momentum indirect IX emission within heterostructures formed between 2D perovskites and monolayer transition metal dichalcogenides. The quantum yield of IX emission reaches 35.2% on average, over 50 times higher than that of the corresponding constituent TMD monolayer, with the highest value exceeding 60%. Notably, the radiative recombination efficiency of this momentum indirect IX exceeds that of momentum direct IXs in monolayer TMD-based heterostructures by two orders of magnitude. We suggest that the remarkably bright IX emission in our heterostructure originates from IX self trapping, induced by strong exciton phonon coupling arising from the soft lattice nature of the 2D perovskite. Our findings provide new insights into achieving high IX emission efficiency and open new avenues for exploring long lifetime excitonic devices.

</details>


### [135] [Topological Impurity Bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09118)
*Arnab Seth,Itamar Kimchi*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that a finite density of randomly distributed localized defects can generate new topological phase transitions through the formation of topological impurity bands (TIB), applicable even when the clean system is a trivial band insulator. This disorder-induced topology arises from an orbital-dependent vorticity of impurity bound states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional studies treat disorder as either preserving or destroying topology, with topological Anderson insulators (TAI) representing disorder-induced enlargement of existing topological phases. However, these approaches rely on perturbatively averaging out long-wavelength randomness, potentially missing new physics that arises when disorder involves localized defects rather than smooth perturbations.

Method: The authors develop an analytical theory based on the orbital-dependent vorticity of impurity bound states, derived near a generic Dirac cone phase transition and validated using Chern insulator lattice models (Qi-Wu-Zhang and Haldane models). They compute numerical phase diagrams at 3% defect density to characterize the resulting topological phases.

Result: The numerical phase diagrams at 3% defect density reveal robust topological transitions occurring in distinct TIB and TAI regimes, demonstrating that localized defects can induce new topological phase transitions not captured by conventional treatments.

Conclusion: A finite density of random localized defects can generate topological phases through TIB formation—a local route to topology that works even from a trivial band insulator starting point, complementing and extending the existing disorder-induced topology paradigm.

Abstract: The effects of disorder on topological phases of matter are typically either preservation of topology or its destruction. For example, the quantum Hall effect's quantization and chiral edge mode remain robust until a strong-disorder percolation transition exits the topological phase. Disorder can also enlarge a preexisting topological phase, yielding a topological Anderson insulator (TAI). However, the reliance of TAI and other theoretical treatments on perturbatively averaging out long-wavelength randomness suggests that additional effects might appear when the disorder involves localized defects. Here we show that a finite density of randomly distributed defects can generate various new topological phase transitions. This route to disorder-induced topology can be viewed as the formation of topological impurity bands (TIB) and is applicable even if the initial clean model is a trivial band insulator. The theory relies on an orbital-dependent vorticity of impurity bound states, which we derive analytically near a generic Dirac cone phase transition as well as using the Chern insulator lattice models of Qi-Wu-Zhang and Haldane. Their numerically computed phase diagrams at 3% defect density show robust topological transitions in distinct TIB and TAI regimes, suggesting a local route to topological phases.

</details>


### [136] [Theory of staggered damping-like spin-orbit torque on synthetic antiferromagnetic domain walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07055)
*Yuta Yamane,Takeshi Seki*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a theory for staggered damping-like spin-orbit torque (SOT) that emerges generically in synthetic antiferromagnets with symmetric heavy-metal layers, deriving closed-form domain-wall velocity expressions and predicting experimental signatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Néel (staggered) SOT enables efficient electrical control of antiferromagnetic order but has been limited to special crystal structures and only field-like components. This motivates searching for a more generic mechanism, particularly the missing damping-like component.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework by deriving a Néel-vector Lagrangian for synthetic antiferromagnets sandwiched between identical heavy-metal layers, from which they obtain closed-form domain-wall velocity expressions. Micromagnetic simulations are used to confirm the analytical predictions.

Result: The domain-wall velocity is found to be bilinear in current and in-plane field, opposite in sign for Néel versus Bloch walls, and tunable via interlayer exchange coupling. Predicted sign reversals, scaling laws, and Néel-Bloch transition thresholds provide concrete experimental targets, all confirmed by micromagnetic simulation.

Conclusion: A generic staggered damping-like SOT exists in symmetric heavy-metal/synthetic-antiferromagnet stacks, enabling DW structure to act as a control knob for motion while DW motion serves as an electrical readout of DW structure. This establishes new experimental directions.

Abstract: The Néel, or staggered, spin-orbit torque (SOT) enables an efficient electrical manipulation of antiferromagnetic order. However, it has been tied to special crystal structures, and only its field-like component is established. We develop a theory of staggered damping-like SOT, which arises generically in synthetic antiferromagnets sandwiched by the same heavy metal on both sides. Deriving a Néel-vector Lagrangian, we obtain the domain-wall (DW) velocity in closed form: bilinear in current and in-plane field, opposite in sign for Néel and Bloch walls, and tunable through the interlayer exchange coupling. DW structure thus becomes a control knob for DW motion, while the DW motion, in turn, offers an electrical readout of its structure. Micromagnetic simulations confirm the theory. The predicted sign reversals and scaling laws of the DW velocity, together with the thresholds for the Néel-Bloch transition, give immediate experimental targets.

</details>


### [137] [Lindbladian Phase Geometry and Hall Transport in Open Bloch Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07228)
*Zhihao Jiang,Longjun Xiang,Jian Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper formulates dc linear response in open Bloch systems using a trace-preserving Lindblad kinetic theory, introducing a physical velocity operator defined through the Liouvillian time derivative of position. It reveals an environment-induced current vertex correction whose momentum-space curl produces new Hall responses governed by a gauge-invariant phase geometry encoded in Lindblad jump amplitudes, yielding interband shift-vector and diagonal-vorticity Hall mechanisms that can produce finite Hall conductivity even when Berry-curvature anomalous Hall effect vanishes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While quantum geometry is known to underlie many transport phenomena in closed Bloch systems, it remains unexplored how quantum-geometric transport is modified when Bloch electrons couple to an environment, since the environment alters both the electronic state and the physical current operator.

Method: The authors formulate dc linear response within a trace-preserving Lindblad kinetic theory, defining the physical velocity as the Liouvillian time derivative of the position operator. This introduces an environment-induced contribution to the current vertex whose momentum-space curl generates new Hall responses governed by the gauge-invariant phase geometry of Lindblad jump amplitudes.

Result: To leading order in dissipative coupling, the Lindbladian phase geometry produces interband shift-vector Hall response (from off-diagonal jump amplitudes) and diagonal-vorticity Hall response (from diagonal jump amplitudes). Both mechanisms can yield finite Hall conductivity even when the conventional Berry-curvature anomalous Hall effect vanishes identically.

Conclusion: The work establishes Lindbladian phase geometry as an independent geometric origin of transverse transport in open quantum matter, extending quantum-geometric transport concepts to dissipative systems.

Abstract: Quantum geometry underlies a wide range of transport phenomena in Bloch systems. How quantum-geometric transport is modified when Bloch electrons are coupled to an environment, however, remains largely unexplored, even though the environment can alter both the electronic state and the physical current operator. Here we formulate dc linear response within a trace-preserving Lindblad kinetic theory by defining the physical velocity as the Liouvillian time derivative of the position operator. This construction reveals an environment-induced contribution to the current vertex whose momentum-space curl generates new Hall responses governed by the gauge-invariant phase geometry encoded by Lindblad jump amplitudes, which characterize electron-environment coupling. To leading order in the dissipative coupling, this geometry gives rise to interband shift-vector and diagonal-vorticity Hall responses arising from off-diagonal and diagonal jump amplitudes, respectively. Remarkably, both mechanisms can produce a finite Hall conductivity even when the conventional Berry-curvature anomalous Hall effect vanishes identically. Our work establishes Lindbladian phase geometry as an independent geometric origin of transverse transport in open quantum matter.

</details>


### [138] [Unveiling the Scaling Potential of Drain Merge through Active (DMtA) in CFETs: Breaking the Super-Via Bottlenecks and Unlocking New PPA Boosters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07329)
*Jingru Jiang,Haoran Lu,Kairong Guo,Yibo Zhang,Yifei Chen,Wanyue Peng,Yu Liu,Jiacheng Sun,Xiaoyan Xu,Ming Li,Yibo Lin,Runsheng Wang,Ru Huang,Heng Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper introduces Drain Merge through Active (DMtA), a novel approach for Complementary FETs (CFETs) where the drain merge via is embedded in the active region rather than through the field, and systematically evaluates it via a DTCO framework from process integration to block-level PPA on a RISC-V Ibex core, showing up to 34.8% frequency gains and 25.7% area reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Drain Merge through Field (DMtF) in CFETs blocks further parasitic optimization and cell scaling because the super via vertically connecting common S/D terminals of stacked n/pFETs occupies routing resources and limits optimization freedom.

Method: The paper develops a comprehensive DTCO (Design Technology Co-Optimization) framework including process integration for DMtA, contact-configuration-dependent (CTCD) compact modeling, standard cell design, ring oscillator evaluation, and block-level PPA benchmarking on a 32-bit RISC-V Ibex core. Additional techniques include Active widening, Area Borrowing, Hyper-cell construction on CFETs, and floating-output-pin-aware post-routing optimization.

Result: DMtA improves RO frequency by 11.7% over DMtF. Active widening and Area Borrowing boost maximum Ibex-core frequency by up to 34.8%. Hyper-cells enabled by DMtA provide an additional 8.7% frequency gain. Post-routing floating-output-pin-aware optimization reduces power by 5.3%. DMtA enables 2.5T cell scaling preserving single-row compatibility, reducing post-PR core area by 25.7%.

Conclusion: Embedding the drain merge via within the active region (DMtA) is a superior and scalable integration scheme for CFETs that improves performance, enables novel cell topologies like Hyper-cells, saves power, and reduces area, making it a promising path for future sub-3nm node scaling.

Abstract: Drain merge (DM), a super via vertically connecting the common S/D terminals of stacked n/pFETs in Complementary FETs (CFETs), blocks further parasitic optimization and cell scaling. For the first time, this work systematically investigates the state-of-the-art Drain Merge through Active (DMtA), a revolutionary technology reported recently with the DM embedded in the active region, through a comprehensive DTCO framework spanning process integration, contact-configuration-dependent (CTCD) compact modeling, standardcell design, RO evaluation and block-level PPA benchmark on a 32-bit RISC-V Ibex core. By reducing DM parasitics and enabling DM-width optimization, DMtA improves RO frequency by 11.7% over its conventional Drain Merge through field (DMtF) counterpart. Active widening and Area Borrowing, the latter first reported in [8] and exploiting spatial slack in adjacent cells to further enlarge the nanosheet width (WNS), increase the maximum Ibex-core frequency by up to 34.8%. More importantly, DMtA also enables the once GAA-exclusive Hyper-cells on CFETs by merging the active regions across adjacent cell rows, providing a further 8.7% frequency gain. A post-routing floating-output-pin-aware optimization further removes redundant S/D contacts (CTs) and reduces power by 5.3%. Finally, DMtA facilitates more area-efficient 2.5T cell scaling by preserving single-row cell compatibility, reducing post-PR core area by 25.7%.

</details>


### [139] [Trion-mediated quantum operations on a double-dot charge qubit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07342)
*Alexander Tsukanov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a laser-pulse-based scheme for controlling a charge qubit in a semiconductor asymmetric double quantum dot with suppressed tunnel coupling. Logical states are mapped to auxiliary trion states via laser pulses, restoring resonant tunneling through Coulomb compensation of structural asymmetry. Conditions for single-qubit rotations are given, and fidelity of the inversion operation is analyzed against laser parameters and dissipation rates, demonstrating high speed and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the challenge that structural asymmetry in double quantum dots suppresses tunnel coupling—required for charge qubit manipulation—by using laser-driven transitions to auxiliary trion states where Coulomb interactions compensate the asymmetry and restore resonant tunneling.

Method: Laser pulses convert the single-electron logical states into auxiliary trion states, in which the Coulomb interaction compensates the structural asymmetry of the quantum dots, thereby restoring the resonant tunneling condition. The conditions for performing orthogonal single-qubit rotations are formulated, and two-dimensional structure qubit parameters are calculated including fidelity dependence on laser field parameters and dissipative process rates.

Result: Qubit parameters are calculated for a two-dimensional structure, and the fidelity of the inversion operation is obtained as a function of laser field parameters and dissipative rates. The algorithm is shown to exhibit high speed and reliability.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme enables fast and reliable single-qubit control in asymmetric double quantum dots by exploiting Coulomb compensation via trion states, offering a viable path for high-fidelity charge qubit operations despite suppressed tunnel coupling.

Abstract: A scheme for controlling a charge qubit on a semiconductor asymmetric double quantum dot with suppressed tunnel coupling between individual quantum dots is proposed. Laser pulses convert the logical single-electron states of the qubit into auxiliary trion states, for which the resonant tunneling condition is restored due to the compensation of structural asymmetry by the Coulomb interaction of the particles. The conditions for performing orthogonal single-qubit rotations are formulated. For a two-dimensional structure, the parameters of the qubit are calculated, and the dependence of the fidelity of the inversion operation on the parameters of the laser field and the rates of dissipative processes is obtained. It is shown that the considered algorithm is characterized by high speed and reliability.

</details>


### [140] [Static-Dynamic Correlations and Complex Spin-Wave Eigenmodes in Single- and Multilayer Diamond-Shaped Nanomagnets Without Bias Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07368)
*Krishna Begari*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates single-layer and multilayer diamond-shaped nanomagnets using micromagnetic simulations, demonstrating that two distinct remanent magnetic configurations can be achieved via simple field initialization, enabling reconfigurable microwave operation with resonance frequency shifts of 0.9 GHz (single-layer) and 3 GHz (multilayer). The enhanced tunability in multilayers stems from strengthened dipolar coupling between vertically stacked magnetic layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore diamond-shaped nanomagnets as a platform for developing microwave devices with reconfigurable characteristics, addressing the need for simple and effective approaches to achieve frequency-tunable microwave functionality.

Method: Systematic micromagnetic simulations of static and dynamic magnetic behavior in single-layer and multilayer diamond-shaped nanomagnets, using magnetic field initialization to create distinct remanent configurations and nanosecond-scale magnetic field pulses to modify resonance characteristics.

Result: Two remanent configurations with different magnetization patterns and dynamic responses were obtained. Resonance frequency shifts of 0.9 GHz (sub-GHz) for single-layer structures and 3 GHz (GHz range) for multilayer structures were observed, with the larger tunability in multilayers attributed to enhanced dipolar coupling between vertically coupled magnetic layers.

Conclusion: Diamond-shaped nanomagnets offer a simple and effective route toward reconfigurable microwave functionality, with potential applications in ultralow-power, ultrafast, and frequency-tunable microwave devices.

Abstract: Diamond-shaped nanomagnets provide a suitable platform for developing microwave devices with reconfigurable characteristics. In this study, the static and dynamic magnetic behaviour of single layer and multilayer diamond-shaped nanomagnets was systematically investigated using micromagnetic simulations. Two distinct remanent magnetic configurations were obtained through a simple magnetic field initialization process. These configurations exhibited different magnetization patterns and dynamic responses. Their resonance characteristics could be modified by applying a nanosecond-scale magnetic field pulse, enabling reconfigurable microwave operation. A clear resonance frequency shift in the sub-GHz (0.9 GHz) range was observed for the single-layer structure, while the multilayer structure exhibited a significantly larger frequency shift in the GHz range (3 GHz). The larger frequency tunability observed in the multilayer configuration arises primarily from the enhanced dipolar coupling between the vertically coupled magnetic layers, which modifies the local effective magnetic field and consequently influences the magnetization dynamics. These findings demonstrate that diamond-shaped nanomagnets can provide a simple and effective route toward reconfigurable microwave functionality, with potential applications in ultralow-power, ultrafast, and frequency tunable microwave devices.

</details>


### [141] [Layer-Dependent Phonons, Excitons, and Magneto-Optical Phenomena in CrSBr: A Mini Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07425)
*Muhammad Aftab,Chinmay Kumar Mohanty,Zain Ashfaq,Warisha Mehmood,Xiaoli Wang,Clément Faugeras,Wajid Ali,Maciej R. Molas*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A review of the two-dimensional layered magnetic semiconductor CrSBr, covering its layer-dependent vibrational and excitonic properties from bulk to monolayer, along with its magneto-optical response and potential applications in spintronic, magneto-optical, and quantum photonic technologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-dimensional layered magnetic materials are important platforms for studying low-dimensional magnetism and coupled many-body interactions. CrSBr is a particularly promising candidate due to its air stability, semiconducting nature, strong in-plane anisotropy, and A-type antiferromagnetic ordering, making it attractive for advanced spintronic and optoelectronic applications.

Method: The paper is a review that summarizes recent advances in understanding CrSBr through examination of its crystal structure, magnetic anisotropy, interlayer spin reorientation, vibrational dynamics, spin-phonon coupling, excitonic landscape (including magnetic-field-sensitive photoluminescence, localized excitonic states, and coexistence of Frenkel- and Wannier-Mott excitons), and its magneto-optical response across layer thicknesses from bulk to monolayer.

Result: The review consolidates findings on the layer-dependent vibrational and excitonic properties of CrSBr and its magneto-optical response, revealing a rich excitonic landscape with magnetic-field-sensitive photoluminescence, localized excitonic states, and mixed Frenkel- and Wannier-Mott excitonic character within the bandgap.

Conclusion: CrSBr exhibits unique layer-dependent properties that hold promise for spintronic, magneto-optical, and quantum photonic technologies, though challenges remain in harnessing these properties for practical device applications.

Abstract: Two-dimensional layered magnetic materials offer a versatile platform for exploring low-dimensional magnetism and coupled many-body interactions in these materials. Chromium sulfur bromide (CrSBr) is a promising candidate for advanced spintronic and optoelectronic applications because of its intrinsic air stability, semiconducting nature, strong in-plane anisotropy, and A-type antiferromagnetic ordering. This review summarizes recent advances in the understanding of the layer-dependent vibrational and excitonic properties of CrSBr, as well as its magneto-optical response, from bulk crystals to the monolayer limit. We examined its crystal structure, magnetic anisotropy, and interlayer spin reorientation, followed by insights into vibrational dynamics and spin-phonon coupling. Particular emphasis is placed on the excitonic landscape, including magnetic-field-sensitive photoluminescence, localized excitonic states, and the coexistence of Frenkel- and Wannier-Mott excitons in the bandgap. Finally, we discuss the challenges and prospects of harnessing the unique layer-dependent properties of CrSBr in spintronic, magneto-optical, and quantum photonic technologies.

</details>


### [142] [Charge-spin conversion in altermagnets: electronic anisotropy versus magnon-mediated spin drag](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07426)
*Koki Mizuno*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether the transport anisotropy in altermagnets originates from electrons or magnons, and whether the magnon-mediated channel can distinguish an altermagnet from a conventional antiferromagnet. Using a common model for metallic ferromagnet, antiferromagnet, and altermagnet, they find that charge-spin conversion anisotropy is dominated by electronic spin splitting, while the magnon-mediated spin-flip channel is even under C4 and carries no first-order anisotropy signature. However, this channel responds linearly to a sublattice-selective perturbation lowering B1g symmetry, yielding a susceptibility that is finite only in altermagnets and measurable without domain preparation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets exhibit momentum-dependent spin splitting without net magnetization or spin-orbit coupling, raising fundamental questions about whether their transport anisotropy is carried by electrons or magnons, and whether magnon-mediated transport provides a unique signature distinguishing altermagnets from conventional antiferromagnets.

Method: The authors compute spin-resolved conductivity within a common model for three magnet types (ferromagnet, antiferromagnet, altermagnet), treating the spin-diagonal channel via magnon self-energy and the spin-flip channel to leading order in electron-magnon coupling. They analyze the symmetry properties of the magnon-mediated channel and probe its response to a sublattice-selective perturbation lowering bulk B1g symmetry.

Result: The anisotropy of charge-spin conversion is dominated by electronic spin splitting, with magnon branches contributing marginally. The magnon-mediated spin-flip channel is even under C4, carrying no first-order anisotropy signature. However, it responds linearly to a sublattice-selective perturbation lowering B1g symmetry: the susceptibility vanishes identically in antiferromagnets, is small in ferromagnets, and is finite in altermagnets. Since it is even in magnetic domain index, no single-domain sample preparation is required for measurement.

Conclusion: The magnon-mediated spin-flip channel alone cannot distinguish an altermagnet from a conventional antiferromagnet at first order in the anisotropy. However, a sublattice-selective perturbation that breaks B1g symmetry reveals a unique, finite susceptibility in altermagnets among magnets with no net magnetization, offering a domain-independent experimental probe of altermagnetic order.

Abstract: Altermagnets acquire a momentum-dependent spin splitting without net magnetization or spin-orbit coupling.
  This raises the question of whether the transport anisotropy originates in the electrons or the magnons, and whether the magnon-mediated channel distinguishes an altermagnet from a conventional antiferromagnet.
  We compute the spin-resolved conductivity of a metallic ferromagnet, antiferromagnet, and altermagnet within a common model, treating the spin-diagonal channel with the magnon self-energy and the spin-flip channel to leading order in the electron-magnon coupling.
  The anisotropy of the charge-spin conversion is dominated by the electronic spin splitting, the magnon branches contributing only marginally.
  The magnon-mediated spin-flip channel is even under $C_{4}$, so it carries no signature at first order in the anisotropy and cannot on its own distinguish an altermagnet from a conventional antiferromagnet.
  It does, however, respond linearly to a sublattice-selective perturbation that lowers the bulk $B_{1g}$ symmetry.
  The resulting susceptibility vanishes identically in the antiferromagnet, is small in the ferromagnet, and is finite in the altermagnet, where the electronic anisotropy ties spin and sublattice together, so that among magnets with no net magnetization it is unique to altermagnetic order.
  Being even in the magnetic domain index, this response can be measured without preparing a single magnetic-domain sample.

</details>
