### [143] [Direct Realization of Near-Ideal Carbyne in Ultrathin Boron Nitride Nanotubes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07443)
*Iryna Ivanenko,Pietro Marabotti,Yifan Zhang,Getulio Silva e Souza Júnior,Johannes M. A. Lechner,Pablo Hernández López,Martin Magg,Shivani Shivaprakash,Carlo Spartaco Casari,Sebastian Heeg,Benjamin S. Flavel*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Researchers encapsulated and thermally converted hydrogen-capped polyynes inside ultrathin boron nitride nanotubes to produce carbyne chains in a near-ideal regime, where endgroup, finite-length, and host-guest perturbations are minimized. Statistical Raman spectroscopy across 245 locations revealed a vibrational frequency distribution an order of magnitude narrower than in carbon nanotubes, anharmonicity consistent with the universal law for carbyne-like materials, and bond length alternation matching correlated calculations for the free chain, with no photoluminescence detected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Carbyne, the sp-hybridized 1D carbon allotrope, is predicted to be the stiffest known material, but its intrinsic properties have never been measured because chains made via molecular chemistry carry endgroup and finite-length perturbations, while chains grown in carbon nanotubes strongly couple to the host, renormalizing their vibrational frequency by up to 110 cm^-1 in a diameter-dependent manner.

Method: Encapsulating hydrogen-capped polyynes inside ultrathin boron nitride nanotubes and thermally converting them to carbyne chains, then characterizing via statistical Raman spectroscopy across 245 locations.

Result: Vibrational frequency distribution an order of magnitude narrower than in carbon nanotubes; anharmonicity consistent with the universal law for carbyne-like materials; bond length alternation matching correlated calculations for the free chain; no photoluminescence detected despite the transparent host, consistent with dipole-forbidden emission of an unperturbed carbyne chain.

Conclusion: Boron nitride nanotubes give experimental access to carbyne in its near-ideal form, where endgroup, finite-length, and host-guest perturbations are reduced to secondary effects.

Abstract: Carbyne, the sp-hybridized one-dimensional allotrope of carbon, is predicted to be the stiffest known material, with electronic and optical properties set by a single structural parameter, the bond length alternation. However, its intrinsic properties have never been measured: chains synthesized through molecular chemistry carry endgroup and finite-length perturbations that persist even in the longest molecules available, while chains grown inside carbon nanotubes strongly couple to the host, which renormalizes their vibrational frequency by up to 110 cm$^{-1}$ in a diameter-dependent manner. Here, we show that encapsulating and thermally converting hydrogen-capped polyynes inside ultrathin boron nitride nanotubes, structural analogues of carbon nanotubes but electrically insulating, yields carbyne chains in a near-ideal regime, where endgroup, finite length, and host-guest perturbations are reduced to secondary effects. Statistical Raman spectroscopy across 245 locations returns a vibrational frequency distribution an order of magnitude narrower than in carbon nanotubes, an anharmonicity consistent with the universal law for carbyne-like materials, and a bond length alternation matching correlated calculations for the free chain. No photoluminescence is detected, despite the transparent host, as expected for the dipole-forbidden emission of an unperturbed carbyne chain. Boron nitride nanotubes give experimental access to carbyne in its near-ideal form.

</details>


### [144] [From London to Morse via Binnig, Quate, and Gerber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08425)
*Sofia Alonso Perez,Matthew O. Blunt,Frederick Carlisle,Neil R. Champness,Janette L. Dunn,Matthew Edmondson,Rowan Evers,Connor Fields,Subhashis Gangopadhyay,James Hayton,Samuel P. Jarvis,Filipe Junqueira,Lev Kantorovich,Brian Kiraly,Natalio Krasnogor,Ioannis Lekkas,Morten Møller,Philip Moriarty,Chris Pakes,Emmanuelle Pauliac-Vaujour,Oliver Phillips,Adrian Radocea,Philipp Rahe,Mohammad Abdur Rashid,Hongqian Sang,Alex Saywell,Nikhil Seeja Sivakumar,Peter Sharp,Andrew Stannard,Julian Stirling,Adam Sweetman,Simon Taylor,Richard A. J. Woolley*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This perspective paper reviews two decades of work from the University of Nottingham group on atomic force microscopy (AFM), spanning interactions from weak van der Waals forces to covalent bonding, and emphasizing the central theme of the probe as an active participant in measurements—covering results from microscopic scales down to single chemical bonds, atom-by-atom assembly, tip-induced effects on molecular diffusion, and the emerging role of machine learning in automated tip-driven manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper celebrates the 40th anniversary of AFM by revisiting the prescient vision of Binnig, Quate, and Gerber, who anticipated AFM's capability to probe a wide spectrum of interactions. The group aims to highlight their two decades of work unified by the theme that the probe is an active participant rather than a passive observer in scanning probe measurements.

Method: The review selects and synthesizes results from the authors' group spanning different force regimes (van der Waals/Hamaker, hydrogen bonding, covalent bonds) and length scales (microscopic to single chemical bond), including vertical tip-sample transfer for atom-by-atom cluster assembly, studies of tip-induced heterogeneity in molecular diffusion, and discussion of machine learning approaches for automating tip-driven atomic and molecular manipulation.

Result: The paper presents a spectrum of interactions covered by AFM from weak van der Waals to strong covalent bonds, demonstrates atom-by-atom metal cluster assembly via vertical tip-sample transfer, provides evidence that tip-induced heterogeneity underpins first-passage dynamics in molecular diffusion, and identifies challenges in acquiring non-invasive measurements of diffusion barriers perturbed by the probe.

Conclusion: The authors conclude by highlighting machine learning's growing role in automating tip-driven atomic and molecular manipulation as the future direction for the field, echoing the original vision of AFM pioneers while extending it to address modern challenges in measurement invasiveness and autonomous experimentation.

Abstract: In their landmark paper introducing the atomic force microscope [Phys. Rev. Lett. \textbf{56}, 930 (1986)], Binnig, Quate, and Gerber presciently anticipated that the technique would ultimately be capable of probing interactions running the gamut from weak van der Waals interactions to strong covalent bonding. They also highlighted that the tip-sample forces central to AFM are present, and often highly influential, in scanning tunnelling microscopy; indeed, this realisation directly inspired the invention of the force microscope. In this perspective for the \textit{Forty Years of AFM} special issue, we review selected aspects of two decades of work from our group at the University of Nottingham that span the force range highlighted by BQG and are united by a common, central theme: the probe as active participant rather than passive observer. Our selection of results also covers length- and correlation-scales from the microscopic right down to the single chemical bond limit, tracking a spectrum of interactions from van der Waals/Hamaker forces, through hydrogen bonding, to covalent bonds and, finally, atom-by-atom assembly of metal clusters via vertical tip-sample transfer. Echoing BQG's own observations on the prevalence of probe-sample forces in STM, we also discuss recent evidence that tip-induced heterogeneity underpins first-passage dynamics in molecular diffusion and highlight the challenges in acquiring non-invasive measurements of diffusion barriers for adsorbed molecules that are readily perturbed by the probe. We close with a perspective on machine learning's growing role in automating tip-driven atomic and molecular manipulation.

</details>


### [145] [Encapsulated macroscopic WS$_2$ monolayers enable room-temperature exciton-polariton lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07587)
*Shiyu Huang,Sander Scheel,Jiang Qu,Johannes Düreth,Dominik Horneber,Edith Wietek,Simon Widmann,Libo Ma,Monika Emmerling,Martin Kamp,Simon Betzold,Alexey Chernikov,Sven Höfling,Sebastian Klembt*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate that 1-dodecanol-encapsulated WS2 monolayers provide millimeter-scale coverage with highly uniform optical properties over ~300 μm distances, enabling room-temperature exciton-photon strong coupling (Rabi splitting ≈ 31 meV) in open microcavities. Using this platform, they realize a two-dimensional polaritonic kagome lattice and directly resolve its band structure, including Dirac dispersive bands and a flat-band-like branch, establishing large-area WS2 as a scalable platform for ambient-condition polaritonics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional exfoliated monolayer semiconductors suffer from small size, optical inhomogeneity, and device-to-device variability, which impede scalable room-temperature polaritonics and the realization of extended polariton lattices spanning many unit cells.

Method: 1-Dodecanol-encapsulated WS2 monolayers are fabricated to achieve millimeter-scale coverage with uniform optical properties. These monolayers are integrated into a tunable open microcavity, and angle-resolved spectroscopy combined with momentum- and real-space imaging are used to probe the polaritonic kagome lattice band structure.

Result: Room-temperature strong coupling is observed with a Rabi splitting of ~31 meV. The polaritonic kagome lattice exhibits Dirac dispersive bands and a weakly dispersive flat-band-like branch within the s-band, matching a linear non-interacting model. Bond-centered and site-centered mode profiles are identified via complementary imaging.

Conclusion: Large-area encapsulated WS2 monolayers in open microcavities constitute a scalable platform for engineering polariton band structures and exploring synthetic quantum materials under ambient conditions.

Abstract: Large-area, optically homogeneous monolayer semiconductors are a critical prerequisite for scalable room-temperature polaritonics and for realizing polariton lattices extending across many unit cells. Yet, the small size, optical inhomogeneity, and device-to-device variability of conventional exfoliated flakes have remained major obstacles. Here, we overcome these limitations using 1-dodecanol-encapsulated WS$_2$ monolayers that combine millimeter-scale coverage with remarkably uniform optical properties over lateral distances approaching $300\,$$μ\mathrm{m}$. Integrated into a tunable open microcavity, these monolayers exhibit robust room-temperature exciton-photon strong coupling, evidenced by a pronounced anti-crossing and a Rabi splitting of $\hbar Ω_{\mathrm{R}} \approx 31\,\mathrm{meV}$. Leveraging the exceptional uniformity of this platform, we realize a two-dimensional polaritonic kagome lattice and directly resolve its characteristic band structure. Angle-resolved spectroscopy reveals Dirac dispersive bands together with a weakly dispersive flat-band-like branch within the $s$-band, in good agreement with a linear non-interacting model. Complementary momentum- and real-space imaging further identifies the associated bond-centered and site-centered mode profiles. These results establish large-area WS$_2$ monolayers in open microcavities as a scalable platform for engineering polariton band structures and exploring synthetic quantum materials under ambient conditions.

</details>


### [146] [Metal-Insulator Coexistence and Gap-Crossing Domain-Wall Modes in an Aubry-André Model with Nonlocal Hopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07704)
*Xiarui Zhan,Mingsheng Tian,Qiongyi He,Kaiye Shi,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates particle transport in an extended Aubry-André model with system-scale (nonlocal) hopping, revealing a metal-insulator coexistence regime where metallic and insulating spatial domains coexist with sharp boundaries. Particles show flat-band-like localization without quasiperiodic potentials and distinct multi-point localization with them, while freely tunneling across disconnected metallic domains, plus unconventional gap-crossing domain-wall modes enabling nonlocal transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interplay between localization, nonlocality, and transport in systems with nonlocal hopping—a relatively unexplored regime compared to conventional local or short-range hopping models.

Method: The study uses an extended Aubry-André model augmented with system-scale (nonlocal) hopping, where the hopping range is proportional to the system size. Analytical and numerical analyses are employed to characterize real-space transport phases, domain structure, localization profiles (flat-band-like and multi-point), and domain-wall modes.

Result: The model exhibits a metal-insulator coexistence phase in real space with sharp spatial boundaries between metallic and insulating domains. Insulating regions show flat-band-like localization without quasiperiodic potentials and distinct multi-point (non-exponential) localization under quasiperiodic potentials. Metallic domains separated by insulating regions remain coupled through free propagation/tunneling, and unconventional gap-crossing domain-wall modes with comb-like spatial profiles mediate nonlocal, multi-point transport.

Conclusion: The findings uncover a rich phase structure unique to systems with nonlocal hopping, demonstrating coexistence of metallic and insulating regions, novel localization mechanisms, and unconventional domain-wall transport channels—broadening understanding of nonequilibrium and localization physics beyond conventional models.

Abstract: Nonequilibrium transport remains a central theme in modern physics, spanning from condensed matter to synthetic systems. Here, we investigate particle transport in an extended Aubry-André model with system-scale hopping, namely nonlocal hopping with a range proportional to the system size, and uncover a metal-insulator coexistence regime in real space, where metallic and insulating spatial domains coexist within the same system and are separated by sharp spatial boundaries. In the insulating region, particles exhibit flat-band-like localization in the absence of quasiperiodic potentials, while a quasiperiodic potential induces distinct multi-point localization, different from conventional exponential localization. Meanwhile, particles can freely propagate and tunnel across spatially disconnected metallic domains separated by the insulating region. Beyond this coexistence phase, we identify unconventional gap-crossing domain-wall modes with comb-like spatial profiles that mediate nonlocal, multi-point transport across separated metallic domains. Our findings reveal a rich interplay between localization, nonlocality, and transport in systems with nonlocal hopping.

</details>


### [147] [Creation and characterization of leviton excitations in tight-binding chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07820)
*Stephen R. McMillan,Thomas Frederiksen,Géza Giedke*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates leviton generation in finite one-dimensional tight-binding chains of non-interacting fermions driven by Lorentzian voltage pulses, identifying an intermediate pulse-speed regime where clean leviton-like states emerge, and showing Lorentzian pulses outperform non-Lorentzian shapes in approaching the low-noise limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Levitons are fundamental resources for electron quantum optics, but their standard formulation assumes continuum scattering descriptions. Realistic platforms for integrated electronic quantum circuits are finite, discrete, and affected by lattice dispersion, so understanding leviton formation beyond the ideal continuum limit is necessary for practical implementation.

Method: The authors study finite one-dimensional tight-binding chains of non-interacting fermions driven by time-dependent voltage pulses using the single-particle density matrix formalism, resolving the excitation above the initial Fermi sea and quantifying quality via average excitation number and its fluctuations across different pulse shapes, amplitudes, and lattice filling conditions.

Result: Clean leviton-like states emerge only in an intermediate regime: pulses must be slow enough to be resolved by lattice dynamics but not so slow that truncation and finite-size effects distort the Lorentzian profile. Lorentzian pulses systematically outperform non-Lorentzian shapes in approaching the low-noise limit with increasing system size. Finite-lattice signatures from band filling, pulse amplitude, linear voltage-drop geometry, and deviations from the continuum integer-charge condition were identified.

Conclusion: The work establishes a microscopic framework for understanding leviton formation beyond the ideal continuum limit and provides criteria for evaluating lattice-based platforms for coherent few-electron transport.

Abstract: Levitons are minimal-excitation electronic wave packets generated by Lorentzian voltage pulses and constitute a central resource for electron quantum optics. Their creation and defining properties are usually formulated in continuum scattering descriptions, whereas many candidate platforms for integrated electronic quantum circuits are finite, discrete, and strongly shaped by lattice dispersion. We study leviton generation in finite one-dimensional tight-binding chains of non-interacting fermions driven by time-dependent voltage pulses. Using the single-particle density matrix, we resolve the excitation above the initial Fermi sea and quantify its quality through the average excitation number and its fluctuations. We find that clean leviton-like states emerge only in an intermediate regime where the pulse is slow enough to be resolved by the dynamics on the lattice, but not so slow that truncation and finite-size effects distort the Lorentzian profile in the time domain. Lorentzian pulses (even if truncated) systematically outperform non-Lorentzian pulse shapes in approaching the low-noise limit with increasing system size. We further identify finite-lattice signatures associated with band filling, pulse amplitude, linear voltage-drop geometry, and residual deviations from the continuum integer-charge condition. These results establish a microscopic framework for understanding leviton formation beyond the ideal continuum limit and for evaluating lattice-based platforms for coherent few-electron transport.

</details>


### [148] [Steady reservoir-mediated self-trapping of polariton condensates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07962)
*Pavel N. Kozhevin,Oleg I. Utesov,Igor S. Aranson,Sergey V. Koniakhin,Anton V. Nalitov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper explains how non-equilibrium exciton-polariton condensates, typically expected to form ballistically expanding states under tight optical pumping, can instead form compact self-trapped states due to reservoir depletion via bosonic stimulated scattering. These self-trapped states feature persistent converging currents, exist below the conventional condensation threshold, remain steady for nanoseconds, and eventually eject due to a bullet-type instability enabling neuromorphic spiking dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the mechanism behind experimentally observed compact self-trapped polariton condensate states, which contradict the standard expectation of ballistically expanding stationary states for incoherently pumped condensates.

Method: Theoretical modeling of the back-action of the condensate on the optically pumped excitonic reservoir via bosonic stimulated scattering, demonstrating that local reservoir depletion creates a self-induced potential trap confining the condensate.

Result: The self-trapped state is formed by persistent converging polariton currents (not radial expansion), exists below the conventional condensation threshold, remains steady over nanosecond timescales, and eventually ejects from the trap due to a bullet-type instability.

Conclusion: This mechanism explains observed self-trapped polariton condensates and provides a pathway for spiking neuromorphic dynamics in networks of polariton condensates.

Abstract: Nonequilibrium bosonic condensates of exciton-polaritons generated by tightly focused incoherent optical beams are typically expected to form a stationary ballistically expanding state with the notable exception of experimentally observed compact self-trapped states [Phys. Rev. Lett. 123, 047401 (2019)]. We show that the back-action of the condensate on the optically pumped excitonic reservoir via bosonic stimulated scattering can cause local reservoir depletion, producing a steady condensate confined in a self-induced potential trap. Notably, this self-trapped state is formed by persistent converging rather than radially expanding polariton currents and exists even below the conventionally defined condensation threshold. This state remains steady over nanosecond-scale times and is eventually ejected from the self-induced trap due to a bullet-type instability, enabling spiking neuromorphic dynamics in networks of polariton condensates.

</details>


### [149] [Nonlinear Topological Orbital Responses of Antiferromagnetic Skyrmions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08022)
*Amir N. Zarezad,Arne Brataas,Alireza Qaiumzadeh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper predicts a nonlinear topological orbital response of isolated antiferromagnetic skyrmions in PT-symmetric hexagonal antiferromagnets without spin-orbit coupling, producing local orbital Hall currents and accumulations that scale quadratically with the applied electric field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Antiferromagnetic skyrmions evade the skyrmion Hall effect but lose the conventional topological charge Hall signal due to compensation. The authors seek an alternative detectable topological response in antiferromagnetic skyrmion systems.

Method: The authors use semiclassical transport theory to analyze the diffusive, weak-emergent-field regime, examining how the spin-dependent emergent Lorentz force from an isolated skyrmion reshapes carrier distributions. They derive local orbital Hall-current corrections and orbital accumulations, with conditions distinguishing spin-asymmetric versus spin-symmetric scattering.

Result: Both the orbital Hall-current correction and orbital accumulation are quadratic in the applied electric field. The current correction requires spin-asymmetric longitudinal scattering, while the accumulation survives spin-symmetric scattering. When spin-diffusion length exceeds strip width, the current correction persists while accumulation tends to zero. Both signals are even under field reversal, odd under topological charge reversal, and helicity independent.

Conclusion: These symmetry properties enable rectified detection and clearly distinguish this nonlinear mechanism from the Q-even quantum-regime topological orbital Hall effect, providing a viable experimental signature for antiferromagnetic skyrmions.

Abstract: Antiferromagnetic skyrmions evade the skyrmion Hall effect, but compensation suppresses their conventional topological charge Hall signal. We predict a semiclassical nonlinear topological orbital response of an isolated skyrmion in a $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric hexagonal antiferromagnet without spin-orbit coupling. In the diffusive, weak-emergent-field regime, the spin-dependent emergent Lorentz force reshapes the carrier distribution, generating a local orbital Hall-current correction and a local orbital accumulation, both quadratic in the applied electric field. The current correction requires spin-asymmetric longitudinal scattering, whereas the accumulation survives spin-symmetric scattering. When the spin-diffusion length greatly exceeds the strip width, the current correction persists while the accumulation approaches zero. Both signals are even under electric-field reversal, odd under reversal of the skyrmion topological charge $Q$, and helicity independent within this model. These symmetries enable rectified detection and distinguish this mechanism from the $Q$-even quantum-regime topological orbital Hall effect.

</details>


### [150] [Edge energy fluctuation: A unified theory of friction scaling laws in twisted layered material interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08063)
*Ze Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes the concept of edge energy fluctuation (EEF) to explain sliding energy barriers in finite-sized twisted van der Waals layered materials, demonstrating that frictional anisotropy and distinct friction scaling laws arise from the slider edge orientation, and introduces a friction engineering paradigm using hierarchical edges for tunable superlubric devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and control friction at twisted van der Waals interfaces by uncovering the microscopic mechanism governing sliding energy barriers in finite-sized sliders.

Method: Building on two insights—the invariance of potential energy of complete moire tiles during sliding and the symmetry-breaking effect induced by slider edges—the authors introduce the edge energy fluctuation (EEF) concept to characterize the sliding energy barrier of finite-sized sliders.

Result: At a fixed twist angle and sliding direction, significant frictional anisotropy emerges depending on the cutting direction of the slider edge, producing distinct friction scaling laws. Additionally, a friction engineering paradigm is demonstrated via intentionally introduced hierarchical edges.

Conclusion: The EEF framework provides a physical basis for friction at moire superlattice interfaces and offers a promising route for friction control in superlubric devices through edge engineering.

Abstract: Interlayer twisting in van der Waals (vdW) layered materials generates moire superlattices, unlocking unique opportunities for engineering their electronic and mechanical functionalities. Here, building on two key insights - the invariance of the potential energy of complete moire tiles during sliding, and the symmetry-breaking effect induced by the slider edge - we propose the concept of edge energy fluctuation (EEF) and demonstrate that the EEF governs the sliding energy barrier of finite-sized sliders. Interestingly, we found that even at a fixed twist angle and sliding direction, significant frictional anisotropy emerges when the slider edge is cut along different directions, resulting in distinct friction scaling laws. Furthermore, we present a friction engineering paradigm for twisted layered material interfaces through the deliberate introduction of hierarchical edges, offering a promising route for friction control in superlubric devices.

</details>


### [151] [Electronic Orbital Angular Momentum Driven by Finite-Momentum Phonons: Beyond Chiral and Axial Phonons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08100)
*Yongho Park,Jeonghun Sohn,Sejoong Kim,Kyoung-Whan Kim*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Finite-momentum phonons can generate electronic orbital angular momentum (OAM) even without phonon axiality or chirality, through a mechanism governed by phonon wave vector and frequency. A general gauge-field theory is developed and q- and ω-scaling laws for AC/DC OAM responses are derived, with AC OAM arising even for linearly polarized phonons and tunable by q.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend phonon-driven OAM generation beyond the previously studied chiral and axial phonon scenarios, demonstrating that finite-momentum (nonaxial, nonchiral) phonons suffice to induce electronic OAM, and to establish a wave-vector-tunable route to orbitronics.

Method: A gauge-field theory is developed where a unitary transformation absorbs phonon displacement into emergent vector and scalar potentials acting on electrons, enabling a perturbative classification of the OAM response in phonon wave vector q and frequency ω. Time-dependent tight-binding simulations under acoustic-phonon driving independently confirm the predictions.

Result: AC OAM is generated even for linearly polarized phonons with magnitude and sign tunable by q. The generation is governed by the matching between phonon geometry and electronic orbital texture, not solely by phonon angular momentum. The DC response connects to electronic Berry curvature, supporting that phonon angular momentum is not the only relevant degree of freedom. Derived q- and ω-scaling laws are confirmed by tight-binding simulations.

Conclusion: Phonon-driven electronic OAM extends well beyond chiral and axial phonon systems, establishing finite-momentum phonons (e.g., surface acoustic waves) as a wave-vector-tunable platform for orbitronics.

Abstract: We show that finite-momentum phonons generate electronic orbital angular momentum (OAM) even without phonon axiality or chirality, with the response controlled by the phonon wave vector $q$ and frequency $ω$. We develop a general gauge-field theory in which a unitary transformation absorbs the phonon displacement into emergent vector and scalar potentials acting on the electrons, providing a perturbative classification of the OAM response in $q$ and $ω$. We derive $q$- and $ω$-scaling laws for AC and DC responses. Notably, AC OAM arises even for linearly polarized phonons, with its magnitude and sign tunable by $q$. Its generation is governed by the matching between the phonon geometry and the electronic orbital texture rather than solely by the phonon angular momentum. The connection of the DC response to the electronic Berry curvature further supports that phonon angular momentum is not the only degree of freedom governing electronic OAM generation. Time-dependent tight-binding simulations under acoustic-phonon driving independently confirm the core predictions. Our results extend phonon-driven OAM beyond chiral and axial phonons and establish a wave-vector-tunable route to orbitronics, accessible with surface acoustic waves.

</details>


### [152] [Tunable topological narrow bands in twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08104)
*Dong Wang,Federico Escudero,Zhen Zhan,Shengjun Yuan*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies the low-energy band structure and topological properties of twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene across four stacking configurations (AB-ABC, BA-ABC, AB-ABA, BA-ABA) using tight-binding and continuum models. It maps topological phase diagrams as functions of twist angle, electric field, and Hartree potential, revealing Chern number transitions and demonstrating that perpendicular electric fields serve as efficient tuning knobs for controlling Chern bands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how stacking configurations, twist angle, and electric fields collectively manipulate the narrow bands and their topological properties in twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene systems, providing guidance for engineering topological bands with tunable Chern numbers.

Method: Combines tight-binding and continuum models to compute band structures, validates their agreement, and tracks flat bands and valley Chern numbers as functions of twist angle, perpendicular electric field, and self-consistent Hartree potential. Topological phase diagrams are constructed in the twist angle-electric field parameter space.

Result: Found topological transitions between narrow bands at relatively large twist angles under electric fields, with total Chern numbers following chiral-limit sum rules. Identified a second type of transition when flat bands hybridize with remote bands, causing Chern number and charge density transfer. The Hartree potential induces weak band shifts/reshaping but combined with electric fields produces rich topological phase diagrams.

Conclusion: Perpendicular electric fields efficiently control the stability and transitions of Chern bands in twisted bilayer-trilayer graphene. The interplay of stacking, twist angle, and electric field is clarified for engineering topological narrow bands with tunable Chern numbers in realistic twisted multilayer graphene systems.

Abstract: We investigate the low-energy band structure and topology of twisted bilayer--trilayer graphene with four stacking configurations: AB--ABC, BA--ABC, AB--ABA, and BA--ABA. Using both tight-binding and continuum models, we first establish that the two approaches show good agreement in the band structure in low-energy regime. We then study the evolution of the flat bands and their valley Chern numbers as functions of twist angle, perpendicular electric field, and the self-consistent Hartree potential. At relatively large twist angles and under electric field, we find a topological transition between the narrow bands, with the total Chern number of the flat bands following the Chern number sum rules derived from the chiral-limit description. We also observe another type of topological transition when the flat bands hybridize with adjacent remote bands, where gap closing and reopening processes lead to Chern number and charge density transfer. By constructing topological phase diagrams in the space of twist angle and electric field, we show that the perpendicular electric field provides an efficient tuning knob for controlling the stability and transitions of the Chern bands. Finally, we find that the Hartree potential mainly induce weak band shifts and reshaping in the narrow bands. However, with a combination of Hartree potential and the electric fields, the narrow bands show rich topological phase diagram. Our results clarify the interplay between the stacking, twist angle and electric field in manipulating the narrow bands and their topology in twisted bilayer--trilayer graphene, and provide guidance for engineering topological narrow bands with tunable Chern numbers in realistic twisted multilayer graphene systems.

</details>


### [153] [The First Magic Angle Beyond the Chiral Limit in Twisted Bilayer Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08347)
*Leonardo A. Navarro-Labastida,Pierre A. Pantaleon,Francisco Guinea,Gerardo G. Naumis*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors develop a squared-Hamiltonian framework for twisted bilayer graphene beyond the chiral limit, showing that lattice relaxation renormalizes tunneling to a special confinement point where the symmetric confinement potential becomes nearly uniform. This balance, combined with a competing inter-sublattice current-like channel, explains why the first magic angle remains robust while higher-order magic angles are destabilized by remote-band hybridization and AA-region localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain the puzzling experimental observation that the first magic angle in twisted bilayer graphene remains robust under lattice relaxation, while higher-order magic angles are strongly destabilized—a phenomenon not captured by perturbative treatments of the non-chiral Bistritzer-MacDonald model.

Method: A squared-Hamiltonian description of twisted bilayer graphene starting from the non-chiral Bistritzer-MacDonald model with finite same-sublattice tunneling. The analysis shows lattice relaxation reshapes the effective confinement landscape rather than acting as a simple perturbation, identifying a special confinement point where oscillatory parts of the symmetric confinement potential nearly cancel, and introducing an inter-sublattice current-like channel competing with the chiral orbital channel.

Result: The realistic relaxation-renormalized tunneling ratio sits close to a special confinement point placing the system near a nearly uniform confinement regime. The first magic angle survives because confinement and current-like contributions remain balanced, while higher-order magic angles lose this balance through stronger remote-band hybridization and enhanced real-space localization around AA regions, breaking the chiral magic-angle hierarchy.

Conclusion: The work provides a single-particle mechanism for the breakdown of the chiral magic-angle hierarchy and clarifies that the experimentally relevant first magic angle remains the most stable remnant of chiral flat-band structure under realistic lattice relaxation conditions.

Abstract: We develop a squared-Hamiltonian description of twisted bilayer graphene beyond the chiral limit to explain why the first magic angle remains robust under lattice relaxation, while higher-order magic angles are strongly destabilized. Starting from the non-chiral Bistritzer--MacDonald model with finite same-sublattice tunneling, we show that lattice relaxation reshapes the effective confinement landscape rather than acting as a simple perturbation of the chiral theory. A central result is that the realistic relaxation-renormalized tunneling ratio lies close to a special confinement point where the oscillatory part of the symmetric confinement potential nearly cancels. This places realistic twisted bilayer graphene near a nearly uniform confinement regime. At the same time, finite same-sublattice tunneling activates an additional inter-sublattice current-like channel that competes with the chiral orbital channel. The first magic angle survives because these confinement and current-like contributions remain balanced, whereas higher-order magic angles lose this balance through stronger remote-band hybridization and enhanced real-space localization around AA regions. Our results provide a single-particle mechanism for the breakdown of the chiral magic-angle hierarchy and clarify why the experimentally relevant first magic angle remains the most stable remnant of the chiral flat-band structure.

</details>


### [154] [Impurity as a probe of Berry curvature and wavefunction winding in gapped two-band models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08447)
*Reda Nabil,Pascal Simon,Andrej Mesaros*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that quasiparticle interference near non-magnetic impurities can be used to extract non-local band properties such as Berry curvature and wavefunction winding in multi-valley systems, applicable to various gapped band structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for experimental probes of topological band properties like Berry curvature and wavefunction winding, which are typically difficult to measure directly. The authors explore whether LDOS around impurities can serve as such a probe.

Method: The authors analyze minimal two-band models with multiple valleys and compute the local density of states (LDOS) induced by point-like non-magnetic impurities for gapped systems with Dirac, semi-Dirac, quadratic touching, and higher-order touching dispersions. They examine how LDOS contours around the impurity encode wavefunction winding and establish a general relation between Berry curvature, winding number, and dispersion parameters.

Result: For sufficiently strong impurity potentials, the LDOS along a contour around the impurity unambiguously reveals the wavefunction winding number, unlike gapless Dirac-like systems where radial LDOS oscillations obscure the winding. The paper also derives a general connection linking local Berry curvature in a valley to winding number and dispersion parameters, making Berry curvature extractable from spectroscopic LDOS data.

Conclusion: Quasiparticle interference imaging provides a viable pathway to directly observe local Berry curvature and wavefunction winding in multi-valley systems with gapped band structures, offering a spectroscopic route to non-local band topology.

Abstract: We explore the possibility of using quasiparticle interference near impurities to extract non-local properties of bands. Focusing on the minimal two-band description of a system with multiple valleys, we show that the local density of states (LDOS) induced by non-magnetic impurities can effectively probe both the Berry curvature and the wavefunction winding, locally in each valley. We analyze gapped models based on Dirac, semi-Dirac, quadratic touching, and higher-order touching energy dispersions, in presence of a point-like potential scatterer. We find that for strong enough impurity potentials the LDOS along a contour around the impurity unambiguously shows the wavefunction winding number, in contrast to the case of gapless Dirac-like dispersions where the winding can be precluded by LDOS oscillations in the radial direction. We also establish a general connection between, on the one hand, the Berry curvature as a function of momentum locally in a valley, and on the other hand, the wavefunction winding number and the energy dispersion parameters, making hence the local Berry curvature directly observable from spectroscopic LDOS information.

</details>


### [155] [Memory Control of Ice Growth During Non-Equilibrium Freezing of Water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08535)
*Abhigyan Hazarika,Sudeep N. Punnathanam,Biman Bagchi,Prabal K. Maiti*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Using TIP4P/Ice molecular dynamics simulations, the authors find that freezing time of supercooled water at 250 K depends non-monotonically on the initial temperature, with slowest crystallization near 300 K—a molecular analogue of the Mpemba effect. A non-stationary generalized Langevin framework reveals two-time memory kernels encode thermal history, while hydrogen-bonded ring analysis identifies five-membered rings as kinetic traps regulating ice nucleation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classic non-equilibrium problem: whether and how thermal history (initial temperature) influences the crystallization of supercooled water remains unclear. Understanding this memory-dependence is fundamental to non-equilibrium phase transitions and could explain anomalous cooling phenomena.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations using the TIP4P/Ice water model; rapid quenching of water from various initial temperatures Ti to 250 K; monitoring evolution of hydrogen-bonded ring structures; non-stationary generalized Langevin equation framework to analyze two-time memory kernels; structural correlation analysis of ring-type distributions.

Result: Freezing time tF depends non-monotonically on initial temperature Ti, with slowest crystallization near 300 K—implying initially hotter water can freeze faster than cooler water (molecular-scale Mpemba effect). Memory kernels retain thermal history information and directly influence crystallization; five-membered rings act as kinetic traps while correlations among ring types gate access to ice-like motifs.

Conclusion: Structural memory—encoded in hydrogen-bonded ring correlations and two-time memory kernels—is a key driver of non-equilibrium phase transitions, providing a molecular origin for memory-driven freezing and the observed Mpemba-like behavior.

Abstract: Freezing of supercooled water is a classic non-equilibrium problem, yet the influence of thermal history on crystallization remains unclear. Using molecular dynamics simulations with the TIP4P/Ice model, we investigate how the initial temperature $T_i$ shapes freezing following rapid quenching to 250 K. By monitoring the evolution of hydrogen-bonded ring structures, we find a non-monotonic dependence of the freezing time $t_F$ on $T_i$, with the slowest crystallization occurring near 300 K. Remarkably, this means that initially hotter water can freeze faster than cooler water, a molecular-scale analogue of the Mpemba effect. A non-stationary generalized Langevin equation framework shows that two-time memory kernels retain information about the system's thermal past, directly influencing crystallization dynamics. Structural analysis further reveals that five-membered rings act as kinetic traps, while correlations among ring types regulate the accessibility of ice-like motifs. These results uncover a molecular origin of memory-driven freezing and establish structural memory as a key driver of non-equilibrium phase transitions.

</details>


### [156] [Equivalent Circuit Representation and Thermodynamic Limitations of Non-Adiabatic Spin-Transfer Torque effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08544)
*Wataru Koshibae*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents an equivalent circuit representation of the Thiele equation for current-driven domain walls, showing that the non-adiabatic spin-transfer torque (β-term) requires active circuit elements (negative dissipation) and thus contradicts thermodynamic principles if the driving current is the only power source. This suggests that interpretations relying solely on the β-term are incomplete and need to include effects beyond pure spin dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine whether the non-adiabatic spin-transfer torque (β-term) in the Thiele equation is physically consistent with thermodynamic principles, by translating the spin dynamics into an equivalent circuit framework where standard energetic constraints apply.

Method: Construct an equivalent circuit representation of the Thiele equation for current-driven domain walls, then analyze what circuit elements are required to represent each term, particularly the β-term.

Result: The β-term cannot be represented by passive elements (resistors, inductors, capacitors) and necessarily requires active elements corresponding to negative dissipation, creating a fundamental contradiction with thermodynamics when the driving current is the sole power source.

Conclusion: The equivalent circuit theory reveals that experiment interpretation based solely on the β-term is incomplete, and physics distinct from pure spin dynamics must be considered to resolve the thermodynamic inconsistency.

Abstract: We present an equivalent circuit representation of the Thiele equation for the current-driven domain walls. We show that the non-adiabatic spin-transfer torque (β-term) cannot be represented by passive elements alone (such as resistors, inductors, and capacitors) and necessarily requires active elements, corresponding to negative dissipation. Consequently, assuming that the driving current is the only power source for the texture dynamics, directly incorporating the β-term introduces a fundamental contradiction with thermodynamic principles. This equivalent circuit theory indicates that the interpretation of experiments solely through the β-term does not fully capture the underlying physics, highlighting the need to account for effects distinct from the spin dynamics.

</details>


### [157] [Wafer-Level 1/f Noise Characterization of Scaled P-Type Nanosheets and Comparison with Planar HKMG Transistors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08674)
*Ruben Asanovski,Anabela Veloso,Ben Kaczer,Naoto Horiguchi,Jacopo Franco*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A wafer-level 1/f noise study of Gate-All-Around p-type nanosheet transistors compared against planar HKMG devices shows comparable effective trap densities, indicating that the GAA architecture does not introduce additional noise and noise remains governed by gate stack quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the impact of the GAA nanosheet device architecture on reliability by benchmarking its 1/f noise (a sensitive reliability indicator) against established planar HKMG transistors with identical gate stacks.

Method: Statistical analysis of wafer-level 1/f noise data from 188 nanosheets to extract effective trap density in the dielectric, compared against large-area planar HKMG devices with matching gate stack, comparable thermal budgets, and no reliability anneals.

Result: The effective trap density extracted from 1/f noise is comparable between planar and nanosheet architectures, indicating GAA nanosheets do not exhibit increased noise.

Conclusion: Transitioning to GAA nanosheet technology does not degrade noise performance; noise remains dominated by gate stack quality rather than device architecture.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive wafer-level 1/f noise study of scaled Gate-All-Around (GAA) p-type nanosheet transistors and benchmark them against large-area planar High- \k{appa} Metal Gate (HKMG) devices to assess the impact of device architecture on reliability. By statistically analyzing noise data from 188 nanosheets, we extract the effective trap density in the dielectric and compare it to that of planar transistors with identical gate stack, comparable processing thermal budgets, and no specific reliability anneals. The effective trap density extracted from 1/f noise is comparable in planar and nanosheet architectures. This indicates that transitioning to GAA nanosheets does not increase noise, which remains governed by gate stack quality.

</details>


### [158] [Observation of linear magnetoresistance and planar Hall effect in the Dirac semimetal PdTe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08675)
*Bhawana Mehra,Bibek Ranjan Satapathy,V S Abhishek,Reena,Ritu Gupta,Yogesh Singh,S. Chakraverty,Amit Vashist*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports the first observation of linear magnetoresistance (LMR) and planar Hall effect (PHE) in high-quality single-crystal PdTe, a Dirac semimetal and superconductor. The LMR is attributed to disorder-driven mobility fluctuations rather than the Abrikosov quantum-limit mechanism, and the PHE originates from anisotropic orbital magnetoresistance rather than the chiral anomaly, both understood within a semiclassical transport framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the superconducting properties of PdTe (a Dirac semimetal hosting both Dirac physics and superconductivity) have been extensively studied, its detailed magnetotransport phenomena have remained unexplored, leaving a gap in understanding the interplay between topological band structure and transport behavior in this material.

Method: High-quality single crystals of PdTe were used to measure magnetoresistance in both in-plane (B‖I) and out-of-plane (B⊥I) configurations. The MR was analyzed as a function of field and temperature, including a crossover from parabolic to linear dependence at a critical field Bc. Planar Hall effect measurements were performed by rotating the magnetic field within the sample plane, with field- and temperature-dependent PHE amplitudes and parametric plots analyzed to identify the origin of the effect.

Result: Temperature-dependent unsaturated LMR was observed in both field configurations. The MR exhibits a parabolic-to-linear crossover at Bc, and analysis indicates disorder-driven mobility fluctuations as the origin. Prominent PHE was observed; field/temperature dependence and parametric plot analysis suggest it arises predominantly from anisotropic orbital magnetoresistance, not the chiral anomaly.

Conclusion: Despite the Dirac point lying close to the Fermi level in PdTe, the observed LMR and PHE can be fully explained within a semiclassical transport framework. This underscores the importance of distinguishing topological (quantum) mechanisms from conventional classical transport mechanisms when interpreting magnetotransport in topological materials.

Abstract: PdTe is a Dirac semimetal that also exhibits superconductivity, providing an intriguing platform to explore topological superconductivity and unconventional magnetotransport phenomena. While the superconducting properties of PdTe have been extensively studied in recent years, the detailed magnetotransport phenomena have remained unexplored. Here, we present the first observation of linear magnetoresistance (LMR) and the planar Hall effect (PHE) in a high-quality single crystal of PdTe. We observe temperature-dependent unsaturated LMR in both in-plane ($B \parallel I$) and out-of-plane ($B \perp I$) configurations. The magnetoresistance (MR) shows a crossover from parabolic to linear dependence at the critical field Bc, and detailed analysis indicates that disorder-driven mobility fluctuations are the origin rather than the Abrikosov quantum-limit mechanism. Furthermore, prominent PHE has been observed by rotating the magnetic field within the plane of the sample. The detailed analysis of the field and temperature dependence of PHE-amplitude, along with the parametric plot, suggests that PHE originates predominantly from the anisotropic orbital magnetoresistance rather than the chiral anomaly. Our results demonstrate that although the Dirac point is close to the Fermi level, the observed LMR and PHE in the Dirac semimetal PdTe can be understood within a semiclassical transport framework, highlighting the importance of distinguishing between topological and conventional classical transport mechanisms in topological materials

</details>


### [159] [Decorated electronic kagome lattice in twisted bilayer germanene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08750)
*Dennis J. Klaassen,Rian A. M. Ligthart,Andrés R. Botello Mendez,Lumen Eek,Esra van 't Westende,Paul L. de Boeij,Y. Wang,D. Vanmaekelbergh,Pantelis Bampoulis,Cristiane Morais Smith,Ingmar Swart,Zeila Zanolli,Harold J. W. Zandvliet*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Commensurate large-angle twisted bilayer germanene on Ge2Pt produces two electronically distinct moiré phases—odd-parity (under sublattice exchange) twisted bilayers are semiconducting, while even-parity ones are metallic and host an empty-state resonance with an emergent decorated kagome structure of C3 symmetry, establishing twisted germanene as a platform for kagome-like electronic states in a buckled 2D material.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial kagome lattices offer routes to electronic flat bands, geometric frustration, and correlation-driven phases, yet their realization in atomically controlled two-dimensional materials remains scarce. The authors aim to create such kagome-like electronic states in a real atomically controlled 2D platform.

Method: The study combines scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) measurements with density functional theory (DFT) calculations on commensurate twisted bilayer germanene grown on Ge2Pt, examining large-angle moiré phases and classifying them by parity under sublattice exchange.

Result: Two electronically distinct large-angle moiré phases are found: odd-parity twisted bilayers are semiconducting, whereas even-parity ones are metallic. The even-parity twisted bilayers exhibit an empty-state resonance showing an emergent decorated kagome structure with C3 symmetry.

Conclusion: Large-angle twisted germanene serves as a platform for engineering kagome-like electronic states in a buckled two-dimensional material, expanding the possibilities for realizing artificial kagome physics in atomically controlled 2D systems.

Abstract: Artificial kagome lattices provide a route to electronic flat bands, geometric frustration, and correlation driven phases, but their realization in atomically controlled two-dimensional materials remains scarce. Here, we show that commensurate twisted bilayer germanene on Ge2Pt produces two electronically distinct large-angle moiré phases. Scanning tunneling microscopy measurements and density functional theory calculations reveal that commensurate twisted bilayers that are odd under an exchange of sublattices are semiconducting, whereas the twisted bilayers with an even parity are metallic. The twisted bilayers with an even parity host an empty state resonance that exhibits an emergent decorated kagome structure with a C3 symmetry. These results establish large-angle twisted germanene as a platform for engineering kagome-like electronic states in a buckled two-dimensional material.

</details>


### [160] [Non-perturbative theory of valley splitting in Si qubits from variational wave function: periodic effects of shear strain and asymptotic freedom in the wiggle-well potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08839)
*Johannes L. P. Steinschuld,Hendrik J. Bluhm,Seyed Akbar Jafari*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper develops an analytical two-band effective-mass framework using the valley-orbit (valleyor) basis to treat intervalley coupling in silicon quantum-well qubits. Closed-form variational solutions are derived for shear strain and wiggle-well potentials, revealing oscillatory |sin(k_min L)| dependence for strain-induced splitting and resonance-driven crossover behavior for wiggle-well potentials, with the latter showing an "asymptotic freedom"-like regime where the empty-box energy dominates near resonance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Valley splitting sets the energy scale at which spin and valley degrees of freedom hybridize in silicon quantum-well qubits, but its sensitivity to interface structure makes it difficult to predict accurately. A tractable analytical framework is needed to understand and control this crucial parameter for spin-qubit device engineering.

Method: The authors formulate a two-band effective-mass model for intervalley coupling in a finite quantum well using the valleyor basis. For uniform shear strain (z-independent coupling V_s τ_2), they construct a variational state from exact plane-wave valleyors and a hard-wall envelope, yielding closed-form expressions. For wiggle-well coupling (V_w cos(k_w z) τ_1), the valleyor representation is used to analyze the resonance crossover. Exact diagonalization is used to benchmark the variational results.

Result: The variational result for shear strain agrees with exact diagonalization to within ~1% for realistic couplings and provides a simple prescription for tuning shear strain away from nodes to enhance valley splitting. Finite barrier heights primarily renormalize the effective quantum-well width. For wiggle-well coupling, a crossover near k_w=2k_1 resonance from wiggle-dominated splitting to orbital quantization is found, with the empty-box energy scale dominating the near-resonant valley splitting at high amplitudes. Away from resonance, splitting shows broadly peaked resonance with diffraction-like side-lobes in detuning.

Conclusion: The valleyor-based analytical framework provides accurate, closed-form predictions for valley splitting under shear strain and wiggle-well potentials, enabling practical tuning strategies for silicon spin qubits. The wiggle-well resonance behavior, characterized as "asymptotic freedom for spin qubits," reveals a regime where the intrinsic quantum-well confinement energy governs the splitting despite strong potential perturbations.

Abstract: Valley splitting sets the energy scale at which spin and valley degrees of freedom hybridize in silicon quantum-well qubits, but its sensitivity to interface structure makes it difficult to predict. Using the valleyor basis, we formulate a two-band effective-mass model for intervalley coupling in a finite quantum well and develop an analytical framework for treating non-perturbative effects of shear strain and wiggle-well potentials. For a $z$-independent coupling $V_sτ_2$, corresponding to uniform shear strain, we construct a variational state from the exact plane-wave valleyors and a hard-wall envelope. This yields closed-form expressions for the two lowest states and their splitting, including the characteristic oscillatory $|\sin(k_{\rm min}L)|$ dependence. The variational result agrees with exact diagonalization to within $\sim1\%$ for realistic couplings, and provides a simple prescription for tuning the shear strain away from the nodes to enhance the valley splitting. We further show that finite barrier heights primarily renormalize the effective width of the quantum well while preserving the oscillatory dependence. For a wiggle-well coupling $V_w\cos(k_wz)τ_1$, the valleyor representation reveals a crossover near the $k_w=2k_1$ resonance from wiggle-dominated splitting to orbital quantization. Remarkably, at high wiggle-well amplitudes, the empty-box energy scale predominantly dictates the near-resonant valley splitting, while the wiggle-well potential enters only as a sub-leading correction. This behavior can be viewed as asymptotic freedom for spin qubits. Away from resonance, the splitting exhibits a broadly peaked resonance with diffraction-like side-lobes as a function of detuning.

</details>


### [161] [Probing Antialtermagnetism via Orbital-Field-Induced Spin Splitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08996)
*Zi-Ting Sun*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Orbital effects of an in-plane magnetic field can convert the hidden spin texture of antialtermagnets into observable spin splittings of opposite momentum parity, enabling spectroscopic identification of the underlying magnetic order without layer resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Layer compensation in antialtermagnets conceals spin polarization behind a spin-degenerate bulk spectrum, preventing spectroscopic identification of the underlying magnetic order.

Method: Using a minimal model, the authors analyze how the orbital effect of an in-plane magnetic field reveals hidden spin textures, and employ band sum rules and net spin polarization measurements in the weak-field regime to resolve the wave character (parity and form factor) of the hidden exchange order.

Result: The induced spin splitting has opposite momentum parity that restores spectroscopic access. Band sum rules isolate the momentum gradient of the form factor, differentiating hidden even-parity orders, while odd-parity sectors show characteristic field-amplitude and angular dependences in induced net spin polarization.

Conclusion: Orbital-field-induced spin splitting provides a generic route to identifying the hidden altermagnetic order parameter without requiring layer resolution.

Abstract: Layer compensation can conceal spin polarization behind a spin-degenerate bulk spectrum in antialtermagnets, preventing spectroscopic identification of the underlying magnetic order. Here we show that the orbital effect of an in-plane magnetic field converts the hidden spin texture into an observable spin splitting of opposite momentum parity, thereby restoring spectroscopic access. Once the parity of the hidden exchange order is determined, complementary response functions in the weak-field regime further resolve its wave character: within a minimal model, we illustrate that a band sum rule over the induced band splittings isolates the momentum gradient of the form factor and differentiates hidden even-parity orders, whereas for odd-parity sectors the induced net spin polarization exhibits characteristic field-amplitude and angular dependences. These results establish orbital-field-induced spin splitting as a generic route to identifying the hidden altermagnetic order parameter without layer resolution.

</details>


### [162] [Hilbert space connectivity in non-Hermitian many-body systems: emergent scale-dependent amplification and constraint-induced skin localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09017)
*Zichang Hao,Wen-Tan Xue,Ching Hua Lee*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper shows that Hilbert space connectivity fundamentally controls spectral amplification and state localization in non-Hermitian many-body systems, with complex Hilbert space graphs featuring competing amplification channels and global feedback loops that lead to unconventional scaling, localization, and skin effects—establishing Hilbert space connectivity as a versatile control knob for many-body non-Hermitian critical transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Hilbert space fragmentation has been linked to exotic many-body phenomena in Hermitian settings (quantum scars, fractons), the role of Hilbert space connectivity in non-Hermitian systems—where it may have an even more universal and fundamental influence—remains unexplored. Non-Hermitian many-body Hilbert space graphs are far more complex than real-space lattices due to competing amplification channels and global feedback loops connecting remote Fock states via particle symmetry.

Method: The authors analyze non-Hermitian many-body Hilbert space graphs, examining how their intricate connectivity structure—including competing amplification channels and global feedback loops between particle-symmetric remote Fock states—controls spectral amplification and state localization. They also investigate how particle occupation constraints selectively remove Hilbert space pathways, modifying localization behavior.

Result: The analysis reveals amplification behavior with unconventional scaling and localization properties originating from the complex graph structures. Particle occupation constraints can remove selected Hilbert space pathways, producing robust unipolar and asymmetric bipolar skin localization even in processes that are otherwise reciprocal. These findings extend beyond simple interacting bosonic models.

Conclusion: Hilbert space connectivity serves as a versatile control knob for many-body non-Hermitian critical transitions, with implications beyond interacting bosonic systems. The results establish a fundamental link between graph topology of the Hilbert space and spectral amplification/localization phenomena in non-Hermitian many-body physics.

Abstract: Various exotic many-body phenomena such as quantum scars and fractons have been linked to Hilbert space fragmentation. In this work, we find that in non-Hermitian settings, Hilbert space connectivity has an even more universal and fundamental influence, tightly controlling the nature of spectral amplification and state localization. Far more complicated than real-space lattices, non-Hermitian many-body Hilbert space graphs not only possess intricate competing amplification channels, but also global feedback loops connecting remote Fock states related by particle symmetry. These features lead to amplification behavior with unconventional scaling and localization properties. Particle occupation constraints can furthermore remove selected Hilbert space pathways, leading to robust unipolar and asymmetric bipolar skin localization in otherwise reciprocal processes. These results extend beyond simple interacting bosonic models and establish Hilbert space connectivity as a versatile control knob for many-body non-Hermitian critical transitions.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.str-el'></div>

# cond-mat.str-el [[Back]](#toc)

### [163] [Kibble-Zurek Dynamics in Two-dimensional Frustrated Systems with a Neural Foundation-state Subspace Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05556)
*Linda Mauron,Luciano Loris Viteritti,Zakari Denis,Riccardo Rende,Giuseppe Carleo*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Neural Foundation-state Subspace (NFS) method for simulating near-adiabatic dynamics of quantum many-body systems across continuous phase transitions in two dimensions. The method uses a foundation neural-network quantum state to represent the ground-state manifold along the driving path, with a small fidelity-selected variational subspace enabling efficient real-time evolution. Validated on the 2D transverse-field Ising model (recovering Kibble-Zurek scaling), the method is then applied to the frustrated J1-J2 Heisenberg model up to 16x16 clusters, providing strong numerical evidence of Kibble-Zurek mechanism across the Néel-to-spin-liquid transition at J2/J1=0.49 with exponents ν=1.23(15) and η=0.409(19) at fixed z=1, supporting continuous critical behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accessing the universal scaling regime of strongly interacting quantum many-body systems driven across continuous phase transitions in two dimensions is numerically challenging because it requires accurate real-time evolution of correlated many-body states across many system sizes and driving rates. A more efficient numerical method is needed.

Method: The NFS method constructs a foundation neural-network quantum state that spans the ground-state manifold along the driving path. A small subset selected by fidelity defines a fixed variational subspace, reducing the many-body Schrödinger equation to evolution of a few linear coefficients. Projected operators are reusable across ramp times, making the approach computationally efficient.

Result: The method reproduces expected Kibble-Zurek scaling and critical exponents consistent with ground-state quantum Monte Carlo for the 2D transverse-field Ising model. For the frustrated J1-J2 Heisenberg model up to 16x16 clusters, it yields ν=1.23(15) and η=0.409(19) at fixed z=1 across the Néel-to-spin-liquid transition at J2/J1=0.49, consistent with static estimates and supporting continuous critical behavior.

Conclusion: The NFS method provides an efficient and accurate approach for probing dynamical critical phenomena in 2D quantum systems, delivering strong numerical evidence for the Kibble-Zurek mechanism and continuous criticality at the Néel-to-spin-liquid transition in the frustrated Heisenberg model.

Abstract: Universal scaling generated when a strongly interacting quantum many-body system is driven across a continuous phase transition provides a dynamical probe of equilibrium criticality. Accessing this regime numerically in two dimensions is challenging because it requires accurate real-time evolution of correlated many-body states over many system sizes and driving rates. We introduce a Neural Foundation-state Subspace (NFS) method for near-adiabatic dynamics. A foundation neural-network quantum state represents the ground-state manifold along the driving path, and a small fidelity-selected subset defines a fixed variational subspace. The many-body Schrödinger equation then reduces to the evolution of a few linear coefficients, with projected operators reusable across ramp times. We validate the method on the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model, recovering the expected Kibble-Zurek scaling and critical exponents in quantitative agreement with ground-state quantum Monte Carlo estimates. Applied to the frustrated square-lattice $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg model up to $16 \times 16$ clusters, our approach provides strong numerical evidence of Kibble-Zurek mechanism across the Néel-to-spin-liquid transition at $J_2/J_1=0.49$, yielding ${ν=1.23(15)}$ and ${η=0.409(19)}$ at fixed $z=1$, consistent with static estimates and supporting the proposed continuous critical behavior.

</details>


### [164] [Deviation from Fermi-liquid $T^2$ resistivity caused by collective transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05724)
*Kamran Behnia*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper examines deviations from Fermi-liquid T² resistivity behavior and shows they have opposite signs in weakly vs. strongly correlated metals. Weakly correlated metals show upward deviation (extra phonon scattering), while strongly correlated metals show downward deviation (reduced scattering or additional conduction), possibly via a sound mode akin to that identified in ³He. Experimental data from UPt₃, Sr₂RuO₄, and heavily overdoped LSCO show excess conductivity amplitudes at T/T_F ≈ 0.02 comparable to ³He, suggesting a common origin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why departure from Fermi-liquid T² resistivity behavior occurs at finite temperature and has opposite signs in different types of Fermi liquids, potentially revealing a universal mechanism of additional conduction in strongly correlated metals.

Method: The authors compare the sign and magnitude of deviations from quadratic temperature dependence of resistivity across weakly correlated metals (attributed to phonon scattering) and strongly correlated metals (attributed to reduced scattering or additional conduction channels). They draw analogy with normal liquid ³He, where a sound mode contributes to heat transport, and compare normalized excess conductivity amplitudes across several correlated metals.

Result: Weakly correlated Fermi liquids show upward deviation from T² resistivity due to additional phonon scattering. Strongly correlated metals show downward deviation, implying reduced scattering or an extra conduction channel. The normalized amplitude of excess conductivity at T/T_F ≈ 0.02 in UPt₃, Sr₂RuO₄, and overdoped LSCO is comparable to that in ³He, where a sound-mode heat transport channel was identified.

Conclusion: The deviations from Fermi-liquid behavior are qualitatively different in weakly vs. strongly correlated systems. In strongly correlated metals, a sound-mode-like conduction channel—analogous to that in ³He—may smoothly transform, across T_F, into the classical-liquid thermal conductivity described by the Bridgman formula. The data suggest, but do not establish, a common origin across these systems.

Abstract: `Fermi-liquid behavior' is nowadays used as a shorthand for quadratic temperature dependence of the electrical resistivity. In a metal, for this to occur, electrons must be cooled well below the Fermi degeneracy temperature, $T_{\mathrm{F}}$. A departure from this behavior is observed at finite temperature. Here, we note that this departure has opposite signs in two distinct types of Fermi liquids. In weakly correlated ones, the upward departure implies a higher exponent of the inelastic resistivity, attributable to \textit{additional scattering} by phonons. In contrast, there is a downward deviation in strongly correlated metals, which implies either \textit{reduced scattering} or \textit{additional conduction}, as in the case of normal liquid $^3$He, in which a distinct contribution to heat transport by a sound mode has been recently identified. Such a channel of conduction smoothly transforms across $T_{\mathrm{F}}$ to the one captured by the Bridgman formula for thermal conductivity of classical liquids. In three metals (UPt$_3$, Sr$_2$RuO$_4$, and heavily overdoped LSCO), according to the available experimental data, the normalized amplitude of excess conductivity at $T/T_{\mathrm{F}}\simeq0.02$ is comparable to what has been seen in $^3$He, suggesting, without establishing, a common origin.

</details>


### [165] [Ultrafast electron crystallography reveals the atomic pathway of a light-driven correlated insulator-to-metal transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05747)
*Arthur Niedermayr,Hongyi Xu,Chin Shen Ong,Patrik Thunström,Michael Yannai,Jianyu Wu,Gaolong Cao,Lior Kornblum,Ido Kaminer,Oscar Grånäs,Xiaodong Zou,Jonas Weissenrieder*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper introduces ultrafast three-dimensional electron diffraction to resolve transient V and O atomic coordinates during the photoinduced insulator-to-metal transition in VO2, revealing a linear real-space atomic trajectory (V-V dimerization and zigzag motions) that directly couples to the electronic gap collapse, transforming time-resolved diffraction into transient crystallography.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultrafast phase transitions in correlated materials have been inferred from selected diffraction peak intensities or displacements, leaving the underlying three-dimensional atomic trajectories unknown. Resolving these trajectories is essential to identify which atomic motions drive changes in electronic properties and how they couple to electronic degrees of freedom.

Method: The authors introduce ultrafast three-dimensional electron diffraction, which enables refinement of the transient unit cell and internal V and O coordinates during the M1-to-R phase transition in VO2. They combine this with quantum many-body calculations treating each V-V pair as a correlated unit.

Result: Refined atomic coordinates follow a linear trajectory in real space during the transition, contrasting with nonlinear or sequential pathways inferred from indirect observables. Many-body calculations show dimerization creates the level splitting that opens the electronic gap, further enhanced by nonlocal electronic interactions between paired V atoms; the gap collapses when dimerization is lost.

Conclusion: This work turns time-resolved diffraction from order-parameter tracking into transient crystallography, directly connecting atomic trajectories to electronic mechanisms in correlated materials.

Abstract: Ultrafast phase transitions in correlated materials are often inferred from selected diffraction peak intensities or diffraction peak displacements, leaving the underlying three-dimensional atomic trajectories elusive. Resolving these trajectories is essential for identifying which atomic motions drive changes in electronic properties and how they couple to electronic degrees of freedom. We address this challenge in vanadium dioxide (VO2), a correlated oxide with a near-room-temperature transition between the insulating monoclinic (M1) phase and metallic rutile (R) phase. For this purpose, we introduce ultrafast three-dimensional electron diffraction, which enables refinement of the transient unit cell and internal V and O coordinates, revealing the V-V dimerization and zigzag motion during the phase transition. The refined atomic coordinates follow a linear trajectory in real space during the transition, in contrast to nonlinear or sequential transformation pathways inferred from more indirect observables in earlier work. Quantum many-body calculations treating each V-V pair as a correlated unit show that dimerization creates the level splitting responsible for the electronic gap, which is further enhanced by nonlocal electronic interactions between the paired V atoms. The gap collapses when dimerization is lost. This work turns time-resolved diffraction from order-parameter tracking into transient crystallography, directly connecting atomic trajectories to electronic mechanisms in correlated materials.

</details>


### [166] [Magnetization processes and spin dynamics across field-induced phase transitions in the quasi-two-dimensional quantum magnet Cu$_2$(OH)$_3$Br](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06246)
*Anneke Reinold,Dirk Wulferding,Laur Peedu,Kirill Amelin,Urmas Nagel,Toomas Rõõm,Zhiying Zhao,Patrick Pilch,Changqing Zhu,Hans Engelkamp,Lucas Berger,Denis I. Gorbunov,Yurii Skourski,Kwang-Yong Choi,Thomas Lorenz,Zhe Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper investigates the magnetic-field-dependent magnetization and spin dynamics of the quasi-two-dimensional spin-$1/2$ magnet Cu₂(OH)₃Br, which features coupled ferromagnetic Cu1 and antiferromagnetic Cu2 spin chains. Using terahertz spectroscopy, Raman spectroscopy, and high-field magnetization measurements, the authors reveal strong field-direction anisotropy: spin-flop transitions and abrupt spectral reconstructions occur for B∥a and B∥c*, while B∥b shows continuous spectral evolution with progressive polarization of the ferromagnetic subsystem, eventually leading to weakened inter-chain coupling at high fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how applied magnetic fields reorganize the coupled ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic chain subsystems in quasi-two-dimensional spin-1/2 magnets, where competing interactions give rise to rich field-induced phase transitions and anisotropic low-energy excitations.
