Compared with the full Kadanoff--Baym equations, its mean-field propagators substantially reduce computational cost, enabling simulations with computational effort that scales linearly with propagation time. However, the time-diagonal structure of the Generalized Kadanoff--Baym Ansatz obscures spectral properties within the collision integral of the transport equation. Here, we recover and investigate these hidden spectral properties using the extended Generalized Kadanoff--Baym Ansatz. For a Hubbard cluster, we compare ground-state spectral functions obtained with the extended Generalized Kadanoff--Baym Ansatz against those from standard Kadanoff--Baym equations. At moderate interaction strengths, the spectral functions show good agreement. At large interaction strengths, however, significant deviations emerge, and the spectral function obtained with the extended Generalized Kadanoff--Baym Ansatz becomes negative. These results demonstrate that, despite its computational advantages, the Generalized Kadanoff--Baym Ansatz has important limitations in strongly interacting regimes and may produce unphysical spectral properties when interactions become sufficiently large.

</details>


### [190] [Green's Functions from Sample-based Krylov Quantum Diagonalization: An Impurity Solver for Dynamical Mean-Field Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09147)
*Jay Patel,Chakradhar Rangi,Ka-Ming Tam*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The authors extend the sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) method from ground-state calculations to computing single-particle Green's functions. By sampling unitary Krylov subspaces in the N±1 particle sectors and evaluating sector-connecting overlaps classically, the method reconstructs Green's functions via a Lanczos continued fraction while keeping the quantum hardware requirement to short-time evolution preparation and sampling only. Tested on the single-impurity Anderson model in chain geometry, it reproduces spectral features across interaction strengths spanning the metal-insulator transition using a small fraction of the Hilbert space, suggesting practical advantages for DMFT impurity solvers on near-term quantum hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable Green's function (spectral) calculations—not just ground states—using shallow-circuit, ancilla-free quantum algorithms, thereby making DMFT impurity solvers with more bath sites practical on near-term quantum hardware.

Method: Generalizes SKQD from ground-state problems to Green's functions by constructing unitary Krylov subspaces separately in the N+1 and N−1 particle-number sectors, computing sector-connecting overlaps classically, and reconstructing the Green's function via a Lanczos continued fraction. Quantum device only needs to prepare and sample short-time evolutions.

Result: Applied to the particle-hole-symmetric single-impurity Anderson model in chain geometry with discrete bath representation used in DMFT, the method recovers the spectral function using a relatively small fraction of the full Hilbert space. Across interaction strengths spanning the metal-insulator transition, the main spectral features are correctly reproduced.

Conclusion: SKQD-based Green's-function calculations may enable DMFT impurity solvers with a larger number of bath sites on near-term quantum hardware than is currently practical.

Abstract: We generalize the sample-based Krylov quantum diagonalization (SKQD) method from ground-state calculations to the evaluation of single-particle Green's functions. By constructing and sampling unitary Krylov subspaces in the N +/- 1 particle-number sectors and evaluating all sector-connecting overlaps classically, the approach reconstructs the Green's function via a Lanczos continued fraction while retaining the shallow-circuit, ancilla-free character of SKQD. The quantum device is required only to prepare and sample short-time evolutions. Applied to the particle-hole-symmetric single-impurity Anderson model in chain geometry, with the discrete bath representation used in dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT), the method recovers the spectral function using a relatively small fraction of the full Hilbert space. Across a range of interaction strengths that spans the metal-insulator transition, the main spectral features are reproduced. These results suggest that SKQD-based Green's-function calculations may allow DMFT impurity solvers with a larger number of bath sites on near-term quantum hardware than is currently practical.

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [191] [Differentiable Solvation Shell Model for Rational Electrolyte Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05671)
*Hancheng Zhao,Hongyi Lin,Celia Kelly,Venkatasubramanian Viswanathan*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a mean-field Ising model-based framework that predicts Li+ solvation shell composition in electrolytes from donor number, acceptor number, molar ratio, and molecular size. The model is end-to-end differentiable, parameterized directly from MD simulations via gradient-based optimization. It achieves high accuracy (10.7% RMSE, R2=0.87 for shell composition; 2.3% RMSE on free solvent ratio) in LHCE systems at a fraction of MD's computational cost, shows generalizability to other electrolyte systems, and demonstrates design utility by predicting fluorobenzene as a promising diluent in the LiTFSI/tetraglyme system—validated by higher-fidelity MD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rational design of localized high-concentration electrolytes (LHCE) for Li metal batteries is limited by a poor quantitative understanding of how molecular properties govern Li+ solvation shell composition. Empirical DN-based design rules lack a thermodynamic basis, and existing approaches cannot efficiently predict solvation structures for rational electrolyte screening.

Method: The authors build a mean-field framework based on an Ising model that predicts solvation shell composition using donor number (DN), acceptor number (AN), molar ratio, and molecular size as inputs. The framework is end-to-end differentiable, allowing parameters to be learned directly from molecular dynamics (MD) solvation structures via gradient-based optimization. The model is trained on LHCE electrolytes and tested for generalizability on other systems, with design predictions validated by higher-fidelity MD.

Result: The model achieves 10.7% RMSE and R2=0.87 for shell composition, and 2.3% RMSE on free solvent ratio in LHCE electrolytes at minimal computational cost. It generalizes to other electrolyte systems. Interaction-term analysis reveals DN dominates Li+ solvation energetics, providing thermodynamic grounding for empirical DN design rules. The model identifies fluorobenzene as a promising diluent in the unseen LiTFSI/G4 system and predicts a favorable salt-concentration window with anion-rich solvation and low free solvent—validated by higher-fidelity MD.

Conclusion: This work establishes an interpretable, differentiable mean-field framework for predicting Li+ solvation shell composition, providing a thermodynamic basis for DN-based design rules and demonstrating practical utility for designing LHCE electrolytes at a fraction of MD's computational cost, with potential to extend to other electrolyte classes.

Abstract: Tailoring Li+ solvation structures has emerged as a promising design principle across multiple Li metal battery (LMB) electrolytes, with localized high concentration electrolytes (LHCE) emerging as a leading candidate. However, rational design of these electrolytes remains limited by a poor quantitative understanding of how molecular properties govern Li+ solvation shell composition. Here we introduce a mean field modeling framework, built on an Ising model, that predicts solvation shell composition from donor number (DN), acceptor number (AN), molar ratio, and molecular size. This framework is end-to-end differentiable, which enables parameterization directly from molecular dynamics (MD) solvation structures via gradient-based optimization. Focusing on LHCE, the model achieves 10.7% RMSE and R2=0.87 for shell composition, 2.3% RMSE on free solvent ratio, and further reproduces solvation trends in real LHCE electrolytes, at a fraction of MD's cost. Our model also shows good generalizability on other electrolyte systems in addition to LHCE. Analysis of its interaction terms shows that DN dominates Li+ solvation energetics, providing a thermodynamic basis for empirical DN-based design rules. We further demonstrate the model's design utility on the LiTFSI/tetraglyme (G4) system absent from training. The model identifies fluorobenzene (FB) as a promising diluent and predicts a salt-concentration window with anion-rich solvation shells and low free solvent, favorable for stable anion-derived SEIs and high oxidative stability, respectively. This prediction is validated by higher-fidelity MD. This work establishes an interpretable differentiable framework for predicting Li+ solvation shell composition, with potential to extend to other electrolyte classes.

</details>


### [192] [cuSOAP: a GPU-accelerated Generator of Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions Descriptor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05709)
*Hongyu Yan,Yuqing Xia,Yong Wei,Minghan Chen,Hanning Chen*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: cuSOAP is a GPU-accelerated PyTorch-based package for generating SOAP descriptors and their analytic derivatives, achieving up to 227x speedup over the CPU reference DScribe and near-linear scaling to million-atom systems, bringing on-the-fly SOAP evaluation for large-scale condensed-phase simulation within reach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The SOAP descriptor is widely used in molecular machine learning, but its evaluation cost and derivative computation remain the principal bottleneck for SOAP-based interatomic potentials, particularly at the large radial and angular basis sizes needed for complex multi-species condensed-phase systems. The naive tensor formulation on GPUs requires multi-gigabyte intermediates, making efficient evaluation challenging.

Method: cuSOAP evaluates closed-form expressions or quadratures for SOAP projection coefficients and their Cartesian gradients using Gaussian-type-orbital and polynomial radial bases. It is built on PyTorch with fused CUDA and Triton kernels that eliminate the large intermediate tensors of naive implementations. It serves as a drop-in replacement for DScribe, reproducing its constructor signature, feature ordering, and output within ~10^-6 accuracy, and accepts ASE Atoms objects directly.

Result: On an NVIDIA Grace-Blackwell (GB200) node, cuSOAP generates descriptor-plus-Jacobian workloads for a 1000-molecule water cluster 13x to 227x faster than DScribe across the (nmax, lmax) hyperparameter map, with speedup growing with lmax. Descriptor-only generation scales as t proportional to n^1.34 up to a million-atom water cluster, processed in 19.5s on one GPU and 5.7s on four GPUs with ~90% parallel efficiency.

Conclusion: cuSOAP demonstrates that GPU-accelerated SOAP evaluation with analytic derivatives is both feasible and highly efficient, achieving near-linear scaling to million-atom systems and making on-the-fly SOAP descriptor generation practical for large-scale condensed-phase simulations.

Abstract: The Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions (SOAP) descriptor is one of the most widely adopted representations of atomic environments in molecular machine learning, but the cost of evaluating it and its derivatives remains a principal bottleneck of SOAP-based interatomic potentials, particularly at the large radial and angular basis sizes demanded by complex, multi-species condensed-phase environments. We present cuSOAP, a GPU-accelerated generator of atom-wise SOAP vectors and their analytic derivatives. Built on PyTorch, cuSOAP evaluates closed-form expressions or quadratures for the projection coefficients and their Cartesian gradients for Gaussian-type-orbital and polynomial radial bases, through fused CUDA and Triton kernels that eliminate the multi-gigabyte intermediates of a naive tensor formulation. The package is a drop-in replacement for the CPU-based reference DScribe, reproducing its constructor signature, feature ordering, and output to within ${\sim}10^{-6}$, and accepts structures directly as Atomistic Simulation Environment (ASE) Atoms objects. On an NVIDIA Grace--Blackwell (GB200) node, a single Blackwell GPU generates the full descriptor-plus-Jacobian workload for a 1000-molecule water cluster up to two orders of magnitude faster than DScribe, i.e., from $13\times$ to $227\times$ across the entire $(n_{\max}, l_{\max})$ hyperparameter map, with the speedup growing with the angular band limit $l_{\max}$. Descriptor-only generation scales as $t \propto n^{1.34}$, close to linear, up to a million-atom water cluster, which is processed in 19.5s on one GPU and, through a shared-memory multiprocessing driver, in 5.7s on the four GPUs of the node, demonstrating $\sim$90% parallel efficiency with no sign of saturation as devices are added. These results bring on-the-fly SOAP evaluation for large-scale condensed-phase simulation within reach.

</details>


### [193] [Performance of a minimally empirical local-hybrid density functional for molecular chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06162)
*Erin R. Johnson,Kyle R. Bryenton*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes LHnz, a minimally empirical local hybrid functional for DFT that uses only three empirical parameters, addressing delocalisation error—argued to be DFT's greatest outstanding challenge—while outperforming all existing global hybrids on the GMTKN55 benchmark with no large outliers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delocalisation error in DFT is a major outstanding challenge. Although local hybrid functionals with position-dependent exact-exchange mixing are a promising remedy, existing versions achieving good thermochemical accuracy tend to be highly empirical and rely on complicated forms (range separation, calibration functions, power series, neural networks). The authors seek to determine the limits of a minimally empirical, simple functional form that avoids such complexity.

Method: The authors propose the LHnz functional, a local hybrid that uses dispersionless exchange and a local mixing fraction dependent on the correlation length and effective exchange–correlation hole normalisations. The functional form is deliberately kept simple, containing only three empirical parameters and avoiding range separation, calibration functions, power-series expansions, and neural networks.

Result: With just three empirical parameters, LHnz outperforms all existing global hybrid functionals on the GMTKN55 molecular-thermochemistry benchmark, achieving this without any large outliers.

Conclusion: A minimally empirical local hybrid functional with a simple form can outperform global hybrids on thermochemistry benchmarks, suggesting that high accuracy does not necessarily require complicated, heavily parameterised functional forms.

Abstract: Delocalisation error has been argued to be the greatest outstanding challenge in density-functional theory (DFT). One of the most promising routes to minimise this error is development of local hybrid functionals, in which the fraction of exact-exchange mixing is position dependent. However, existing local hybrids capable of good thermochemical accuracy are often highly empirical, and tend to have complicated functional forms that involve some combination of range separation, calibration functions, power-series expansions, or even neural networks. In this work, we explore the limits of what can be achieved with a minimally empirical local hybrid functional form that avoids such complexities. The ``LHnz'' functional is proposed, which uses dispersionless exchange and a local mixing fraction dependent on the correlation length and effective exchange--correlation hole normalisations. With only three empirical parameters, LHnz is shown to outperform all existing global hybrid functionals for the GMTKN55 molecular-thermochemistry benchmark with no large outliers.

</details>


### [194] [Tensor-based Approximation of Molecular Kinetics: Generator Learning, Reaction Coordinates and Incremental Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06446)
*Minakshi Verma,Peter Benner,Feliks Nüske*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a tensor-based approach for analyzing long-timescale kinetics in molecular dynamics simulations by approximating the infinitesimal generator using tensor train formats. The method discretizes the variational eigenvalue problem with tensor-product basis functions, derives analytical expressions for tensor operators in TT format, and provides efficient algorithms for solving the resulting linear problems, including the case of state-dependent diffusion fields. The approach is validated on fast-folding protein MD simulations using TICA coordinates, demonstrating its effectiveness in identifying meta-stable states and transition timescales, with the ability to incrementally expand the number of reaction coordinates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analyzing long-timescale kinetics in molecular dynamics simulations, specifically meta-stable states and transition timescales, is computationally challenging. Existing methods face difficulties when dealing with high-dimensional reaction coordinate spaces, motivating the need for a more efficient and scalable tensor-based framework to approximate the infinitesimal generator.

Method: The method starts from a variational approximation of low-lying generator eigenvalues and discretizes the eigenvalue problem using a tensor-product basis. Analytical expressions for the tensor operators are derived in tensor train (TT) format, and efficient algorithms are provided to solve the corresponding linear problems. The approach also handles state-dependent diffusion fields and enables gradual incremental addition of reaction coordinates without re-solving the variational problem from scratch.

Result: Using MD simulations of fast-folding proteins and TICA coordinates as reaction coordinates, the method effectively identifies long-timescale transitions and meta-stable states, demonstrating its practical applicability and computational efficiency in realistic biomolecular systems.

Conclusion: The tensor-based approximation of the infinitesimal generator using TT format provides an efficient and scalable framework for analyzing long-timescale kinetics in MD simulations, with demonstrated success in protein systems and the flexibility to expand reaction coordinate dimensionality progressively.

Abstract: We present an approach to analyze long-timescale kinetics of molecular dynamics simulations - meta-stable states and transition timescales - by a tensor-based approximation of the infinitesimal generator. We start from the variational approximation of low-lying generator eigenvalues, and discretize the associated eigenvalue problem using a tensor-product basis. We derive analytical expressions for the resulting tensor operators in tensor train (TT) format, and provide efficient algorithms to solve the corresponding linear problems. We also treat the case of a state-dependent diffusion field, which is relevant when working in reaction coordinates. We show how the number of reaction coordinates can be gradually increased without having to solve the variational problem from scratch. Using MD simulations of fast-folding proteins and TICA coordinates as reaction coordinates, we demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method at identifying long-timescale transitions and meta-stable states.

</details>


### [195] [Out-of-Distribution Inverse Design of Elastic Networks with Differentiable Graph Neural Network Molecular Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06655)
*Sergey A. Shteingolts,Salman N. Salman,Ron Levie,Dan Mendels*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A differentiable inverse design framework using a graph neural network molecular dynamics simulator enables optimization of material properties well beyond the training data distribution, demonstrated by designing auxetic networks (Poisson's ratio down to -0.3) from training data limited to non-auxetic systems (0.1-0.4), while generalizing across system sizes up to 5000 nodes despite training on fewer than 200.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional machine-learning-based inverse design using structure-property models requires large training datasets and generalizes poorly beyond the training distribution, limiting their practical utility for discovering novel materials with targeted properties.

Method: The framework combines a differentiable graph neural network molecular dynamics simulator with a short dynamical initialization plus physics-based refinement during simulation. This physics-informed approach enables optimization that extends well beyond the conditions represented in the training data.

Result: The framework designed strongly auxetic networks with Poisson's ratios as low as -0.3 from training data limited to non-auxetic systems (0.1-0.4), produced stress-strain responses and localized mechanical defects outside training ranges, and generalized from networks with fewer than 200 training nodes to systems with up to 5000 nodes.

Conclusion: The physics-informed differentiable inverse design framework significantly extends the reach of machine-learning-based materials design beyond training distribution limits, enabling discovery of out-of-distribution structures and properties while maintaining scalability.

Abstract: Machine-learning-based inverse design can accelerate the discovery of materials with targeted properties, but conventional structure--property models often require large training datasets and generalize poorly beyond their training distribution. Here, we present a differentiable inverse design framework based on a graph neural network molecular dynamics simulator. By combining a short dynamical initialization with physics-based refinement during simulation, the framework enables optimization well beyond the conditions represented in the training data. Using disordered elastic networks, we show that a simulator trained only on non-auxetic systems with Poisson's ratios between 0.1 and 0.4 can design strongly auxetic networks with values as low as -0.3. The framework also produces stress--strain responses outside the range observed during training, creates localized mechanical defects absent from the training data, and generalizes across system size, enabling optimization of networks tested up to 5000 nodes despite being trained only on networks with fewer than 200 nodes.

</details>


### [196] [First-order Degenerate Symmetry-Adapted Perturbation Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07215)
*Dominik Cieśliński,Tijs Karman,Anthony Scemama,Michał Hapka,Piotr S. Żuchowski*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a first-order degenerate formulation of symmetry-adapted perturbation theory (dSAPT) with weak symmetry forcing to quantify configuration mixing in degenerate electronic states in terms of electrostatic and exchange contributions. The method is applied to spatially degenerate systems (H2-F and H2-NO), demonstrating that exchange effects significantly contribute to configuration mixing at intermediate separations, second-order dispersion is needed for the angular dependence of the diabatic mixing angle in H2-NO, and Pauli repulsion controls excitation delocalization in water and benzene excimers at short separations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Degenerate electronic states govern excimer photophysics and dynamics near conical intersections, where intermolecular interactions mix electronic configurations on degenerate or near-degenerate potential energy surfaces. A quantitative understanding of what drives this mixing (electrostatics vs. exchange) is needed, motivating a perturbation theory framework that can decompose these contributions.

Method: The authors develop a first-order degenerate symmetry-adapted perturbation theory (dSAPT) with weak symmetry forcing. Two approaches to resolving the degeneracy are compared: antisymmetrization enforced either after or before diagonalization of the first-order eigenproblem. Calculations use CASSCF monomer wave functions for H2-F(2P) and H2-NO(2Π), and CASSCF or CIS wave functions for water and benzene excimers.

Result: Exchange effects contribute significantly to configuration mixing already at intermediate intermolecular separations for the spatially degenerate model systems. For H2-NO(2Π), second-order dispersion energy is essential for qualitatively reproducing the angular dependence of the diabatic mixing angle. For water and benzene excimers, Pauli repulsion controls delocalization of the excitation at short intermolecular separations.

Conclusion: dSAPT provides a physically interpretable decomposition of degenerate-state configuration mixing into electrostatic and exchange contributions, revealing that Pauli repulsion (exchange) plays a dominant role in driving configuration mixing and excitation delocalization at short-to-intermediate intermolecular distances, with dispersion needed for accurate angular behavior in some systems.

Abstract: Degenerate electronic states govern the photophysics of excimers and the molecular dynamics near conical intersections. In regions where potential energy surfaces are degenerate or nearly degenerate, intermolecular interactions mix the corresponding electronic configurations. We introduce a first-order degenerate formulation of symmetry-adapted perturbation theory (dSAPT) with weak symmetry forcing that quantifies the mixing in terms of electrostatic and exchange (Pauli repulsion) contributions. We compare two alternative ways of resolving the degeneracy, in which antisymmetry is enforced either after or before diagonalization of the first-order eigenproblem. Using CASSCF monomer wave functions, we apply dSAPT to two model spatially degenerate systems, H$_2$--F(${}^2P$) and H$_2$--NO(${}^2Π$), and show that exchange effects contribute significantly to configuration mixing already at intermediate intermolecular separations. For the H$_2$--NO(${}^2Π$) complex, we further demonstrate that second-order dispersion energy is essential for qualitatively reproducing the angular dependence of the diabatic mixing angle. Finally, using water and benzene excimers with monomers described either by CASSCF or CIS wave functions, we show that Pauli repulsion controls delocalization of the excitation at short intermolecular separations.

</details>


### [197] [Mixed quantum-classical evolution in open molecular systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07272)
*Michael Galperin,Abraham Nitzan*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a mixed quantum-classical formulation for open nonequilibrium molecular systems, starting from the pseudoparticle nonequilibrium Green's function (PP-NEGF) method to derive a density-matrix equation of motion. The authors compare their quantum-classical dynamics with prior formulations, highlighting key differences arising from the open and nonequilibrium nature of the system, and critically analyze the approximations required to reduce this dynamics to the fewest-switches surface hopping (FSSH) method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous quantum-classical framework for open nonequilibrium molecular systems, which require combining quantum electronic descriptions with classical nuclear dynamics while properly accounting for open-system (bath-coupled) and nonequilibrium effects.

Method: The authors use PP-NEGF as a fully quantum reference, from which they introduce classical treatment of nuclear dynamics to derive a density-matrix equation of motion for the quantum-classical evolution. They then examine the assumptions required to reduce this general formulation to FSSH.

Result: The derived density-matrix equation differs from previous quantum-classical formulations due to the open and nonequilibrium character of the system. The analysis shows that the approximations needed to obtain FSSH from the full quantum-classical dynamics become unreasonable when adiabatic surfaces approach degeneracy or near-degeneracy.

Conclusion: A new quantum-classical density-matrix formulation for open nonequilibrium systems is established from PP-NEGF, and FSSH is shown to be an unjustifiable reduction of this more complete dynamics when adiabatic surfaces are close in energy.

Abstract: We consider a mixed quantum-classical formulation for open nonequilibrium molecular systems. We employ the pseudoparticle nonequilibrium Green's function (PP-NEGF) method as a fully quantum description and a starting point for introducing classical nuclear dynamics for an open system. We use this formulation to derive a density-matrix equation of motion describing the quantum-classical evolution. We compare our results with previous formulations and highlight important differences related to the nonequilibrium and open character of the molecular system. We also analyze the approximations needed to reduce full quantum-classical dynamics to the fewest-switches surface hopping (FSSH) method and show that these approximations are unreasonable when adiabatic surfaces come close to each other.

</details>


### [198] [Strong Evidence for Formation of Hydroxyl Anion via 2-Particle-1-Hole Feshbach Resonances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07324)
*Siddique Ali,Meenakshi Rana,Soumya Ghosh,Narayan Kundu,Aryya Ghosh,Dhananjay Nandi*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This study uses CAP-EOM-EA-CCSD calculations and ToF mass spectrometry to show that OH⁻ formation from 2-propanol via dissociative electron attachment (7-11 eV) is driven by 2p-1h Feshbach resonances. A dense manifold of anionic states with avoided crossings enables nonadiabatic population transfer, where six long-lived resonances (notably states 25 and 28) funnel the electron into the σ*(C-OH) orbital, confirmed by a measured OH⁻ yield peak at 8.6 eV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the detailed mechanism of OH⁻ formation via dissociative electron attachment in 2-propanol as a model system, with the goal of establishing a site-specific fragmentation mechanism generalizable to broader classes of organic and inorganic molecules.

Method: High-level CAP-EOM-EA-CCSD (complex absorbing potential equation-of-motion electron-attachment coupled-cluster) calculations combined with advanced time-of-flight (ToF) mass spectrometry. Potential-energy curves were computed to map anionic states, and survival-probability analysis was applied to identify long-lived Feshbach resonances driving fragmentation.

Result: OH⁻ formation between 7-11 eV is dominated by 2p-1h Feshbach resonances. A dense manifold of anionic states coupled via avoided crossings facilitates nonadiabatic population transfer during C-OH bond dissociation. Six long-lived resonances were identified, with states 25 and 28 serving as primary drivers by funneling the attached electron into the localized σ*(C-OH) antibonding orbital. Experimental OH⁻ yield peaked at 8.6 eV, confirming the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: The combined theoretical-experimental approach confirms a site-specific fragmentation mechanism for OH⁻ formation via dissociative electron attachment in 2-propanol, which generalizes to the broader class of molecules containing similar structural motifs.

Abstract: This study investigates the formation of the hydroxyl anion (OH$^-$) via dissociative electron attachment in 2-propanol as a model system for studying both organic and inorganic molecules. Using high-level CAP-EOM-EA-CCSD calculations and advanced ToF mass spectrometry, we demonstrate that OH$^-$ formation at electron energies between 7 and 11 eV is dominated by two-particle-one-hole (2p-1h) Feshbach resonances. The potential-energy curves reveal a dense manifold of anionic states coupled through numerous avoided crossings, facilitating nonadiabatic population transfer during C-OH bond dissociation. Survival-probability analysis identifies a subset of six long-lived resonances that persist long enough to drive fragmentation, with states 25 and 28 acting as primary drivers by funneling the attached electron into the localized $σ^*(\mathrm{C{-}OH})$ antibonding orbital. These theoretical predictions are confirmed by experimental observations of a prominent OH$^-$ yield peaking at 8.6 eV, supporting a site-specific fragmentation mechanism that generalizes to the broader class of molecules.

</details>


### [199] [Low-rank approximation of Moment Tensor Potential enables reducing training set size without loss of accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07372)
*Anna Bondarenko,Nikita Rybin,Maxim Rakhuba,Ivan S. Novikov*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper implements a low-rank tensor-train (TT) decomposition of the Moment Tensor Potential (MTP), creating a tensor-factorized MTP (TFMTP) that requires two times fewer training configurations than the original MTP under 1.5-fold compression while maintaining equal accuracy, validated on actively trained models for FLiNaK molten salt and MoNbTaWV random alloy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce the data requirements and computational cost of training machine-learned interatomic potentials like MTP, which typically require large numbers of configurations for accurate fitting, by exploiting low-rank structure in the potential's tensor representation.

Method: The authors implement a low-rank approximation of MTP via tensor train (TT) decomposition, yielding a tensor-factorized MTP (TFMTP). Both TFMTP and the original MTP are actively trained using a MaxVol-based algorithm during molecular dynamics simulations (FLiNaK molten salt) and geometry optimizations (MoNbTaWV random alloy).

Result: Under 1.5-fold compression, TFMTP achieves the same accuracy as the original MTP while requiring two times fewer fitting configurations. Physical properties predicted by both models—density and viscosity of FLiNaK (600-1200 K), and elastic constants and bulk modulus of MoNbTaWV at zero temperature—show negligible differences.

Conclusion: Tensor-train based low-rank factorization is an effective strategy to improve data efficiency of MTP training without sacrificing predictive accuracy, making TFMTP a viable and more efficient alternative for constructing accurate interatomic potentials for complex multicomponent systems.

Abstract: In this study, we implement a low-rank approximation of Moment Tensor Potential (MTP) based on the tensor train (TT) decomposition. The implemented tensor-factorized MTP (TFMTP) and the original MTP model are actively trained via a MaxVol-based algorithm during molecular dynamics simulations of a four-component molten salt mixture, LiF-NaF-KF (FLiNaK), and geometry optimizations of a five-component equiatomic MoNbTaWV random alloy. We demonstrate that under a 1.5-fold compression, TFMTP requires two times fewer configurations for fitting than the original MTP model, while maintaining an indistinguishable level of accuracy. These actively trained MTP and TFMTP models are further used to evaluate the density and viscosity of FLiNaK at temperatures ranging from 600 to 1200 K, as well as the elastic constants and bulk modulus of the MoNbTaWV alloy at zero temperature. For both atomic systems, the differences in physical properties predicted by MTP and TFMTP are negligible.

</details>


### [200] [Simulation-Supervised Foundation Models for Retention Time Prediction in High-Performance Liquid Chromatography beyond Experimental Data Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07531)
*Stephen Wu,Yufeng Han,Yasuhiro Mito,Yoshiyuki Watabe,Yoshihiro Hayashi,Hikaru Takaya,Takuya Kubo,Ryo Yoshida*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: FUSE-RT is a multitask foundation model for predicting HPLC retention times across diverse molecules and chromatographic methods. It integrates data from 179 chromatographic methods and uses a novel Sim2Real transfer learning approach, transferring molecular representations learned from large-scale computational simulation data (PolyOmics: 39 properties for ~21,400 molecules from MD and DFT calculations) to experimental RT prediction. The model generalizes effectively under molecular, method, and joint distribution shifts, and RT-prediction error follows a power-law decrease with increasing simulation data size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate HPLC retention time prediction across diverse molecules and chromatographic methods is challenging because experimental training data only cover limited regions of chemical and methodological space, hindering generalization to unseen molecules and methods.

Method: The authors develop FUSE-RT, a multitask foundation model trained on RT data from 179 chromatographic methods. They introduce simulation-to-real (Sim2Real) transfer learning, where molecular representations pre-trained on PolyOmics computational data (39 physicochemical properties for ~21,400 molecules from molecular dynamics and DFT calculations) are transferred to experimental RT prediction tasks. The model is evaluated under molecular, method, and joint molecular-method distribution shifts, including few-shot adaptation scenarios.

Result: Simulation-derived supervision substantially improves transfer beyond the experimental molecular domain, particularly under pronounced coverage gaps and few-shot adaptation settings. RT-prediction error decreases systematically with increasing simulation data size, following a significant power-law relationship.

Conclusion: Sim2Real transfer learning is a scalable strategy for extending retention time prediction beyond the finite coverage of experimental chromatographic data, establishing a generalizable framework for integrating computational simulation supervision into experimental prediction models.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) retention times (RTs) across diverse molecules and chromatographic methods remains challenging because experimental training data cover only a limited region of chemical and method spaces. Here, we develop FUSE-RT (Foundation model Unifying Simulation and Experimental supervision for Retention Time), a multitask foundation model that integrates RT data from 179 chromatographic methods and adapts to unseen molecules and methods using limited target-domain data. To extend transferability beyond experimental coverage, we introduce simulation-to-real (Sim2Real) transfer learning, in which molecular representations learned from large-scale computational data are transferred to experimental RT prediction. Specifically, we use PolyOmics, comprising 39 properties for approximately 21,400 molecules generated by molecular dynamics and density-functional theory calculations, as auxiliary supervision. We evaluate generalization under molecular, method, and joint molecular--method distribution shifts. Simulation-derived supervision substantially improves transfer beyond the experimental molecular domain, particularly under pronounced coverage gaps and few-shot adaptation. Moreover, RT-prediction error decreases systematically with increasing simulation-data size, following a significant power-law relationship. These results establish Sim2Real transfer as a scalable strategy for extending RT prediction beyond the finite coverage of experimental chromatographic data.

</details>


### [201] [Hemoglycin as a Candidate for Permanent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07760)
*Julie E. M. McGeoch,Malcolm W. McGeoch*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper describes a three-dimensional lattice formed by the space-derived Hemoglycin polymer of glycine, terminated by iron and silicon, which contains divalent cation (Mg/Ca) binding sites around silicon tetrahedral vertices. The spatial distribution of these cations encodes information. Calcium-dominant configurations bind more deeply, and a specific low-energy state with two Mg-to-Ca substitutions enables long-term chemical modification. This state can be read via pulse trains that render insulating lattice regions conductive, while writing is achieved through spatial modulation of the Na/Ca exchanger in lipid membranes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the space-derived Hemoglycin polymer could function as a substrate for information storage and processing in biological systems, potentially acting as a molecular-scale memory or signaling mechanism in nervous tissue.

Method: Computational energy minimization of magnesium/calcium occupancy permutations across lattice vertices; analysis of the structural lattice formed by glycine-derived Hemoglycin polymers terminated by iron and silicon; theoretical modeling of electron conduction paths through divalent cation bridges and of information read/write mechanisms involving pulse trains and membrane Na/Ca exchangers.

Result: Calcium-dominated configurations exhibit deeper binding energies. A specific low-energy conformation involving two independent Mg-to-Ca substitutions is identified as key for stable chemical modification. The authors propose a mechanism where this encoded state is read by pulse-train-induced calcium redistribution that converts insulating lattice regions to conductive paths, potentially serving as a recognition signaling mechanism.

Conclusion: Hemoglycin lattices with cation-encoded vertices may constitute a plausible molecular information storage medium arising from space chemistry, with calcium-mediated configurational states enabling long-term memory-like modification readable through electrical pulse patterns and writable via Na/Ca exchanger modulation across lipid membranes in neurons or glia.

Abstract: The Hemoglycin polymer of glycine derived from space is terminated by iron and silicon to form a three-dimensional regular lattice with a high density of silicon tetrahedral vertices. Divalent cation bridges bypass vertices to facilitate electron conduction axially from layer to layer of the lattice. In principle, the exact distribution of Mg or Ca ions around each vertex can encode a very large amount of information. In space where the polymer forms in a molecular cloud the current derived from photon input will fade but in-fall hemoglycin in life forms on habitable planets like Earth, could be contained behind lipid electronic barriers of the nervous system in neurons or glia. Energy minimization of the permutations for magnesium or calcium occupancy showed deeper binding for calcium-dominated configurations. A low energy state with two independent magnesium to calcium substitutions appears to be key to the maintenance of long-term chemical modification. This state could be read via a pulse train that induced the matching spatial distribution of calcium to render adjacent insulating lattice regions conductive and provide a completed conduction path, signaling recognition. Writing of information may involve spatial modulation of the Na/Ca exchanger within the lipid membrane.

</details>


### [202] [Construction of downfolded Hamiltonians from projective transcorrelation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08087)
*Seiichiro Ten-no*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper replaces the 3-body term in projective transcorrelation with an effective operator of rank at most two using the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation to the three-particle reduced density matrix, reducing computational cost while preserving accuracy in electronic structure calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Projective transcorrelation introduces a 3-body term in the effective Hamiltonian, which is computationally expensive. The authors aim to replace this term with lower-rank effective operators to reduce cost while maintaining accuracy.

Method: The 3-body term is approximated using the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation to the three-particle reduced density matrix. The 2C 3-body energy is expressed without explicit cumulants, and the effective one- and two-body interactions are derived as partial derivatives with respect to the reduced density matrices.

Result: The truncation is validated on atomization and reaction energies of the HEAT set using CCSD(T), and on the CAS-pTC model where the downfolded Hamiltonian held on a qubit register has its number of Pauli strings reduced from the sixth power of the orbital count to the fourth.

Conclusion: The 2C approximation effectively reduces the 3-body term to one- and two-body operators, significantly lowering computational and quantum resource overhead while maintaining accuracy for benchmark energy calculations.

Abstract: Projective transcorrelation recovers the short-range electron correlation by a similarity transformation with a geminal function $f(r_{12})$, at the cost of an effective Hamiltonian containing a 3-body term. We replace the term by an effective operator of rank at most two, obtained from the two-body cumulant (2C) approximation to the three-particle reduced density matrix. The 2C 3-body energy is written without cumulants, and the effective one- and two-body interactions are derived as its partial derivatives with respect to the reduced density matrices. The truncation is assessed on the atomization and reaction energies of the HEAT set with CCSD(T), and on the CAS-pTC model, whose downfolded Hamiltonian is held on a qubit register with the number of Pauli strings reduced from the sixth power of the orbital count to the fourth.

</details>


### [203] [Fixed-Dimensional Latent Flow for Generating Variable-Size 3D Molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08333)
*Weichi Yao,Cameron Gruich,Bryan R. Goldsmith,Yixin Wang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: EF-TALFM is a two-stage generative framework for variable-size 3D molecular design that relies on a single fixed-dimensional molecule-level latent representation. It combines a second-stage flow matching latent sampler with an autoregressive Transformer decoder that determines size, atom types, coordinates, and chemical states. It avoids equivariant layers through canonical ordering and pose alignment, and achieves state-of-the-art validity/uniqueness/novelty on PCQM4Mv2 while boosting DFT-verified hit rates in property-conditioned generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most 3D molecular generators require molecule size (number of atoms) to be specified upfront, coupling size to composition, structure, and properties. The authors aim to enable open-ended generation where molecule size is itself a learnable output, without sacrificing efficiency or relying on expensive equivariant architectures.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) a fixed-dimensional molecule-level latent space is learned via an autoencoder; (2) a flow matching model samples this latent vector for unconditional or property-conditioned generation, while an autoregressive Transformer decoder generates size, atom types, coordinates, and chemically informative states. Canonical atom ordering and rigid-pose alignment let them use standard Transformers (no equivariant layers). Joint decoding of geometry with an enriched chemical state enables deterministic chemistry-guided graph recovery without a learned dense pairwise bond decoder. Optional property supervision provides an internal ranking readout.

Result: On PCQM4Mv2, EF-TALFM achieves 89.4% of molecules that are unique, training-set novel, and pass sanitization plus PoseBusters checks, vs 75.6% (UAE-3D) and 69.8% (FlowMol), with higher throughput in training and sampling. Across ten target HOMO-LUMO gaps, internal ranking doubles DFT-verified hit rate within 0.1 eV while preserving 97% novelty among unique verified hits.

Conclusion: Fixed-dimensional molecule-level generation followed by symmetry-resolved autoregressive realization is a practical and efficient architecture for open-ended and property-directed 3D molecular design, decoupling size prediction from generation without equivariant layers or external predictors.

Abstract: In molecular discovery, molecule size is coupled to composition, structure, and other target properties. Yet most 3D generators require molecule size to be specified before generation. Here, we introduce Equivariant-Free Transformer-Autoencoded Latent Flow Matching, a two-stage generative framework that relies entirely on a single fixed-dimensional molecule-level latent representation to generate variable-size molecules. The second-stage flow matching model samples this latent vector, and an autoregressive Transformer decoder then determines molecule size while generating atom types, coordinates, and chemically informative states. Canonical atom ordering and rigid-pose alignment enable standard Transformers without equivariant layers, while joint decoding of molecular geometry and an enriched chemical state enables reliable, deterministic, chemistry-guided graph recovery without requiring a learned dense pairwise bond decoder. The same fixed-dimensional latent supports unconditional and property-conditioned flow matching, while optional property supervision adds an internal ranking readout, with no separate predictor or reference calculations. On PCQM4Mv2, EF-TALFM achieves the highest fraction of molecules that are unique, training-set novel, pass sanitization and PoseBusters sanity checks, 89.4\%, compared with 75.6\% for UAE-3D and 69.8\% for FlowMol. EF-TALFM also achieves higher measured computational throughput for training and sampling. Across ten target HOMO--LUMO gaps, internal ranking doubles the density functional theory (DFT)-verified hit rate within $0.1\,\mathrm{eV}$, while preserving 97\% novelty among unique verified hits. These results demonstrate that fixed-dimensional molecule-level generation followed by symmetry-resolved autoregressive realization provides a practical architecture for open-ended and property-directed 3D molecular design.

</details>


### [204] [TSBench: A physics-grounded benchmark for evaluating LLM understanding of chemical reaction mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08503)
*Xiaohu Xu,Tong Zhu*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: TSBench is a new benchmark where LLM agents construct 3D transition-state guesses for chemical reactions, verified by automated quantum chemistry. Across 546 evaluations of seven frontier LLMs on 78 reactions, success rates rose from 50.4% to 66.8% with diagnosis-driven revision, though performance dropped sharply with mechanistic complexity. Most failures produced locally plausible but globally incorrect saddle points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chemistry benchmarks for LLMs focus on factual knowledge and text-based reasoning, but do not assess whether models can actually bridge symbolic reactant-product descriptions to three-dimensional mechanistic pathways of atom rearrangement—a core capability needed for tasks like synthesis planning and autonomous experimentation.

Method: TSBench gives an LLM agent structure-editing tools to construct 3D transition-state guesses, which are then verified by an automated quantum-chemical pipeline that yields a physics-grounded pass/fail verdict. The benchmark spans 78 elementary reactions and was evaluated on 546 runs across seven frontier LLMs, including diagnosis-driven revision iterations.

Result: Aggregate success rate improved from 50.4% to 66.8% under diagnosis-driven revision. The best models approached ~90% accuracy on the simplest reactions but performance degraded sharply with increasing mechanistic complexity. Most failures yielded locally plausible saddle points whose reaction paths connected the wrong reactant-product pairs.

Conclusion: LLM agents exhibit strong local geometric intuition for transition states but lack a robust global grasp of the reaction coordinate. TSBench provides a mechanism-level evaluation yardstick for LLM agents in mechanism-sensitive tasks such as synthesis planning and autonomous experimentation.

Abstract: Understanding a chemical reaction requires mapping a symbolic reactant-product description onto the three-dimensional pathway through which atoms rearrange, yet chemistry benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) largely probe factual knowledge and text-based reasoning. Here we introduce TSBench, a benchmark in which an LLM agent uses structure-editing tools to construct three-dimensional transition-state (TS) guesses verified by an automated quantum-chemical pipeline, yielding a physics-grounded pass/fail verdict. Across 546 evaluations of seven frontier LLMs on 78 elementary reactions, the aggregate success rate rose from 50.4% to 66.8% under diagnosis-driven revision; the best models approached 90% on the simplest reactions, yet performance dropped sharply with mechanistic complexity. Most failed attempts produced locally plausible saddle points whose reaction paths led to the wrong reactant-product pair, revealing local geometric intuition without a robust grasp of the global reaction coordinate. TSBench establishes a mechanism-level yardstick for LLM agents in mechanism-sensitive tasks such as synthesis planning and autonomous experimentation.

</details>


### [205] [Competing Ring-Opening and Hofmann Elimination Pathways in Aqueous TEMPO Catholytes: A First-Principles Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08721)
*Line Mouaffac,Maiwenn Souetre,Guillaume Jeanmairet,Mathieu Salanne*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper uses ab initio molecular dynamics and enhanced sampling to study the degradation of five TEMPO derivatives in aqueous redox-flow batteries, finding that ring opening is the kinetically preferred degradation pathway over Hofmann elimination for all derivatives, with barrier magnitudes varying significantly based on molecular functionalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TEMPO-based aqueous redox-flow batteries are promising for large-scale energy storage but suffer from chemical instability of their oxidized N-oxoammonium state, limiting practical application. The degradation pathways are difficult to resolve experimentally, motivating computational investigation.

Method: The authors employed ab initio molecular dynamics combined with enhanced sampling to investigate the degradation of five TEMPO derivatives. They examined two proposed degradation mechanisms—ring opening and Hofmann elimination—and compared their corresponding activation free energies.

Result: For all five TEMPO derivatives, ring opening exhibited a lower activation free energy than Hofmann elimination, identifying it as the kinetically preferred degradation pathway. The ring-opening barrier magnitude varied significantly between molecules, indicating that functionalization strongly influences stability. The predicted preference aligns with available experimental studies that identified or inferred ring-opening degradation.

Conclusion: The results provide an atomistic picture of degradation pathways that are experimentally difficult to resolve, highlighting the importance of molecular structure in controlling the kinetic stability of TEMPO derivatives in aqueous electrolytes.

Abstract: Aqueous redox-flow batteries based on TEMPO derivatives are promising for large-scale energy storage, but their practical use is limited by the chemical instability of the oxidized N -oxoammonium state. In this work, we investigate the degradation of five TEMPO derivatives using ab initio molecular dynamics combined with enhanced sampling. Two proposed degradation mechanisms, ring opening and Hofmann elimination, are examined and their corresponding activation free energies are compared. For all derivatives considered, ring opening exhibits a lower activation free energy than Hofmann elimination, identifying it as the kinetically preferred degradation pathway. The magnitude of the ring-opening barrier, however, varies significantly between molecules, showing that different functionalizations strongly influence its stability toward degradation. The predicted preference for ring opening is consistent with available experimental studies, which have identified or inferred ring-opening degradation for several TEMPO-based catholytes. These results provide an atomistic picture of degradation pathways that are difficult to resolve experimentally and highlight the importance of molecular structure in controlling the kinetic stability of TEMPO derivatives in aqueous electrolytes.

</details>


### [206] [Examination of Cluster Based Methods to Fermionic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08773)
*Jeffrey Keyes,Carlos Jimenez-Hoyos*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces cluster mean field (cMF) theory for fermionic systems, along with its extensions to cluster perturbation theory (cPT) and cluster coupled cluster (cCC) methods. The authors address how fermionic commutation rules modify the approach, benchmark the methods on the Hubbard model and conjugated molecules, and compare performance against CAS-SCF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop new computational methods for strongly correlated fermionic systems that are more efficient or accurate than existing approaches like CAS-SCF, while properly accounting for the fermionic commutation relations that distinguish them from bosonic cluster methods.

Method: The authors present cluster mean field (cMF) as a reference method, extending it to cluster perturbation theory (cPT) and cluster coupled cluster (cCC). They discuss adaptations required due to fermionic commutation rules and other computational considerations, then apply these methods to the Hubbard model and selected conjugated molecules.

Result: Calculations on the Hubbard model and conjugated molecules demonstrate the efficacy of the cMF, cPT, and cCC methods. Comparisons with CAS-SCF illustrate the relative performance and accuracy of the cluster-based approaches.

Conclusion: The cluster mean field framework and its perturbative/coupled-cluster extensions provide viable alternatives for treating fermionic strongly correlated systems, with results benchmarked against CAS-SCF on model and molecular systems.

Abstract: We present the cluster mean field(cMF) and its extensions into perturbation theory(cPT) and coupled cluster(cCC) methods for fermionic systems. We discuss how the commutation rules for fermionic systems modifies the method as well as other computational details. We then perform calculations on the Hubbard model and a few select conjugated molecules to demonstrate the efficacy of the method. We will also use these examples to compare cluster methods to CAS-SCF.

</details>


### [207] [Orbital choice in constructing model Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08838)
*David W. O. Sousa,Elvira R. Sayfutyarova*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that different chemically reasonable choices of localized orbitals—arising from variations in the localization functional and the model orbital space—can yield substantially different model Hamiltonian parameters (e.g., Hubbard hoppings or Heisenberg exchange couplings) even when producing nearly identical electronic energies, using Density Matrix Downfolding across π-conjugated systems and transition metal complexes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model Hamiltonians rely on localized orbitals for construction, but the choice of localization functional and orbital space—often treated as mere technical details—may introduce significant ambiguity in derived Hamiltonian parameters. The authors seek to systematically expose and quantify how these seemingly arbitrary choices affect effective Hamiltonian parameters.

Method: The authors employ Density Matrix Downfolding to systematically investigate how two factors affect effective Hamiltonians derived from ab initio calculations: (1) the choice of localization functional and (2) the choice of model orbital space (the orbital space to which localization is applied). They test representative π-conjugated systems and transition metal complexes.

Result: Different chemically reasonable localized orbital choices can lead to substantially different model Hamiltonian parameters, even when the resulting electronic energies are nearly identical. Both the localization functional and, more importantly, the model orbital space choice significantly influence the effective Hamiltonian parameters.

Conclusion: The construction of model Hamiltonians is highly sensitive to localized orbital choices that are often overlooked. These choices must be explicitly considered and reported, as ambiguity in Hamiltonian parameters can arise despite nearly identical electronic energies, suggesting that orbital-space definitions warrant careful treatment in model Hamiltonian construction.

Abstract: Model Hamiltonians (e.g., the Hubbard or Heisenberg models) provide a simple yet physically meaningful description of complex electronic phenomena through a small number of parameters, such as electron hopping integrals or magnetic exchange couplings. Their construction typically exploits the local nature of electron correlation effects and therefore relies on localized orbitals. Although many localization schemes are available, the resulting localized orbitals depend not only on the chosen localization functional but also on the orbital space to which the localization procedure is applied. These choices are often treated as technical details and are rarely discussed explicitly. Here, we demonstrate that different, chemically reasonable choices of localized orbitals can lead to substantially different model Hamiltonian parameters, even when they produce nearly identical electronic energies. Using Density Matrix Downfolding, we systematically investigate how the localization functional and, more importantly, the choice of model orbital space affect effective Hamiltonians derived from ab initio calculations for representative $π$-conjugated systems and transition metal complexes.

</details>


### [208] [Balanced and broadly-normed meta-generalized gradient approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09034)
*Yanyong Wang,Chandra Shahi,John P. Perdew,Adrienn Ruzsinszky*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces bn-r²SCAN, a meta-GGA exchange-correlation functional that improves band-gap predictions to near the accuracy of the ultra-nonlocal LAK meta-GGA while maintaining lattice constants nearly as accurate as r²SCAN, by modifying the interpolation function between the one-electron and uniform electron gas limits and including spin-unpolarized bonded systems in the training norms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semi-local density functionals systematically underestimate fundamental band gaps. While meta-GGAs can partially overcome this via orbital dependence through the kinetic energy density, balancing performance across diverse properties (atomization energies, reaction barriers, lattice constants, and band gaps) is challenging because these properties exhibit different sensitivities to the large meta-GGA parameter space.

Method: The authors explore the space of norms guiding the r²SCAN-type functional construction through the interpolation function connecting the one-electron limit and the uniform electron gas limit. They introduce local modifications to a strongly-smoothed interpolation function and include spin-unpolarized bonded systems among the set of norms to create the bonded-norm bn-r²SCAN meta-GGA.

Result: The bn-r²SCAN meta-GGA achieves a balanced performance: band-gap predictions approaching the accuracy of the ultra-nonlocal LAK meta-GGA, while lattice constants remain nearly as accurate as r²SCAN.

Conclusion: By incorporating bonded-system norms and locally modifying the interpolation function in the r²SCAN construction, the meta-GGA framework can simultaneously deliver improved band-gap accuracy without sacrificing lattice-constant performance, demonstrating that a carefully engineered meta-GGA can balance properties previously thought to push the framework to its limits.

Abstract: Conventional semi-local density functional approximations for the exchange-correlation energy systematically underestimate fundamental band gaps. Advanced meta-generalized gradient approximations (meta-GGAs) can partially overcome this limitation through their orbital dependence via the kinetic energy density. In principle, the flexible meta-GGA form should allow for balanced performance across diverse properties. However, atomization energies, reaction barrier heights, and lattice constants, as well as band gaps, exhibit different sensitivities to the large parameter space of meta-GGA functionals. In this work, we explore the space of appropriate norms that guide the r$^2$SCAN-type functional construction through the interpolation function connecting two fundamental paradigms: the one-electron limit and the uniform electron gas limit. We introduce local modifications to a strongly-smoothed interpolation function and investigate their impact on functional performance. To further probe the limits of the meta-GGA framework, we include spin-unpolarized bonded systems among the set of norms. The resulting bonded-norm bn-r$^2$SCAN meta-GGA achieves balance: band-gap predictions approaching the accuracy of the ultra-nonlocal LAK meta-GGA and lattice constants of nearly r$^2$SCAN accuracy.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [209] [Nonequilibrium stochastic thermodynamics of boundary functionals: From Zubarev's ensemble to stochastic particle separation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08477)
*V. V. Ryazanov*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper extends Zubarev's nonequilibrium statistical operator method to simultaneously incorporate additive and nonlocal boundary functionals of trajectories, constructing a three-parameter model of nonequilibrium systems that includes first-passage time, dwell time above a given level, and absolute extremum of the process. The work establishes a correspondence between the maximum information entropy method for trajectories and the Donsker-Varadhan large deviation formalism, and uses Doob's h-transform to show that fixing extremal functionals induces non-Markovian transport with dynamic adaptation to historical records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to generalize Zubarev's nonequilibrium statistical operator method to handle both additive and nonlocal boundary functionals of trajectories simultaneously, enabling a unified thermodynamic description of rare events (first-passage times, dwell times, and extrema) in nonequilibrium systems.

Method: The authors construct a three-parameter nonequilibrium model via a unified thermodynamic (maximum entropy) approach, establishing a formal connection with the Donsker-Varadhan large deviation formalism, and employ Doob's h-transform to demonstrate that conditioning on extremal functionals of history generates effective non-Markovian transport with historical adaptation.

Result: The framework demonstrates that fixing extremal functionals of the history produces efficient non-Markovian transport with dynamic memory-based adaptation. Applicability criteria are formulated for the large-time regime, and potential optimizations for stochastic particle separation in periodic potentials are discussed.

Conclusion: The generalized nonequilibrium statistical operator method provides a unified thermodynamic framework for rare-event statistics in nonequilibrium systems, with demonstrated applicability to optimal stochastic particle separation in periodic potentials within the large-time regime.

Abstract: This paper develops a generalization of Zubarev's nonequilibrium statistical operator method for the case of simultaneous inclusion of additive and nonlocal boundary functionals of trajectories. Using a unified thermodynamic approach, a three-parameter model of nonequilibrium systems is constructed, including the first-passage time, the dwell time above a given level, and the absolute extremum of the process. A correspondence is demonstrated between the maximum information entropy method for trajectories and the Donsker-Varadan large deviation formalism. Using the Doob`s h-transform, it is demonstrated that fixing extremal functionals of history induces efficient non-Markovian transport in the system with dynamic adaptation to historical records. Criteria for the applicability of the developed apparatus in the "large time" domain and the possibilities of its use for optimizing stochastic particle separation in periodic potentials are discussed.

</details>


### [210] [Distribution of currents in a system of active particles with biased hops and Ising interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05649)
*Evgeniy Khain,Shriram Ramesh,Vladimir Chernyak*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies an ensemble of particles performing a biased random walk with nearest-neighbor Ising adhesion on a two-dimensional lattice. The authors theoretically compute the distribution of particle currents—verified by stochastic simulations—using rate equations akin to Becker-Döring cluster equations to derive probabilities of single, pair, and triplet configurations. A notable finding is the asymmetry between left- and right-handed triplet concentrations when both hopping asymmetry and inter-particle interactions are non-zero.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between microscopic particle interactions and macroscopic current statistics in driven lattice gases, by deriving analytical expressions for current distributions that account for adhesive (nearest-neighbor Ising) interactions between particles.

Method: The authors formulate rate equations for concentrations of distinct particle configurations (singlets, pairs, and various three-particle clusters), analogous to Becker-Döring cluster equations. These probabilities are used to analytically compute the distribution of macroscopic particle currents, and the results are validated against stochastic particle simulations.

Result: The theoretical current distributions match stochastic simulation results. Importantly, the concentration of left-handed triplets is found to differ from that of right-handed triplets when both hopping asymmetry and inter-particle adhesion are non-zero, revealing a chirality-breaking effect in the particle configurational statistics.

Conclusion: Adhesive interactions combined with biased hopping on a 2D lattice produce nontrivial configurational asymmetries (chiral imbalance in triplets) that must be accounted for to accurately predict macroscopic current statistics. The Becker-Döring-type cluster-rate-equation approach provides a tractable theoretical framework for such systems.

Abstract: We consider an ensemble of particles performing a biased random walk on a two-dimensional lattice. Due to the adhesion between particles (nearest-neighbor Ising interactions), the probability of hopping depends on the number of neighbors and the strength of adhesive interaction. The asymmetry in the hopping directions leads to a macroscopic current of particles through the system. We theoretically compute the distribution of currents and verify the results in stochastic particle simulations. To make theoretical progress, one has to determine the probabilities of various particle configurations such as single particles, pairs, and different configurations of three-particle clusters. These probabilities are theoretically derived by formulating rate equations for the concentrations of various structures resembling Becker-Döring cluster equations. One intriguing finding is that the concentration of left-handed triplets is not equal to the concentration of right-handed triplets for non-zero asymmetry of hops and non-zero interaction between particles.

</details>


### [211] [Foundations of Many-Body Theory of Quantum Unified Statistics: Green functions and Linear Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05878)
*M. Beyrami,M. N. Najafi,H. Esmaili,M. Dournama,H. Mohammadzadeh*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper develops a comprehensive many-body theory for quons (particles obeying quantum unified statistics, interpolating between Bose and Fermi statistics). It constructs a full formalism including Fock space properties, S-matrix expansion, a generalized Wick's theorem, Green functions at zero and finite temperatures, and diagrammatic rules. Using the random phase approximation, the authors derive the dielectric function, revealing anomalous plasmon modes unique to infinite statistics, and analyze ground-state energy, energy-loss functions, Thomas-Fermi screening, and Friedel oscillations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a rigorous many-body theoretical framework for interacting quon systems, which occupy an intermediate statistical regime between bosons and fermions, and to understand how infinite statistics qualitatively modifies collective phenomena such as plasmons and screening compared to conventional quantum statistics.

Method: The authors (1) explore the Fock space structure of quons obeying quantum unified statistics; (2) formulate a systematic S-matrix expansion and generalized Wick's theorem for these systems; (3) construct a consistent Green-function formalism at both zero and finite temperatures with a generalized Wick's theorem appropriate for infinite-statistics operator algebras; (4) establish diagrammatic perturbation rules; and (5) apply the random phase approximation (RPA) to derive the dielectric function and related physical quantities.

Result: The RPA analysis reveals the emergence of anomalous plasmon modes with no direct Bose or Fermi counterparts. The authors also derive the ground-state energy, energy-loss function, generalized Thomas-Fermi screening wave vectors, and Friedel oscillations, demonstrating that infinite statistics qualitatively changes collective behavior and screening properties of the many-body system.

Conclusion: Infinite (quantum unified) statistics fundamentally alters many-body physics: it gives rise to novel collective excitations and modified screening behavior that cannot be reproduced in ordinary Fermi or Bose systems, and the developed Green-function/diagrammatic formalism provides a general tool for investigating interacting quon systems.

Abstract: We develop a comprehensive many-body theory for systems of particles obeying quantum unified statistics, or quons. After exploring the properties of the Fock space of this system, we formulate a systematic S-matrix expansion and a generalized Wick's theorem. A consistent Green-function formalism is constructed at both zero and finite temperatures, accompanied by a generalized Wick's theorem appropriate for infinite-statistics operator algebras. Within this framework, we establish diagrammatic rules for interacting quon systems. Employing the random phase approximation, we derive the dielectric function and reveal the emergence of anomalous plasmon modes that have no direct counterpart in conventional Bose or Fermi systems. We further analyze the ground-state energy, energy-loss function, generalized Thomas-Fermi screening wave vectors, and Friedel oscillations, elucidating how infinite statistics qualitatively modifies collective behavior and screening properties.

</details>


### [212] [Nanothermodynamics: stable thermal equilibrium and nanoscale fluctuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06121)
*Ralph V. Chamberlin*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This review reassesses nanothermodynamics, a framework where large systems subdivide into equilibrium distributions of small subsystems, emphasizing Hill's subdivision potential (Ɛ) that ensures thermodynamic laws hold at any size. It highlights diverse applications: explaining thermodynamic heterogeneity via multiple effective temperatures in materials, providing a stable solution to Ising's finite-chain model, resolving Gibbs' paradox with exactly extensive entropy, modifying fluctuation relations in molecular dynamics when local degrees of freedom outpace bath coupling, and demonstrating via Creutz-like spin-bath simulations that maximizing total entropy requires an intrinsically irreversible step—contradicting the claim that statistical mechanics emerges from reversible dynamics. Fluctuations in this model are better described by Einstein's reversal of Boltzmann's relation than by recent fluctuation theorems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a comprehensive review and reassessment of nanothermodynamics, showing that Hill's subdivision potential (Ɛ) gives new insight into measurements, theories, and simulations that standard thermodynamics cannot address, particularly regarding thermodynamic heterogeneity, finite-size corrections, and the foundations of statistical mechanics.

Method: The paper synthesizes and reassesses diverse evidence: (1) measurements demonstrating thermodynamic heterogeneity through multiple effective temperatures (T_i) in materials, (2) analytic theoretical results including a stable solution of Ising's original model for finite chains requiring Ɛ=0 and a solution to Gibbs' paradox making semiclassical ideal gas entropy exactly extensive, (3) molecular dynamics simulations showing modified fluctuation relations when local fluctuations surpass bath coupling rates, and (4) a Creutz-like model of Ising spins coupled to Einstein-oscillator heat baths used to probe the 2nd law via total entropy maximization.

Result: Key findings include: Ising's finite-chain model has a stable solution that requires Ɛ=0 (a result Ising could not access 40 years before Hill); Gibbs' paradox receives a novel resolution yielding exactly extensive entropy; molecular dynamics shows fluctuation relations are modified when local degrees of freedom fluctuate faster than heat-bath coupling, consistent with measured heterogeneity; and Creutz-like model simulations show maximizing total entropy (S_t) requires an intrinsically irreversible step, providing a counterexample to emergence of statistical mechanics from reversible dynamics. Model fluctuations align best with Einstein's reversal of Boltzmann's relation and the 2nd law, not recent fluctuation theorems.

Conclusion: Nanothermodynamics provides a unifying framework with broad applicability across measurement, theory, and simulation. The findings challenge deep-seated assumptions—that statistical mechanics emerges purely from reversible dynamics and that recent fluctuation theorems universally describe small-system fluctuations—while establishing the centrality of Hill's subdivision potential in maintaining thermodynamic rigor at all system sizes.

Abstract: Nanothermodynamics describes the process where large systems subdivide into equilibrium distributions of small subsystems. A key ingredient is Hill's subdivision potential (E) that ensures adherence to the 1st and 2nd laws of thermodynamics in systems of any size. In this review and reassessment, it is emphasized that nanothermodynamics gives new insight into many measurements, theories, and simulations.
