Measurements establishing the need for nanothermodynamics show thermodynamic heterogeneity from multiple effective temperatures (T_i) inside most types of materials. One theoretical result that requires E=0 is the stable solution of Ising's original model for finite chains of interacting spins, a solution Ising could not have found 40 years before Hill's work. Another result is a novel solution to Gibbs' paradox that makes the entropy of the semiclassical ideal gas exactly extensive. Molecular dynamics simulations reveal how a standard fluctuation relation is modified when local degrees of freedom fluctuate faster than their coupling to the heat bath, consistent with the measured thermodynamic heterogeneity. Simulations of a Creutz-like model, comprised of Ising spins coupled to an explicit heat bath of Einstein oscillators, are used to study the 2nd law. It is found that maximizing the total entropy (S_t) requires an intrinsically irreversible step, providing a counterexample to the usual claim that statistical mechanics emerges from reversible dynamics. Furthermore, fluctuations of this model are best described by Einstein's reversal of Boltzmann's relation and the 2nd-law, not by recent fluctuation theorems.

</details>


### [213] [Statistical mechanical evaluation of a spread-spectrum watermarking model with image restoration II AT stability of a hybrid system with message decoding and image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06405)
*Tatsuya Uezu,Kao Hayashi,Masaki Kawamura*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper investigates the de Almeida-Thouless (AT) stability of the replica symmetric (RS) solution in a blind spread-spectrum watermarking model with image restoration based on Bayes estimation, revealing that the disagreement between theoretical and simulation results in a certain parameter range is due to the breaking of AT stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In the previous paper, the RS solution and Markov chain Monte Carlo simulation results showed disagreement in some parameter ranges when analyzing the watermarking model built on the infinite range Ising model. This discrepancy needs to be understood to validate and refine the theoretical framework.

Method: The authors treat the blind case (where both the original message and original image are unknown) of the spread-spectrum watermarking model, which is based on Bayes estimation with prior probabilities and a Gaussian channel model for attacks. They employ the replica method from statistical mechanics and perform an AT stability analysis of the RS solution.

Result: The AT stability analysis reveals that the RS solution becomes unstable (AT stability is broken) precisely in the parameter range where theoretical predictions and simulation results disagree.

Conclusion: The disagreement between the RS theoretical results and Markov chain Monte Carlo simulations in the earlier work is attributed to the breaking of replica symmetry, as demonstrated by the AT stability analysis in the blind case. This explains the observed discrepancy and indicates that replica symmetry breaking may be needed for accurate predictions in that parameter regime.

Abstract: In the previous paper (arxiv.org/abs/1209.4772), we proposed a spread-spectrum watermarking model with image restoration based on Bayes estimation assuming several prior probabilities and adopting the Gaussian channel model to represent attacks from unauthorized users. When an image is generated from the infinite range Ising model, we analyzed the model using the statistical mechanical method, the replica method, and derived the replica symmetric (RS) solution and performed Markov chain Monte Carlo simulations. The theoretical results of the RS solution and the simulation results were in good agreement except for some range of parameters. We treated the informed case where only the original image is known and the blind case where both the original message and the original image are unknown and found that the difference between these cases was small as long as the embedding and attack rates were small. In this paper, we treat the blind case and investigate the de Almeida-Thouless (AT) stability of the RS solution and reveal that the AT stability is broken in the range of parameters where theoretical and simulation results do not agree.

</details>


### [214] [Improved mean squared displacement analysis for anomalous single particle trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06828)
*Jakub Ślęzak,Joanna Janczura,Diego Krapf,Ralf Metzler*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of estimating diffusion parameters from short and ultra-short single-particle trajectories in heterogeneous media. The authors apply a generalized least squares framework to account for correlations introduced by time-averaging and inherent to anomalous diffusion, substantially reducing variance and bias in parameter estimates. The method is fully automated, prediction of estimation error distributions (asymptotically Gaussian) is enabled, and a deconvolution algorithm is introduced to reconstruct the underlying particle ensemble structure from experimental data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical regression methods perform poorly when estimating diffusion parameters from time-averaged MSD in log-log space, because time-averaging introduces correlations that are aggravated by correlations inherent to anomalous diffusion. This is especially problematic for short (~100 points) and ultra-short (~10 points) single-particle trajectories in heterogeneous systems, where extracting maximal information from each measured trajectory is essential.

Method: The authors apply a generalized least squares framework to estimate diffusion parameters, which explicitly accounts for the correlations present in time-averaged MSD data. The method is fully automated and requires no supervision. Additionally, the framework enables prediction of the estimation error probability density (asymptotically Gaussian), which underpins a specialized deconvolution algorithm for reconstructing the underlying particle ensemble structure.

Result: The generalized least squares approach substantially reduces variance and bias in diffusion parameter estimates compared to classical regression methods, particularly for short (~100 points) and ultra-short (~10 points) trajectories. The method also enables prediction of estimation error probability densities (asymptotically Gaussian) and the deconvolution algorithm can reconstruct ensemble structure from experimental data.

Conclusion: The proposed generalized least squares framework overcomes limitations of classical regression for MSD-based diffusion analysis in heterogeneous media, providing more reliable estimates from very short trajectories. It additionally supports error prediction and ensemble deconvolution, offering a complete, automated, and unsupervised pipeline for analyzing single-particle tracking data.

Abstract: The mean squared displacement (MSD) is a cornerstone in the analysis of diffusion processes in complex media. When the system is heterogeneous and, in particular, when single-particle trajectories are short, it is essential to extract maximal information from each measured trajectory. This is typically done by time-averaging squared increments and examining the scaling of the time-averaged MSD in log-log space. However, classical regression methods perform poorly in this setting because time-averaging introduces correlations aggravated by those inherent to anomalous diffusion. We tackle these limitations by applying a generalized least squares framework, which substantially reduces variance and bias in diffusion parameter estimates, especially for short (around 100 points) and ultra-short (around 10 points) trajectories. The method is fully automated and requires no supervision. Furthermore, it enables prediction of estimation error probability density, which is asymptotically Gaussian, for both classical and enhanced approaches. Leveraging this prediction, we introduce a specialized deconvolution algorithm that reconstructs the underlying particle ensemble structure from experimental data.

</details>


### [215] [Relaxation of Sliding Friction from a Statistical Model of Aging Contacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06998)
*Ryudo Suzuki*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces a minimal theoretical model linking microscopic contact statistics (logarithmic aging and power-law contact-size distribution from fractal roughness) to macroscopic frictional relaxation after a sudden velocity change, deriving an analytical relaxation function that predicts plateau, logarithmic, or power-law decay depending on the size-distribution exponent — in contrast to the exponential decay assumed in standard phenomenological laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect the phenomenological laws governing frictional stability of steady sliding and seismic faults with measurable microscopic contact statistics, since the microscopic origins of frictional relaxation after velocity changes are not fully understood.

Method: A minimal analytical model is constructed incorporating (i) logarithmic aging of contact forces and (ii) a power-law contact-size distribution that reflects fractal interface roughness. The relaxation function is then derived analytically as a function of the power-law exponent α.

Result: The derived relaxation function yields three distinct intermediate-time regimes depending on the exponent α: a plateau, logarithmic decay, or power-law decay. These results contrast with the exponential decay assumed by conventional phenomenological friction laws.

Conclusion: The theory offers experimentally testable predictions connecting microscopic contact statistics to macroscopic frictional relaxation, potentially improving the physical basis of friction laws used in earthquake and sliding stability modeling.

Abstract: Frictional relaxation after a sudden velocity change underlies the phenomenological laws that describe the stability of steady sliding and of seismic faults. To connect this relaxation with measurable statistics of microscopic contacts, we introduce a minimal model incorporating logarithmic aging of contact forces and a power-law contact-size distribution reflecting fractal interface roughness, and derive the relaxation function analytically. Depending on the power-law exponent $α$, the intermediate-time response exhibits a plateau, logarithmic decay, or power-law decay, in contrast to the exponential decay assumed in the phenomenological laws. The theory provides experimentally testable links between microscopic contact statistics and macroscopic frictional relaxation.

</details>


### [216] [Irreversibility of many-body harmonic oscillators characterized by generalized Husimi's adiabaticity parameter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07039)
*Kyosuke Watanabe,Yuki Izumida*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper generalizes Husimi's classical result on the non-decrease of averaged energy under cyclic operations for a single harmonic oscillator to a many-body system of coupled harmonic oscillators on an arbitrary connected network with common time-dependent spring constants. By decomposing the system into independent normal modes and defining a generalized Husimi adiabaticity parameter as the arithmetic mean of individual mode parameters, the authors derive the exact time evolution of averaged energy and prove its non-decrease under cyclic operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Husimi's 1953 result established that for a single harmonic oscillator with time-dependent frequency, the averaged energy cannot decrease after a cyclic operation, quantified by an adiabaticity parameter. This work aims to extend this foundational irreversibility framework from a single oscillator to a general many-body system of oscillators coupled on an arbitrary connected network, addressing the question of whether similar energy non-decrease guarantees hold in more complex interacting settings.

Method: The method involves: (1) transforming the coupled oscillator system to mass-weighted coordinates and diagonalizing the resulting positive definite matrix to decompose the dynamics into independent normal modes; (2) assigning each normal mode its own Husimi adiabaticity parameter; (3) defining a generalized Husimi adiabaticity parameter as the arithmetic mean of these mode-wise parameters; (4) deriving the exact time evolution of the averaged energy using this generalized parameter; and (5) numerically verifying the results on both a uniform nearest-neighbor chain and a heterogeneous network.

Result: The generalized Husimi adiabaticity parameter allows derivation of the exact time evolution of the averaged energy for the many-body oscillator system. It is established that the averaged energy cannot decrease under cyclic operations, extending Husimi's original single-oscillator irreversibility result. Numerical simulations on both a uniform nearest-neighbor chain and a heterogeneous network confirm the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: Husimi's irreversibility framework generalizes successfully to arbitrary connected networks of coupled harmonic oscillators with common time-dependent spring constants. The system remains non-decreasing in averaged energy under cyclic operations, and the generalized adiabaticity parameter (arithmetic mean over normal modes) serves as the natural quantification of this irreversibility.

Abstract: In a classical paper [K. Husimi, Prog. Theor. Phys. 9, 381 (1953)], Husimi showed that, for a single harmonic oscillator with a time-dependent angular frequency and initial conditions sampled from an equilibrium distribution, the averaged energy cannot decrease after a cyclic operation. This irreversibility is quantified by Husimi's adiabaticity parameter constituted with adiabatic invariants. In this work, we generalize Husimi's framework to a many-body system of one-dimensional harmonic oscillators coupled on an arbitrary connected network, with all spring constants sharing a common time dependence. For a system attached to a fixed wall, the dynamics can be decomposed into independent normal modes by transforming to mass-weighted coordinates and diagonalizing the resulting positive definite matrix, with each mode characterized by its own Husimi's adiabaticity parameter. By defining the generalized Husimi's adiabaticity parameter as their arithmetic mean, we derive the exact time evolution of the averaged energy and establish its non-decrease under cyclic operations. Numerical simulations confirm these results for both a uniform nearest-neighbor chain and a heterogeneous network.

</details>


### [217] [Probing magnetic correlations in space and time within predefined topological sectors of a macroscopic spin liquid](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07140)
*Rémy Dangoisse,Jeanne Colbois,Laurent Del Rey,Nicolas Rougemaille,Johann Coraux*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper presents an experimental realization of the triangular Ising antiferromagnet using a macroscopic lattice of millimeter-sized NdFeB cylinders driven by a mechanical shaker, demonstrating that its low-energy physics and true ground state can be reached experimentally. The authors additionally probe magnetic properties within manually preselected pockets of the ground-state manifold, emulating behavior in distinct topological sectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The triangular Ising antiferromagnet is a canonical two-dimensional spin liquid with residual entropy density and topological sector structure, but these properties have been mainly studied theoretically. The authors ask to what extent these properties can be transposed to real materials and seek experimental verification.

Method: The authors construct an artificial macroscopic realization of the triangular Ising antiferromagnet using a lattice of millimeter-sized NdFeB cylinders agitated by a mechanical shaker. They experimentally reach the very-low-energy physics and the true ground state, and probe magnetic properties in space and time within manually preselected pockets of the ground-state manifold that approximate distinct topological sectors.

Result: The authors demonstrate that the very-low-energy physics and the true ground state of the triangular Ising antiferromagnet model can be reached experimentally in this macroscopic realization, and successfully probe magnetic properties within manually selected ground-state manifold pockets that emulate behaviors in different topological sectors.

Conclusion: This macroscopic experimental platform opens new avenues for naked-eye visualization and hands-on manipulation of many-body phenomena in frustrated magnetism and statistical physics models, bridging theoretical predictions with observable realizations.

Abstract: The triangular Ising antiferromagnet, with its residual entropy density and critical correlations at absolute zero, is the archetype of a two-dimensional spin liquid. Its ground state is partitioned into topological sectors connected by global spin flip events that wrap the lattice boundaries, and become statistically irrelevant in the thermodynamic limit where magnetic fluctuations are restricted to the dominant sector. Thus far, these properties have been mainly investigated from a theoretical perspective, and one might wonder to what extent they can be transposed to real materials. Here, we present experimental observations obtained in an artificial, macroscopic realisation of the seminal triangular Ising antiferromagnet that consists of a lattice of millimeter-sized NdFeB cylinders put into motion by a mechanical shaker. Specifically, we demonstrate that the very-low-energy physics and the true ground state of this model can be reached experimentally. Besides, we are able to probe, in space and time, the magnetic properties within manually preselected pockets of the ground-state manifold that emulate, to a good approximation, the behaviour in distinct topological sectors. Our approach opens new avenues for naked-eye visualisation and hand manipulation of many-body phenomena associated to frustrated magnetism and models of statistical physics.

</details>


### [218] [Reverse engineering of mechano-kinetic parameters from stochastic force profiles in heterogeneous ensembles of molecular motors and tracks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07170)
*Valentina Buonfiglio,Irene Pertici,Pasquale Bianco,Duccio Fanelli,Stefano Gherardini*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper presents a stochastic framework for modeling heterogeneous ensembles of myosin motors with distinct mechano-kinetic properties interacting with actin filaments. The framework derives the probability distribution of isometric force, characterizes finite-size force fluctuations, and includes an estimation procedure that simultaneously infers mechano-kinetic parameters and ensemble size without prior assumptions. Validation against synthetic and experimental data demonstrates accurate force fluctuation capture and realistic ensemble size estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneity in contractile systems of molecular motors plays a key role in many cellular physiological and pathological processes, yet cannot be captured by theoretical models that assume identical mechano-kinetic properties for all motors. A quantitative framework is needed to handle heterogeneous motor ensembles.

Method: Developed a stochastic framework describing heterogeneous ensembles of myosin motors comprising two populations with distinct mechano-kinetic properties. Motors interact with the actin filament as independent force generators. The framework derives the probability distribution of isometric force and characterizes finite-size force fluctuation statistics. It also includes an estimation procedure to simultaneously infer mechano-kinetic parameters and ensemble size. Validation against synthetic and experimental data was performed.

Result: The model accurately captures force fluctuations and provides realistic estimates of ensemble size. The framework enables quantitative assessment of ensemble heterogeneity and supports inference of unknown motor species properties when validated against synthetic and experimental data.

Conclusion: This approach provides a quantitative tool for characterizing heterogeneous actin-myosin systems, such as those arising from co-presence of different protein isoforms in cardiac and skeletal muscle, or from partial replacement of native proteins with mutation-derived or engineered variants.

Abstract: Heterogeneity in contractile systems of molecular motors interacting with their tracks plays a key role in numerous cellular physiological and pathological processes. However, its effects cannot be captured by theoretical models assuming identical mechano-kinetic properties for all motors. We developed a stochastic framework to describe heterogeneous ensembles of myosin motors comprising two populations with distinct mechano-kinetic properties. Assuming that motors interact with the actin filament (their track) as independent force generators, we derived the probability distribution of the isometric force and characterised the statistics of finite-size force fluctuations. The proposed framework includes an estimation procedure that simultaneously infers the mechano-kinetic parameters and the size of the motor ensemble, eliminating the need to prescribe it a priori. Validation against synthetic and experimental data shows the model accurately captures force fluctuations and provides realistic estimates of the ensemble size. Furthermore, the framework enables a quantitative assessment of ensemble heterogeneity and the inference of an unknown motor species properties. This approach provides a quantitative tool for characterising heterogeneous actin-myosin systems, such as those arising from the co-presence of different protein isoforms in cardiac and skeletal muscle, or from the partial replacement of native proteins with mutation-derived or engineered variants.

</details>


### [219] [Emergent Equilibrium Structure Along a Critical Cluster Recursion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07332)
*Shuo Wei,Abbas Ali Saberi,Youjin Deng*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper shows that a bicolored cluster recursion constrained to remain critical at every generation, with no equilibrium spin measure or fixed coupling, develops an Ising/FK-compatible conditional structure in both 2D and 3D, despite critical universality not determining microscopic conditional structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether criticality alone can constrain the microscopic conditional structure of a probability measure, without imposing an equilibrium spin measure or fixed couplings, and whether Ising/FK compatibility can emerge along critical trajectories.

Method: Study a history-dependent bicolored cluster recursion kept critical at every generation. Analyze the resulting conditional laws in 2D and 3D, examining second-shell dependence, nearest-neighbor effective couplings, and cluster/interface observables. Use an exact cluster-coloring factorization to examine the q=2 special point.

Result: In both 2D and 3D, the critical recursion produces a common Ising/FK structure: second-shell dependence of one-site conditional laws is strongly suppressed, effective nearest-neighbor couplings converge toward the critical Ising value, and observables organize around FK geometry. An exact factorization singles out q=2 as the point where residual connectivity weight vanishes from the two-color spin marginal.

Conclusion: Equilibrium-compatible conditional structure can emerge along a trajectory that remains critical throughout, even when no equilibrium spin measure or fixed coupling is imposed, demonstrating that criticality alone can shape conditional structure toward Ising/FK compatibility.

Abstract: Critical universality does not determine the microscopic conditional structure of a probability measure. We study a bicolored cluster recursion constrained to remain critical at every generation, with no equilibrium spin measure or fixed coupling imposed. In both two and three dimensions, the resulting history-dependent sequence develops a common Ising/Fortuin--Kasteleyn (FK) compatibility structure: the second-shell dependence of a one-site conditional law is strongly suppressed, nearest-neighbor effective couplings move progressively toward one another near the critical Ising value, and cluster and interface observables organize around the corresponding FK geometry. An exact cluster-coloring factorization singles out $q=2$ as the point where the residual connectivity weight disappears from the two-color spin marginal. Thus equilibrium-compatible conditional structure can emerge along a trajectory that remains critical throughout.

</details>


### [220] [Fragility from Enthalpy Fluctuations and Fictive-Temperature Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07396)
*Biman Bagchi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper formulates a direct connection between glass fragility and fictive temperature through configurational enthalpy fluctuations, showing that fictive-temperature dynamics during cooling or aging are governed by enthalpy–enthalpy correlation functions and the frequency-dependent configurational heat capacity, unified within a common time-domain framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fragility and fictive temperature are two central measures of glass formation, yet their relationship is typically indirect. The authors aim to establish a direct physical connection between them via configurational enthalpy fluctuations.

Method: The approach links the time evolution of fictive temperature to enthalpy–enthalpy correlation functions that also determine frequency-dependent configurational heat capacity. Fragility enters through the temperature dependence of structural relaxation time, which controls how the enthalpy-relaxation spectrum shifts to long times near the glass transition, enabling a direct time-domain formulation including the Debye limit.

Result: A direct time-domain description of the onset and growth of the fictive-temperature lag is achieved, demonstrating that fragility controls how rapidly the enthalpy-relaxation spectrum shifts to long times near the glass transition.

Conclusion: The work connects calorimetric relaxation, fragility, and nonequilibrium structural arrest within a common framework, unifying these central concepts of glass formation.

Abstract: Fragility and fictive temperature are two central measures of glass formation, but their connection is usually expressed indirectly through structural relaxation times or cooling-rate dependence. Here we formulate this connection in terms of configurational enthalpy fluctuations. The time evolution of the fictive temperature during cooling or aging is controlled by the enthalpy--enthalpy correlation function that also determines the frequency-dependent configurational heat capacity. Fragility enters through the strong temperature dependence of the structural relaxation time and therefore controls how rapidly the corresponding enthalpy-relaxation spectrum shifts to long times near the glass transition. This leads to a direct time-domain description of the onset and growth of the fictive-temperature lag, including the Debye limit, and connects calorimetric relaxation, fragility, and nonequilibrium structural arrest within a common framework.

</details>


### [221] [Exact Hamiltonian Dynamics of Rare Events in Active Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07496)
*Andrea Crisanti,Matteo Paoluzzi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper establishes an exact mapping between the optimal paths of a non-Markovian active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particle and a higher-dimensional Hamiltonian dynamical system, enabling systematic computation of optimal paths for arbitrary force fields via Hamilton's equations. Tuning the conserved energy reveals diverse dynamical regimes, from classical zero-energy instanton trajectories to closed periodic orbits at non-zero energies—a distinct signature of non-Markovian active dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active systems navigate via non-equilibrium fluctuations, making standard equilibrium transition-rate theories inapplicable. Furthermore, transition rates alone yield only partial insight into how non-equilibrium dynamics explore metastable states; knowing the optimal paths provides deeper physical understanding, yet these paths are non-Markovian and difficult to compute directly.

Method: Using an active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particle (a stochastic dynamics driven by exponentially correlated noise) as a paradigmatic model, the authors derive an exact mapping of the non-Markovian optimal path onto a higher-dimensional Hamiltonian system. Optimal paths are then computed systematically by solving the corresponding Hamilton equations for arbitrary force fields, with the conserved energy used to tune between different dynamical regimes.

Result: The mapping allows exploration of diverse dynamical regimes by tuning the conserved energy: classical instanton trajectories strictly confined to the zero-energy surface, and closed periodic orbits that emerge only at non-zero energies, the latter being a distinct signature of the non-Markovian active dynamics.

Conclusion: The work provides a systematic Hamiltonian framework for computing optimal paths in active non-Markovian systems, revealing unique dynamical phenomena (non-zero-energy periodic orbits) that distinguish active dynamics from standard equilibrium behavior and offer deeper physical insight beyond transition rates.

Abstract: Active systems navigate complex environments through non-equilibrium fluctuations, rendering standard equilibrium transition-rate theories inadequate. Moreover, transition rates provide only partial information on how non-equilibrium dynamics explore metastable states, whereas knowledge of the optimal paths offers deeper physical insight. Using an active Ornstein-Uhlenbeck particle as a paradigmatic model, i.e., a stochastic dynamics driven by exponentially correlated noise, we establish an exact mapping of the non-Markovian optimal path onto a higher-dimensional Hamiltonian dynamical system so that, for arbitrary force fields, optimal paths can be computed systematically by solving the corresponding Hamilton equations. Tuning the conserved energy allows us to explore diverse dynamical regimes: ranging from classical instanton trajectories strictly confined to the zero-energy surface, to closed periodic orbits that emerge only at non-zero energies, representing a distinct signature of the non-Markovian active dynamics.

</details>


### [222] [Microcanonical Hamiltonian Monte Carlo and the Helmholtz Theorem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07620)
*Heinrich von Campe,Bjoern Malte Schaefer*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper analyzes Microcanonical Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (MCHMC) from a thermodynamic perspective, deriving state variables and potentials to show it formally represents a microcanonical ensemble. It proves the algorithm satisfies the Helmholtz theorem (first law of thermodynamics) analytically and numerically, extends MCHMC to lower-dimensional inference problems, and argues canonical MCMC methods are more natural thermodynamically and information-theoretically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Microcanonical Hamiltonian Monte Carlo algorithm has not been examined in detail from a thermodynamic standpoint, and the authors aim to fill this gap by formally establishing its thermodynamic foundations and exploring its implications.

Method: The authors derive thermodynamical state variables and potentials for MCHMC to demonstrate it formally represents a microcanonical thermodynamic ensemble. They analytically and numerically verify the Helmholtz theorem. They also construct a new sampling algorithm extending MCHMC to lower-dimensional inference problems, and provide thermodynamic/information-theoretic arguments comparing canonical and microcanonical MCMC approaches.

Result: The paper demonstrates that MCHMC formally represents a microcanonical thermodynamic ensemble and satisfies the Helmholtz theorem both analytically and numerically. A new variant extending the algorithm to lower-dimensional inference is constructed, and arguments are presented that canonical MCMC is more natural than MCHMC from thermodynamic and information-theoretic perspectives.

Conclusion: MCHMC is formally a microcanonical thermodynamic ensemble satisfying the first law of thermodynamics, but canonical MCMC methods are argued to be more natural choices from thermodynamic and information-theoretic standpoints.

Abstract: The recently proposed Microcanonical Hamiltonian Monte Carlo algorithm has not yet been studied in detail from a thermodynamic point of view; this work aims to fill that gap. We demonstrate how thermodynamical state variables and potentials can be derived and thereby demonstrate that the construction of the algorithm formally represents a microcanonical thermodynamic ensemble. In particular, we demonstrate (analytically and numerically) that the algorithm fulfils the Helmholtz theorem, an alternative formulation of the first law of thermodynamics. Furthermore, we construct a new sampling algorithm that extends the original to lower-dimensional inference problems. Finally, we argue that canonical Markov Chain Monte Carlo algorithms are more natural than Microcanonical Hamiltonian Monte Carlo from the thermodynamic and information-theoretic point of view.

</details>


### [223] [Topographic Disorder, Wind Coupling, and Directional Fire Spread: Critical Behavior in a Terrain-Weighted Forest Fire Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07763)
*Juan M. Florez,Eric Suárez Morell,Cristian Millan,J. Restrepo*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Terrain-Weighted Forest Fire Model (TFFM), a lattice model coupling fire spread to a spatially correlated Gaussian height field with asymmetric bond probabilities and wind bias. Simulations up to L=8192 reveal a sharp active-to-inactive transition with critical exponents that match neither directed nor isotropic percolation, showing that terrain slope acts as a fire suppressant. The model reproduces satellite burn-scar signatures and yields fire-risk thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how terrain relief (height gradients) fundamentally alters fire percolation dynamics compared to conventional isotropic bond-disorder models, and to build a fire model that reproduces realistic ecological/observational signatures (burn scars and risk thresholds) while revealing new universality classes of nonequilibrium phase transitions.

Method: A lattice percolation-style model where fire spreads on a spatially correlated Gaussian height field via asymmetric bond probability p_{i→j}=clip[exp(-β+γ(h_j-h_i)),0,1] plus an additive wind bias. Large-scale simulations up to linear system size L=8192 with finite-size scaling, front-velocity measurements, single-seed survival probability analysis, and comparison to satellite burn-scar data.

Result: The critical suppression threshold β_c is even in γ, decreases with |γ| and with smaller terrain correlation length. Fire fails to percolate on rough low-density terrain even at zero suppression. Front-velocity exponent δ=0.34±0.02 (universal across terrain roughness), distinct from both directed (≈0.56) and isotropic percolation (≈0.18). Survival probability at criticality decays ulta-slowly (running exponent 0.04-0.09), is L-independent, and remains finite (~P*≈0.5), excluding directed percolation. Wind raises β_c by 2-4×, induces sharp downwind scar drift, and creates a terrain-wind competition that reduces burned fraction at high terrain coupling.

Conclusion: Terrain slope asymmetry fundamentally reorganizes fire percolation, acting as a suppressant with universality distinct from standard percolation classes. The model captures realistic fire-risk thresholds and burn-scar signatures matching satellite data, making it a valuable tool for wildfire risk assessment; correlation-length exponent remains unresolved suggesting slow crossover from quenched correlated disorder.

Abstract: We introduce the Terrain-Weighted Forest Fire Model (TFFM), a lattice model in which fire spreads on a spatially correlated Gaussian height field with the asymmetric bond probability $p_{i\to j}=\mathrm{clip}[e^{-β+γ(h_j-h_i)},0,1]$, plus an additive wind bias. Simulations up to $L=8192$ reveal a sharp active-to-inactive transition whose critical suppression threshold $β_c$ is even in $γ$, decreases with $|γ|$, and decreases as the terrain correlation length $σ_h$ is reduced: slope asymmetry acts as a suppressant because downhill bonds are penalized and fire stalls at local elevation maxima. For rough terrain and low tree density, the fire fails to percolate even at zero suppression. Finite-size scaling gives a front-velocity exponent $δ=0.34\pm0.02$, identical for smooth and rough terrain and matching neither directed percolation ($ν_\parallel-ν_\perp\approx0.56$) nor isotropic percolation ($\approx0.18$). The single-seed survival probability at $β_c$ is independent of $L$ and decays extremely slowly, with a running exponent falling from $\approx0.09$ to $\approx0.04$, excluding directed percolation and suggesting a survival probability that remains finite at criticality, consistent with the $L$-independent value $P^*\approx0.5$ at which $P_{\rm surv}$ drops to zero. The correlation-length exponent cannot be resolved at these sizes, consistent with slow crossover corrections from quenched correlated disorder. Wind raises $β_c$ by a factor of 2--4, produces a sharp onset of downwind fire-scar drift at weak coupling, and, at high terrain coupling, decreases the burned fraction at boundary crossing---a terrain-wind competition effect absent from isotropic bond-disorder models. The model yields fire-risk thresholds and fire-scar signatures comparable to satellite burn-scar data.

</details>


### [224] [Directional memory of early spectral selection during spinodal decomposition in finite systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07764)
*Boliang Yu,Ruixin Zhou,Zheng Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether the spectral anisotropy axis selected early in Cahn-Hilliard phase separation remains correlated with the axis favored later in the same evolution. Using 2D simulations with isotropic initial fluctuations, the authors introduce Q2 as a normalized angular measure of spectral anisotropy. They find that the early axis remains correlated with the late axis during coarsening, and that including the early Q2 direction in predictions reduces mean absolute error for late Q2 by 3.53%, while retaining only magnitude or using a direction from another evolution eliminates this improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether spatial direction information captured early in Cahn-Hilliard spinodal decomposition is predictive of the coarsening orientation late in the evolution, despite isotropic initial conditions and no imposed direction.

Method: Two-dimensional Cahn-Hilliard simulations with isotropic initial fluctuations and no imposed directional bias are analyzed. A measure Q2, a normalized angular average over the Fourier spectrum, is introduced to quantify the strength and direction of spectral anisotropy. Early and late axes are compared, and predictions of late Q2 are constructed from quench depth, mean composition, box size, and early Q2 information.

Result: Linear growth amplifies different wave numbers at different rates, selecting an early axis from initial angular imbalances. This early axis remains correlated with the late axis during coarsening. Shallow quenches show stronger early-late alignment at fixed time, but this advantage vanishes when quenches are compared at similar stages of domain growth. Adding early Q2 direction to prediction models reduces mean absolute error for late Q2 by 3.53%; this reduction disappears if only its magnitude is kept or if its direction is substituted from another evolution.

Conclusion: The early Fourier intensity carries genuine directional information about the later state of the same evolving field, establishing that spectral anisotropy direction early in spinodal decomposition is predictive of late-stage coarsening orientation in the same trajectory.

Abstract: Cahn-Hilliard phase separation turns small composition fluctuations into coarsening domains. In Fourier space, the pattern forms a ring whose angular intensity favors an axis. We ask whether the axis favored early in an evolution remains related to the axis favored later in the same evolution. We analyze two-dimensional simulations with isotropic initial fluctuations and no imposed direction. We introduce $Q_2$, a normalized angular average over the Fourier spectrum, as a measure of the strength and direction of spectral anisotropy. Linear growth amplifies different wave numbers at different rates, selecting an early axis from the angular imbalances in the initial spectrum. This axis remains correlated with the late axis during coarsening. At a fixed time, shallow quenches show stronger early-late alignment. This advantage disappears when the quenches are compared at similar stages of domain growth. Adding the early $Q_2$ to a prediction based on quench depth, mean composition, and box size reduces the mean absolute error for late $Q_2$ by $3.53\%$. This reduction disappears when only the magnitude of the early $Q_2$ is retained, or when its direction is replaced by the direction from another evolution. The early Fourier intensity therefore carries information about the later state of the same evolving field.

</details>


### [225] [Tensor network investigation of the monomer-dimer model on the honeycomb lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07899)
*De-Zhang Li,Jie Liu,Xin Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies the monomer-dimer model on the honeycomb lattice (with activities = 1) by mapping it to ground states of an antiferromagnetic Ising model on the Kagomé lattice at critical field H_ex=4J. A tensor network based on local Ising triangle ground states is contracted using VUMPS, yielding high-precision results. The work also revisits edge coloring on the honeycomb lattice, reformulates the model as a sixteen-vertex model, and discusses integrability properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The monomer-dimer model is a longstanding unsolved problem in lattice statistical mechanics. The authors aim to obtain high-precision numerical results for this model on the honeycomb lattice using modern tensor network techniques, and to clarify its connections with related problems such as edge coloring and vertex models.

Method: The monomer-dimer configurations are exactly mapped to ground states of the antiferromagnetic Ising model on the Kagomé lattice at the critical field H_ex=4J. A tensor network is constructed from local ground states of each Ising triangle, and the VUMPS (variational uniform matrix product state) algorithm is used to contract the network. Connections to edge coloring and the sixteen-vertex model formulation are also explored.

Result: The VUMPS approach provides high-precision results for the monomer-dimer problem on the honeycomb lattice. The study also provides insights into the relationship between the monomer-dimer model and edge coloring on the honeycomb lattice, and clarifies the non-integrability of the general monomer-dimer model versus the integrability of the pure dimer model through the sixteen-vertex model formulation.

Conclusion: A tensor-network-based exact mapping from the monomer-dimer model to an Ising model enables high-precision computation via VUMPS. The work further establishes formal connections between monomer-dimer, edge-coloring, and vertex-model formulations, and clarifies integrability distinctions between general monomer-dimer and pure dimer models.

Abstract: The monomer-dimer model is one of the most well-known unsolved lattice models. In this paper we study the monomer-dimer model on the honeycomb lattice using the tensor network method, in the case that the dimer and monomer activities are 1. The monomer-dimer configurations are exactly mapped into the ground states of the antiferromagnetic Ising model on the Kagomé lattice in the critical field $H_{\rm{ex}}=4J$, and the tensor network is constructed based on the local ground states of each Ising triangle. The VUMPS approach is employed to contract the tensor network, providing a high-precision result of the monomer-dimer problem. We also revisit the edge coloring problem on the honeycomb lattice and discuss its relationship to the monomer-dimer model. Finally we formulate the monomer-dimer problem in the language of the sixteen-vertex model, and discuss the non-integrability of the general monomer-dimer model and the integrability of the pure dimer model.

</details>


### [226] [Leveraged Learning: entropy destroyed per bit received](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08054)
*Daniel Chernowitz*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper analyzes information acquisition through a Bayesian learner answering boolean questions, defining "leverage" as table entropy destroyed per bit of surprisal. It derives exact thermodynamic-limit leverage curves, shows exchangeable priors (via de Finetti) yield flat profiles and hyperbolic leverage confined to a boundary layer, and constructs a simplicity prior based on Boolean polynomial degree over F2 whose Reed-Muller capacity realizes any nonincreasing profile and leverage curve macroscopically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how intelligent (informative) priors can amplify the information gained per answer beyond the unit ratio of a uniform prior, by characterizing the relationship between surprise (information expected) and uncertainty destruction across unasked questions, in a thermodynamic limit as the number of questions and input bits grow.

Method: Define leverage as entropy destroyed per bit of surprisal. Encode the exact average over truth prior and question order via a single generating sequence: mean entropy G_l of answers to l questions. Pass to the thermodynamic limit (fixed asked fraction t = l/Q, infinite input bits) where increments form profile gamma(t). Derive the leverage functional L(t) = [eta_0 - (1-t)gamma(t)]/integral_0^t gamma. For exchangeable priors, de Finetti forces flat gamma(t), yielding hyperbolic decay. Construct a simplicity prior grading Boolean maps by polynomial degree over F2 with weight budget across degree shells controlled by a CDF F; Reed-Muller capacity then yields gamma(t) = 1 - F(t) exactly.

Result: Leverage is found exactly in the thermodynamic limit and generated by the single sequence G_l. Exchangeable priors give flat gamma and hyperbolic leverage confined to t near 0. The constructed Reed-Muller simplicity prior delivers gamma(t)=1-F(t) exactly, so any nonincreasing profile and any associated leverage curve is realizable at macroscopic times, escaping the boundary-layer limitation.

Conclusion: A learner's leverage—entropy destroyed per bit of surprisal—is fundamentally determined by a single entropy profile gamma(t). While exchangeable (de Finetti) priors are confined to flat profiles and boundary-layer effects, a polynomial-degree simplicity prior over F2 realizes arbitrary nonincreasing profiles and arbitrary (thermodynamically consistent) leverage curves, demonstrating that intelligent priors achieve leverage above unity at macroscopic scales.

Abstract: A learner holds a prior belief over boolean maps that answer a finite set of $Q$ questions, and receives answers one by one. Each answer costs surprisal and destroys uncertainty, not only about the question asked but every question still unasked. We call the ratio the leverage: table entropy destroyed per bit of surprisal received. It is unit for a uniform prior, but with an intelligent prior can be higher (not lower).
  Averaged over the truth prior and over the question order, the leverage is found exactly, and is generated by one sequence: the mean entropy $G_\ell$ of the answers to $\ell$ questions. Sending the number of input bits to infinity at fixed asked fraction $t = \ell/Q$, the increments of that sequence become a profile $γ(t)$, and initial question entropy $η_0$. The leverage closes to a thermodynamic limit. $L(t) = [η_0 - (1-t)γ(t)]/\int_0^t γ$.
  Exchangeable priors, by de Finetti, all give a flat $γ(t)$ and hence a hyperbolic $L(t)$, their deduction confined to a boundary layer at $t = 0$. We construct a simplicity prior that escapes this, grading Boolean maps by the degree of their polynomial over $\mathbb{F}_2$ and budgeting weight across degree shells by a CDF $F$. Reed-Muller capacity then gives $γ(t) = 1 - F(t)$ exactly, so any nonincreasing profile, and any leverage curve it generates, is realizable at macroscopic times.

</details>


### [227] [Speed Limit for Information Acquisition in Stochastic Learning Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08219)
*Shuta Kobayashi,Andreas Dechant*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper formulates SGD as a Markovian stochastic process and derives a Fisher-information flow speed limit bounding the rate at which trainable parameters acquire information about latent variables in the data-generating process. The bound decomposes information flow into drift and noise contributions and is verified in tractable basis-function linear regression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a quantitative, information-theoretic framework for diagnosing when and how different aspects of the data-generating mechanism are acquired during stochastic learning.

Method: Formulates SGD as a Markovian stochastic process and derives a Fisher-information flow speed limit. The resulting inequality decomposes information flow into drift (deterministic learning forces) and noise (SGD-induced fluctuations) contributions.

Result: The bound is verified in analytically tractable basis-function linear regression, where the information budget predicted by the bound reproduces the ordering and characteristic time scales with which different latent variables are encoded in learned parameters.

Conclusion: Fisher-information speed limits establish a quantitative framework for diagnosing when and how different aspects of the data-generating mechanism are acquired during stochastic learning.

Abstract: Neural networks acquire internal representations through learning. In this work, we formulate stochastic gradient descent (SGD) as a Markovian stochastic process and derive a Fisher-information flow speed limit that bounds the rate at which trainable parameters can acquire information about latent variables in the data-generating process. The resulting inequality decomposes the information flow into drift and noise contributions, thereby quantifying the roles of deterministic learning forces and SGD-induced fluctuations from an information-theoretic perspective. We verify the bound in analytically tractable basis-function linear regression, where the information budget predicted by the bound reproduces the ordering and characteristic time scales with which different latent variables are encoded in the learned parameters. These results establish Fisher-information speed limits as a quantitative framework for diagnosing when and how different aspects of the data-generating mechanism are acquired during stochastic learning.

</details>


### [228] [When does nonreciprocity matter? Scale-dependence and nonequilibrium signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08820)
*Sarah A. M. Loos,Robert L. Jack*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This Perspective paper examines nonreciprocity as a unifying concept for nonequilibrium phenomena across physics, chemistry, and biology, focusing on how nonreciprocal interactions and their irreversible signatures emerge, disappear, or transform under coarse-graining across different scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonreciprocity is a fundamental unifying concept linking diverse nonequilibrium phenomena, but it is scale-dependent—it can emerge or vanish under coarse-graining. A central open challenge is understanding when and how nonreciprocity manifests across different scales via irreversible fluctuations or macroscopic currents.

Method: The paper presents a Perspective-style discussion analyzing the physical origin, fate, and scale-dependence of effective nonreciprocal interactions and their characteristic irreversible dynamics across different length and time scales.

Result: The work clarifies how nonreciprocity manifests on different scales through irreversible fluctuations or macroscopic currents, and addresses the conditions under which effective nonreciprocal interactions emerge or disappear under coarse-graining.

Conclusion: Understanding the scale-dependent nature of nonreciprocity is key to connecting nonequilibrium phenomena across disciplines, and the paper provides a framework for tracing nonreciprocal signatures across scales.

Abstract: Nonreciprocity is increasingly recognized as a unifying concept linking diverse nonequilibrium phenomena found across physics, chemistry, and biology. It gives rise to distinctive behavior including run-and-chase dynamics and spatio-temporal patterns, often associated with a breaking of time-reversal symmetry. However, nonreciprocity and its nonequilibrium signatures are fundamentally scale-dependent, and may emerge or disappear under coarse-graining. A central challenge is therefore to understand when and how nonreciprocity manifests itself on different scales, for example via irreversible fluctuations or macroscopic currents. In this Perspective, we discuss the physical origin and fate of effective nonreciprocal interactions and the characteristic irreversible dynamics they give rise to across scales.

</details>


### [229] [Stochastic Processes as Non-Metric Geodesics in Information Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08870)
*T. Koide,A. van de Venn*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper establishes a one-to-one correspondence between α-connection geodesics on the Gaussian statistical manifold and continuous stochastic processes with time-independent noise. Geodesics in expectation parameters fall into three geometric classes, with the Boundary-connecting class yielding an explicit linear stochastic realization (generalized bridge process) that reduces to Ornstein-Uhlenbeck relaxation or free Brownian diffusion under operational limits. The physical restoring force in OU relaxation determines the α parameter, linking either to scalar curvature or non-metricity — drawing an analogy with the Geometrical Trinity of Gravity. In driven stochastic thermodynamics, the work-minimizing optimal protocol in the slow-driving limit coincides with an expectation geodesic equipped with non-metricity (g, Γ^(1/2), Γ^(-1/2)), highlighting the physical role of non-metricity in information geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge information geometry and stochastic processes by showing that geometric geodesics on statistical manifolds correspond to physically realizable stochastic dynamics, and to demonstrate that the otherwise abstract connection parameter α can be physically constrained through measurable quantities (restoring force), while also exploring the geometric interpretation via scalar curvature vs. non-metricity (Geometrical Trinity analogy) and its implications for optimal control in stochastic thermodynamics.

Method: The authors build a one-to-one mapping between geodesics of the one-parameter family of α-connections on the Gaussian statistical manifold and a class of continuous stochastic processes with time-independent noise intensity. Geodesics in expectation parameters are classified into three geometric categories. The Boundary-connecting class is used to construct an explicit linear stochastic realization with constant diffusion, representing a generalized bridge process. The framework is then applied to driven stochastic thermodynamics to analyze the slow-driving optimal protocol.

Result: Geodesics classify into three geometric types; the Boundary-connecting class yields a generalized bridge process with constant diffusion that reduces to OU relaxation or free Brownian motion under appropriate limits. The restoring force of the resulting OU dynamics determines the α connection parameter, and can be attributed either to scalar curvature or purely to non-metricity depending on the affine connection representation. The work-minimizing optimal protocol in the slow-driving limit coincides with an expectation geodesic of the non-metric manifold (g, Γ^(1/2), Γ^(-1/2)).

Conclusion: This work establishes a deep correspondence between geometric geodesics on statistical manifolds and stochastic dynamics, showing that the connection parameter α has a concrete physical interpretation via the restoring force. It further reveals an analogy with the Geometrical Trinity of Gravity (curvature vs. non-metricity), and demonstrates that non-metricity plays an active, physically relevant role in information geometry by governing optimal protocols in stochastic thermodynamics.

Abstract: We establish a one-to-one correspondence between geodesics associated with the one-parameter family of $α$-connections on the Gaussian statistical manifold and a class of continuous stochastic processes characterized by a time-independent noise intensity. We demonstrate that geodesics in expectation parameters naturally classify into three distinct geometric categories, among which the Boundary-connecting class allows us to construct an explicit linear stochastic realization with constant diffusion, representing a generalized bridge process. This result demonstrates how continuous stochastic processes within this Gaussian class can be extended along geometric curves. Under appropriate operational limits, this generalized bridge process reduces to fundamental stochastic dynamics, either Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) relaxation or free Brownian diffusion. Crucially, the physical restoring force governing the resulting OU relaxation directly determines the underlying connection parameter $α$, providing a concrete physical observable to constrain the manifold geometry. Depending on the chosen affine connection representation, this restoring force can be attributed either to scalar curvature or purely to non-metricity, establishing a direct conceptual analogy with the Geometrical Trinity of Gravity. Furthermore, applying this framework to driven stochastic thermodynamics, we show that the work-minimizing optimal protocol in the slow-driving limit coincides precisely with an expectation geodesic of the statistical manifold with non-metricity equipped with $(g, {}^{(1/2)}Γ, {}^{(-1/2)}Γ)$, highlighting the active physical role of non-metricity in information geometry.

</details>


### [230] [Effects of Interaction Range on Fluid Multicriticality: A Computational Study of an Interconverting Lattice Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09074)
*Thomas J. Longo,Sergey V. Buldyrev,Frédéric Caupin,Mikhail A. Anisimov*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper investigates how varying interaction range affects fluid multicriticality using an interconverting lattice model exhibiting both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality. Through meanfield theory and 3D Monte Carlo simulations with interacting neighbor numbers ranging from 6 to 388, the authors find that nearest-neighbor systems show only two types of multicritical behavior while larger interaction ranges yield four distinct archetypes, with results converging to meanfield predictions as interaction range increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The range of intermolecular interactions is central to phase behavior and critical phenomena. While it is known from Ising model studies that increasing interaction range suppresses critical fluctuations and pushes systems toward meanfield behavior, the role of interaction range in fluid multicriticality—particularly the evolution of competing critical points—remains unexplored.

Method: The authors employ an interconverting lattice model that exhibits both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality (analogous to superfluid 4He-3He mixtures). They systematically vary the number of interacting neighbors Zn from 6 to 388 and analyze the model using both meanfield theory and three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations.

Result: The nearest-neighbor system (Zn=6) exhibits only two types of multicritical behavior, whereas larger interaction ranges produce four distinct archetypes of multicriticality. Simulation results converge to meanfield theory predictions as the number of interacting neighbors tends toward infinity.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a systematic evolution of multicritical behavior with interaction range, showing how increasing interaction range drives the system from fluctuation-dominated behavior toward meanfield predictions, with results discussed within the framework of crossover critical phenomena.

Abstract: The range of intermolecular interactions plays a central role in determining the nature of phase behavior and critical phenomena. It is well established through studies of the Ising model that as interaction range increases, Monte Carlo simulations progressively approach meanfield predictions as the effects of critical fluctuations are suppressed. In this work, we investigate how varying interaction range influences fluid multicriticality using an interconverting lattice model that exhibits both Ising-like liquid-gas criticality and symmetric fluid tricriticality (similar to that in the superfluid $^4$He-$^3$He mixture). This minimal model serves as a representative system for exploring the evolution of competing critical points within a generic framework. We analyze the model using both meanfield theory and three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations while systematically varying the number of interacting neighbors, $Z_n$, from 6 to 388. We find that the system with nearest-neighbor interactions ($Z_n=6$) reveals only two types of multicritical behavior, while for larger interaction ranges, four distinct archetypes emerge. We demonstrate the convergence of the simulation results to those of the meanfield theory as the number of interacting neighbors tends to infinity, and we discuss the results within the framework of crossover critical phenomena.

</details>


### [231] [Large-scale dynamics of integrable quenches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09139)
*David X. Horvath,Bruno Bertini*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper extends the Ballistic Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (BMFT) from quasi-equilibrium settings to integrable quench problems, deriving a saddle-point path integral that yields partial differential equations for the time evolution of the full-counting-statistics (FCS) of conserved charges. The theory is validated by recovering known exact results for free fermionic systems and the interacting cellular automaton Rule 54, with the latter being completed across the entire ballistic window.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Ballistic Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (BMFT) is a powerful path-integral framework for describing large-scale correlations in interacting integrable systems, but it has previously only been applied to quasi-equilibrium settings. The authors aim to extend this framework to integrable quench problems, which are far-from-equilibrium scenarios, thereby broadening the applicability of BMFT to non-equilibrium dynamics.

Method: The approach identifies the relevant path integral describing the FCS dynamics of a conserved charge in integrable quenches, solves it via the saddle-point method, and derives a set of partial differential equations that govern the time evolution of the FCS. These equations are then solved explicitly for two benchmark systems: free fermionic systems and the interacting cellular automaton Rule 54.

Result: For both free fermionic systems and Rule 54, the derived equations reproduce known exact results for the FCS. Additionally, for Rule 54, the construction completes the solution of the FCS across the entire ballistic window, filling gaps left by previous approaches.

Conclusion: The extension of BMFT to integrable quench problems provides a general and systematic framework for computing the FCS of conserved charges out of equilibrium. The successful validation against exact results and the completion of the Rule 54 solution demonstrate the framework's robustness and its potential for broader applications in non-equilibrium integrable systems.

Abstract: The Ballistic Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory (BMFT) is a path-integral based approach to treat large-scale correlations in interacting integrable systems which, so far, has only been applied to quasi-equilibrium settings. In this paper we extend this approach to integrable quench problems and, to illustrate it, we compute the time evolution of the full-counting-statistics (FCS) of a conserved charge. We identify the relevant path integral, solve it via saddle-point, and obtain a set of partial differential equations describing the dynamics of the FCS. We solve these equations explicitly for free fermionic systems and for the interacting cellular automaton Rule 54. In both cases, we recover the known exact results. For Rule 54, our construction moreover completes the solution across the entire ballistic window.

</details>

# physics.atm-clus [[Back]](#toc)

### [232] [Core-Level Spectroscopy Decodes Bond-Alternation Dynamics of Cyclo[18]Carbon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06916)
*Minrui Wei,Zeyu Liu,Sheng-Yu Wang,Jun-Rong Zhang,Lu Zhang,Guoyan Ge,Weijie Hua*

Main category: physics.atm-clus

TL;DR: The paper uses first-principles simulations on cyclo[18]carbon to investigate whether time-resolved X-ray spectroscopy can probe global BLA dynamics. Core ionization is found to symmetrize the ground-state double-well potential, and calculated X-ray spectra (C1s IPs and NEXAFS π* peaks) show large, quantitative sensitivity to bond-length variations, providing a spectroscopy–structure dictionary for future ultrafast experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-resolved X-ray spectroscopy is powerful for probing local atomic environments, but it remains an open question whether localized core excitations can report on global collective distortions such as bond-length alternation dynamics. C18 serves as an ideal minimal model to address this long-standing issue.

Method: First-principles simulations mapping two-dimensional potential energy surfaces of C18 in its polyynic (D9h) ground state and cumulenic (D18h) transition state, combined with calculated core-level X-ray spectra (C1s ionization potentials and NEXAFS) as a function of the BLA coordinate.

Result: Core ionization symmetrizes the ground-state double-well in C18. C1s ionization potentials vary by up to 2.4 eV across the BLA coordinate (1.1–1.4 Å), with 0.9 eV variation between minima from different functionals; NEXAFS π* peaks shift by up to 4 eV over the same BLA range, demonstrating pronounced spectral sensitivity.

Conclusion: The predicted quantitative X-ray signatures establish a spectroscopy–structure dictionary capable of decoding transient structures and monitoring bond-alternation dynamics in real time in future ultrafast X-ray experiments.

Abstract: The advent of X-ray free-electron lasers and high-harmonic generation has made time-resolved X-ray spectroscopy a powerful tool for probing local atomic environments, yet whether localized core excitations can report on global collective distortions remains open. Cyclo[18]carbon (C$_{18}$), with its polyynic ground state (D$_\text{9h}$) and cumulenic transition state (D$_\text{18h}$), provides an ideal model to address this long-standing issue in bond-length alternation (BLA) dynamics. Mapping two-dimensional potential energy surfaces by first-principles simulations, we find that core ionization symmetrizes the ground-state double-well potential along the BLA coordinate. Our calculated X-ray spectra reveal remarkable sensitivity to bond-length variations: C1s ionization potentials vary by up to 2.4~eV across the BLA coordinate (1.1--1.4~Å), with a 0.9~eV variation for minima predicted by different functionals, while NEXAFS $π^*$ peaks shift by up to 4~eV across the same coordinate. These predicted signatures provide a quantitative spectroscopy--structure dictionary for decoding transient structures in future ultrafast X-ray experiments and monitoring bond-alternation dynamics in real time.

</details>
